### [1] [High-Efficiency Wakefield Acceleration of Positrons via a Slit Plasma Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04261)
*Yu-Jie Wang, Xing-Long Zhu, Hong-Kang Zuo, Zheng-Ming Sheng*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes slit plasma wakefield acceleration (SPWA), in which a relativistic electron beam in a slit channel between two parallel plasma slabs excites crossing plasma-electron filamentary structures and tailored wakefields that simultaneously accelerate and transversely focus a trailing positron bunch. A theoretical model and 3D particle-in-cell simulations support the scheme, showing nearly 100% positron trapping efficiency with low emittance growth and narrow energy spread.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasma-based wakefield acceleration has been highly successful for electrons, but efficient positron acceleration remains a long-standing challenge because positrons experience severe defocusing in the accelerating phases of such structures. The work aims to provide a robust high-efficiency positron acceleration approach relevant to future compact electron-positron colliders and high-energy positron applications.

Method: The proposed slit plasma wakefield acceleration (SPWA) scheme uses a relativistic electron beam propagating through a slit channel formed by two parallel plasma slabs. This excites crossing plasma-electron filamentary structures within the slit, generating intense wakefields tailored to provide simultaneous longitudinal acceleration and transverse focusing for a trailing positron bunch. A theoretical model is developed to characterize the wakefield structure responsible for positron acceleration and focusing, and three-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations are used to validate the model.

Result: Three-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations validate the theoretical model and show that the SPWA scheme achieves a positron trapping efficiency of nearly 100% while maintaining low emittance growth and narrow energy spread for an injected positron beam. Specific numerical values for energy gain, final emittance, and energy spread are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The findings establish SPWA as a new and robust approach to high-efficiency positron acceleration, with promising implications for future compact electron-positron colliders and high-energy positron applications.

Abstract: Although plasma-based wakefield acceleration has achieved remarkable success for electrons, efficient positron acceleration remains a long standing challenge, as positrons suffer severe defocusing in the accelerating phases of such structures. Here, we propose a slit plasma wakefield acceleration (SPWA) scheme, in which a relativistic electron beam propagating through a slit channel formed by two parallel plasma slabs excites crossing plasma-electron filamentary structures within the slit, generating intense wakefields tailored to simultaneously provide longitudinal acceleration and transverse focusing for a trailing positron bunch. A theoretical model is developed to characterize the wakefield structure responsible for positron acceleration and focusing. Three-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations validate the model, and demonstrate that the SPWA scheme achieves a positron trapping efficiency of nearly 100%, while maintaining low emittance growth and narrow energy spread for an injected positron beam. These findings establish a new and robust approach to high-efficiency positron acceleration, with promising implications for future compact electron-positron colliders and high-energy positron applications.

</details>


### [2] [Hydrogen Electron Cyclotron Resonance Dissociator Demonstrator for the Project 8 Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03972)
*Manjinder Oueslati, Walter Pettus, Anna Reine, Hamish Robertson*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Project 8 uses cyclotron radiation emission spectroscopy (CRES) for a model-independent neutrino-mass measurement from tritium beta decay, targeting 40 meV/c^2. The abstract describes a tritium beamline and a prototype electron cyclotron resonance dissociator, with engineering, gas-diagnostic, and hydrogen-plasma spectroscopy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by a model-independent neutrino-mass measurement via tritium beta decay using Project 8's CRES technique, with a final electron-weighted neutrino-mass sensitivity goal of 40 meV/c^2.

Method: Project 8 is developing a beamline that dissociates molecular tritium, surface-cools atoms through accommodation, evaporatively cools and slows them, and traps them in a CRES resonant cavity. It is prototyping an electron cyclotron resonance dissociator using a tunable 1 kW 2.45 GHz RF generator and electromagnets to ignite a hydrogen plasma in an aluminum resonant cavity, designed for high atom flux (~10^19 atoms/second) near room temperature. The abstract reports engineering development, gas diagnostics, and spectroscopy data from the hydrogen plasma.

Result: The prototype ECR dissociator is designed to produce a high atom flux of approximately 10^19 atoms/second near room temperature. Engineering development, gas diagnostics, and spectroscopy data from the hydrogen plasma are presented, but the abstract does not provide quantitative spectroscopy results, achieved flux, or final neutrino-mass measurement outcomes.

Conclusion: The ECR dissociator and beamline development support progress toward Project 8's final electron-weighted neutrino-mass sensitivity goal of 40 meV/c^2. The abstract does not state a completed neutrino-mass measurement or a demonstrated sensitivity result.

Abstract: Project 8, a neutrino mass experiment, employs the novel frequency technique of cyclotron radiation emission spectroscopy (CRES) to perform a model independent measurement of neutrino mass via tritium beta decay. Our pioneering experiment will consist of a beamline that dissociates molecular tritium, surface cools atoms through accommodation, evaporatively cools and slows atoms, and traps them in a CRES resonant cavity to advance towards our sensitivity goal of 40 meV/c^2 for our final electron-weighted neutrino mass measurement. We are prototyping an electron cyclotron resonance (ECR) dissociator that uses a tunable 1 kW 2.45 GHz RF generator and electromagnets to ignite a hydrogen plasma in an aluminum resonant cavity. Our ECR source is designed to produce a high atom flux (~10^19 atoms/second) near room temperature. We present the engineering development, gas diagnostics, and spectroscopy data from our hydrogen plasma.

</details>


### [3] [LADA: A differential algebra-based simulation package for arbitrary-order charged-particle and light optics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01770)
*Dmitry Varentsov, Sergey Litvinov, Maksym Miski-Oglu, Sergiy Trotsenko*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: LADA is a Python differential-algebra code for charged-particle and light-optical transfer maps, combining map methods with ion stopping, scattering, charge-state and fragmentation models, RF field integration, and section-wise tracking; it is validated to order nine and benchmarked against COSY.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation. It implies a need for an accessible Python framework that combines differential-algebra transfer maps with ion-material, charge-state, fragmentation, field-integration and tracking capabilities, while retaining analytic parameter dependence so beam observables and sensitivities can be obtained without particle tracking at every setting.

Method: LADA computes charged-particle and light-optical transfer maps by differential algebra in Python with run-time order selection. It combines map methods with ATIMA-based stopping, straggling and scattering; GLOBAL, CHARGE and ETACHA charge-state models; EPAX fragmentation; six-dimensional integration through measured or computed RF fields with reference-energy rebasing; and section-wise particle tracking with aperture and RF-window losses. Maps retain analytic dependence on external parameters, and contraction with input moments yields beam observables and sensitivities. Lattices are imported from COSY, MAD-X, Xsuite and MIRKO with per-record status and code comparisons. NumPy is the only required dependency.

Result: The code is validated through order nine. On the HHT benchmark, the optional compiled DA kernel on eight cores matches COSY performance at orders 3 and 5 and is 1.8 and 2.9 times faster at orders 7 and 9. Applications include the SIBAF irradiation facility through CALINA, proton microscopy, and beam-line studies at GSI and FAIR.

Conclusion: LADA provides an integrated Python differential-algebra framework for charged-particle and light-optical transfer maps, with broad lattice import, physics models, tracking and parameter-sensitivity capabilities, requiring only NumPy and demonstrating competitive or faster performance than COSY on the reported benchmark.

Abstract: LADA computes charged-particle and light-optical transfer maps by differential algebra in Python, with run-time order selection validated through order nine. It combines these established map methods with ATIMA-based stopping, straggling and scattering, the GLOBAL, CHARGE and ETACHA charge-state models, EPAX fragmentation, six-dimensional integration through measured or computed RF fields with reference-energy rebasing, and section-wise particle tracking with aperture and RF-window losses. Maps retain analytic dependence on external parameters; contraction with input moments gives beam observables and their sensitivities without particle tracking at each setting. Lattice import from COSY, MAD-X, Xsuite and MIRKO provides per-record status and comparisons with available codes. NumPy is the only required dependency. On the HHT benchmark, the optional compiled DA kernel on eight cores matches COSY in performance at orders 3 and 5 and is 1.8 and 2.9 times faster at orders 7 and 9. Applications include the SIBAF irradiation facility through CALINA, proton microscopy and beam-line studies at GSI and FAIR.

</details>


### [4] [Inverse modelling of a laser-plasma accelerator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00108)
*Mykyta Lenivenko, Arnaud Beck, Hayg Guler, Gueladio Kane, Sophie Kazamias, Viacheslav Kubytskyi, Bruno Lucas, Marceau Pintonato, Jana Serhal, Slava Smartsev, Kalyani Swain, Kevin Cassou*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper addresses the inverse problem of inferring laser-plasma accelerator settings from measured electron beams. An ensemble of mixture density networks predicts multiple possible settings with probabilities and uncertainties; predictions are accurate on average (R² = 0.91), but about two-thirds of beams have two competing solutions linked by nearly the same a₀²p₁. A second measurement at a deliberately different plasma density is the most effective way to resolve the ambiguity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electron beams from a laser-plasma accelerator depend non-linearly on laser and gas settings. Prior work used particle-in-cell simulations and machine-learning models to predict the beam from the settings; this paper addresses the reverse problem: given a measured beam, infer which settings produced it. The solution is not guaranteed to be unique.

Method: The authors infer three settings: gas pressure p₁, normalised vector potential a₀, and nitrogen concentration c_N₂. They train an ensemble of mixture density networks that, for each beam, outputs several possible setting sets with probabilities and uncertainties, and test them on simulated beams with realistic measurement noise. They also use a forward model as a check and test two ambiguity-resolution approaches: adding the full electron energy spectrum, and adding a second measurement at a deliberately different plasma density, evaluated with paired PIC simulations.

Result: On average, predictions are accurate (R² = 0.91), but for about two-thirds of beams the inferred distribution has two competing solutions. These share nearly the same value of a₀²p₁, which controls laser self-focusing and, in earlier PALLAS studies, when electron injection starts; keeping this quantity fixed gives beams practically indistinguishable with realistic diagnostics. The forward model removes unphysical solutions but cannot choose between the two plausible ones. Adding the full electron energy spectrum improves the mean prediction (R² = 0.94), mostly through a₀, and changes the weights of the two solutions but does not move them. A second measurement at a deliberately different plasma density reduces the error on a₀ by a factor of 2.6 in paired PIC simulations, whereas repeating the measurement at the same density brings almost no gain.

Conclusion: The inverse problem is generally non-unique, with a common two-solution ambiguity governed by a₀²p₁. A forward-model check cannot resolve this ambiguity, and adding the full electron energy spectrum only improves accuracy and changes solution weights without separating the competing solutions. A second measurement at a deliberately different plasma density is more effective, substantially reducing the a₀ error, while repeating at the same density is ineffective.

Abstract: The electron beams of a laser-plasma accelerator depend non-linearly on the laser and gas settings. This dependence is explored using particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations, and machine-learning models trained on these simulations predict the beam from the settings. Here we address the reverse problem: given a measured beam, which settings produced it? We infer three of them: the gas pressure $p\_1$, the normalised vector potential $a\_0$ and the nitrogen concentration $c\_{N\_2}$. The solution is not guaranteed to be unique. We train an ensemble of mixture density networks that, for each beam, output several possible sets of settings with their probabilities and uncertainties, and test them on simulated beams with realistic measurement noise. On average, the predictions are accurate ($R^2 = 0.91$), yet for about two-thirds of the beams the inferred distribution has two competing solutions. These share nearly the same value of $a\_0^2 p\_1$, which controls how strongly the laser focuses itself in the plasma and, in earlier PALLAS studies, when electrons start to be injected. Keeping this quantity fixed gives beams that are practically indistinguishable with realistic diagnostics. A forward model used as a check removes unphysical solutions but cannot choose between the two plausible ones. We then test two ways to resolve the ambiguity. Adding the full electron energy spectrum improves the mean prediction ($R^2 = 0.94$), mostly through $a\_0$. It changes the weights of the two solutions but does not move them. A second measurement at a deliberately different plasma density is more effective. In paired PIC simulations, it reduces the error on $a\_0$ by a factor of 2.6, whereas repeating the measurement at the same density brings almost no gain.

</details>


### [5] [Numerical Study of Two-Frequency Heating in ECR Ion Source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02814)
*E. Yu. Kidanova, V. E. Mironov, D. K. Pugachev*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A numerical NAM-ECRIS study of two-frequency heating in the DECRIS-PM ECR ion source compares single-frequency 14.51 GHz heating with dual-frequency 14.51 GHz plus 13 GHz heating at equal total power. Global plasma parameters change little, but the peak density of highly anisotropic electrons falls by about 20% and their distribution broadens radially; this is attributed to lower-frequency-wave-induced randomization of electron gyromotion, suppressing micro-instabilities and improving ion production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-frequency heating is widely used to improve electron cyclotron resonance ion source performance, but its underlying physical mechanisms remain insufficiently understood. The paper aims to clarify the TFH regime numerically.

Method: The authors use the NAM-ECRIS code to simulate the DECRIS-PM ECR ion source. They compare single-frequency heating at 14.51 GHz and 500 W with two-frequency heating at 14.51 GHz and 13 GHz using 450 W and 50 W, respectively, at equal total power.

Result: Global plasma parameters remain largely unchanged between the two cases. In the TFH regime, the peak density of highly anisotropic electrons with E > 1 keV and Vperp/Vlong > 10 decreases by approximately 20%, and their distribution broadens radially. The abstract does not provide quantitative details on the magnitude of improved ion production or experimental validation.

Conclusion: The reduction in electron anisotropy in TFH is attributed to the randomizing effect of the lower-frequency wave on electron gyromotion. This suppresses plasma micro-instabilities and leads to improved ion production, according to the abstract.

Abstract: The two-frequency heating (TFH) method is widely used to improve the performance of electron cyclotron resonance (ECR) ion sources, yet its physical mechanisms remain insufficiently understood. In this work, the TFH regime in the DECRIS-PM ECR ion source is studied numerically using the NAM-ECRIS code. Single-frequency heating at 14.51 GHz (500 W) is compared to two-frequency heating at 14.51 GHz and 13 GHz (450 W + 50 W) at equal total power. It is found that while the global plasma parameters remain largely unchanged, the peak density of highly anisotropic electrons (E > 1 keV, Vperp/Vlong > 10) decreases by ~20% and their distribution broadens radially in the TFH regime. This reduction in electron anisotropy is attributed to the randomizing effect of the lower-frequency wave on electron gyromotion, which suppresses plasma micro-instabilities and leads to improved ion production.

</details>

*K. Reindersma, M. Malaussena, S. Anantaram, K. Patel, V. Kumar, N. Babusis, M. Slipchenko, A. Shashurin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The abstract reports using Thomson coherent microwave scattering to measure photoionization rates in O2 and N2 at 800 nm across about three orders of magnitude, from roughly mid-10^9 to mid-10^12 s^-1, at laser intensities near 10^13–10^14 W/cm^2, a range relevant to filamentation physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract identifies practical relevance to filamentation physics as the motivation for characterizing photoionization rates in oxygen and nitrogen at 800 nm over this intensity range; it does not provide further motivation.

Method: The method is Thomson coherent microwave scattering (CMS), used to characterize photoionization rates in oxygen and nitrogen at 800 nm.

Result: Photoionization rates were characterized over approximately three orders of magnitude, from about the mid-10^9 s^-1 range to the mid-10^12 s^-1 range, corresponding to laser intensities in the vicinity of 10^13–10^14 W/cm^2.

Conclusion: The abstract indicates that CMS can characterize photoionization rates in oxygen and nitrogen over a range of significant practical relevance to filamentation physics; no further conclusions are provided.

Abstract: In this study, we report on the use of Thomson coherent microwave scattering (CMS) to characterize photoionization rates in oxygen and nitrogen at 800 nm over a range spanning approximately three orders of magnitude, from about mid-10$^9$ s$^{-1}$ range to the to mid-10$^{12}$ s$^{-1}$ range, corresponding to laser intensities in the vicinity of 10$^{13}$-10$^{14}$ W/cm$^2$, a range of significant practical relevance to filamentation physics.

</details>


### [7] [Influence of Laguerre-Gaussian pulse mode purity on positron acceleration in nonlinear wakefields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06420)
*Lars Reichwein*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Particle-in-cell simulations indicate that Laguerre-Gaussian-driven positron wakefield acceleration depends on mode order and purity: some mode impurities are mitigated as the wake propagates through plasma, while phase differences between constituent modes can strongly affect acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positron acceleration in wakefields is a crucial challenge. Laguerre-Gaussian laser pulses have been proposed as drivers because their inherent field structure provides corresponding accelerating and focusing regions.

Method: The authors use particle-in-cell simulations to study how mode order and mode purity affect the positron acceleration process when Laguerre-Gaussian laser pulses are used as drivers.

Result: Some mode impurities can be mitigated during propagation of the wake through the plasma, but phase differences between the modes composing the total pulse can strongly affect the acceleration process. The abstract does not provide quantitative results or identify specific mode orders.

Conclusion: Mode purity and the relative phase of the modes composing the Laguerre-Gaussian pulse are important for positron wakefield acceleration. Impurity mitigation during plasma propagation should be considered together with inter-mode phase effects; detailed design criteria and quantitative conclusions are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: The acceleration of positrons in wakefields remains a crucial challenge. One proposal has been to use Laguerre-Gaussian laser pulses as drivers, which provide corresponding accelerating and focusing regions due to their inherent field structure. Here, we study the effect of mode order and mode purity on the acceleration process by means of particle-in-cell simulations. We find that some mode impurities can be mitigated during the propagation of the wake through the plasma, but also that phase difference between the modes that the total pulse is composed of can strongly affect the acceleration process.

</details>


### [8] [First ray-tracing simulations of mode conversion using metaplectic geometrical optics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06114)
*Rui Kai Lee, Valerian H. Hall-Chen, I. Y. Dodin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces metaplectic geometrical optics (MGO) for reduced simulation of linear mode conversion in magnetically confined fusion plasmas. In a one-dimensional O–X mode-conversion test near the critical density, MGO removes spurious geometrical-optics singularities and agrees well with an approximate analytical solution, enabling fast ray-tracing modeling when the symplectic ray curvature R^{-1} is small.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advanced radiofrequency power-deposition scenarios, especially those using electron Bernstein waves, involve linear mode conversion in regions where waves reflect. Full-wave simulations are time-consuming, while geometrical-optics modeling is typically restricted to single waves and produces spurious singularities at cutoffs, so a fast reduced model is needed.

Method: The algorithm uses metaplectic geometrical optics, which applies metaplectic transformations to eliminate geometrical-optics singularities under the condition that the symplectic curvature of the rays R^{-1} is small. As a proof of concept, the authors simulate one-dimensional O–X mode conversion driven by a plasma-density gradient near the critical density and compare the numerical MGO result with an available approximate analytical solution, without using approximate analytical formulas for conversion efficiency in the simulation.

Result: Numerical MGO shows good agreement with the approximate analytical theory for one-dimensional O–X mode conversion. The abstract reports this as the first reduced simulation overcoming the stated limitations; quantitative error metrics, computational-performance data, and higher-dimensional tests are not provided.

Conclusion: MGO provides a basis for fast and accurate ray-tracing modeling of linear mode conversion, including cases relevant to radiofrequency power deposition, whenever R^{-1} << 1. The demonstrated scope is a one-dimensional proof of concept.

Abstract: Advanced scenarios of radiofrequency power deposition into magnetically confined fusion plasmas, particularly those using electron Bernstein waves, involve linear mode conversion (energy transfer between separate dispersion branches) in regions where the waves experience reflection. Full-wave simulations of this conversion are time-consuming, and geometrical-optics (GO) modeling is problematic because it is typically restricted to single waves and leads to spurious singularities at cutoffs. Here, we report the first reduced simulations that overcome these limitations without resorting to approximate analytical formulas for the conversion efficiency. The algorithm is based on the recently developed metaplectic geometrical optics (MGO), which eliminates GO's spurious singularities by metaplectic transformations, provided that the symplectic curvature of the rays $R^{-1}$ is small. To prove the concept, we simulate one-dimensional O--X mode conversion caused by the plasma-density gradient near the critical density, a problem for which an approximate analytical solution is available and can be compared with. Numerical MGO demonstrates good agreement with said theory. This opens a way for fast and accurate ray-tracing modeling of any linear mode conversion at $R^{-1} \ll 1$.

</details>


### [9] [Spontaneous Enhancement of Inward Impurity Pinch during Massive Impurity Injection in Tokamak Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05850)
*L. Zeng, W. Xia, D. Hu, T. Tang, X. Zhu, S. Zhao, T. Shi, D. Chen, Y. Duan, L. Xu, G. Li, G. Zuo, J. Hu, X. Gao, Y. Sun, Z. Gao*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: On EAST, massive impurity injection causes a spontaneous strengthening of the neoclassical inward impurity pinch before the fast quench, marked by a rapid magnetic-island rotation-frequency shift toward the electron diamagnetic direction within a few hundred microseconds. An extended KDG model attributes this to impurity-driven increases in bulk ion collisionality, banana-regime transition, modified parallel viscosity, and a radial electric field excursion of about -10 kV/m consistent with experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and explain the spontaneous enhancement of the inward impurity pinch observed prior to the fast quench under massive impurity injection on the EAST tokamak, and to assess its implications for ITER disruption mitigation.

Method: EAST tokamak observations of magnetic-island rotation frequency and an extended KDG model analysis of impurity-induced bulk ion collisionality, banana-regime transition, nonlinear parallel-viscosity modification, radial electric field, plasma flow, and electrostatic potential response.

Result: The transition features a rapid shift of magnetic-island rotation frequency toward the electron diamagnetic direction within a few hundred microseconds. The extended KDG model shows that impurity-induced increased bulk ion collisionality can drive a banana-regime transition, nonlinearly modify parallel viscosity, and produce a radial electric field excursion of approximately -10 kV/m, consistent with experiment. The self-organized plasma-flow and electrostatic-potential response can modify parallel friction and further strengthen the neoclassical inward impurity pinch.

Conclusion: The identified mechanism is expected to enhance impurity penetration and thereby improve the overall effectiveness of ITER disruption mitigation; the abstract does not provide quantitative projections for impurity penetration or mitigation performance.

Abstract: A spontaneous enhancement of inward impurity pinch is identified prior to the fast quench under massive impurity injection on the EAST tokamak. The transition is marked by a rapid shift of the magnetic-island rotation frequency toward the electron diamagnetic direction within a few hundred microseconds. An extended KDG model shows that the impurity-induced increase in bulk ion collisionality can drive a transition across banana regime, nonlinearly modifying the parallel viscosity and producing a radial electric field excursion of approximately -10 kV/m, consistent with experiment. The associated self-organized response of the plasma flow and electrostatic potential can modify the parallel friction and further strengthen the neoclassical inward impurity pinch. Projections for ITER disruption mitigation suggest that this mechanism is expected to enhance impurity penetration, thereby improving the overall effectiveness of mitigation.

</details>


### [10] [Renormalisation of Hydrogen Partition Function and Saha Ionisation Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03671)
*George Savvidy, Pengming Zhang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper addresses the divergent neutral-hydrogen partition function in the Saha ionization equation by proposing a regularization and renormalization procedure, then uses the resulting well-defined partition function to compute level populations and ionization states versus temperature and pressure, recovering the Saha limit at low temperature and deviations at higher temperature, with application to the solar core and atmosphere.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The neutral-hydrogen partition function, an important quantity in the Saha ionization equation, is divergent because the degeneracy factor of excited n-states grows rapidly, causing immense uncertainty.

Method: The authors suggest a regularization and renormalization procedure for the partition function of neutral hydrogen atoms.

Result: The renormalized partition function is well defined and allows calculation of energy-level populations and the ionization state of hydrogen plasma as functions of temperature and pressure. In the low-temperature limit, the behavior smoothly transforms to the Saha equation; at higher temperatures, the renormalized ionization equation describes deviation from the Saha equation. The application of the renormalized ionization equation to the solar core and atmosphere is considered.

Conclusion: The proposed regularization and renormalization procedure yields a well-defined neutral-hydrogen partition function and a renormalized ionization equation that recovers the Saha equation at low temperature, predicts deviations at higher temperature, and is applied to the solar core and atmosphere.

Abstract: An important physical quantity that is part of the Saha ionisation equation is the partition function of the neutral hydrogen atoms. An immense uncertainty is caused by the divergency of the neutral atom partition function arising due to the fast growing degeneracy factor of its excited n-state. We suggested a regularisation and renormalisation procedure for the partition function of the neutral hydrogen atoms. The renormalised partition function is well defined and allows to calculate the population of the energy levels and the ionisation state of the hydrogen plasma as a function of its temperature and pressure. In the low-temperature limit the behaviour of these functions smoothly transforms to the Saha equation, which is used in the description of the low-temperature plasma. At higher temperatures the renormalised ionisation equation describes the deviation from the Saha equation. The application of the renormalised ionisation equation to the solar core and atmosphere is considered.

</details>


### [11] [Electron Kinetics in the Sheath of Atmospheric Pressure Plasmas in Helium and Helium:H$_2$O Vapor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05653)
*Uwe Kortshagen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A hybrid fluid–Monte Carlo study shows that electron kinetics in atmospheric-pressure helium and helium–water-vapor sheaths can be local or nonlocal, controlled by the ratio of electron energy relaxation length to sheath thickness; nonlocal conditions strongly reduce electron mean energy and excitation/ionization rates near the wall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Boltzmann solvers generally do not adequately describe electron kinetics in the sheath and near plasma-surface interfaces because they neglect the sheath electric field and electron losses to the surface, even though the EEDF governs electron-induced reaction rates in the bulk, sheath, and at the interface.

Method: The authors use a hybrid model combining a fluid description of the collisional sheath with a particle kinetic Monte Carlo model for electrons to investigate atmospheric-pressure plasmas in pure helium and helium–water-vapor mixtures. The floating wall potential is determined by accounting for electron diffusion through the sheath and the non-Maxwellian EEDF.

Result: Electron kinetics in the sheath exhibit both local and nonlocal behavior even at atmospheric pressure, with the transition governed primarily by the ratio of the electron energy relaxation length to sheath thickness. At high plasma densities, where the sheath is thinner than the relaxation length, EEDFs approach the nonlocal limit and electron kinetic energy changes significantly toward the wall. Adding water vapor reduces the energy relaxation length and shifts kinetics toward the local regime. In the nonlocal regime, electron mean kinetic energy decreases substantially toward the wall, with reductions of more than an order of magnitude in electron-impact excitation and ionization rate coefficients.

Conclusion: Modeling electron-induced processes in sheaths and at plasma-surface interfaces requires accounting for nonlocal electron kinetics and electron losses to the surface, even at atmospheric pressure.

Abstract: The electron energy distribution function (EEDF) plays a central role in determining the rates of electron-induced reactions in the bulk plasma, the sheath, and at the plasma-surface interface. However, the electron kinetics within the sheath and near the surface are generally not well described by conventional Boltzmann solvers, which neglect the sheath electric field and electron losses to the surface. In this study, we investigate electron kinetics in atmospheric pressure plasmas in pure helium and helium-water vapor mixtures using a hybrid model that combines a fluid description of the collisional sheath with a particle kinetic Monte Carlo model for electrons. The floating wall potential is determined taking into account electron diffusion through the sheath and the non-Maxwellian EEDF. Our results demonstrate that electron kinetics within the sheath can exhibit both local and nonlocal behavior, even at atmospheric pressure. The transition between these regimes is governed primarily by the ratio of the electron energy relaxation length to the sheath thickness. At high plasma densities, where the sheath thickness becomes smaller than the electron energy relaxation length, the EEDFs approach the nonlocal limit, exhibiting significant changes in electron kinetic energy toward the wall. The addition of water vapor reduces the energy relaxation length and shifts the electron kinetics toward the local regime. In the nonlocal regime, the electron mean kinetic energy decreases substantially toward the wall, accompanied by reductions of more than an order of magnitude in electron-impact excitation and ionization rate coefficients. These findings demonstrate that modeling of electron-induced processes in the sheath and at plasma-surface interfaces requires accounting for nonlocal electron kinetics and electron losses to the surface, even at atmospheric pressure.

</details>


### [12] [A trust layer for fast pedestal-stability surrogates: calibrated error bars, out-of-distribution alarms and few-shot recalibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06597)
*Amin Vakhshouri*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a model-agnostic trust layer for machine-learning peeling-ballooning stability surrogates, combining conformal error bars, out-of-distribution alarms that route doubtful queries to physics codes, and recalibration using a few simulations of a new design. It is tested on JET-like ideal-MHD data, resistive BOUT++ surrogates, SPARC/DIII-D equilibria, and a custom neural-operator surrogate, with pre-registered hypotheses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast surrogate models are mostly point estimators and do not report how wrong they may be or whether a query is outside their training distribution. For a fusion company designing a new device, essentially every equilibrium of interest is outside existing surrogates' training data, making uncertainty and out-of-distribution handling essential.

Method: A model-agnostic trust layer consisting of conformal error bars, out-of-distribution alarms that route doubtful cases to the physics code, and recalibration from a few simulations of a new design. The authors test it with pre-registered hypotheses on a large public dataset of JET-like equilibria with ideal-MHD growth rates, resistive BOUT++ surrogates, equilibria from the SPARC library and DIII-D coil set, and their own neural-operator surrogate.

Result: On JET-like ideal-MHD data, error bars have nominal coverage in distribution but fall short under a plasma-shape shift; a few dozen simulations of the new shape or routing alarm-flagged cases restores coverage. On resistive BOUT++ surrogates, bars remain calibrated on held-out runs, alarms flag tokamaks absent from training, and a scenario scan is screened in seconds instead of hours. Across SPARC-library and DIII-D-coil-set equilibria, a surrogate trained on the authors' graded BOUT++ labels beats the baseline on unseen equilibria, whereas a surrogate trained elsewhere ranks the same cases backwards and its alarm declines them. The harness exposed two input defects in the authors' neural-operator surrogate, and fixing them closed its gap to a simple network. For every surrogate tested, coverage stays near target, with bar width set by that surrogate's own error. Not every pre-registered hypothesis held, and those results and limitations are reported.

Conclusion: The trust layer provides conformal uncertainty, out-of-distribution routing, and recalibration for stability surrogates, keeping coverage near target across tested surrogates and supporting screening of new designs. Coverage can degrade under distribution shift but is restored by limited new simulations or alarm-based routing. Some pre-registered hypotheses did not hold, and the paper reports those failures and the limits of the approach.

Abstract: Machine-learning surrogates of linear peeling-ballooning stability now run in milliseconds, but they are mostly deployed as point estimators: they do not say how wrong they may be, or when a query lies outside what they were trained on. For a fusion company designing a new device, every equilibrium of interest is outside the training data of existing surrogates, so this is the case that matters. We describe a model-agnostic trust layer for stability surrogates, made of conformal error bars, out-of-distribution alarms that route doubtful cases to the physics code, and recalibration from a few simulations of a new design, and we test it with pre-registered hypotheses. On a large public dataset of JET-like equilibria with ideal-MHD growth rates, the error bars hold their nominal coverage in distribution and fall short under a shift in plasma shape; a few dozen simulations of the new shape, or routing the cases the alarms flag, restore it. On resistive BOUT++ surrogates, the bars stay calibrated on held-out runs, the alarm flags tokamaks absent from training, and a scenario scan is screened in seconds instead of hours. Across equilibria from the SPARC library and the DIII-D coil set, a surrogate trained on our own graded BOUT++ labels beats the baseline on unseen equilibria, while a surrogate trained elsewhere ranks the same cases backwards and its alarm declines them. Building our own neural-operator surrogate, the harness exposed two input defects whose fix closed its gap to a simple network. For every surrogate tested, the trust layer keeps coverage near target, with a bar width set by that surrogate's own error. Not every pre-registered hypothesis held, and we report those results and the limits of all of them.

</details>


### [13] [Single-crystal X-ray diffraction of BC8 carbon near terapascal pressures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06045)
*Liang Sun, Bo Chen, Zhiyu He, Pin Yang, Zanyang Guan, Guo Jia, Fan Zhang, Yi Yang, Bo Xu, Pei Guo, Yonggang Liu, Wei Fan, Tao Xi, Yulong Li, Lingbiao Meng, Wei Kang, Zhiyong Xie, Yuqiong Tu, Cheng Ji, Xiuguang Huang, Zhongjing Chen, Ji Yan, Jiamin Yang, Wenge Yang, Toshimori Sekine, Zongqing Zhao, Jiayu Dai, Ho-Kwang Mao*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper reports the first direct crystallographic identification of single-crystal BC8 carbon, formed from molten carbon during laser-driven decaying-shock compression of [100]-oriented diamond loaded above 1.1 TPa and identified using in situ orientation-resolved single-crystal X-ray diffraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whether carbon forms crystalline structures beyond diamond at terapascal pressures remains unresolved; the denser BC8 phase was predicted for nearly four decades but lacked direct crystallographic evidence.

Method: Laser-driven decaying-shock compression of [100]-oriented diamond after loading above 1.1 TPa formed BC8 carbon as a single crystal from molten carbon. In situ orientation-resolved single-crystal X-ray diffraction measured discrete Bragg spots, whose spacings and angular relationships were reproduced by a common orientation matrix within each indexed BC8 reflection group. Further experimental details are not provided.

Result: The study provides the first direct crystallographic identification of BC8 carbon. Orientation-resolved single-crystal X-ray diffraction yielded discrete Bragg spots consistent with indexed BC8 reflections and common orientation matrices, providing constraints beyond powder peak matching.

Conclusion: The single-crystal formation strategy and direct diffraction evidence resolve the decades-long quest for a post-diamond carbon structure and demonstrate that dynamic loading pathways can make predicted high-pressure phases experimentally accessible.

Abstract: Whether carbon forms crystalline structures beyond diamond at terapascal pressures remains unresolved. Predicted for nearly four decades, the denser BC8 phase has lacked direct crystallographic evidence. Here we report the first direct crystallographic identification of BC8 carbon, formed as a single crystal from molten carbon during laser-driven decaying-shock compression of [100]-oriented diamond following loading above 1.1 TPa. Using in situ orientation-resolved single-crystal X-ray diffraction, we observe discrete Bragg spots whose spacings and angular relationships are reproduced by a common orientation matrix within each indexed BC8 reflection group, providing constraints beyond powder peak matching. Our single-crystal formation strategy and direct diffraction evidence resolve the decades-long quest for a post-diamond carbon structure, demonstrating how dynamic loading pathways can make predicted high-pressure phases experimentally accessible.

</details>


### [14] [Modeling runaway electron mitigation via passively driven plasma instabilities in a high-current tokamak](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04906)
*R Datta, C Clauser, G Hu, A Braun, N Ferraro, R Sweeney, A Tinguely and*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A first self-consistent 3D extended-MHD simulation shows that a passively driven runaway-electron mitigation coil can reduce runaway-electron current by more than an order of magnitude during a SPARC disruption, with final losses enhanced by cold vertical displacement and magnetic stochasticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokamak fusion power plants face a critical challenge from high-current, relativistic runaway electron beams formed during disruptions; these beams can significantly damage machine components and threaten operational and economic viability if unmitigated.

Method: The paper presents the first self-consistent extended magnetohydrodynamic computational study of runaway-electron mitigation using a passively driven runaway-electron mitigation coil in a high-current tokamak. Runaway-electron generation and transport are coupled with coil-driven MHD activity in a 3D simulation that models the thermal and current quench phases of a mid-plane disruption, along with vertical displacement and final termination of the plasma and runaway-electron beam on SPARC, a high-field, high-current tokamak designed for fusion gain Q > 1.

Result: The simulation demonstrates that mitigation-coil-driven magnetic stochasticity can reduce the runaway-electron current by over an order of magnitude compared with unmitigated disruptions. Final termination is mediated by a post-thermal-quench cold vertical displacement of the plasma, which, together with magnetic stochasticity, increases runaway-electron losses via scrape-off once the plasma contacts the first wall.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a viable mechanism for runaway-electron mitigation using passively driven plasma instabilities in high-current tokamaks.

Abstract: A critical challenge for tokamak-based fusion power plants is the formation of high-current, relativistic runaway electron (RE) beams during abrupt confinement loss events called disruptions. RE beams may significantly damage machine components if left unmitigated, jeopardizing the operational and economic viability of fusion power plants. We present the first self-consistent extended magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) computational study of RE mitigation with a passively driven RE mitigation coil (REMC) in a high-current tokamak. RE generation and transport are coupled with REMC-driven MHD activity in a 3D simulation that models the thermal and current quench phases of a mid-plane disruption, together with the vertical displacement and final termination of the plasma and RE beam on SPARC -- a high-field, high-current tokamak designed to achieve a fusion gain Q > 1. We demonstrate that REMC-driven magnetic stochasticity can reduce the RE current by over an order-of-magnitude when compared to unmitigated disruptions. The final termination is mediated via a post-thermal-quench "cold" vertical displacement of the plasma, which, together with magnetic stochasticity, increases RE losses via scrape-off once the plasma contacts the first wall. These results demonstrate a viable mechanism for RE mitigation using passively driven plasma instabilities on high-current tokamaks.

</details>


### [15] [Eddy currents and error fields in the toroidally segmented shell configuration aka Armadillo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04651)
*D. Abate, A. Corbioli, G. Marchiori*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents and verifies a stream-function method for computing eddy currents in the toroidally segmented Armadillo conducting shell, showing how toroidal and poloidal current components scale with gap number and produce non-axisymmetric error fields that peak near an aspect-ratio-dependent transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Armadillo configuration requires characterization of its eddy currents and error fields as input for future resistive wall mode and tearing mode stability analyses.

Method: A stream function formulation is used to calculate eddy currents in a toroidally segmented conducting shell. The governing Poisson-like equation is solved spectrally with a Discrete Sine Transform, which enforces the zero-current boundary condition at each gap exactly by construction. The radial magnetic field at the plasma surface is then obtained by Biot-Savart integration and compared with an analytical wall time model of the Armadillo configuration.

Result: The toroidal eddy current flows in the same direction on every sector with a cosine-like poloidal distribution, while the poloidal return current required by current continuity peaks near each gap and reverses sign between adjacent sectors. Scaling of both components with the number of gaps is governed by competition between the poloidal geometric scale of the shell and the toroidal segmentation scale, with the transition set by the device aspect ratio—around eight gaps for an RFX-mod2-like geometry, as predicted by the analytical wall time model. At the gap, the poloidal return current produces a radial field of opposite sign to that far from the gap; the resulting non-axisymmetric error field peaks near this transition and becomes negligible for highly segmented shells.

Conclusion: These results provide a first characterization of the eddy currents and error fields of the Armadillo configuration, which is needed for future resistive wall mode and tearing mode stability analyses.

Abstract: A stream function formulation for the calculation of the eddy current distribution in a toroidally segmented conducting shell (Armadillo) is presented and verified. The governing Poisson-like equation is solved spectrally via a Discrete Sine Transform, which enforces the zero-current boundary condition at each gap exactly by construction, and the related radial magnetic field at the plasma surface is obtained by Biot-Savart integration. The toroidal eddy current flows in the same direction on every sector with a cosine-like poloidal distribution, while the poloidal return current, required by current continuity, peaks near each gap and reverses sign between adjacent sectors. The scaling of both components with the number of gaps is governed by the competition between the poloidal geometric scale of the shell and the toroidal segmentation scale. The transition between the two regimes is set by the aspect ratio of the device, around eight gaps for an RFX-mod2-like geometry, as predicted by the analytical wall time model of the Armadillo configuration. At the gap, the poloidal return current produces a radial field of opposite sign to the value far from the gap; the resulting non-axisymmetric error field peaks near this transition and becomes negligible for highly segmented shells. These results provide a first characterisation of the eddy currents and error fields of the Armadillo configuration, needed for future resistive wall mode and tearing mode stability analyses.

</details>


### [16] [The preplasma origin of the plasma-mirror reflectivity response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04355)
*Zhongwei Wang, Douglass Schumacher*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Prepulse-generated preplasma causes the subrelativistic reflectivity decline of plasma mirrors primarily through absorption in p polarization, while s-polarized absorption remains weak. Particle-in-cell simulations with a measured prepulse profile reproduce the decline, and a model applied to BELLA and CoReLS double-plasma-mirror systems captures p- and s-polarized responses across the operating range, though it misses the high-fluence s-polarized rollover.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasma mirrors improve the temporal contrast of high-power lasers, but their reflectivity decreases at subrelativistic intensities, limiting energy throughput. The paper aims to identify the mechanism and polarization dependence of this loss and connect prepulse history to plasma-mirror operating conditions.

Method: The authors use particle-in-cell simulations with a measured prepulse profile, near- and far-field energy accounting to separate absorption from scattering, and optical-cycle-resolved electron tracking. They then apply the resulting model to two representative double-plasma-mirror systems, BELLA and CoReLS.

Result: The p-polarized reflectivity decrease is driven primarily by absorption, with only a weak scattering contribution. Electron tracking reveals successive electron bunches extracted from the plasma surface, supporting a vacuum-heating interpretation. Under the simulated conditions, s-polarized absorption remains weak and no comparable decline occurs. The model captures both p- and s-polarized reflectivity responses across the operating range for BELLA and CoReLS, but still misses the high-fluence s-polarized reflectivity rollover, and further study of scattering may be needed.

Conclusion: Prepulse history is linked to polarization-dependent energy loss in plasma mirrors, informing the choice of plasma-mirror operating conditions. The model explains the main p-polarized absorption-driven decline and the weak s-polarized response under the studied conditions, while the high-fluence s-polarized rollover and the scattering channel require further investigation.

Abstract: Plasma mirrors (PM) enhance the temporal contrast of high-power lasers, but declining reflectivity at subrelativistic intensities limits their energy throughput. Here we show that prepulse-generated preplasma drives this decline in p polarization primarily through absorption. Particle-in-cell simulations using a measured prepulse profile reproduce the reflectivity decrease, with near- and far-field energy accounting showing a weak contribution from scattering. Optical-cycle-resolved electron tracking reveals successive electron bunches extracted from the plasma surface, supporting a vacuum-heating interpretation. Under the simulated conditions, s-polarized absorption remains weak and no comparable decline occurs. Applying the model to two representative double-plasma-mirror (DPM) systems, BELLA and CoReLS, it captures both the p-polarized and s-polarized reflectivity responses across the operating range. Yet, the model still misses the high-fluence s-polarized reflectivity rollover, and further investigation of the scattering channel might be needed. These findings connect prepulse history to polarization-dependent energy loss, informing the choice of plasma-mirror operating conditions.

</details>


### [17] [Conceptual Design of Pumping Systems up to 1000 m3/s Based on Non-Evaporable Getters in a Gas-Dynamic Magnetic Trap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04322)
*Alexey M. Semenov, Nataliy G. Vasilieva, Sergey V. Polosatkin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Experimental characterization of Ti-Zr-Al non-evaporable getters supports a conceptual NEG-based pumping system for the GDMT end expanders, with measured hydrogen/deuterium sticking factors, cycling durability, and a proposed getter-panel pumping speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Gas-Dynamic Magnetic Trap (GDMT), a new linear magnetic facility for confining high-temperature hydrogen plasma under development at BINP SB RAS, requires removal of approximately 10^22 molecules/s from its end expanders, corresponding to a pumping speed on the order of 500 m3/s. Non-evaporable getters are considered a possible pumping method for this application.

Method: The paper reports experimental studies of Ti-Zr-Al getters manufactured by Polema LLC. Pumping speed, sorption capacity, and getter-surface sticking factors were measured, including for hydrogen and deuterium. The getter pumping speed was tested over 60 air-vent/reactivation cycles. Based on these results, a conceptual design of a NEG-based pumping system for the GDMT end expanders was proposed, and its design and required heating power were discussed.

Result: The sticking factors were 2.23% for hydrogen and 1.54% for deuterium. The getter pumping speed was maintained over 60 air-vent/reactivation cycles. The conceptual NEG-based pumping system provides a specific pumping speed of 74 m3/(m3s) for the getter panel; the proposed design and required heating power are discussed.

Conclusion: The measured Ti-Zr-Al getter performance supports a conceptual NEG-based pumping system for the GDMT end expanders, with a getter-panel specific pumping speed of 74 m3/(m3s). The abstract does not provide a final assessment of full-system feasibility beyond the conceptual design and heating-power discussion.

Abstract: The Gas-Dynamic Magnetic Trap (GDMT), a new linear magnetic facility for confining high-temperature hydrogen plasma, is currently under development at the Budker Institute of Nuclear Physics (BINP SB RAS). The total hydrogen flow that must be removed from the end expanders by the vacuum system is approximately 10^22 molecules/s, which corresponds to a required pumping speed on the order of 500 m3/s. Non-evaporable getters (NEG) are considered as a possible pumping method for this application. This article presents experimental studies of Ti-Zr-Al getters manufactured by Polema LLC. The pumping speed, sorption capacity, and sticking factors of the getter surface were measured. The sticking factors for hydrogen and deuterium are 2.23% and 1.54%, respectively. The getter pumping speed is maintained over 60 air-vent/reactivation cycles. Based on these results, a conceptual design of a NEG-based pumping system for the GDMT end expanders is proposed, providing a specific pumping speed of 74 m3/(m3s) for the getter panel. The proposed pumping system design and the required heating power are discussed.

</details>


### [18] [A Two-Dimensional Physics-Informed Neural Network Model of a Hall Effect Thruster Plasma: Self-Consistent Electrostatics and Physical-Consistency Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04194)
*Josh Adjedj, Subrata Roy*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A two-dimensional axisymmetric PINN reconstructs eleven coupled plasma fields in the P5 Hall thruster discharge channel at 300 V and 5.4 A from 145 internal probe measurements per field. Data-fit accuracy is good, but physical-consistency tests expose serious inconsistencies; after corrections, particle and current balances improve, yet full ion and electron self-consistency is not achieved within the tested single-velocity fluid formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to build a physics-informed reconstruction of Hall-thruster discharge plasma and, importantly, to test the fitted solution against physical requirements that a data fit alone does not reveal, such as global particle and current balances, momentum-implied electron current, field-aligned electron force balance, and axial ion momentum budget.

Method: The authors use a two-dimensional axisymmetric PINN of the P5 Hall thruster discharge channel at 300 V and 5.4 A, solving eleven coupled plasma fields and assimilating 145 internal probe measurements per field reconstructed from Haas and Gallimore data. Electric potential is obtained from Poisson's equation in charge-separation form, n_e = n_i(1+eta), with eta bounded by construction. The reference solution is then tested against global particle and current balances, the electron current implied by the momentum equation, field-aligned electron force balance, and axial ion momentum budget. Corrections use a divergence-free magnetic field fitted to measured data, uncertainty-weighted data, the electron pressure gradient, and progressively imposed field-aligned and current constraints.

Result: The reference solution agrees with training profiles with relative L2 errors of 0.017, 0.073, and 0.205 for potential, electron temperature, and ion density, respectively, and held-out tests show potential is more robust than ion density to withdrawal of measurement lines. Physical-consistency tests expose a neutral inflow six times the measured flow, a bound in the potential representation, and a momentum-implied electron current that is more than 99% field-aligned and exceeds the discharge current by orders of magnitude. After corrections, the solution conserves particles within 3%, holds current between 5.1 and 5.4 A, and places 97% of potential and 88% of temperature measurements within their uncertainties. However, ion density, potential, and ion velocity could not be matched together within the tested single-velocity fluid formulation, and the field-aligned electron balance remains the main obstacle to electron self-consistency.

Conclusion: The PINN can fit the measured plasma profiles and, after physics-based corrections, improve particle and current consistency, but the original data-fit solution violates key physical constraints. Fullself-consistency is not achieved within the tested single-velocity fluid formulation: the ion density, potential, and ion velocity cannot all be matched, and the field-aligned electron force balance remains the principal obstacle to electron self-consistency.

Abstract: We present a two-dimensional axisymmetric physics-informed neural network (PINN) of the discharge channel of the P5 Hall thruster at 300 V and 5.4 A, which solves eleven coupled plasma fields and assimilates 145 internal probe measurements per field reconstructed from the data of Haas and Gallimore. The electric potential is obtained from Poisson's equation in charge-separation form, n_e = n_i(1+eta), with eta bounded by construction, which keeps the stiff electrostatic constraint active and trainable. The reference solution agrees with the training profiles to relative L2 errors of 0.017, 0.073 and 0.205 for the potential, electron temperature and ion density, and held-out tests show the potential to be far more robust than the ion density to the withdrawal of measurement lines. We then test the solution against requirements the data fit does not reveal: global particle and current balances, the electron current implied by the momentum equation, the field-aligned electron force balance and the axial ion momentum budget. These expose a neutral inflow six times the measured flow, a bound in the potential representation, and a momentum-implied electron current, more than 99% field-aligned, exceeding the discharge current by orders of magnitude. Correcting these, with a divergence-free magnetic field fitted to measured data, uncertainty-weighted data, the electron pressure gradient and progressively imposed field-aligned and current constraints, yields a solution that conserves particles to within 3%, holds the current between 5.1 and 5.4 A and places 97% of the potential and 88% of the temperature measurements within their uncertainties. Within the tested single-velocity fluid formulation, however, the ion density, potential and ion velocity could not be matched together, and the field-aligned electron balance remains the main obstacle to electron self-consistency.

</details>


### [19] [Dynamics of Vortex Electrons in a Uniform Magnetic Field Across Nonrelativistic and Relativistic Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04162)
*Yujian Lin, Zhigang Bu, Shiyu Liu, Taoming Guo, Liangliang Ji*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A quantum framework shows that vortex electrons in a transverse uniform magnetic field undergo cyclotron motion plus internal wavefunction evolution; nonrelativistic intrinsic OAM dynamics deviate from rigid point-dipole Larmor precession, and relativistic spectral nonlinearity causes dephasing, wave-packet spreading and deformation, and modified OAM, with a dimensionless parameter giving an approximate criterion to mitigate distortion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the limitations of the rigid point-dipole model and the essential role of internal wavefunction structure in vortex-electron dynamics under a transverse uniform magnetic field, and to provide theoretical guidance for understanding and manipulating relativistic vortex electrons.

Method: The authors develop a quantum framework for vortex electrons in nonrelativistic and relativistic regimes. A discrete Bessel-Gaussian initial wave packet is expanded in Dirac eigenstates to obtain the time-dependent wavefunction and analyze its spatiotemporal evolution. Further numerical or experimental details are not provided.

Result: In the nonrelativistic regime, results deviate significantly from the rigid point-dipole model: intrinsic kinetic orbital angular momentum dynamics are more complex than simple Larmor precession because of the wave packet's nonstationary internal current distribution. In the relativistic regime, nonlinear energy-level dependence on quantum numbers destroys equal spectral spacing; relative phases among eigenstate components evolve nonuniformly, causing wave-packet spreading and deformation with modified intrinsic orbital angular momentum dynamics. A dimensionless parameter quantifies this relativistic dephasing and provides an approximate criterion for selecting physical parameters and evolution times to mitigate wave-packet distortion. Exact parameter form and quantitative values are not provided.

Conclusion: The results clarify the limitations of the rigid point-dipole model, highlight the essential role of internal wavefunction structure, and provide theoretical guidance for understanding and manipulating relativistic vortex electrons.

Abstract: The dynamics of a vortex electron in a transverse uniform magnetic field involves not only the cyclotron motion of the wave packet center but also the evolution of its internal wavefunction structure. To study this process, we develop a quantum framework and investigate the dynamics of vortex electrons in both the nonrelativistic and relativistic regimes. A discrete Bessel-Gaussian initial wave packet is expanded in the basis of Dirac eigenstates to obtain the time-dependent wavefunction and to analyze its spatiotemporal evolution. In the nonrelativistic regime, the results deviate significantly from the rigid point-dipole model, as the intrinsic kinetic orbital angular momentum exhibits dynamics more complex than simple Larmor precession, owing to the nonstationary internal current distribution of the wave packet. In the relativistic regime, the nonlinear dependence of the energy levels on the quantum numbers destroys the equal spacing of the spectrum. Consequently, the relative phases among the eigenstate components of the wave packet evolve nonuniformly, resulting in wave-packet spreading and deformation, accompanied by modified intrinsic orbital angular momentum dynamics. We introduce a dimensionless parameter to quantify this relativistic dephasing, providing an approximate criterion for selecting physical parameters and evolution times to mitigate the resulting wave-packet distortion. These results clarify the limitations of the rigid point-dipole model, highlight the essential role of internal wavefunction structure, and provide theoretical guidance for understanding and manipulating relativistic vortex electrons.

</details>


### [20] [Stabilization of kinetic plasma instabilities via instantaneous external field control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03720)
*Jiyoung Yoo, Jingwei Hu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes an instantaneous, PDE-constrained optimization framework for stabilizing kinetic plasma instabilities with external fields, using a PIC-based Discretize-then-Optimize approach and an objective based on electromagnetic field energy and low-order moments; it is tested on several benchmark instabilities and suppresses field energy growth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The aim is to stabilize kinetic plasma instabilities using external fields while avoiding global-in-time optimization; the control is determined at each time step, and the objective is made practical by using electromagnetic field energy and low-order moments rather than the full distribution function.

Method: The control problem is formulated as a PDE-constrained optimization accounting for nonlinear plasma dynamics. A Discretize-then-Optimize approach is used: the forward equations are first discretized with a particle-in-cell method, and the control computation is derived from the discrete particle update to ensure consistency with the forward solver. The framework is applied in electrostatic and electromagnetic settings.

Result: The framework is evaluated on the two-stream, bump-on-tail, Dory--Guest--Harris, and Weibel instabilities. Across these tests, the control suppresses field energy growth and keeps the distribution function close to its initial state.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed instantaneous control strategy in suppressing kinetic plasma instabilities.

Abstract: We propose an instantaneous control framework for the stabilization of kinetic plasma instabilities using external fields. The control problem is formulated as a PDE-constrained optimization that accounts for nonlinear plasma dynamics, while the control is determined at each time step to avoid global-in-time optimization. The Discretize-then-Optimize (DtO) approach is adopted, with the forward equations first discretized using a particle-in-cell (PIC) method. The subsequent control computation is derived from the discrete particle update, ensuring consistency with the forward solver. The control objective is formulated in terms of electromagnetic field energy and low-order moments rather than the full distribution function, making the optimization more amenable to practical implementation. The framework is applied to both electrostatic and electromagnetic settings and evaluated on several benchmark problems, including the two-stream, bump-on-tail, Dory--Guest--Harris, and Weibel instabilities. Across these tests, the control successfully suppresses field energy growth and keeps the distribution function close to its initial state. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed strategy in suppressing kinetic plasma instabilities.

</details>


### [21] [From Forecasting to Feedback: Evaluating Learned Dynamics for Tokamak Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03690)
*Guoyang Shi, Siqi Ding, Xuanhe Wang, Pei Guo, Changquan Yu, Jiale Hu, Jiayi Zhi, Zhengyuan Chen, Jianguo Chen, Tianyuan Liu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the gap between logged-trajectory forecasting accuracy and closed-loop control performance for learned tokamak magnetic-control surrogates. Using four datasets, 16 action-conditioned Transformers, and PPO policies evaluated on six reference-solver conditions, it finds that prediction error alone is insufficient and that evaluation should combine prediction error, episode completion, condition-level duration, and tracking quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned temporal models are increasingly used for interactive decision making rather than offline forecasting, but accuracy on logged trajectories need not predict closed-loop behavior under policies optimized inside the learned model. This forecasting-control gap is important in tokamak magnetic control, where coupled plasma-circuit dynamics and actuator constraints can amplify errors through feedback.

Method: The authors use a calibrated free-boundary Grad-Shafranov solver to collect four datasets in a 2x2 design over physics-parameter and current-reference coverage. At matched budgets of 500, 1k, 3k, and 5k rollouts, they train 16 action-conditioned Transformers. PPO policies optimized in the 5k surrogates are evaluated without adaptation on six reference-solver conditions. They also compare policies trained with action noise against nominal training.

Result: Only joint-coverage D4 shows monotonically decreasing cross-condition mean error, while D1 improves in distribution but degrades out of distribution. Binary completion masks substantial closed-loop differences: D2-D4 each complete 83.3% of conditions, but reach on average 99.0%, 94.7%, and 90.5% of the evaluation horizon, with worst-condition reach of 94.2%, 68.2%, and 43.2%. Policies trained with action noise complete 5/24 matched conditions versus 19/24 under nominal training and have a lower mean horizon-capped episode length.

Conclusion: Based on one trained policy per configuration and one evaluation realization per condition, the results support evaluating learned surrogates for control using a combination of prediction error, episode completion, condition-level duration, and tracking quality rather than prediction accuracy alone.

Abstract: Learned temporal models are moving beyond offline forecasting toward interactive environments for decision making, yet logged-trajectory accuracy need not predict closed-loop behavior under policies optimized in the learned model. This forecasting-control gap matters in tokamak magnetic control, where coupled plasma-circuit dynamics and actuator constraints can amplify errors through feedback. Using a calibrated free-boundary Grad-Shafranov solver, we collect four datasets in a 2x2 design over physics-parameter and current-reference coverage. At matched budgets of 500, 1k, 3k, and 5k rollouts, we train 16 action-conditioned Transformers; PPO policies optimized in the 5k surrogates are then evaluated without adaptation on six reference-solver conditions. Only joint-coverage D4 shows monotonically decreasing cross-condition mean error, whereas D1 improves in distribution but degrades out of distribution. Binary completion masks substantial closed-loop differences: D2-D4 each complete 83.3% of conditions, but reach on average 99.0%, 94.7%, and 90.5% of the evaluation horizon, while their worst conditions reach 94.2%, 68.2%, and 43.2%. Policies trained with action noise complete 5/24 matched conditions, compared with 19/24 under nominal training, and have a lower mean horizon-capped episode length. Based on one trained policy per configuration and one evaluation realization per condition, the results support combining prediction error, episode completion, condition-level duration, and tracking quality.

</details>


### [22] [Shock wave transmission across solid-water interfaces in underwater electrical foil explosions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03657)
*Francesc Hernández Garcia, Brian Bertacco, Tobia De Benedictis, Sebastián Rojas Mata, Jergus Strucka, Michael Liverts*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Underwater electrical explosions of copper foils were used to study shock transmission through aluminum, copper, and 3D-printed PLA substrates of varying thickness. Shadowgraphy, fiber-optic hydrophone measurements, and 1D MHD simulations show that transmission depends on impedance mismatch, finite substrate thickness, and the substrate's dynamic response, supporting a quantitative framework for controlling shocks and assessing solid supports in shock-generating geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand and control the transmission of shock waves from underwater electrical foil explosions across solid materials with different acoustic impedances and thicknesses, and to assess the feasibility and limitations of using solid substrates as structural supports in complex shock-generating geometries.

Method: Copper foils 10 and 18 micrometers thick were exploded on planar aluminum, copper, and 3D-printed PLA substrates up to 5 mm thick using a multi-gigawatt pulsed-power electrical discharge. Shocks in water were visualized with high-speed shadowgraphy at 5 million frames per second, and high-resolution fiber-optic hydrophone measurements provided direct quantitative measurements of shock strength after transmission through impedance-mismatched materials. One-dimensional magnetohydrodynamic simulations reproduced foil explosion dynamics and provided insight into the foil's thermodynamic, acoustic, and electrical properties during explosion.

Result: Shock transmission was governed not only by impedance mismatch at the solid-water interface but also by the finite thickness and dynamic response of the supporting material. The abstract does not provide specific numerical shock pressures, attenuation values, or thickness-dependent quantitative outcomes.

Conclusion: The findings provide a quantitative framework for understanding and controlling shock transmission in underwater electrical foil explosions and establish the feasibility and limitations of using solid substrates as structural supports in complex shock-generating geometries.

Abstract: The transmission of shock waves generated by underwater electrical foil explosions across solid materials with varying acoustic impedances and thicknesses is investigated. Copper foils with thicknesses of 10 and 18 $μ$m are exploded on planar substrates of aluminum, copper, and 3D-printed PLA with thicknesses up to 5 mm using a multi-gigawatt pulsed-power electrical discharge. The resulting shocks are visualized in water using high-speed shadowgraphy at 5 million frames per second, while high-resolution fiber optic hydrophone measurements provide direct quantitative measurements of shock strength after transmission through the impedance-mismatched materials. One-dimensional magnetohydrodynamic simulations are used to reproduce the foil explosion dynamics and provide insight into the thermodynamic, acoustic, and electrical properties of the foil during the explosion. The results show that shock transmission is governed not only by the impedance mismatch at the solid--water interface but also by the finite thickness and dynamic response of the supporting material. These findings provide a quantitative framework for understanding and controlling shock transmission in underwater electrical foil explosions and establish the feasibility and limitations of using solid substrates as structural supports in complex shock-generating geometries.

</details>

*M. S. Kirsanova, S. A. Balashev, M. V. Barkov, E. O. Vasiliev, D. S. Wiebe, I. I. Zinchenko, A. V. Moiseev, M. S. Murga, I. N. Nikonorov, M. E. Sachkov, I. S. Khrykin, B. M. Shustov, Yu. A. Schekinov, A. D. Yarovova*
