Abstract: This article presents an overview of key scientific challenges in the astrophysics and astrochemistry of the interstellar and intergalactic media. Addressing many of these challenges will become possible with the Russian space observatory Spektr-UF, which is currently being prepared for launch. The ultraviolet (UV) spectral range is critically important for studying the evolution of the Universe, as it contains fundamental absorption and emission lines of most chemical elements and the most important molecules. These lines make it possible to reliably determine the physical conditions and chemical composition of various astrophysical objects, as well as the characteristics of the processes occurring within them. The article examines the prospects for UV observations across a broad range of scientific problems, from investigating the epoch of helium reionization and the thermal history of the Universe to mapping ongoing star formation in galaxies. Dedicated sections address the study of the chemical evolution of galaxies at intermediate redshifts, the distribution of "hidden" baryons and metals, the small-scale structure of interstellar clouds, the physics of photodissociation regions and protoplanetary disks, and the modeling of UV emission from nebulae surrounding fast-moving pulsars. The high sensitivity and spectral resolution of the Spektr-UF instruments are expected to provide fundamentally new data across all these research areas, filling existing gaps in our understanding of the interstellar and intergalactic media.

</details>


### [24] [Counterexample-driven autonomous research with application to excess radiated energy in a Z-pinch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03721)
*Bowen Zhu, Jian Wu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Simjecture is an open-source framework using language-model agents to pursue human-defined physical conjectures through counterexample-driven falsification, hypothesis repair, and computational physics testbeds. An 82.6 h Z-pinch investigation identified competing energy conversion, retention, and release-timing effects and demonstrated a new paradigm for physics research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended machine search has outperformed human mathematicians in some AI-assisted mathematics, but transferring this paradigm to physics requires refactoring the research workflow because physics is empirical, no model is final, and physical theories must interact with the real world.

Method: The framework uses language-model agents to pursue human-defined physical conjectures. It structures inquiry around counterexample-driven falsification and prospective hypothesis repair, uses computational physics as an executable testbed, separates qualification of numerical instruments from scientific claim review so solvers and diagnostics can be adapted within a recorded investigation, and applies this approach to an 82.6 h study of radiated energy exceeding imploding-shell kinetic energy in a Z-pinch.

Result: The investigation reveals competing effects of energy conversion, retention, and release timing. Stronger coherent helical deformation increases resistive conversion while reducing radiation and increasing retained energy. A broader trailing-plasma profile delays the later radiation maximum and recovers much of its early bolometric deficit, with a spectral response that changes during the pulse. Quantitative values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The results provide new insight into energy conversion in high-energy-density plasma physics and demonstrate a new paradigm for physics research.

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs in artificial-intelligence-assisted mathematics show that open-ended machine search can outperform human mathematicians, yet transferring this paradigm to physics requires refactoring the research workflow. We introduce Simjecture, an open-source framework in which language-model agents pursue human-defined physical conjectures. The framework tackles two central challenges in this transfer: first, because physics is empirical and no model is final, it structures inquiry around counterexample-driven falsification and prospective hypothesis repair; second, because physical theories must interact with the real world, it uses computational physics as an executable testbed while separating the qualification of numerical instruments from scientific claim review so solvers and diagnostics can be adapted within a recorded investigation. We apply the framework in an 82.6 h investigation to tackle the long-standing problem of radiated energy exceeding the imploding-shell kinetic energy in a Z-pinch. The investigation reveals competing effects of energy conversion, retention, and release timing. Stronger coherent helical deformation increases resistive conversion while reducing radiation and increasing retained energy. A broader trailing-plasma profile delays the later radiation maximum and recovers much of its early bolometric deficit, with a spectral response that changes during the pulse. These results provide new insight into energy conversion in high-energy-density plasma physics and demonstrate a new paradigm for physics research.

</details>


### [25] [Structure of energy flux densities in Earth's magnetotail reconnection regions: Bridging simulation and observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02870)
*Cara L. Waters, Jonathan P. Eastwood, Harry C. Lewis, Naïs Fargette, Marit Øieroset, Tai D. Phan, David L. Newman, Martin V. Goldman*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A machine-learning method trained on simulations identifies magnetic separatrix regions in in situ magnetotail reconnection events and shows that separatrices carry the largest electron energy flux and strongly enhanced Poynting flux, while ion enthalpy flux peaks in the exhaust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic reconnection changes magnetic field topology and converts magnetic energy into particle energy; determining where this conversion occurs is critical for understanding how reconnection partitions energy.

Method: The authors use a novel machine-learning approach based on simulations that explicitly identifies separatrix regions for the first time, then perform a statistical study of magnetotail reconnection events observed in situ to evaluate the partition of energy flux densities within the reconnection site.

Result: The separatrices have the largest electron energy flux densities, about twice those in the exhaust, and Poynting flux densities about four times those in the inflows, whereas typical ion enthalpy flux densities are largest in the exhaust.

Conclusion: The separatrices are sites of intense electron and electromagnetic energy transport, although their small thickness means that the exhaust may still carry most of the total energy flux.

Abstract: Magnetic reconnection is important in both space and laboratory contexts because it changes the topology of magnetic fields and converts magnetic energy into particle energies. Establishing where this energy conversion occurs is critical for understanding how reconnection partitions energy. Using a novel machine learning approach based on simulations that explicitly identifies the separatrix regions for the first time, the partition of energy flux densities within the magnetic reconnection site is evaluated through statistical study of magnetotail reconnection events observed in situ. The separatrices are found to have the largest electron energy flux densities, roughly twice those in the exhaust, and Poynting flux densities about four times those in the inflows, whereas typical ion enthalpy flux densities are largest in the exhaust. The separatrices are therefore sites of intense electron and electromagnetic energy transport, although their small thickness means that the exhaust may still carry most of the total energy flux.

</details>


### [26] [Comparison of models for dynamical structures of solar plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02818)
*S. N. Artekha*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The abstract presents axisymmetric Navier-Stokes MHD models of dynamical plasma structures generated by convective instability and excess heat/magnetic energy, comparing ideal-hydrodynamic, refined rotating, and full rotating-plasma models. Analytical block solutions show similar flow structures across models, with ambient axisymmetric magnetic field gradients having little effect, and discusses applications to solar structures including jets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Possible mechanisms for generating dynamical plasma structures are linked to the Schwarzschild criterion for convective instability and to excess energy in the form of heat or magnetic energy: a superadiabatic temperature gradient causes overheated plasma to rise. The paper aims to model these structures across different stages of development.

Method: The complete axisymmetric plasma model is formulated within the Navier-Stokes MHD equations, including equations for the stream function, azimuthal velocity, density change, poloidal magnetic field flow function, and azimuthal magnetic field component. Within ideal hydrodynamics, the simplest model applies to the initial generation stage and uses rigorous analytical solutions constructed from blocks. A refined model treats rotating plasma at the growth stage with unidirectional differential rotation. The full model describes rotating plasma from the beginning through the highest development stage, with the velocity field adjusted to boundary conditions and velocity components changing coordinately. A stationary axisymmetric ambient magnetic field and its gradients are included.

Result: The movement structures are similar for all models: radial flows converge toward the axis at the bottom, and there is outflow to the periphery near the top of the plasma structure. The maximum vertical velocity component occurs at the center of the axis. The stationary axisymmetric ambient magnetic field and its gradients have little effect on the plasma structure and velocity field. Velocity components change in a coordinated manner.

Conclusion: The models provide a staged description of plasma-structure generation: the simplest ideal-hydrodynamic model for the initial stage, a refined model for the rotating growth stage, and the full model for development through the highest stage. All yield similar flow morphology, and the ambient axisymmetric magnetic field has little influence. Applications to solar structures, including jets, are discussed; quantitative validation and detailed observational comparisons are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Possible mechanisms of generation of dynamical plasma structures are associated with the Schwarzschild criterion for convective instability and with excess energy (heat, magnetic): due to the superadiabatic temperature gradient, the overheated plasma starts to rise. In the framework of Navier-Stokes MHD equations, the complete system of equations for the full axisymmetric plasma model includes equations for the stream function, the azimuthal velocity component, the density change, the flow function of poloidal magnetic field, and the azimuthal component of the magnetic field. Within the framework of ideal hydrodynamics, the simplest plasma model is applicable to the initial stage of generation of plasma structures. In this case, rigorous analytical solutions are constructed from blocks. Within the framework of ideal hydrodynamics, some intermediate model - the refined model considers rotating plasma at the growth stage. In this case, some unidirectional differential rotation occurs. The full model studies the development of structures as rotating plasma from the very beginning to the highest development stage. The velocity field is adjusted to boundary conditions. Velocity components change in a coordinated manner. The stationary axisymmetric ambient magnetic field and its gradients have little effect on the plasma structure and velocity field. The movement structures for all models are similar. Radial flows converge towards the axis at the bottom, and there is an outflow to the periphery near the top of the plasma structure. The maximum vertical velocity component is reached at the center of the axis. Applications of these models to solar structures, including jets, are discussed.

</details>


### [27] [Influence of the Coulomb Interaction on the Radiation of a Few Body System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01777)
*Zukhriddin Ruziev, Caterina Cocchi, Anvar Khujakulov*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A classical two-particle study of intense-laser energy absorption finds that electron-electron Coulomb coupling does not significantly alter energy transfer within the plasma approximation, whereas electron-proton mass asymmetry causes the proton to gain substantially less energy than the electron.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy absorption in laser-plasma interactions is important for inertial confinement fusion and particle acceleration. At a fundamental level it is governed by charged-particle motion, but mutual Coulomb interactions can modify dynamics and absorption when multiple particles are present, motivating a two-particle investigation.

Method: The authors solve the classical equations of motion for two coupled charged particles interacting with an intense laser field. They examine electron-electron coupling within the plasma approximation and use the classical electron-proton interaction as a mass-asymmetric target system.

Result: The Coulomb interaction between electrons does not significantly affect energy transfer during laser-matter interaction in the plasma approximation. In the electron-proton case, the proton acquires substantially less energy from the field than the electron because of its much slower dynamics, producing a pronounced asymmetry in energy distribution between the species. No quantitative values are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Coulomb coupling between electrons is not a significant factor in the studied energy-transfer scenario, while particle mass is critical: mass asymmetry leads to strongly unequal energy acquisition and an asymmetric energy distribution between electrons and protons in laser-plasma interactions.

Abstract: Understanding energy absorption in laser-plasma interactions is crucial for applications ranging from inertial confinement fusion to particle acceleration. At the most fundamental level, this energy exchange is governed by the motion of individual charged particles in the laser field. However, when multiple particles are present, their mutual Coulomb interaction can modify the dynamics and, consequently, the energy absorption. In this work, we investigate this scenario by solving the classical equations of motion for two coupled charged particles interacting with an intense laser field. Within the plasma approximation, we find that the Coulomb interaction between the electrons does not significantly affect the energy transfer during the laser-matter interaction. Additionally, we explore the role of mass asymmetry by considering the classical electron-proton interaction as a target system. Our results highlight the critical importance of mass: the proton, due to its much slower dynamics, acquires substantially less energy from the field compared to the electron, leading to a pronounced asymmetry in the energy distribution between the two species.

</details>


### [28] [First-principles simulation of free-electron lasers with the Galilean electromagnetic particle-in-cell method: even harmonics of planar undulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01640)
*Alexander Pukhov*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Unaveraged GEM-PIC and GEM-PIC-RZ simulate free-electron lasers while resolving electron motion within the undulator period, capturing even harmonics that averaged codes omit. The methods are validated against radiation integrals and GENESIS and predict a 12 MW second harmonic at 10.6 nm, about 0.11% of the fundamental.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Averaged FEL codes average electron motion over the undulator period and include radiation harmonics through on-axis coupling factors. This is accurate for the fundamental, but even harmonics of a planar undulator vanish on axis and are not modelled at all, so an unaveraged approach is needed to capture them.

Method: The paper uses the Galilean electromagnetic particle-in-cell method (GEM-PIC), which resolves electron motion within the undulator period and the carrier of the forward radiation field without a Courant limit. It also uses the quasi-cylindrical code GEM-PIC-RZ. Results are compared with the radiation integral, time-dependent GENESIS runs with the same physics, and the unaveraged space-charge-free code Puffin for a seeded planar FEL at 10.6 nm.

Result: GEM-PIC simulates FELs on two CPU cores at two to three times the cost of a time-dependent GENESIS run. GEM-PIC-RZ reproduces coherent spontaneous emission of a planar undulator in azimuthal modes m = 0-3 to within 2% of the radiation integral and seeded amplifiers to within a few per cent of GENESIS with the same physics. For a seeded planar FEL at 10.6 nm, it predicts 12 MW of second-harmonic power at saturation, 1.1e-3 of the fundamental, a harmonic absent from the averaged model. Puffin gives a comparable 9-11 MW for the space-charge-widened and initial beam size and the same transverse structure.

Conclusion: Unaveraged PIC modelling can simulate planar-undulator FELs, including even harmonics and multiple azimuthal modes, with validated accuracy and feasible computational cost. The results indicate that the second harmonic can be significant and is missed by averaged FEL models.

Abstract: Free-electron laser (FEL) codes average the electron motion over the undulator period and add the radiation harmonics through on-axis coupling factors. This is accurate for the fundamental, but the even harmonics of a planar undulator, which vanish on the axis, are not modelled at all. We show that the Galilean electromagnetic particle-in-cell method (GEM-PIC), which resolves the electron motion within the undulator period and the carrier of the forward radiation field without a Courant limit, simulates FELs on two CPU cores at two to three times the cost of a time-dependent run of the averaged code GENESIS. The quasi-cylindrical code GEM-PIC-RZ reproduces the coherent spontaneous emission of a planar undulator in each azimuthal mode $m = 0$--3 to within 2\,\% of the radiation integral, and seeded amplifiers to within a few per cent of GENESIS run with the same physics. For a seeded planar FEL at 10.6\,nm it predicts 12\,MW of second-harmonic power at saturation, $1.1\times10^{-3}$ of the fundamental; this harmonic is absent from the averaged model. The unaveraged code Puffin, which has no space charge, gives a comparable level, 9 to 11\,MW for the space-charge-widened and the initial beam size, and the same transverse structure.

</details>


### [29] [Implicit-Explicit Methods for the GX Gyrokinetics Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00675)
*Patrick S. Kim, Noah R. Mandell, Ian G. Abel, William Dorland, Greg W. Hammett, Daniel R. Reynolds*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces implicit-explicit timestepping methods for the GX gyrokinetics code to accelerate costly turbulent-transport simulations, especially those with kinetically evolved electrons. Ion and electron streaming terms and electron mirror terms are treated implicitly, while other terms are explicit. The methods include lower-order Lie-Trotter splitting and higher-order additive Runge-Kutta schemes, exploit sparsity for efficient operator inversion with limited GPU memory, and are tested on linear electrostatic/electromagnetic and nonlinear electrostatic simulations in general toroidal geometry, achieving up to the maximum theoretical linearly stable timestep with significant wall-clock time improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gyrokinetic simulations are crucial for understanding turbulent transport in magnetic confinement fusion devices, but they are typically extremely expensive, particularly when electrons are kinetically evolved because electrons have a much greater thermal speed than ions. The work aims to accelerate such simulations in the GX code.

Method: The authors present new implicit-explicit timestepping methods for GX in which the ion and electron streaming terms and the electron mirror terms are treated implicitly, while the remaining terms are treated explicitly. The methods include a lower-order Lie-Trotter splitting method and a higher-order additive Runge-Kutta method. The schemes exploit the sparsity of the GX equations to efficiently invert the linear operators arising from the implicit scheme without consuming significant amounts of limited GPU memory.

Result: The integration schemes are demonstrated for linear electrostatic and electromagnetic simulations and for nonlinear electrostatic simulations in general toroidal geometry. The methods achieve up to the maximum theoretical linearly stable timestep for these simulations while maintaining a significant improvement in wall-clock time.

Conclusion: The proposed implicit-explicit schemes can accelerate GX gyrokinetic simulations, including cases with kinetically evolved electrons, by allowing timesteps up to the maximum theoretical linearly stable limit with significant wall-clock time savings and memory-efficient implicit operator inversion via sparsity.

Abstract: Gyrokinetic simulations are crucial for understanding turbulent transport in magnetic confinement fusion devices. Unfortunately, these simulations are typically extremely expensive, particularly when kinetically evolving electrons due to their significantly greater thermal speed compared to that of ions. In this work, we present new implicit-explicit timestepping methods for the GX gyrokinetics code to accelerate these simulations where the ion and electron streaming terms as well as the electron mirror terms are treated implicitly, while the remaining terms are treated explicitly. These methods include a lower-order Lie-Trotter splitting method as well as a higher-order additive Runge-Kutta method. Our schemes take advantage of the sparsity of the GX equations, which allows us to efficiently invert the linear operators that arise from the implicit scheme without consuming significant amounts of limited GPU memory. We demonstrate our new integration scheme for both linear (electrostatic and electromagnetic) and nonlinear (electrostatic) simulations in general toroidal geometry, and show that our methods can achieve up to the maximum theoretical linearly stable timestep for these simulations while maintaining a significant improvement in wall-clock time.

</details>


### [30] [Towards reactor-relevant electromagnetic turbulence in the MAST-U spherical tokamak](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00218)
*Daniel Kennedy, Yujia Zhang, Bhavin S Patel, Sam Blackmore, Charles Vincent, Colin M Roach*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Local gyrokinetic simulations of MAST-U H-mode plasmas show microtearing and electromagnetic ballooning-like ion-scale turbulence. Without equilibrium flow shear, at least one studied surface exceeds the non-zonal q²βe threshold and has heat fluxes far above power balance; strong NBI-driven flow shear suppresses this, and including carbon impurity brings ion-scale flux close to power balance. The results imply that without rotation the transition could limit accessible profiles and degrade performance for heating schemes lacking momentum input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether electromagnetic ion-scale turbulence in a high-performing MAST-U H-mode plasma approaches the non-zonal transition: a nonlinear transition to a high-flux state above a critical q²βe value previously found in STEP simulations, where q is the safety factor and βe is the electron thermal pressure-to-magnetic pressure ratio.

Method: The authors use local gyrokinetic simulations of two flux surfaces in MAST-U: one at t = 531 ms, close to peak normalized pressure βN at normalized poloidal flux ψN = 0.50, and one at t = 591 ms and ψN = 0.60, described as the ideal-ballooning-stable surface with the largest q²βe inside ψN = 0.8 at that time. They compare cases without equilibrium flow shear, with strong neutral-beam-injection-driven flow shear, and with the carbon impurity included. Exact simulation parameters and the numerical value of the critical q²βe threshold are not provided in the abstract.

Result: On both surfaces, the dominant ion-scale instabilities are microtearing and electromagnetic ballooning-like modes, as in STEP. Without equilibrium flow shear, at least one surface lies above the large-flux threshold for every set of kinetic species simulated, with ion-scale heat fluxes far exceeding power balance in turbulence with a highly stochastic magnetic field. Strong NBI-driven flow shear suppresses the turbulence and holds the plasma below the transition. Including the carbon impurity makes the ion-scale flux close to power balance on both surfaces.

Conclusion: The simulations suggest that, without rotation, gyrokinetic turbulence could drive the plasma toward the non-zonal transition and thereby limit the accessible profiles. This could degrade MAST-U plasma performance for heating schemes that lack a momentum source, and the authors state that this will be tested in forthcoming electron Bernstein wave heating experiments.

Abstract: We investigate electromagnetic ion-scale turbulence in a high-performing H-mode plasma of the Mega Ampere Spherical Tokamak Upgrade (MAST-U) with local gyrokinetic simulations, asking whether it approaches the non-zonal transition: the nonlinear transition to a high-flux state above a critical value of $q^{2}β_{e}$ found in simulations of STEP, where $q$ is the safety factor and $β_{e}$ the ratio of electron thermal pressure to magnetic pressure. We examine two flux surfaces: one at $t = 531\,\mathrm{ms}$, close to peak normalised pressure $β_N$, at normalised poloidal flux $ψ_N = 0.50$; and one at $t = 591\,\mathrm{ms}$ and $ψ_N = 0.60$, the ideal-ballooning-stable surface of largest $q^{2}β_e$ inside $ψ_N = 0.8$ at that time. On both surfaces the dominant ion-scale instabilities are microtearing and electromagnetic ballooning-like modes, as in STEP. Without equilibrium flow shear, at least one of the surfaces lies above the large flux threshold with every set of kinetic species we simulate, with ion-scale heat fluxes far exceeding power balance, in turbulence characterised by a highly stochastic magnetic field. Strong flow shear driven by neutral-beam injection in the real experiment suppresses this turbulence and holds the plasma below the transition. When the carbon impurity is included in the simulation, the ion-scale flux is then close to power balance on both surfaces. Without rotation, gyrokinetics suggests the transition would limit the accessible profiles. This could degrade MAST-U plasma performance using heating schemes that lack a momentum source, and this will be tested in forthcoming electron Bernstein wave heating experiments.

</details>


### [31] [Solar Wind Turbulence Spectra and Correlation Scale from 4 to 50 $r_\odot$: STEREO and PUNCH Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03328)
*F. Pecora, G. Nisticò, R. Chhiber, Y. Yang, S. Adhikari, J. Wang, R. Patel, Z. Gao, C. DeForest, S. E. Gibson, N. Viall, M. N. Beasley, R. C. Colaninno, J. M. Hughes, R. Killough, D. A. Lamb, G. T. Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper tracks solar wind turbulence from 4 to 50 solar radii using STEREO/COR2 and PUNCH/WFI brightness observations, finding that line-of-sight integration steepens apparent spectral slopes by about -0.5 and that the density-fluctuation correlation scale increases with heliocentric distance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to investigate the radial evolution of solar wind turbulence over a wide heliocentric distance range by combining remote-sensing observations from STEREO and PUNCH.

Method: The authors use remote-sensing brightness observations from STEREO's COR2 coronagraph (4-9 r_sun) and PUNCH's Wide Field Imager (21-50 r_sun), exploiting their combined wide fields of view to track the radial evolution of the power spectral density and correlation scale of density fluctuations from 4 to 50 r_sun.

Result: Line-of-sight integration systematically steepens the usual turbulence scaling laws by a constant offset of about -0.5 across all resolved scales: the energy-containing range nominally k^-1 appears as k^-1.5, and the inertial range nominally k^-5/3 appears as k^-2.1, consistent with previous synthetic-image simulations. The correlation scale increases with heliocentric distance across the full probed range, in accordance with previous theoretical models and observations at closer distances from the Sun.

Conclusion: The combined STEREO and PUNCH observations show that apparent turbulence spectra are systematically steepened by line-of-sight integration and that the density-fluctuation correlation scale grows with heliocentric distance from 4 to 50 r_sun, supporting earlier simulations, theoretical models, and closer-distance observations.

Abstract: We investigate the radial evolution of solar wind turbulence using remote-sensing brightness observations from the Solar Terrestrial Relations Observatory (STEREO) and the first science-ready images from the Polarimeter to Unify the Corona and Heliosphere (PUNCH) mission. Exploiting the combined wide fields of view of STEREO's COR2 coronagraph (4-9 r_sun) and PUNCH's Wide Field Imager (WFI, 21-50 r_sun), we track the radial evolution of the power spectral density and correlation scale of density fluctuations from 4 to 50 r_sun. Line-of-sight integration systematically steepens the "usual" turbulence scaling laws by a constant offset of about -0.5 across all resolved scales, so that the energy-containing range (nominally k^-1) and the inertial range (nominally k^-5/3) appear as k^-1.5 and k^-2.1, respectively -- consistent with previous synthetic-image simulations. The correlation scale increases with heliocentric distance across the full range of heliodistances probed, in accordance with previous theoretical models and observations at closer distances from the Sun.

</details>


### [32] [The Birth of Plasmoids: Weibel-Driven Secondary Tearing and its Suppression in Relativistic Reconnection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02126)
*Matthew Goodbred, Anatoly Spitkovsky*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Two-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations show that plasmoids in relativistic pair-plasma reconnection are seeded by a current-filamentation/Weibel instability near primary x-lines; the unstable region is limited to a distance scaling with magnetization, implying plasmoid formation is suppressed farther from x-lines even in ultra-relativistic reconnection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasmoids, or magnetic islands, dominate relativistic reconnection layers in kinetic simulations, but the instability responsible for creating them is poorly understood.

Method: The authors track newborn islands in 2D particle-in-cell simulations of pair-plasma reconnection with magnetizations 0.1 <= sigma_0 <= 1000 and compare measured instability growth rates with linear theory evaluated on the simulated distributions.

Result: Plasmoids are seeded by a current-filamentation/Weibel instability driven by counter-streaming out-of-plane current carriers near primary x-lines. Growth rates are about 0.1 times the background plasma frequency and agree with linear theory. Away from the x-line, inflowing plasma loads the sheet, the current is carried by more particles, and the out-of-plane drift slows; the instability shuts off when this drift becomes comparable to the momentum spread in the outflow direction, at a distance ell_iso proportional to sigma_0 from the x-line. Because the unstable region extends farther at higher sigma_0, plasmoid formation is predicted to be suppressed beyond about ell_iso from primary x-lines even in ultra-relativistic reconnection.

Conclusion: The results account for the onset of plasmoid-dominated reconnection in the relativistic regime but predict that plasmoid formation is suppressed beyond roughly ell_iso from primary x-lines, even in ultra-relativistic reconnection. Particle acceleration, variability, and coherent radio emission attributed to plasmoid-dominated reconnection may therefore be confined to smaller regions than previously assumed.

Abstract: Plasmoids, or magnetic islands, dominate relativistic reconnection layers in kinetic simulations, yet the instability that creates them remains poorly understood. By tracking newborn islands in 2D particle-in-cell simulations of pair-plasma reconnection with magnetizations $0.1\leσ_0\le1000$, we show that plasmoids are seeded by a current-filamentation/Weibel instability driven by counter-streaming out-of-plane current carriers near primary x-lines. Measured growth rates are $\sim0.1$ times the background plasma frequency and agree with linear theory evaluated on the simulated distributions. As inflowing plasma loads the sheet away from the x-line, the current is carried by more particles and their out-of-plane drift slows. The filamentation instability shuts off once this drift becomes comparable to the momentum spread in the outflow direction, which occurs at a distance $\ell_{\rm iso}\proptoσ_0$ from the x-line. Because the unstable region extends farther at higher $σ_0$, our results account for the onset of plasmoid-dominated reconnection in the relativistic regime but predict that plasmoid formation is suppressed beyond $\sim\ell_{\rm iso}$ from primary x-lines, even in ultra-relativistic reconnection. Particle acceleration, variability, and coherent radio emission attributed to plasmoid-dominated reconnection may therefore be confined to smaller regions than previously assumed.

</details>


### [33] [Learned Surrogates of Three-Dimensional Magnetohydrodynamic Turbulence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01356)
*Semih Kacmaz, E. A. Huerta*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents first learned surrogates of three-dimensional resistive MHD turbulence and compares a convolutional UNet, a shifted-window transformer, and a Fourier neural operator as autoregressive emulators of decaying turbulence at 128^3 resolution for Reynolds numbers 1000, 3000, and 6000, using 500 simulations per Reynolds number and a two-stage single-step then short-rollout training procedure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamic turbulence is costly to simulate, motivating learned surrogate models and a comparison of standard neural backbones in that role. The authors describe their work as the first learned surrogates of three-dimensional resistive MHD turbulence.

Method: The models are trained as autoregressive emulators of decaying MHD turbulence at a resolution of 128^3 and Reynolds numbers of 1000, 3000, and 6000, with 500 simulations per Reynolds number. Training uses one two-stage procedure, single steps followed by short rollouts, and is stopped by one rule. The compared backbones are a convolutional UNet, a shifted-window transformer, and a Fourier neural operator. The same backbones and training procedure are also applied to two public benchmarks.

Result: Over a window of about one large-eddy turnover time, the UNet and transformer reach relative velocity errors of 0.018 to 0.053 and preserve current sheets, enstrophy, current density, and energy spectra; the more accurate of the two depends on Reynolds number, with the UNet leading at 3000 and 6000 and the transformer at 1000. On FactFormer's isotropic turbulence benchmark, the models reach the lowest error reported to date, 39% below the best published model. On The Well's compressible MHD benchmark, rollout errors are one-third to one-half below those of the best baseline. They also match a 1.3 B-parameter pretrained foundation model in its rollout windows with 20 to 62 times fewer parameters. Comparative results for the Fourier neural operator are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Learned autoregressive surrogates can emulate three-dimensional resistive MHD turbulence accurately over the reported rollout window, with UNet and transformer backbones preserving key physical structures and spectra and improving on published benchmark results. The abstract does not identify a single best backbone across all Reynolds numbers; code, model configurations, evaluation scripts, and the data generator with simulation seeds are to be released upon publication.

Abstract: Three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence, the flow of astrophysical and fusion plasmas, is costly to simulate. We present, to our knowledge, the first learned surrogates of three-dimensional resistive MHD turbulence, and compare three standard backbones in that role: a convolutional UNet, a shifted-window transformer and a Fourier neural operator. They are trained as autoregressive emulators of decaying MHD turbulence at a resolution of $128^3$ and Reynolds numbers of 1000, 3000 and 6000, on 500 simulations per Reynolds number, each with one two-stage procedure, single steps and then short rollouts, and stopped by one rule. Over a window of about one large-eddy turnover time, the UNet and the transformer reach relative velocity errors of 0.018 to 0.053 and keep the current sheets, the enstrophy, the current density and the energy spectra of the simulation; which of the two is the more accurate depends on the Reynolds number, the UNet leading at 3000 and 6000 and the transformer at 1000. Applied with the same backbones and training procedure to two public benchmarks, the models reach the lowest error reported to date on the isotropic turbulence set of FactFormer, 39% below the best published model. On the compressible MHD set of The Well, their rollout errors are a third to a half below those of the best of The Well's baselines. They also match a 1.3 B-parameter pretrained foundation model in its rollout windows, with 20-62 times fewer parameters. The code, the configuration of every model, the evaluation scripts and the data generator with the seed of every simulation will be released upon publication.

</details>

*František Lukáč, Jakub Klečka, Jiří Kozlík, Kristína Bartha, Jozef Veselý, Kateřina Ficková, Jakub Minařík, Jan Čížek, Josef Stráský*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A spherical refractory complex concentrated alloy powder (Al10Nb20Ta15Ti30V5Zr20) was produced by mechanical alloying followed by radio-frequency inductively coupled plasma spheroidization; the powder is suitable for additive manufacturing and has a mainly BCC microstructure with local B2 phase, with complex carbide formation studied by 3D atom probe tomography.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Additive manufacturing requires high-quality metal powders with tailored compositions, but conventional metallurgical preparation of refractory complex concentrated alloy powders is challenging because of their high melting point and chemical reactivity.

Method: The authors used mechanical alloying followed by radio-frequency inductively coupled plasma spheroidization to produce spherical Al10Nb20Ta15Ti30V5Zr20 refractory complex concentrated alloy powder. Powder morphology and microstructure were assessed, and complex carbide formation was studied by 3D atom probe tomography. Detailed processing parameters are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The resulting powder exhibited spherical morphology and a microstructure consisting of major BCC phases with local B2 phase, making it suitable for additive manufacturing processes. Complex carbide formation was examined by 3D atom probe tomography; specific quantitative results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The presented mechanical alloying plus radio-frequency inductively coupled plasma spheroidization route yields spherical refractory complex concentrated alloy powder suitable for additive manufacturing, with a predominantly BCC microstructure containing local B2 phase and complex carbides investigated by atom probe tomography. Additive manufacturing build performance and quantitative powder properties are not reported in the abstract.

Abstract: Recent advances in additive manufacturing (AM) have significantly increased the demand for high-quality metal powders with tailored composition. For refractory complex concentrated alloys (RCCA) preparing such powders by conventional metallurgical method remains challenging due to their high melting point and chemical reactivity. In this paper, we present a method to produce spherical RCCA powders (here Al10Nb20Ta15Ti30V5Zr20) via mechanical alloying followed by radio-frequency inductively coupled plasma spheroidization. The resulting powder is suitable for AM processes because of its spherical morphology and a microstructure consisting of major BCC phases with local B2 phase. Complex carbide formation was studied by 3D atom probe tomography.

</details>

### [35] [Beam Transport Through a Quadrupole with Offsets in Both Transverse Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06730)
*Sergey Tomin*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives a compact analytical 6×6 transfer matrix for a beam traversing an offset quadrupole that bends the reference trajectory and creates dispersion in both transverse planes, using Bishop coordinates and a weak-curvature approximation, and compares this model with sliced combined-function bend maps and direct numerical integration of the Lorentz equations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In accelerator switchyards with shared magnets, a beam can pass through a quadrupole with offsets in both transverse planes. This off-axis configuration cannot be represented by a single conventional horizontal or vertical combined-function bend, yet it bends the reference trajectory and generates dispersion simultaneously in both planes.

Method: The authors derive a compact analytical 6×6 transfer matrix about the curved reference trajectory. The model uses Bishop coordinates and a weak-curvature approximation that retains the longitudinal variation of both reference-curvature components and the resulting dispersion in both transverse planes. The analytical linear model is compared with a sliced representation based on conventional second-order combined-function bend maps and with direct numerical integration of the Lorentz equations in the ideal Cartesian quadrupole field.

Result: The abstract reports that the analytical linear model is compared with a sliced combined-function bend-map representation and with direct numerical integration of the Lorentz equations. It does not provide quantitative results or state which comparison outcome was obtained.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that an off-axis quadrupole in a shared-magnet switchyard bends the reference trajectory and generates dispersion in both transverse planes, and it provides a compact analytical 6×6 transfer-matrix model for this configuration. The abstract does not provide further conclusions or numerical validation findings.

Abstract: In accelerator switchyards with shared magnets, a beam may traverse a quadrupole with offsets in both transverse planes, a configuration that cannot be represented by a single conventional horizontal or vertical combined-function bend. Such an off-axis quadrupole bends the reference trajectory and generates dispersion simultaneously in both planes. We derive a compact analytical $6\times6$ transfer matrix about the curved reference trajectory. The model is formulated in Bishop coordinates using a weak-curvature approximation that retains the longitudinal variation of both reference-curvature components and the resulting dispersion in both transverse planes. The analytical linear model is compared with a sliced representation based on conventional second-order combined-function bend maps and with direct numerical integration of the Lorentz equations in the ideal Cartesian quadrupole field.

</details>

It identifies priority R&D activities, evaluates leverage of existing laboratory capabilities and accelerator infrastructure, and outlines a staged approach to technology demonstrations and integrated system validation.

Conclusion: A coordinated national research strategy is emphasized to complement international efforts and establish a technically credible path toward a future Muon Collider, with priority R&D and staged validation needed before proceeding to technical design.

Abstract: The National Laboratory Accelerator Study Group (NLASG) was established to assess the accelerator research and development required to advance a future high-energy Muon Collider, with particular emphasis on opportunities for coordinated contributions from U.S. national laboratories. This report examines the principal accelerator subsystems, their technical maturity, and the critical challenges that must be addressed before proceeding to a technical design. Topics include the proton driver, target and capture system, muon cooling, acceleration, collider ring, and machine-detector interface. The study identifies priority R&D activities, evaluates opportunities to leverage existing laboratory capabilities and accelerator infrastructure, and outlines a staged approach to technology demonstrations and integrated system validation. Particular emphasis is placed on developing a coordinated national research strategy that complements international efforts and establishes a technically credible path toward a future Muon Collider.

</details>


### [37] [High-transformer-ratio plasma-based afterburner for extending X-ray free-electron laser photon energies to $\gtrsim$50 keV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05957)
*Songtang Han, Letian Liu, Zhiyan Yang, Zhuo Pan, Xueqing Yan, Xinlu Xu*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: A simulation study proposes an elliptical-driver plasma afterburner that suppresses witness-beam hosing via betatron decoherence, accelerating an 8 GeV beam to about 30 GeV and enabling compact ≥50 keV XFEL radiation at about 10 GW peak power and about 10 fs duration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bright coherent X-rays at ≳50 keV are desirable for materials science, high-energy-density physics, and nuclear physics but are difficult to obtain from current XFEL facilities. Plasma-based afterburners offer a compact way to increase electron-beam energy, but high transformer ratio operation is vulnerable to hosing instability, which can severely degrade witness-beam quality.

Method: The authors propose using a transversely elliptical driver beam to excite an elliptical nonlinear plasma wake with an axially varying focusing gradient, suppressing witness-beam hosing through betatron decoherence. They evaluate the scheme with three-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations and start-to-end simulations for a 3 kA driver and a 2.35 kA witness beam with 4 μm rms length and 94 μm beam separation (TR ≈ 3).

Result: Simulations show acceleration of an 8 GeV witness beam to ∼30 GeV, with nearly half of the electrons meeting XFEL requirements. Start-to-end simulations indicate the beam can drive ≳50 keV XFEL radiation with ∼10 GW peak powers and ∼10 fs durations, even when quantum diffusion is included.

Conclusion: The proposed elliptical plasma-wake scheme can suppress hosing while maintaining high transformer ratio and beam quality, offering a compact route to extend existing XFEL facilities to higher photon energies. The abstract reports simulation results rather than experimental demonstration.

Abstract: Bright coherent X-rays in the $\gtrsim$50 keV range are highly desirable for applications in materials science, high-energy-density physics, and nuclear physics, but are difficult to obtain from present X-ray free-electron laser (XFEL) facilities. Plasma-based afterburners offer a compact route to increasing the electron-beam energy, yet high transformer ratio (TR) operation is vulnerable to hosing instability, which can severely degrade the witness beam quality. We propose using a transversely elliptical driver beam to excite an elliptical nonlinear plasma wake with an axially varying focusing gradient, thereby suppressing the witness beam hosing through betatron decoherence. Three-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations show that, for a 3 kA driver and a 2.35 kA witness beam with an rms length of 4 $\mathrmμ$m and a beam separation of 94 $\mathrmμ$m (TR $\approx 3$), this scheme can accelerate an 8 GeV witness beam to $\sim30$ GeV while nearly half of the electrons meet the XFEL requirements. Start-to-end simulations show that the resulting beam can drive $\gtrsim50$ keV XFEL radiation with peak powers of $\sim10$ GW and durations of $\sim10$ fs, even when quantum diffusion is included, offering a compact route to extend existing XFEL facilities to higher photon energies.

</details>


### [38] [Estimation of Very High Energy Electron (VHEE) beam parameters using an in-air screen](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05973)
*Joel Valerian, Paul Giansiracusa, Vincent Lu, Paarangat Pushkarna, Roger Rassool, Suzie Lyn Sheehy, Yaw-Ren Eugene Tan, Matteo Volpi*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops and validates a Multiple Coulomb Scattering-aware beam model that extends quadrupole scans to estimate VHEE beam parameters in the presence of a downstream scatterer, with simulated and experimental validation at the Australian Synchrotron PEER end station.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Very high energy electron beams are promising for radiotherapy, but accurate beam delivery requires a beam model that accounts for scattering along the beam propagation path.

Method: Multiple Coulomb Scattering is incorporated as an additive term to the covariance matrix transformation for a drift space, allowing conventional quadrupole scans to estimate beam parameters when a downstream scatterer is present. The scattering correction factor is fitted to Monte Carlo simulations for a range of materials and thicknesses. The model is validated by recovering initial beam parameters from simulated quadrupole scans of a realistic beamline and is then applied to in-air screen measurements at the PEER end station of the Australian Synchrotron.

Result: After fitting the correction factors, the mean and standard deviation of the estimation errors for final emittance, diameter, divergence, and mean kinetic energy were (-0.06 ± 3.17)%, (0.15 ± 0.66)%, (0.18 ± 2.18)%, and (-0.03 ± 0.06)%, respectively. Simulated quadrupole scans recovered the initial beam parameters with errors of at most 13%. PEER measurements gave emittance estimates from 0.238 to 1.753 mm mrad across scans, within the expected range. Following an initial quadrupole scan, the method allows the in-air beam size to be estimated for other quadrupole configurations.

Conclusion: The MCS-aware beam model enables quadrupole-scan-based estimation of VHEE beam parameters with a downstream scatterer and supports in-air beam-size prediction for other quadrupole settings. Further work is required to improve the consistency and reliability of the experimental estimates.

Abstract: Very high energy electron (VHEE) beams offer several promising properties for radiotherapy. Accurate beam delivery requires a beam model that accounts for scattering along the beam propagation path. This work develops and validates a beam model incorporating Multiple Coulomb Scattering (MCS) to estimate beam parameters. MCS is incorporated as an additive term to the covariance matrix transformation for a drift space, allowing conventional quadrupole scans to estimate beam parameters in the presence of a downstream scatterer. The scattering correction factor is fitted to Monte Carlo (MC) simulations for a range of materials and thicknesses. The model is then validated by recovering the initial beam parameters from simulated quadrupole scans of a realistic beamline. Finally, the method is applied to in-air screen measurements at the Pulsed Energetic Electrons for Research (PEER) end station of the Australian Synchrotron. After fitting the correction factors, the mean and standard deviation of the estimation errors for the final emittance, diameter, divergence, and mean kinetic energy were $(-0.06 \pm 3.17)\%$, $(0.15 \pm 0.66)\%$, $(0.18 \pm 2.18)\%$, and $(-0.03 \pm 0.06)\%$, respectively. Simulated quadrupole scans successfully recovered the initial beam parameters, with errors of at most $13\%$. PEER measurements yielded emittance estimates ranging from $0.238$ to $1.753~\mathrm{mm\,mrad}$ across scans, which were within the expected range. Following an initial quadrupole scan, the method allows the in-air beam size to be estimated for other quadrupole configurations. Further work is required to improve the consistency and reliability of the experimental estimates.

</details>


### [39] [Single-pass stochastic cooling for electron microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03581)
*River Robles, Peter Hommelhoff*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: Strong electron-light coupling in nanophotonic structures enables single-pass, laser-free stochastic cooling inside an electron microscope, losslessly reducing longitudinal emittance and offering a route to brighter electron beams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-electron applications such as electron microscopes and particle accelerators are limited by electron-source brightness. Accelerators can use phase-space cooling, but electron microscopes rely on particle filtering, which increases coherence only by reducing brightness. The paper aims to achieve lossless single-pass cooling compatible with normal continuous-wave electron-microscope operation.

Method: The authors use strong electron-light coupling in nanophotonic structures to implement stochastic cooling inside an electron microscope. They derive a fully quantum mechanical model of stochastic cooling valid in the strong-coupling regime and support it with numerical simulations.

Result: The model yields an analytic asymptotic formula for the optimal cooled energy spread, sigma_E_f ≈ 0.88 (hbar omega)^(2/3) sigma_E_0^(1/3), where hbar omega is the cooling photon energy and sigma_E_0 is the initial energy spread. Numerical simulations confirm that the energy-spread reduction appears as increased purity of the electron quantum state and reduced longitudinal phase-space area. The abstract does not provide experimental validation details.

Conclusion: Strong-coupling nanophotonic stochastic cooling provides a laser-free, continuous-wave-compatible route to brighter electron beams for microscopy, with potential for higher image quality, enhanced spectral resolution, and approaching fundamental quantum limits on electron-beam phase-space area.

Abstract: Scientific applications of free electrons, such as in electron microscopes and particle accelerators, are limited by the brightness of available electron sources. Accelerators can use phase space cooling to surpass these source limitations, whereas electron microscopes can only rely on particle filtering to increase beam coherence at the cost of reduced brightness. We show that strong electron-light coupling in nanophotonic structures makes it possible to achieve single-pass stochastic cooling inside an electron microscope for a lossless improvement of longitudinal emittance. This approach is laser-free and compatible with normal CW operation of electron microscopes. We derive a fully quantum mechanical model of stochastic cooling valid in the strong coupling regime, finding an analytic asymptotic formula for the optimal cooled energy spread $σ_{E_f}\simeq0.88(\hbarω)^{2/3}σ_{E_0}^{1/3}$, with $\hbar ω$ the cooling photon energy and $σ_{E_0}$ the initial energy spread. Numerical simulations confirm that the energy spread reduction is reflected in an increase in the purity of the electron's quantum state and a reduction of the longitudinal phase space area. Our work paves a path towards even brighter beams for electron microscopy, promising higher image quality, enhanced spectral resolution, and a route to reaching fundamental quantum limits on electron beam phase space area.

</details>


### [40] [Impact of Rapid Acceleration on Beam Dynamics in the Rapid-Cycling Synchrotrons of a Muon Collider](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02865)
*Lisa Soubirou, Antoine Chancé*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates beam-dynamics challenges for a four-RCS muon acceleration chain from 63 GeV to 5 TeV, focusing on RF cavity distribution, energy-field ramp mismatch, and magnetic-field jitter; the abstract reports the scope of the studies but no quantitative outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Circular muon colliders are a promising route to multi-TeV centre-of-mass energies with high luminosity, but the short muon lifetime requires up to 70% muon survival during acceleration from 63 GeV to 5 TeV in about 10 ms, creating demanding beam-dynamics constraints.

Method: The work analytically models the mismatch between discretely increasing beam energy and continuously ramped pulsed dipole and quadrupole fields, evaluates it with tracking simulations, studies the impact of RF cavity distribution on transverse emittance growth, and assesses sensitivity to magnetic-field ramp jitter.

Result: The abstract states that the impacts of RF cavity distribution, energy-field ramp mismatch, and magnetic-field ramp jitter on transverse emittance, beam trajectory, and optical functions were studied, modelled, or assessed, but it provides no quantitative results.

Conclusion: The paper addresses key beam-dynamics challenges for the RCS-based muon acceleration chain, but the abstract does not provide explicit quantified conclusions or demonstrated performance against the stated survival and acceleration-time requirements.

Abstract: Circular muon colliders offer a promising route to multi-TeV centre-of-mass energies with high luminosity. The baseline design for the high-energy complex includes a chain of four Rapid-Cycling Synchrotrons (RCSs) accelerating the beam from \qty{63}{\GeV} to \qty{5}{\TeV}. The chain combines a conventional synchrotron with three hybrid synchrotrons, which use fixed-field superconducting dipoles and rapidly ramped normal-conducting dipoles. The short muon lifetime (\qty{2.2}{\micro\second} in the rest frame) imposes stringent constraints on the acceleration process: achieving the target luminosity requires a muon survival rate of up to \qty{70}{\percent} throughout the acceleration chain, with the full acceleration completed in approximately \qty{10}{\milli\second}. These demanding acceleration conditions raise several beam-dynamics challenges, investigated in this work. First, the impact of the RF cavity distribution on transverse emittance growth is studied. Second, the mismatch between the discretely increasing beam energy and the continuously ramped fields of the pulsed dipoles and quadrupoles is modelled analytically, and its impact is evaluated through tracking simulations. Finally, the sensitivity of the beam trajectory, optical functions, and emittance growth to magnetic-field ramp jitter is assessed.

</details>


### [41] [OPALX: A performance-portable framework for particle accelerator simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00124)
*Michail Zampetakis, Ryan Sean Ammann, Yuanjie Bi, John Biddiscombe, Pedro Calvo, Carl Jolly, Severin Andrey Klapproth, Alexander Paul Liemen, Sriramkrishnan Muralikrishnan, Chris Rogers, Mohsen Sadr, Jonathan Thompson, Andreas Adelmann*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents OPALX, a performance-portable accelerator simulation framework built on OPAL and using IPPL plus Kokkos to run distributed-memory simulations on multicore CPUs and GPUs from a common codebase, combining relativistic tracking, prescribed fields, particle-in-cell space charge, and spin tracking; initial tests and a 3D space-charge benchmark demonstrate CPU/GPU execution and high GPU throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate charged-particle beam simulations require self-consistent collective fields and realistic accelerator models, but their computational cost motivates efficient use of heterogeneous computing systems.

Method: OPALX is a performance-portable accelerator simulation framework that builds on OPAL's beam-dynamics capabilities. Its modular architecture uses the Independent Parallel Particle Layer (IPPL) and Kokkos to support distributed-memory execution on multicore CPUs and GPUs from a common codebase. It combines relativistic particle tracking with prescribed accelerator fields, particle-in-cell space charge calculations, and spin tracking. The paper describes the physical and numerical models and their integration into the simulation workflow. Assessment uses disorder-induced heating and frozen-spin test cases, comparisons of spin evolution with G4Beamline and beam transport with TraceWin, and a three-dimensional space charge benchmark evaluating throughput on CPU and GPU systems.

Result: The implemented models receive initial verification. The tests demonstrate CPU and GPU execution of self-consistent space charge calculations. In the three-dimensional space charge benchmark, throughput reaches approximately 10^9 macroparticles per second on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU.

Conclusion: OPALX provides a common foundation for extending accelerator models and numerical methods across heterogeneous computing platforms.

Abstract: Accurate charged-particle beam simulations require self-consistent collective fields and realistic accelerator models, while their computational cost motivates efficient use of heterogeneous computing systems. We present OPALX, a performance-portable accelerator simulation framework that builds on the beam-dynamics capabilities of OPAL. Its modular architecture uses the Independent Parallel Particle Layer (IPPL) and Kokkos to support distributed-memory execution on multicore CPUs and GPUs from a common codebase. The framework combines relativistic particle tracking with prescribed accelerator fields, particle-in-cell space charge calculations, and spin tracking. We describe the physical and numerical models and their integration into the simulation workflow. The implementation is assessed using disorder-induced heating and frozen-spin test cases, together with comparisons of spin evolution with G4Beamline and beam transport with TraceWin. A three-dimensional space charge benchmark evaluates throughput on CPU and GPU systems, reaching approximately $10^9$ macroparticles per second on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU for this benchmark. These results provide initial verification of the implemented models and demonstrate CPU and GPU execution of self-consistent space charge calculations. OPALX provides a common foundation for extending accelerator models and numerical methods across heterogeneous computing platforms.

</details>


### [42] [SensOFormer: Foundation Models for Particle Accelerators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02031)
*Mahindra Rautela, Alexander Scheinker*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: SensOFormer is a denoising masked transformer with a Perceiver-style encoder-decoder that is self-supervised pretrained on historical particle-accelerator sensor data to learn reusable sensor representations, handling variable sensor sets, heterogeneous sampling rates, and noisy telemetry, and is transferred to missing-data imputation, anomaly detection, cavity identification, and fault identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale scientific facilities such as particle accelerators are complex systems with thousands of interacting components and extensive sensor networks that generate large volumes of heterogeneous, incomplete, and noisy telemetry, often sampled at different rates across subsystems; robust general-purpose sensor representations are needed to support diagnostics and control and ensure safe, reliable operation.

Method: The paper adopts the foundation-model paradigm and introduces SensOFormer, a denoising masked transformer with a Perceiver-style encoder-decoder architecture designed to handle variable sensor sets, accommodate heterogeneous sampling rates, and learn robust representations from noisy measurements. The model is pretrained in a self-supervised manner on multiple particle-accelerator datasets and transferred to downstream tasks including missing-data imputation, anomaly detection, cavity identification, and fault identification.

Result: Through extensive evaluations and comparisons, the paper demonstrates that a single self-supervised model can learn reusable representations for diverse operational tasks in large-scale scientific facilities. The abstract does not provide specific quantitative results.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a self-supervised foundation-model approach can learn reusable sensor representations for diverse operational tasks in large-scale scientific facilities. The abstract does not provide further limitations or detailed conclusions.

Abstract: Large-scale scientific facilities, such as particle accelerators, are complex systems composed of thousands of interacting components that must operate collectively to support diagnostics and control, and ensure safe, reliable operation. These facilities rely on extensive sensor networks that generate large volumes of heterogeneous, incomplete, and noisy telemetry, often sampled at different rates across subsystems. In this paper, we adopt the foundation-model paradigm to learn general-purpose sensor representations from historical accelerator data through self-supervised pretraining. We introduce SensOFormer, a denoising masked transformer with a Perceiver-style encoder-decoder architecture designed to handle variable sensor sets, accommodate heterogeneous sampling rates, and learn robust representations from noisy measurements. The model is pretrained on multiple particle-accelerator datasets and transferred to downstream tasks, including missing-data imputation, anomaly detection, cavity identification, and fault identification. Through extensive evaluations and comparisons, we demonstrate that a single self-supervised model can learn reusable representations for diverse operational tasks in large-scale scientific facilities. Project page: https://mahindrautela.github.io/sensoformer.

</details>


### [43] [LH$μ$C Concept: Energy-Frontier Antimuon-Proton Collisions at CERN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01117)
*Arda Goroglu, Saleh Sultansoy*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents the LHμC concept for an energy-frontier antimuon-proton collider based on the HL-LHC, compares two μp collision schemes, and discusses detector design, physics potential, and challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy-frontier lepton-hadron colliders are described as indispensable QCD explorers, and the paper reviews the history of muon-proton collider proposals before comparing ways to realize μp collisions at the LHC.

Method: The LHμC concept is based on the HL-LHC. Two realization approaches are compared: muon-collider beams and ultra-cold μ+ beams as proposed for μTRISTAN. For the latter, a μTRISTAN-type booster and reuse of the LHeC energy-recovery-linac tunnel are discussed. The study considers colliding 1 TeV antimuons with 7 TeV protons, yielding √s=5.3 TeV, and discusses detector concept, physics potential including searches for colour-octet muons and R-parity-violating squarks, and main challenges.

Result: Colliding 1 TeV antimuons with 7 TeV protons gives √s=5.3 TeV and peak luminosities of 8.8×10^33 and 7.2×10^33 cm^-2 s^-1, respectively, for the two compared schemes.

Conclusion: The abstract presents the LHμC concept and discusses its detector concept, physics potential, and main challenges, but it does not state a definitive conclusion beyond this.

Abstract: The concept of the LH$μ$C, an energy-frontier antimuon-proton collider based on the HL-LHC, is presented. After recalling why energy-frontier lepton-hadron colliders are indispensable as QCD explorers and reviewing the history of muon-proton collider proposals, two ways of realising $μp$ collisions at the LHC are compared: with muon-collider beams and with ultra-cold $μ^+$ beams as proposed for $μ$TRISTAN. For the latter, a $μ$TRISTAN-type booster and the re-use of the LHeC energy-recovery-linac tunnel are discussed; colliding 1 TeV antimuons with 7 TeV protons gives $\sqrt{s}=5.3$ TeV and peak luminosities of $8.8\times10^{33}$ and $7.2\times10^{33}$ cm$^{-2}$s$^{-1}$, respectively. The detector concept and the physics potential, including searches for colour-octet muons and R-parity-violating squarks, are discussed together with the main challenges.

</details>

### [44] [Collectively pumped superradiant laser with coherence scaling beyond the standard quantum limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05976)
*Ori Somech, Howard M. Wiseman*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces generalized far-above-threshold laser models by replacing the bosonic lowering operator with a general lowering operator in the master-equation superoperators. It shows that an optical-frequency bad-cavity superradiant laser with feedback-controlled collective pumping realizes such a model and can surpass the standard quantum limit, achieving coherence scaling up to Θ(μ^{8/3}).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard quantum limit was long believed to constrain laser coherence to Θ(μ^2), while the Heisenberg limit allows Θ(μ^4). Beyond-SQL proposals had been restricted to circuit QED at microwave frequencies, so the paper aims to identify an optical-frequency setting that can exhibit beyond-SQL coherence scaling.

Method: The authors define generalized far-above-threshold laser models by substituting a general lowering operator L for the ordinary bosonic lowering operator a in the superoperators of the master equation governing the laser dynamics. They then show that an optical-frequency superradiant laser in the bad-cavity limit with feedback-controlled collective pumping realizes one such model, with L identified as the collective superradiant lowering operator J.

Result: The model can surpass standard quantum limit scaling, with laser coherence reaching as large as C=Θ(μ^{8/3}) in the optimal parameter regime. In the bad-cavity limit, μ is approximately N/2, where N is the number of superradiant atoms. The abstract does not report an experimental demonstration.

Conclusion: An optical-frequency superradiant laser with feedback-controlled collective pumping can realize a generalized far-above-threshold model that exceeds the standard quantum limit and supports coherence scaling up to Θ(μ^{8/3}), although this is below the Θ(μ^4) Heisenberg-limit scaling cited in the abstract.

Abstract: For more than half a century, the standard quantum limit (SQL) was thought to limit the laser coherence $\mathfrak{C}$ -- the number of photons emitted from the laser into the beam in one coherence time -- to a scaling $Θ(μ^2)$, where $μ$ is the mean number of optical-frequency excitations stored inside the laser. However, recently it has been shown [Baker et al., Nat. Phys. 17, 179 (2021)] that the Heisenberg limit -- an achievable ultimate limit set by quantum mechanics for the task of producing a beam with the standard properties of a laser beam -- is $\mathfrak{C}=Θ(μ^4)$, a quadratic enhancement. So far, proposals to demonstrate beyond-SQL scaling of $\mathfrak{C}$ have been limited to circuit QED, at microwave frequencies (i.e., a maser). Here, we introduce a class of generalized far-above-threshold (FAT) laser models, obtained by replacing the ordinary bosonic lowering operator $\hat{a}$ by a general lowering operator $\hat{L}$ in the superoperators appearing in the master equation that governs the FAT laser dynamics. We then show that an optical-frequency superradiant laser in the bad-cavity limit, with feedback-controlled collective pumping, realizes one such model, in which $\hat{L}$ is identified with the collective superradiant lowering operator $\hat{J}$. This model can surpass the SQL scaling, with the coherence exhibiting a scaling as large as $\mathfrak{C}= Θ(μ^{8/3})$ in the optimal parameter regime. Here, in the bad-cavity limit, $μ\approx N/2$, where $N$ is the number of superradiant atoms.

</details>

*Ramy Tannous, Dogan Sinar, Sagar Patel, Tabitha D. Arulpragasam, Issa Rishmawi, Mark Kirby, Vlad Paserin, Mihaela Vlasea, Thomas Jennewein*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A fully monolithic optomechanical chassis was fabricated by laser powder bed fusion from low-thermal-expansion Invar36, integrating optical mounts, flexures, and alignment features into one compact structure that reduces footprint versus conventional optomechanical assemblies.

The work also targets dimensional stability under varying environmental conditions and reduced system footprint.

Method: The authors designed and fabricated a fully monolithic optomechanical chassis using laser powder bed fusion additive manufacturing with thermally stable Invar36, a low-thermal-expansion alloy. Embedded optical mounts, flexures, and alignment features were incorporated directly into the chassis geometry.

Result: The monolithic chassis was successfully fabricated, and integrating the optomechanical support structure into a single compact unit reduced the footprint compared with traditional optomechanical assemblies. The abstract reports demonstration of potential for lightweight, highly customized optical systems but provides no quantitative optical, mechanical, or thermal performance data.

Conclusion: Additive manufacturing can produce lightweight, highly customized monolithic optomechanical systems, opening possible new pathways for next-generation quantum sensors and optical instrumentation.

Abstract: Additive manufacturing is increasingly being explored as an approach for producing complex structures for optical hardware. In this work, we present a fully monolithic optomechanical chassis fabricated using laser powder bed fusion additive manufacturing. The structure was fabricated from thermally stable Invar36, a low-thermal-expansion alloy that enables high dimensional stability under varying environmental conditions. Our monolithic design incorporates embedded optical mounts, flexures, and alignment features directly into the chassis geometry that exceed the practical limits of conventional subtractive manufacturing. By integrating the optomechanical support structure into a single compact unit, the system achieves a reduced footprint compared to traditional optomechanical assemblies. The results demonstrate the potential of additive manufacturing to produce lightweight, and highly customized optical systems, opening the possibility of new pathways for next-generation quantum sensors and optical instrumentation.

</details>


### [46] [Photonic and Electronic Interaction of TMD-Hybridized ZnO Nanowire Lasers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06740)
*Moritz Willems, Edwin Eobaldt, Alexander Zaunick, Aurelia Eberhard, Maximilian Tomoscheit, Lukas Raam Jäger, Xiao Chen, Preeti Sharma, Nicolas Tancogne-Dejean, Antony George, Andrey Turchanin, Silvana Botti, Giancarlo Soavi, Carsten Ronning*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Single ZnO nanowire lasers were sequentially hybridized with WS2 and MoS2 monolayers, and with thicker MoS2, to study photonic and electronic coupling. Monolayer hybridization raised the lasing threshold, quenched emission at high pump densities, and blueshifted the mode envelope; thicker MoS2 fully suppressed lasing. Simulations and DFT indicate that wavelength-dependent evanescent absorption and hole transfer from ZnO into the TMD jointly suppress lasing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semiconductor nanowire lasers are promising on-chip coherent light sources for photonic integrated circuits, but integration inevitably brings them into contact with other materials and changes their properties through hybridization. The paper aims to clarify the photonic and electronic coupling between single ZnO nanowire lasers and transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs).

Method: The authors used deterministic nanomanipulation to hybridize the same ZnO nanowire first with a WS2 monolayer and then with a MoS2 monolayer, and also examined hybridization with bilayer, trilayer, and bulk MoS2. They used Finite-Difference Eigenmode simulations to analyze evanescent-field absorption by the TMD and density functional theory calculations including the interface dipole to assess band alignment and charge transfer at the ZnO(10-1bar0)/TMD interface.

Result: Hybridization with a WS2 or MoS2 monolayer increased the lasing threshold, strongly quenched emission at high pump power densities, and blueshifted the mode envelope; the small difference between the two monolayers is attributed to wavelength-dependent removal of low-energy modes by TMD absorption, while the blueshift follows increased carrier density at threshold. Hybridization with bilayer, trilayer, and bulk MoS2 fully suppressed lasing. Simulations show that the TMD absorbs part of the evanescent field. DFT predicts type-I alignment at the ideal ZnO(10-1bar0)/TMD interface, becoming type II for a moderately reduced dipole as expected for hydroxylated facets in air; in both cases holes transfer from the nanowire into the TMD and are removed from the gain medium. Numerical threshold values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Evanescent absorption and hole transfer jointly suppress lasing in ZnO nanowire lasers hybridized with TMDs, and both effects must be accounted for when integrating nanowire lasers with 2D materials.

Abstract: Semiconductor nanowire lasers are promising on-chip coherent light sources for photonic integrated circuits, but integration inevitably places them in contact with other materials, thereby changing their properties upon hybridization. In this study, we examine the photonic and electronic coupling between single ZnO nanowire lasers and TMDs. Using deterministic nanomanipulation, we first hybridized the same nanowire first with a WS$_2$ monolayer and then with a MoS$_2$ monolayer. The respective lasing threshold rises, the emission is strongly quenched at high pump power densities, and the mode envelope blueshifts. Upon hybridization with bilayer, trilayer, and bulk MoS$_2$, lasing is fully suppressed. Finite-Difference Eigenmode simulations show that the TMD absorbs part of the evanescent field. Because this absorption is wavelength dependent, it also removes modes from the low-energy flank of the envelope, which accounts for small difference between the two monolayers, while the blueshift itself follows the increase of the carrier density at threshold. Density functional theory calculations that include the interface dipole predict a type-I alignment at the ideal ZnO(10$\bar{1}$0)/TMD interface, which turns into type~II for a moderately reduced dipole, as expected for hydroxylated facets in air. In both cases, holes are transferred from the nanowire into the TMD and removed from the gain medium.
