Evanescent absorption and hole transfer thus jointly suppress lasing, and both must be accounted for when integrating nanowire lasers with 2D materials.

</details>


### [47] [Wavefront engineering with atomic Huygens' surfaces made simple](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04538)
*K. E. Ballantine, J. Ruostekoski*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A minimal atomic bilayer metasurface—a square lattice with a two-atom unit cell—uses nonlocal radiative excitations to achieve near-unity reflectionless transmission and full 2π phase control of transmitted light without site-dependent level shifts, enabling atomic Huygens' surface wavefront engineering and connection to single-photon storage protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic arrays offer cooperative light-mediated interactions for nonlocal metasurfaces operating in the quantum regime without nanofabricated optical elements or intrinsic material absorption. The work seeks a minimal atomic implementation for wavefront engineering and a near-ideal intrinsic atomic Huygens' surface, avoiding the localized site-dependent level shifts of previous proposals and the need to simultaneously excite exactly matched pure electric-dipole and magnetic-dipole responses.

Method: The paper proposes a bilayer square lattice with a two-atom unit cell, not restricted to small lattice spacings. Nonlocal radiative excitations provide the usual electric-dipole response with even radiation parity and an odd-parity radiation channel whose leading multipolar interpretation combines magnetic-dipole and electric-quadrupole contributions in atoms exhibiting only electric-dipole transitions. A two-mode model is derived and compared with full coupled-dipole simulations near optimal lattice spacings.

Result: The design yields near-unity reflectionless transmission and full 2π phase control of transmitted light without localized site-dependent level shifts. The two-mode model accurately captures full coupled-dipole simulations near the optimal lattice spacings. The platform also connects directly to established subradiance-protected single-photon storage protocols.

Conclusion: A simple atomic bilayer can combine wavefront engineering, storage, and retrieval, suggesting a route toward quantum-regime metasurfaces without nanofabrication or material absorption. Because coupled-dipole models are widely applicable, the microscopic analysis is also relevant beyond atomic physics to nanostructured metasurfaces.

Abstract: Atomic arrays provide a cooperative platform for light-mediated interactions in nonlocal metasurfaces which can operate in the quantum regime without nanofabricated optical elements or intrinsic material absorption. We present a minimal atomic implementation to harness wavefront engineering and create a near-ideal intrinsic atomic Huygens' surface. A simple bilayer square lattice with a two-atom unit cell, not limited to small lattice spacings, provides near-unity reflectionless transmission and a full $2π$ phase control of transmitted light without the localized site-dependent level shifts of previous proposals. This simplification is achieved through nonlocal radiative excitations that provide the familiar electric-dipole response with even radiation parity as well as an odd-parity radiation channel whose leading multipolar interpretation combines magnetic-dipole and electric-quadrupole contributions in atoms exhibiting only electric dipole transitions. This eliminates the need to simultaneously excite pure electric-dipole and magnetic-dipole responses with exactly matched radiation. We derive a two-mode model for this response and show that it accurately captures full coupled-dipole simulations near the optimal lattice spacings. Because of the wide applicability of coupled-dipole models, the relevance of our microscopic analysis extends beyond atomic physics to nanostructured metasurfaces. Moreover, the same platform also connects directly to established subradiance-protected single-photon storage protocols, suggesting a route toward combining wavefront engineering, storage, and retrieval in a simple atomic bilayer.

</details>


### [48] [Geometric Pumping of Optical Work in Coherent Wave Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06666)
*Zeyu Kuang, Konstantin Y. Bliokh, Stefan Rotter*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a geometric theory of optical work in coherent electromagnetic scattering, showing that net work over an adiabatic modulation cycle is set by the Berry curvature of the outgoing field in parameter space. It requires cyclic modulation of at least two independent parameters, vanishes for maximally mixed illumination, increases with illumination coherence, and admits optimal wavefront and modulation trajectories for fully coherent light.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optical work exchanged between light and time-varying matter is important for emerging photonic technologies, but a general geometric theory describing its generation and control has been lacking.

Method: The authors establish a general geometric theory for coherent electromagnetic scattering. They analyze adiabatic modulation cycles, relate accumulated optical work to the Berry curvature of the outgoing field in parameter space, examine dependence on illumination coherence, and derive optimal incident wavefronts and modulation trajectories for fully coherent illumination.

Result: The net optical work accumulated during an adiabatic modulation cycle is a geometric quantity determined by the Berry curvature of the outgoing field in parameter space. Nonzero optical work can be generated by cyclically modulating two or more independent system parameters. Optical work pumping vanishes under maximally mixed illumination, greater illumination coherence increases the maximum work attainable through wavefront shaping, and for fully coherent illumination the optimal incident wavefront and modulation trajectory maximizing work per cycle are derived.

Conclusion: The framework unifies time-varying photonics, optomechanics, and geometric transport within a common scattering formalism.

Abstract: Optical work exchanged between light and time-varying matter underlies a wide range of emerging photonic technologies, yet a general geometric theory describing its generation and control has been lacking. Here we establish such a theory for coherent electromagnetic scattering. We show that the net optical work accumulated during an adiabatic modulation cycle is a geometric quantity determined by the Berry curvature of the outgoing field in parameter space. Nonzero optical work can thus be generated by cyclically modulating two or more independent system parameters. In contrast to conventional adiabatic pumping between incoherent reservoirs, optical work pumping vanishes under maximally mixed illumination. Greater illumination coherence increases the maximum work attainable through wavefront shaping. For fully coherent illumination, we derive the optimal incident wavefront and modulation trajectory that maximize the optical work per cycle. Our framework unifies time-varying photonics, optomechanics, and geometric transport within a common scattering formalism.

</details>


### [49] [Phase matching and mode coupling in photonic-crystal ring resonators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06456)
*Alexander E. Ulanov, Lukas Bengel, Tobias Herr*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Relative phases between superimposed refractive-index modulations in photonic-crystal ring resonators strongly affect nonlinear dynamics by adding phase and mode selection rules to phase matching, potentially suppressing nonlinear coupling and imposing forward-backward spectral chirp.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photonic-crystal ring resonators enable mode-by-mode dispersion engineering for nonlinear photonics, but when multiple modulation components are superimposed, the impact of their relative angular alignment (phase) on nonlinear dynamics has remained unclear.

Method: The authors analyze interference between nonlinear processes in the forward and backward directions in resonators with multiple periodic refractive-index modulations, and illustrate the resulting phase and mode selection rules for parametric oscillation and dissipative Kerr solitons.

Result: The relative phases between modulation patterns have a decisive impact: they add a phase and mode selection rule to established phase-matching conditions, can fully suppress nonlinear coupling, and impose a relative spectral chirp between forward- and backward-propagating waveforms.

Conclusion: The work extends the concept of phase matching to resonators with interfering nonlinear pathways and establishes the alignment of index gratings as a design dimension for nonlinear microresonators.

Abstract: Photonic-crystal ring resonators, in which periodic refractive-index modulations couple counter-propagating modes, have become a widely used platform for nonlinear photonics, offering mode-by-mode dispersion engineering. When several modulation components are superimposed, their relative angular alignment (phase) is an additional degree of freedom whose impact on the nonlinear dynamics has remained unclear. Here we show that the relative phases between the modulation patterns have a decisive impact. Through the interference of the nonlinear processes in forward and backward direction, they add a phase and mode selection rule to the established phase-matching conditions, which can fully suppress nonlinear coupling. They also impose a relative spectral chirp between forward- and backward-propagating waveforms. We illustrate these effects for parametric oscillation and dissipative Kerr solitons. Our results extend the concept of phase matching to resonators with interfering nonlinear pathways and establish the alignment of index gratings as a design dimension for nonlinear microresonators.

</details>


### [50] [Directional Control of Chiral Polymer Emission Dissymmetry by Plasmonic Metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06400)
*Debapriya Pal, Louis Minion, David Reger, Jessica Wade, Matthew J. Fuchter, A. Femius Koenderink*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper integrates chiral organic polymer thin films with planar chiral plasmonic metasurfaces to control circularly polarized emission. Angle-resolved Stokes Fourier polarimetry reveals directional emission, near-normal dissymmetry factors above 0.7, and direction-dependent reversal of emission handedness at fixed molecular stereochemistry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Circularly polarized emission is important for next-generation photonic and optoelectronic technologies, but simultaneously controlling its directionality, intensity, and handedness remains challenging. Conventional thin films offer limited control over the angular distribution of circularly polarized emission and often show strongly angle-dependent chiral emission, while metasurfaces have mainly been studied with intrinsically achiral emitters.

Method: The authors integrate organic chiral polymer thin films with planar chiral plasmonic metasurfaces and characterize the resulting chiral light-matter interactions using angle-resolved Stokes Fourier polarimetry.

Result: Metasurface coupling produces directional circularly polarized emission, near-normal dissymmetry factors exceeding 0.7, and a direction-dependent reversal of the emission handedness at fixed molecular stereochemistry.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a platform that enhances chiral light-matter interactions while controlling the directionality and angular dependence of circularly polarized emission, with potential relevance to next-generation photonic and optoelectronic technologies.

Abstract: Circularly polarized (CP) emission is central to next-generation photonic and optoelectronic technologies, yet simultaneously controlling its directionality, intensity, and handedness remains a major challenge. Advances in molecular and materials design have enabled strong chiral light-matter interactions and large dissymmetry between opposite CP states. However, conventional thin-film architectures provide limited control over the angular distribution of CP emission and often exhibit strongly angle-dependent chiral emission. Metasurfaces, on the other hand, offer powerful control over emission directionality and polarization, but have predominantly been explored with intrinsically achiral emitters. For the first time, we integrate organic chiral polymer thin films with planar chiral plasmonic metasurfaces to enhance chiral light-matter interactions while controlling the directionality and angular dependence of chiral emission. Using angle-resolved Stokes Fourier polarimetry, we show that metasurface coupling produces directional emission, near-normal dissymmetry factors exceeding 0.7, and, remarkably, direction-dependent reversal of the emission handedness at fixed molecular stereochemistry.

</details>


### [51] [Evanescent-Depth-Engineered High-Q topological Cavities for Strong Photon-Exciton Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06351)
*Zihan Mo, Renxuan Peng, Haoyang Zhang, Yu Tian, Ying Gu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes engineering the transverse evanescent depth of topological edge states in a pseudo-spin topological photonic crystal to suppress scattering in irregular topological resonant cavities, achieving high Q factors and strong photon-exciton coupling at the single-quantum level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological resonant cavities formed by interference of topological edge states suffer from inherent edge-state scattering, which results in a relatively low quality factor Q. The work aims to overcome this limitation and enable high-Q topological cavities suitable for strong photon-exciton coupling.

Method: The authors adjust the intra- and inter-cell coupling strengths in a pseudo-spin topological photonic crystal to greatly reduce the transverse evanescent depth of edge states, thereby suppressing scattering in topological resonant cavities. This mechanism is applied to irregularly shaped cavities.

Result: Various shaped topological resonant cavities with sizes of 2 to 3 micrometers achieve Q factors up to 10^5 to 10^6 in the visible range. Strong photon-exciton coupling at the single-quantum level is thereby achieved.

Conclusion: The proposed high-Q topological cavities are promising for integrated lasers, sensors, cavity quantum electrodynamics, and quantum photonics. Specific platform, material, exciton, temperature, and fabrication details are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Due to the inherent scattering in topological edge states, the topological resonant cavity (TRC) formed by their interference exhibits a relatively low quality factor (Q). To address this issue, we propose the mechanism of engineering the transverse evanescent depth of edge states to achieve high-Q in irregular shaped TRCs, which further enables strong photon-exciton coupling at the single-quantum level. By adjusting the intra- and inter-cell coupling strengths in the pseudo-spin topological photonic crystal, the transverse evanescent depth of edge states is greatly reduced, thereby suppressing the scattering of TRCs. Consequently, the Q of various shaped TRCs with 2 ~ 3 um size is up to 10^5 ~ 10^6 in the visible, so that the strong photon-exciton coupling is achieved. The high-Q topological cavities hold promising application in integrated lasers, sensors, cavity quantum electrodynamics, and quantum photonics.

</details>


### [52] [A Design Rule for Passive Reconfigurable Microwave Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06279)
*Ali Nadarloo, Mohammad Javad Hajiahmadi, Mohammad Soleimani*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A fixed, linear, passive matching network cannot increase the tuning capability of a reconfigurable microwave device; it can only transmit or reduce the tuning element's intrinsic tuning budget. The paper formalizes this budget using Smith-chart hyperbolic distance and derives closed-form performance limits for many tunable microwave components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconfigurable microwave devices place a tuning element inside a fixed passive network, and design practice often credits the network with producing large response changes from small element changes. The paper aims to show that such a fixed, linear, passive network cannot add to the element's tuning capability and to quantify the resulting limits.

Method: The paper defines a dimensionless tuning budget for each tuning element from its own impedance trajectory, measured as hyperbolic distance on the Smith chart: distance from a match is the logarithm of the voltage standing-wave ratio, and distance between two states is the logarithm of their commutation quality factor. From one inequality, it derives closed-form limits for analog and digital phase shifters, true-time-delay lines, switches, attenuators, vector modulators, impedance tuners, tunable filters, reconfigurable antennas, metasurface cells, and multiport networks. It also introduces the Lambda-chart for graphical reading of budgets and requirements and applies it to four published microwave case studies.

Result: A fixed, linear, passive network can transmit a tuning element's budget or lose part of it, but cannot enlarge it. The derived closed-form limits cover analog and digital phase shifters, true-time-delay lines, switches, attenuators, vector modulators, impedance tuners, tunable filters, reconfigurable antennas, metasurface cells, and multiport networks. The two-state and attenuation limits are attained by explicit circuits, and the phase limit by a constant-loss trajectory. The Lambda-chart is introduced and applied to four published microwave case studies.

Conclusion: Fixed passive networks cannot add tuning capability beyond the tuning element's own budget. The tuning-budget formulation, closed-form limits, explicit attaining circuits, and Lambda-chart provide ready-to-use tools for analyzing and designing reconfigurable microwave devices and multiport networks.

Abstract: Every reconfigurable microwave device places a tuning element - a varactor, a diode, transistor, microelectromechanical or phase-change switch, a ferroelectric or liquid-crystal load - inside a fixed passive network. The network can make a small change of the element produce a large change of the response, and design practice often credits it with the device's tuning capability. This paper shows that a fixed, linear, passive network cannot add to that capability. Each tuning element carries a tuning budget, a dimensionless length computed from its own impedance trajectory, and a fixed passive network can transmit that budget or lose part of it, never enlarge it. The unit of the budget is the hyperbolic distance of the Smith chart: the distance from a match is the logarithm of the voltage standing-wave ratio, and the distance between two states sets their mutual mismatch and is the logarithm of their commutation quality factor. From one inequality we derive closed-form, ready-to-use limits for analog and digital phase shifters, true-time-delay lines, switches, attenuators, vector modulators, impedance tuners, tunable filters, reconfigurable antennas, metasurface cells and multiport networks; the two-state and attenuation limits are attained by explicit circuits, and the phase limit by a constant-loss trajectory. We introduce the $Λ$-chart, a Smith-chart companion on which budgets and requirements are read graphically, and apply it to four published microwave case studies.

</details>


### [53] [Hyperbolic Nanocavities for Single-Molecule Vibrational Strong Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06240)
*Stavros Athanasiou, Olivier J. F. Martin, Francisco J. García-Vidal, Johannes Feist*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper theoretically demonstrates that hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) nanocavities confining hyperbolic phonon polaritons can achieve single-molecule vibrational strong coupling, with quality factors around 600 and effective mode volumes near 10^-7 λ^3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperbolic phonon polaritons in low-loss anisotropic materials such as hBN provide extreme sub-diffractional mid-infrared field confinement and can enhance light interaction with molecular vibrations. Collective vibrational strong coupling with molecular ensembles and HPhPs has been shown in extended slabs, but reaching this regime with a single molecule remains a major challenge and could enable studies of how vacuum electromagnetic fields influence molecular dynamics and chemical reactivity.

Method: The authors perform a theoretical study of hBN nanocavities that laterally confine HPhPs within a finite volume. They combine full-wave electromagnetic simulations with a few-mode quantization approach to analyze the nanocavity resonances, their frequencies, spectral density enhancement, quality factors, and effective mode volumes, and they relate the resonance frequencies to simple HPhP ray trajectories and cavity aspect ratio.

Result: The nanocavities support a rich set of asymmetric resonances that strongly enhance the local spectral density. Their frequencies follow from simple HPhP ray trajectories and can be tuned through the cavity aspect ratio. The authors identify resonances with quality factors of about 600 and effective mode volumes of |V_eff| ~ 10^-7 λ^3, which are sufficient for single-molecule strong coupling to vibrational transitions with dipole moments on the order of 1 D.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic nanocavities are established as a promising platform for single-molecule vibrational strong coupling and open new routes toward local control of vibrational dynamics and molecular energy flow.

Abstract: Hyperbolic phonon polaritons (HPhPs) in low-loss anisotropic materials such as hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) provide extreme sub-diffractional field confinement in the mid-infrared, making them attractive for enhancing the interaction of light with molecular vibrations. Collective vibrational strong coupling between molecular ensembles and HPhPs has already been demonstrated in extended slabs. Reaching this regime with a single molecule, however, remains a major challenge, and would provide a platform for investigating how vacuum electromagnetic fields influence molecular dynamics and chemical reactivity. Here, we show theoretically that single-molecule vibrational strong coupling can be reached in hBN nanocavities that laterally confine HPhPs within a finite volume. Combining full-wave electromagnetic simulations with the few-mode quantization approach, we find that these nanocavities support a rich set of asymmetric resonances that strongly enhance the local spectral density, with frequencies that follow from simple HPhP ray trajectories and can be tuned through the cavity aspect ratio. We identify resonances with quality factors of about $600$ and effective mode volumes of $|V_{\mathrm{eff}}| \sim 10^{-7}λ^{3}$, sufficient for single-molecule strong coupling with vibrational transitions with dipole moments on the order of $1\,\mathrm{D}$. These results establish hyperbolic nanocavities as a promising platform for single-molecule vibrational strong coupling and open new routes toward the local control of vibrational dynamics and molecular energy flow.

</details>


### [54] [Spatial structure of surface plasmon resonance under narrow-beam illumination and its implications for sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06222)
*Tristan Miralles, Rémi Pollès, Pauline Bennet, Denis Langevin, Martine Mihailovic, Emmanuel Centeno, Jack Mock, David R. Smith, Antoine Moreau*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper models and simulates spatial interference between direct Fresnel reflection and plasmon-radiated light in a surface plasmon resonance structure, then defines a reflected-beam spatial observable that is more than an order of magnitude more sensitive than amplitude-based measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When the beam waist is only about an order of magnitude larger than the surface plasmon propagation length, the direct and plasmon-mediated contributions separate spatially, and their destructive interference forms a dark fringe. The work aims to exploit this effect and the resulting sensitivity to the outer medium.

Method: The authors develop an analytical model of the interference, design a realistic setup using off-the-shelf components, and simulate the complete experiment from illumination to detection with an open-source framework. They define an observable in the regime where interference is maximized. The abstract does not specify the components or framework.

Result: In the maximized-interference regime, the reflected beam's spatial structure becomes extremely sensitive to the outer medium. The proposed observable is more than an order of magnitude more sensitive than amplitude-based measurements, with an expected comparable improvement in resolution. No numerical sensitivity or resolution values are provided.

Conclusion: The spatial structure of the reflected beam provides a promising route to improved surface plasmon resonance sensing sensitivity and resolution. The abstract reports modeling, setup design, and simulation rather than experimental validation, and does not provide specific quantitative performance figures.

Abstract: The reflection of light on a surface plasmon resonance structure can be understood as the interference between the direct Fresnel reflection and the light radiated back by the plasmon as it propagates along the metal film. When the beam waist is only an order of magnitude larger than the surface plasmon propagation length, these two contributions separate spatially, and their destructive interference localizes into a dark fringe within the reflected beam. We develop an analytical model of this effect, design a realistic setup based on off-the-shelf components to observe it, and simulate the complete experiment, from illumination to detection, with an open-source framework. In the regime where the interference is maximized, the spatial structure of the reflected beam becomes extremely sensitive to the outer medium. This allows us to define an observable more than an order of magnitude more sensitive than amplitude-based measurements, and we expect a comparable improvement in resolution.

</details>


### [55] [Closed-form dyadic Green functions for transversely uniaxial bianisotropic medium with equal magnetoelectric scalars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06172)
*Akhlesh Lakhtakia, Tom G. Mackay*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper revisits Weiglhofer's 1994 claim that closed-form dyadic Green functions do not exist for a transversely uniaxial bianisotropic medium with equal magnetoelectric scalars, reproduces the key derivation, and shows that closed-form expressions do exist for all four dyadic Green functions of the unbounded medium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to correct Weiglhofer's 1994 conclusion, obtained via the scalar factorization method, that the dyadic Green functions of this medium could not be found in closed form.

Method: The paper reproduces the essentials of the derivation and provides closed-form expressions for all four dyadic Green functions of the unbounded medium, relating electric and magnetic fields to electric and magnetic source current densities. The abstract does not provide the explicit formulas or further derivation details.

Result: The earlier conclusion is shown to be incorrect: closed-form expressions are given for all four dyadic Green functions of the unbounded transversely uniaxial bianisotropic medium with equal magnetoelectric scalars.

Conclusion: Closed-form dyadic Green functions do exist for this medium, refuting Weiglhofer's 1994 impossibility claim. The abstract does not provide explicit expressions or additional validation details.

Abstract: A transversely uniaxial bianisotropic medium with equal magnetoelectric scalars was conceived by Weiglhofer in 1994. Using the scalar factorization method, he concluded that the dyadic Green functions of this medium could not be found in closed form. That conclusion is incorrect. The essentials of the derivation are reproduced here, and closed-form expressions are given for all four dyadic Green functions of the unbounded medium, linking the electric and magnetic fields to electric and magnetic source current densities.

</details>


### [56] [BIC-Engineered Silicon Nitride Microring Resonators Enabled by an Etch-Free Laser-Writing Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06167)
*Leila Hajshahvaladi, Mohammad Amin Salmani, Media Abbasi, Mobin Yaghoobi, Mohammad Asgari, Gholam-Mohammad Parsanasab*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: An etch-free photonic platform uses SU-8 ridges written by direct UV laser lithography on unpatterned silicon nitride, with bound states in the continuum used to suppress radiation from the ridge sidewalls; demonstrated waveguides reach about 1.5 dB/cm near the first BIC and a microring reaches loaded Q up to 1.6e4 and estimated intrinsic Q of 3.3e5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper seeks a complementary, etch-free fabrication approach with fewer steps that avoids scattering from patterned nitride sidewalls while still controlling radiative loss by design, using BICs to manage radiation from SU-8 ridges on an unpatterned silicon nitride film.

Method: SU-8 ridges are written by direct ultraviolet laser lithography on an unpatterned silicon nitride film to define waveguides and microring resonators. The guided transverse-magnetic mode lies within the transverse-electric slab-mode continuum, and BICs are used as a design tool. Coupled-mode analysis and full-vectorial simulations predict sidewall-radiation cancellation at discrete widths and curvature-dependent BIC bands; laser-written waveguides and a microring are measured, with a coupler model used to support the over-coupled assignment for extracting intrinsic Q.

Result: Simulations predict a first BIC at 2.25 micron width, radiation below 1 dB/cm over a 0.3 micron width window and about 200 nm bandwidth, and curvature-confined cancellation at discrete bending-radius and width bands. Measured laser-written waveguides follow the predicted width dependence of loss, reaching approximately 1.5 dB/cm near the BIC where non-radiative loss dominates. A microring designed on a radius-selected BIC band shows loaded quality factors up to 1.6e4 and, under the over-coupled assignment supported by a coupler model, an intrinsic quality factor of 3.3e5.

Conclusion: The initial experimental demonstration supports an etch-free, fewer-step approach in which a patterned low-index SU-8 layer on an unetched nitride film sets radiation via BIC design, avoiding patterned nitride sidewall scattering. The approach could be extended to other nanostructures, and optimization of writing conditions and materials is ongoing.

Abstract: We report the initial experimental demonstration of a complementary, etch-free approach with fewer fabrication steps, in useful SU-8 ridges written by direct ultraviolet laser lithography on an unpatterned silicon nitride film define waveguides and microring resonators. Because the nitride film is never patterned, scattering at patterned nitride sidewalls does not arise, and the remaining radiative loss is engineered by design. The guided transverse-magnetic mode lies within the continuum of transverse-electric slab modes, and we use bound states in the continuum (BICs) as a design tool to control this radiation. Coupled-mode analysis and full-vectorial simulations show that radiation from the two ridge sidewalls cancels by destructive interference at discrete widths, with a first BIC at 2.25 micron and radiation below 1 dB/cm over a 0.3 micron width window and about 200 nm of bandwidth, and that curvature confines this cancellation to discrete bands of bending radius and width. Laser-written waveguides follow the predicted width dependence of the loss, reaching approximately 1.5 dB/cm near the BIC, where non-radiative loss dominates. A microring designed on a radius-selected BIC band exhibits loaded quality factors up to 1.6 \times 10^4 and, under the over-coupled assignment supported by a coupler model, an intrinsic quality factor of 3.3 \times 10^5. Because radiation is set by a patterned low-index layer on an unetched film, the same approach could be extended to other nanostructures. Optimization of the writing conditions and materials is ongoing.

</details>


### [57] [Finesse evolution in a bichromatic cavity under blue and red resonant illumination in vacuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00277)
*M. S. Caracas Nuñez, A. Kadakkavattathu, G. Gatta, L. Duca, N. Mizukami, C. Sias*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A bichromatic high-finesse cavity coated for 455 nm and 671 nm was tested in vacuum under high circulating power. The 455 nm finesse degraded substantially over 11 hours, while 671 nm showed no measurable degradation; 671 nm operation caused no measurable loss at either wavelength. Partial recovery was achieved with 455 nm illumination in air, and the damage kinetics were compatible with nucleation-and-growth models without identifying a unique microscopic mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The long-term performance of high-finesse optical cavities depends on mirror-coating stability under vacuum and continuous high circulating power. For cavities supporting multiple wavelengths, it is important to determine whether degradation at one wavelength compromises performance at another, especially for cavity quantum electrodynamics experiments requiring diverse optical fields for cavity stabilization, trapping, and light–matter coupling.

Method: The authors investigated wavelength-dependent degradation and recovery of a bichromatic cavity with high-reflectivity coatings at 455 nm and 671 nm in vacuum. They used cavity ring-down measurements during continuous exposure to high circulating power, compared oxygen-depletion and Avrami-type loss models, and tested recovery by illumination at 455 nm in air.

Result: After 11 hours of continuous high-power exposure, the 455 nm finesse decreased from 7.7×10^3 to 1.9×10^3, while simultaneous measurements showed no measurable degradation at 671 nm. Continuous operation at 671 nm produced no measurable degradation at either wavelength under the tested conditions. Model comparison indicated kinetics compatible with nucleation and growth but did not uniquely identify the microscopic mechanism. Partial finesse recovery was demonstrated with 455 nm illumination in air. The abstract reports an estimate of a safe blue-light intensity but does not provide its numerical value.

Conclusion: Laser-induced optical damage in the bichromatic cavity is wavelength-dependent, with degradation observed at 455 nm but not measurably at 671 nm under the tested conditions, and partial recovery possible after 455 nm illumination in air. The results provide guidance for safely usable blue-light intensity in such cavities.

Abstract: The long-term performance of high-finesse optical cavities depends on the stability of their mirror coatings under vacuum and under continuous exposure to high circulating power. In cavities supporting multiple wavelengths, a central question is whether degradation at one wavelength compromises performance at another. Understanding this interplay is essential for those cavity quantum electrodynamics experiments that need diverse optical fields for cavity stabilization, trapping, and light--matter coupling. Here, we investigate wavelength-dependent degradation and recovery of a bichromatic cavity with high-reflectivity coating at 455 nm and 671 nm in vacuum. Cavity ring-down measurements reveal a decrease in the 455 nm finesse from 7.7*10^3 to 1.9*10^3 after 11 hours of continuous exposure to high circulating power. Simultaneous measurements show no measurable degradation at 671 nm. Continuous operation at 671 nm, instead, produces no measurable degradation at either wavelength under the tested conditions. Comparison of oxygen depletion and Avrami-type loss models indicates kinetics compatible with nucleation and growth, without uniquely identifying the microscopic mechanism. We demonstrate partial recovery of the finesse with illumination at 455 nm in air. Our results indicate a wavelength dependence of laser-induced optical damage in bichromatic cavities and provide an estimate of the blue light intensity that can be safely used in such cavities.

</details>


### [58] [Colloidal 2D WS2 nanosheets exhibiting high-purity single photon emission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06144)
*Markus Xemaire, Jakob Keck, Marco Kögel, Marc Brecht, Jannik C. Meyer, Alfred J. Meixner, Jannika Lauth*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Wet-chemically synthesized monolayered 2D WS2 nanosheets about 14 ± 6 nm in lateral size emit single photons from exciton recombination without substrate patterning, strain/defect engineering, or spectral filtering; single-particle spectroscopy of 70 nanosheets shows exciton, trio, and defect-localized emission with fast dynamics, no blinking, limited spectral diffusion, and strong antibunching at 5 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper seeks quantum emitters that deliver single photons on demand for photonic technologies and addresses the need for simpler emitters than micrometer-sized WS2 monolayers requiring local defect or strain engineering, while overcoming blinking and longer emission cycles in established colloidal quantum dot systems.

Method: Monolayered 2D WS2 nanosheets were synthesized wet-chemically with lateral sizes of 14 ± 6 nm. Single-particle spectroscopy was performed on 70 individual nanosheets without substrate-induced patterning, strain/defect engineering, or spectral filtering. Emission was studied under pulsed excitation at 80 MHz and at 5 K.

Result: Photoluminescence arose from excitons at 2.02 eV, trios at 1.94 eV, and defect-localized states at 1.85 eV, with fast emission (t1 < 0.2 ns, t2 = 3.2 ns). Colloidal WS2 nanosheets showed limited spectral diffusion, no blinking, and distinct photon antibunching with g2(0) = 0.07 at 5 K under 80 MHz pulsed excitation.

Conclusion: Colloidal WS2 nanosheets can act as deterministic single-photon emitters, retaining high single-photon purity while tackling blinking and longer emission cycles associated with established colloidal quantum dot systems. Elevated-temperature performance is not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: The search for quantum emitters, delivering single photons "on demand" at cryogenic and elevated temperatures for photonic technologies is ever progressing. For example, micrometer-sized WS2 monolayers exhibit single photon emission by elaborated local defect or strain engineering. Here, we show that wet-chemically synthesised monolayered two-dimensional (2D) WS2 nano sheets (NSs) with lateral sizes of 14 (+/- 6 nm) readily emit single photons from exciton recombination without the need for substrate-induced patterning, strain/defect engineering or spectral filtering. Single particle spectroscopy of 70 individual NSs shows photoluminescence from excitons (2.02 eV), trios (1.94 eV) and defect-localized states (1.85 eV) with fast emission (t1<0.2 ns, t2=3.2 ns), allowing highly frequent excitation cycles. Pulsed at 80 MHz, colloidal WS2 NSs exhibit limited spectral diffusion, no blinking and distinct photon antibunching (g2(0)=0.07 at 5 K), thereby tackling challenges of established colloidal quantum dot systems (blinking, longer emission cycles), while retaining high single photon purity as key quality of deterministic quantum emitters.

</details>


### [59] [Brillouin comb line amplifiers for optical atomic clocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03038)
*Ryan L. Russell, Lachlan Pointon, Sarah Katherine Scholten, Choon Kong Lai, Lisa-Sophie Haerteis, Cong Tinh Bui, Alvaro Casas Bedoya, Andreas Boes, Duk Yong Choi, Stephen J. Madden, Andre Luiten, Benjamin J. Eggleton, Moritz Merklein*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes making an optical frequency comb the central engine of an optical clock by using Brillouin-amplified comb lines for atomic spectroscopy, thereby removing the need for stable clock lasers. It demonstrates a rubidium optical clock with competitive stability and a chip-integrated Brillouin amplifier with high gain and noise suppression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optical atomic clocks offer orders-of-magnitude better performance than microwave clocks, and on-chip integration could enable widespread deployment. However, conventional optical clocks require narrow-linewidth clock lasers plus an optical frequency comb, and the lasers impose significant size, weight, power, and cost penalties that block low-cost, scalable, fully on-chip optical clocks.

Method: The authors position the frequency comb as the clock’s central engine and use Brillouin-amplified comb lines for atomic spectroscopy, eliminating stable clock lasers. They characterize the amplified comb lines’ frequency noise, realize a clock based on the 5S1/2 to 5D5/2 transition in 87Rb, and demonstrate a photonic chip-integrated Brillouin amplifier pumped by a simple, low-cost diode laser.

Result: The amplified comb lines reach an ultra-low frequency noise floor of 6 Hz^2/Hz at 30 kHz offset, an order of magnitude below a high-end commercial fiber laser. The Rb clock achieves fractional frequency stability of 2×10^-13 at τ = 1 s, averaging to 9×10^-15 at τ = 1000 s. The chip-integrated Brillouin amplifier achieves a record 62.9 dB gain and up to 40 dB transferred noise suppression.

Conclusion: The work opens a route to compact optical clocks and enables use of atomic transitions that lack low-noise lasers by broadening a comb to the required wavelength and amplifying individual lines. It supports a lower-cost, scalable approach to optical clock deployment.

Abstract: Optical atomic clocks outperform the microwave clocks at the heart of current timing and navigation infrastructure by orders-of-magnitude, and recent progress in on-chip integration provides the opportunity to widely deploy them. Conventionally, a narrow linewidth laser interrogates the atomic transition, and an optical frequency comb divides its stabilised frequency (hundreds of terahertz) down to a microwave that is electronically countable. The laser(s) impose a significant size, weight, power and cost penalty, which is a current barrier to low-cost, scalable, fully on-chip optical clocks. Here, we position the frequency comb as the central engine of the clock by using Brillouin-amplified comb lines for atomic spectroscopy, which eliminates the need for stable clock lasers. Amplified comb lines reach an ultra-low frequency noise floor of $6\,\mathrm{Hz^2/Hz}$ at $30\,\mathrm{kHz}$ offset, an order-of-magnitude below a high-end commercial fibre laser. We realize a clock based on the $\mathrm{5S}_{1/2}\rightarrow\mathrm{5D}_{5/2}$ transition in $^{87}\mathrm{Rb}$ with competitive fractional frequency stability of $2\times10^{-13}$ at $τ= 1\,\mathrm{s}$, averaging to $9\times10^{-15}$ at $τ= 1000\,\mathrm{s}$. Lastly, we demonstrate a photonic chip-integrated Brillouin amplifier with a record 62.9 dB gain and up to 40 dB transferred noise suppression when pumped with a simple, low-cost diode laser. This opens a new route to compact optical clocks and enables use of atomic transitions lacking low-noise lasers by broadening a comb to the required wavelength and amplifying single lines.

</details>


### [60] [Millimeter-Wave Gaussian-to-Flat-Top Beam Shaping Using a Single-Curvature Lens: A Novel Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06053)
*Ehsan Imanbeygi, Mohammad Hossein Koohi Ghamsari, Mehdi Ahmadi-Boroujeni*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A single-sided plano-convex lens is designed by combining collimator and beam-shaper profiles and optimized by full-wave simulation to convert a 100 GHz conical horn’s Gaussian beam into a 400 mm-radius flat-top beam at 3 m for stand-off millimeter-wave imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Millimeter-wave sources emit divergent Gaussian beams, but active stand-off imaging requires uniform flat-top illumination; the paper aims to achieve this with a single curved surface rather than a more complex double-sided lens.

Method: The lens surface combines collimator and beam-shaper profiles. A plano-convex lens profile is derived and placed 52 mm from a 100 GHz conical horn with an 11.9 mm incident beam radius; it is optimized using a full-wave-simulation-based procedure and compared with a previously designed concave-convex double-sided lens. Frequency sensitivity is also investigated.

Result: Simulations show the optimized single-sided lens yields a flat-top intensity distribution with satisfactory ripple and roll-off within the desired field of view, producing a 400 mm-radius uniform beam at 3 m; quantitative ripple, roll-off, and frequency-sensitivity values are not given in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed single curved-surface lens is suitable for generating uniform illumination in active stand-off millimeter-wave imaging systems; the abstract reports that frequency sensitivity was investigated but does not provide its detailed outcomes.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel method for converting the divergent Gaussian intensity distribution of a millimeter-wave source into a uniform (flat-top) distribution using a single curved surface. The proposed method combines the surface profile of a collimator with that of a beam shaper. To validate the method, the profile of a single-sided (plano-convex) lens is derived and placed 52 mm in front of a conical horn antenna operating at 100 GHz. The lens transforms the Gaussian radiation of the horn, with an incident beam radius of 11.9 mm, into a uniform beam with a radius of 400 mm at a distance of 3 m. The lens surface profile is optimized using a full-wave-simulation-based procedure, and the resulting output beam is compared with that of a previously designed double-sided (concave-convex) lens. Simulation results show that, within the desired field of view, the optimized single-sided lens produces a flat-top intensity distribution with satisfactory ripple and roll-off. Finally, the sensitivity of the optimized lens to frequency variation is investigated. The proposed lens is suitable for generating uniform illumination in active stand-off millimeter-wave imaging systems.

</details>


### [61] [Inducing cooperative dynamics in an inhomogeneous many-emitter ensemble with a pulse sequence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02973)
*Vincent P. Iglesias-Cardinale, Shreekanth S. Yuvarajan, Herbert F. Fotso*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper shows that periodic pulse driving can enhance cooperative superradiant dynamics in a spectrally inhomogeneous ensemble of quantum emitters, with signatures emerging when dephasing is suppressed by enough pulses within the emitter lifetime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative superradiance in dense quantum-emitter ensembles is typically suppressed by spectral disparities among emitters; the authors aim to recover or enhance such nonequilibrium cooperative dynamics using an appropriately tuned external periodic pulse sequence.

Method: The authors use a semi-analytical mean-field solution and digital quantum simulation to examine ensemble coherence and emitted-radiation intensity as functions of spectral disparity, time, atom number, and inter-pulse delay.

Result: Clear signatures of enhanced cooperative dynamics are identified for the pulse-driven inhomogeneous ensemble when dephasing is sufficiently suppressed by a sufficient number of pulses during the individual emitter lifetime. The abstract does not provide quantitative values or specific experimental parameters.

Conclusion: Externally applied control fields provide a post-fabrication tuning knob for controlling the dynamics of many-emitter quantum ensembles.

Abstract: Driving a many-particle quantum system with an appropriately tuned external field can give rise to features in its nonequilibrium dynamics that are absent in the equilibrium counterpart. Cooperative dynamics in a dense ensemble of quantum emitters manifested through superradiance, a burst in the emission intensity as a function of time, is typically suppressed by spectral disparities among emitters in the ensemble. We demonstrate the enhancement of cooperative dynamics in a spectrally inhomogeneous ensemble of quantum emitters when it is driven by a periodic pulse sequence. Using both a semi-analytical mean-field solution and a digital quantum simulation, we examine the coherence of the pulse-driven ensemble and the intensity of the emitted radiation as a function of the spectral disparity in the ensemble, as a function of time, the number of atoms in the system, and as a function of the inter-pulse delay. We identify clear signatures of the enhanced cooperative dynamics for the pulse-driven inhomogeneous ensemble when dephasing in the dynamics of the ensemble is suppressed by a sufficient number of pulses during the individual emitter lifetime. These results demonstrate a post-fabrication tuning knob based on externally applied control fields for the dynamics of the many-emitter ensemble.

</details>


### [62] [Single-sided lens terahertz flat-top beam shaping in the Fresnel region](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06050)
*Ehsan Imanbeygi, Mohammad Hossein Koohi Ghamsari, Mehdi Ahmadi-Boroujeni, Babak Honarbakhsh*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces an analytical method for designing a single-sided plano-convex lens that combines collimation and THz flat-top beam shaping in the Fresnel region. Two lenses designed for 100 and 200 GHz produce flat-top beams with target radii of 400 and 500 mm at 3 and 4 m, respectively. Analytical, integral-equation, and finite-element evaluations show performance comparable to a conventional double-sided concave-convex lens, with robustness to frequency and alignment variations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to simplify fabrication by integrating collimating and beam-shaping functions into a single optical surface and to provide a robust single-curvature design suitable for practical THz imaging systems.

Method: A lens profile is derived from geometrical optics principles for a single-sided plano-convex lens. Two lenses are designed to convert a divergent Gaussian beam from a monochromatic THz source at 100 and 200 GHz into flat-top beams with specified target radii and distances. The resulting fields are evaluated using analytical, integral equation, and finite element techniques and compared with those from a conventional double-sided concave-convex lens. Sensitivity to frequency and alignment variations is also investigated.

Result: The designed lenses produce flat-top beams with target radii of 400 and 500 mm at distances of 3 and 4 m, respectively. The resulting fields are comparable to those produced by a conventional double-sided concave-convex lens. The design is reported to be robust under frequency and alignment variations. Quantitative performance metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The single-curvature plano-convex lens approach is effective and robust for THz flat-top beam shaping, simplifies fabrication by combining collimation and shaping on one surface, and is suitable for integration into practical THz imaging systems.

Abstract: In this paper, a novel, to our knowledge, analytical approach is introduced for the design of a single-sided plano-convex lens (PCL) capable of terahertz (THz) flat-top beam shaping in the Fresnel region. The lens profile, derived from geometrical optics principles, integrates collimating and beam-shaping functions into a single optical surface, thereby simplifying fabrication. The efficiency of the method is demonstrated by designing two PCLs that convert a divergent Gaussian beam from a monochromatic THz source (at 100 and 200 GHz) into flat-top beams with target radii of 400 and 500 mm at distances of 3 and 4 m, respectively. The resulting fields, evaluated through analytical, integral equation, and finite element techniques, are shown to be comparable to those produced by a conventional double-sided concave-convex lens. Furthermore, the sensitivity of the design to frequency and alignment variations is investigated, confirming the robustness of this single-curvature approach for integration into practical THz imaging systems.

</details>


### [63] [Pump-scheme dependence of few-emitter lasers: exact multilevel correlations and photon statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01205)
*Stephan Hartmann*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper tests whether a few-emitter laser's multilevel excitation cycle can be replaced by a single Markovian two-level pump jump, using an exact master-equation solution and showing that this replacement fails for key coherence and photon-statistics predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-emitter laser models commonly replace the gain-medium excitation cycle with a single Markovian pump jump of a two-level emitter. The authors test this replacement while holding cavity and laser-transition parameters fixed and fitting the two-level pump rate to the same mean photon number.

Method: They use an exact damping-basis continued-fraction solution of the cavity-emitter master equation for N identical d-level emitters with arbitrary independent incoherent transitions and one cavity-coupled transition, restricted to the known reduced space of level populations and lasing coherences. They also use a pump-quantum filtration, exact finite-system series for the leading inter-emitter coherence coefficient, and long-time output-counting Fano-factor and g2(0) calculations.

Result: The pump-quantum filtration shows that inter-emitter coherence in Lambda and ladder media begins at second rather than first order in the pump; its leading coefficient is evaluated and the recycling rate at which its sign changes is mapped. At matched photon number, rate-limited pump-cycle steps push the long-time output-counting Fano factor of two and three emitters well below one: for two emitters it reaches 0.66 at mean cavity occupation 0.3 and remains 0.74 at occupation one, where g2(0) is within 3% of unity because the anticorrelation occurs at delays of about half a cavity lifetime rather than at zero delay. For two and three emitters, the fitted two-level model gets both statistics wrong and places the output above the shot-noise level. Compared with independent single-emitter lasers at the same output, the common cavity erodes rather than enhances this single-emitter effect.

Conclusion: The single-Markovian-pump two-level replacement is inadequate for describing few-emitter lasers with rate-limited excitation cycles, since it misses multilevel coherence and output-statistics effects. Sub-shot-noise output can arise without zero-delay antibunching, and a common cavity can reduce rather than enhance the single-emitter effect.

Abstract: Few-emitter laser models usually replace the excitation cycle of the gain medium by a single Markovian pump jump of a two-level emitter. We test this replacement with the cavity and laser-transition parameters held fixed and the two-level pump rate fitted to the same mean photon number, using an exact damping-basis continued-fraction solution of the cavity--emitter master equation for $N$ identical $d$-level emitters with arbitrary independent incoherent transitions and one cavity-coupled transition, in the known reduced space of level populations and lasing coherences. First, a pump-quantum filtration shows that the inter-emitter coherence of $Λ$ and ladder media starts at second rather than first order in the pump; we evaluate its leading coefficient from the exact finite-system series and map the recycling rate at which its sign changes. Second, at matched photon number, rate-limited steps in the pump cycle push the long-time output-counting Fano factor of two and three emitters well below one: for two emitters it reaches 0.66 at a mean cavity occupation of 0.3 and is still 0.74 at an occupation of one, where $g^{(2)}(0)$ is within 3% of unity, because the anticorrelation sits at delays of about half a cavity lifetime rather than at zero delay. For two and three emitters the fitted two-level model gets both statistics wrong and places the output above the shot-noise level. Against independent single-emitter lasers at the same output, the common cavity erodes rather than enhances this single-emitter effect.

</details>


### [64] [Optical angular momentum and pseudospin-orbit coupling in photonic graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05944)
*Omri Bahat-Treidel, Daniel Leykam, Anton S. Desyatnikov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper studies structured laser beams with angular momentum undergoing conical diffraction in photonic graphene and shows that their orbital angular momentum follows lattice pseudospin dynamics rather than that of the slowly varying envelope.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to clarify the angular-momentum dynamics of structured laser beams during conical diffraction in photonic graphene and to test whether lattice pseudospin behaves as a genuine angular-momentum degree of freedom.

Method: The study combines numerical simulations with analytical calculations for laser beams carrying angular momentum in photonic graphene, and also examines propagation in staggered honeycomb lattices described by the massive Dirac equation.

Result: The orbital angular momentum of the beam tracks the dynamics of the lattice pseudospin instead of the angular momentum of the slowly varying envelope. In staggered honeycomb lattices, the orbital angular momentum is highly sensitive to both the band-gap size and the envelope's initial angular momentum. The numerical results are interpreted through an analogy with the orbital magnetization of electrons.

Conclusion: The findings provide evidence that pseudospin can act as a genuine form of angular momentum, and they establish that orbital angular momentum in massive Dirac-type photonic lattices depends strongly on band-gap size and the initial envelope angular momentum.

Abstract: We investigate the dynamics of structured laser beams carrying angular momentum (AM) as they undergo conical diffraction in photonic graphene. Using a combination of numerical simulations and analytical calculations, we demonstrate a surprising result: the beam's orbital AM tracks the dynamics of the lattice pseudospin rather than the AM of its slowly varying envelope. This finding provides evidence that pseudospin behaves as a genuine form of angular momentum. Furthermore, we explore beam propagation in staggered honeycomb lattices governed by the massive Dirac equation, revealing that the orbital AM is highly sensitive to both the band gap size and the envelope's initial AM. We explain our numerical results by drawing an analogy with the orbital magnetization of electrons.

</details>


### [65] [Time-resolved infrared spectroscopy with entangled photons and visible-light detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05800)
*Jasleen Kaur, Yu Mukai, Ryo Okamoto, Shigeki Takeuchi*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates an ultrafast, time-resolved form of quantum infrared spectroscopy that uses frequency-entangled photon pairs, a picosecond visible pump, and silicon-detector readout of visible partner photons. It resolves picosecond carrier dynamics in a semiconductor saturable absorber mirror near 1529 nm with a picowatt-level probe, unaffected by thermal background and by sample infrared photoluminescence 50 times stronger than the probe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-resolved infrared spectroscopy can reveal molecular structural changes and charge-carrier dynamics on intrinsic timescales, but conventional implementations require complex ultrashort infrared pulse sources, cryogenically cooled detectors to overcome thermal background, and intense probes that may perturb the sample. Quantum infrared spectroscopy avoids these constraints by detecting only the visible partner photon, but it had been limited to steady-state measurements; the motivation is to extend it to ultrafast, time-resolved measurements.

Method: The authors use a picosecond visible pump pulse and a silicon detector. Each visible pulse generates frequency-entangled photon pairs within a picosecond window; the infrared photon of a pair probes the excited sample at a set delay after excitation, and the transient response is read out from the count rate of its visible partner photon. They demonstrate the approach on a semiconductor saturable absorber mirror near 1529 nm using a picowatt-level probe.

Result: The approach resolves picosecond carrier dynamics in a semiconductor saturable absorber mirror near 1529 nm with a picowatt-level probe. The measurement is unaffected by thermal background and by sample infrared photoluminescence 50 times stronger than the probe.

Conclusion: Ultrafast, time-resolved quantum infrared spectroscopy is demonstrated with visible-pump excitation and silicon-detector readout of visible partner photons. The authors state that the approach is applicable to the mid-infrared fingerprint region, where infrared detection is hardest, and to the terahertz region.

Abstract: Time-resolved infrared (TR-IR) spectroscopy reveals molecular structural changes and charge-carrier dynamics on their intrinsic timescales, in photosynthesis, catalysis and optoelectronic materials, but requires complex sources of ultrashort infrared pulses, cryogenically cooled detectors to overcome thermal background, and intense probes that can perturb the sample. Quantum infrared spectroscopy avoids these constraints: harnessing quantum interference between the generation processes of frequency-entangled photon pairs, it reads out the infrared response of a sample by detecting only the visible partner photon, for which efficient detectors exist. To date, it has been limited to steady-state measurements. Here we demonstrate its ultrafast, time-resolved form with a picosecond visible pump pulse and a silicon detector. Each visible pulse generates photon pairs within a picosecond window; the infrared photon of a pair probes the excited sample at a set delay after excitation, and the transient response is read out from the count rate of its visible partner. We resolve picosecond carrier dynamics in a semiconductor saturable absorber mirror near 1529 nm with a picowatt-level probe, unaffected by thermal background and by sample IR photoluminescence 50 times stronger than the probe. The approach is applicable to the mid-infrared, fingerprint region, where infrared detection is hardest, and to the terahertz region.

</details>


### [66] [A closed-form design rule for photonic hopfions of arbitrary order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05595)
*Ali Nadarloo, Mohammad Javad Hajiahmadi, Mohammad Soleimani*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper replaces numerical optimization for photonic hopfions with a design rule: two scalar beams—an optical vortex of order ℓ and a field with n dark rings—whose phases on one plane form two metasurface masks controlling orthogonal polarizations. Three algebraic conditions on the ring coefficients and vortex order guarantee a hopfion of order nℓ. Scalar simulations produce hopfions up to order nine with one terahertz metasurface geometry, and full-wave simulation of the first-order device preserves topology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological textures of light are robust against perturbation and are emerging as information carriers and probes of light-matter interaction. The photonic hopfion, in which loops of different polarization states are linked, is the most intricate such texture, but it has so far been assembled from selected superpositions of laser modes whose weights were set by hand or found by numerical optimization. The paper aims to replace this search with a design rule.

Method: Two scalar beams—an optical vortex of order ℓ and a field with n dark rings—are propagated in closed form. Their phases on one plane are used as the two masks of a metasurface that controls two orthogonal polarizations. The authors prove that the resulting beam is a hopfion of order nℓ whenever three algebraic conditions on the ring coefficients and the vortex order hold, with each condition guarding against a way the topology can escape. The order is chosen on paper and the device follows directly without optimization. Scalar simulation of a terahertz metasurface geometry and full-wave simulation of the complete first-order device are performed.

Result: The paper proves that, when the three algebraic conditions hold, the beam is a hopfion of order nℓ and the polarization loops of every state that the far field does not reach link exactly nℓ times. In scalar simulation, one terahertz metasurface geometry produces hopfions up to order nine. A full-wave simulation of the complete first-order device preserves its topology.

Conclusion: The proposed design rule allows the hopfion order to be chosen on paper, after which the device follows directly without optimization. The rule applies to any platform that shapes the phases of two orthogonal polarizations. Details such as experimental fabrication and measurement are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Topological textures of light are robust against perturbation and are emerging as carriers of information and probes of light-matter interaction. The most intricate of them, the photonic hopfion, in which the loops of different polarization states are linked, has so far been assembled from chosen superpositions of laser modes, with weights set by hand or found by numerical optimization. Here we replace this search with a design rule. Two scalar beams, an optical vortex of order $\ell$ and a field with n dark rings, propagate in closed form, and their phases on one plane are the two masks of a metasurface that controls two orthogonal polarizations. Although a beam, unlike the closed spaces of topology, extends to infinity, we prove that it is a hopfion of order $n\ell$ whenever three algebraic conditions on the ring coefficients and the vortex order hold, and that the polarization loops of every state that the far field does not reach then link exactly $n\ell$ times; each condition guards against one way in which the topology can escape. The order is thus chosen on paper and the device follows directly, without optimization. In scalar simulation, one terahertz metasurface geometry produces hopfions up to order nine, and a full-wave simulation of the complete first-order device preserves its topology. The rule applies to any platform that shapes the phases of two orthogonal polarizations.

</details>


### [67] [Mueller Matrix Decomposition for a General Rotating Compensator Ellipsometer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05545)
*Aaron Chew, Purnima P. Balakrishnan, Bryan M. Barnes, John H. Burnett, Colton K. Dudley, Steven E. Grantham, Stephanie L. Moffitt, Charles Tarrio, Thomas A. Germer*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Mueller matrix decomposition M = T1 D T2 for nondepolarizing matrices, generalizes it to depolarizing matrices, and uses it to calibrate a prototype reflective rotating-compensator ellipsometer for EUV operation, with testing on Au mirror reflection data at 266 nm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to calibrate a prototype reflective rotating-compensator ellipsometer intended for extreme ultraviolet radiation and to enable full Mueller matrix measurement of a sample without full knowledge of the compensators or source polarization while permitting significant diattenuation and retardance in the optics.

Method: A Mueller matrix decomposition is developed that factors a nondepolarizing Mueller matrix M into M = T1 D T2, where D corresponds to a diagonal Jones matrix and is separated from rotations and circular diattenuation represented by T1 and T2. The decomposition is used for calibration, and a generalization is developed for depolarizing Mueller matrices. The technique is tested on reflection data collected on an Au mirror at 266 nm.

Result: The decomposition enables measurement of the full Mueller matrix of a sample without full knowledge of the compensators or the source polarization while allowing significant diattenuation and retardance for the optics. A depolarizing generalization is provided, and the technique is tested on Au mirror reflection data at 266 nm; quantitative performance details are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed Mueller matrix decomposition supports calibration of a prototype reflective rotating-compensator ellipsometer for EUV operation and extends to depolarizing Mueller matrices, with experimental testing on an Au mirror at 266 nm.

Abstract: This work develops a Mueller matrix decomposition that factors a nondepolarizing Mueller matrix $M$ into three components $M = T_1 D T_2$, where the elements $D$, which correspond to a diagonal Jones matrix, are separated from rotations and circular diattenuation $T_1, T_2$. This decomposition is employed to calibrate a prototype reflective rotating-compensator ellipsometer designed to operate using extreme ultraviolet (EUV) radiation. This decomposition allows for the measurement of the full Mueller matrix of a sample without full knowledge of the compensators or the source polarization, while allowing for significant diattenuation and retardance for the optics. A generalization of the decomposition is developed for depolarizing Mueller matrices, and the technique is tested on reflection data collected on an Au mirror at $266$ nm.

</details>


### [68] [Neuromorphic Camera for Multi-Target Pointing Acquisition and Tracking in Free Space Optical Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05503)
*Francesco Nardo, Nigitha Selvaraj, Klaus Schertler, Peter Krötz, Ricardo Barrios, Sebastian Houben, Sebastian Randel*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper investigates an event-based neuromorphic vision sensor for Pointing, Acquisition, and Tracking in Free-Space Optical Communication and comparatively evaluates event-based algorithms for simultaneous acquisition and tracking of multiple optical beacons under realistic dynamic HAP/UAV conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PAT in FSOC requires low jitter and a large field of view to support beacon acquisition and multi-target tracking. Conventional frame-based sensors offer angular coverage but are limited by latency, frame rate, and motion blur.

Method: The system uses a neuromorphic event-based sensor that asynchronously records brightness changes with microsecond-level temporal resolution. The main contribution is a comparative analysis of event-based tracking algorithms that operate directly on the asynchronous event stream for simultaneous acquisition and tracking of multiple optical beacons. The strategies are validated on an experimental dataset representing realistic dynamic High-Altitude Platform and Unmanned Aerial Vehicle network conditions, with comparisons of tracking accuracy and processing latency under various signal-to-background conditions.

Result: The paper demonstrates the application of an event-based vision sensor to PAT tasks in FSOC and validates the compared tracking strategies on an experimental dataset. It reports comparisons of tracking accuracy and processing latency across signal-to-background conditions; specific quantitative results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Event-based vision is explored as an approach for PAT in FSOC, particularly for simultaneous acquisition and tracking of multiple optical beacons using asynchronous event data. The abstract does not provide detailed conclusions or quantitative performance findings.

Abstract: Pointing, Acquisition, and Tracking (PAT) in Free-Space Optical Communication (FSOC) applications must achieve both low jitter and a large Field of View (FoV) to support beacon acquisition and multi-target tracking. While conventional frame-based sensors provide the necessary angular coverage, their performance is limited by latency, available frame-rate, and motion blur. This work explores and demonstrates the application of an event-based vision sensor for PAT tasks in FSOC. The system employs a neuromorphic sensor that asynchronously records brightness changes (events) with microsecond-level temporal resolution. The main contribution of this paper is a comparative analysis of event-based tracking algorithms for the simultaneous acquisition and tracking of multiple optical beacons that operate directly on this asynchronous stream. The performance of these strategies is validated on an experimental dataset representing realistic operational conditions for dynamic High-Altitude Platform (HAP) and Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) networks. The analysis compares key metrics, such as tracking accuracy and processing latency, under various signal-to-background conditions.

</details>


### [69] [Perturbation-Based Reconstruction of Depth-Resolved OCT Attenuation without Requiring a Fully Decayed Signal Tail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05983)
*Jinglun Yu, Yaning Wang, Shuwen Wei, Jin U. Kang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A perturbation-based Beer-Lambert reconstruction combining a zeroth-order exponential estimate with a first-order correction reduces dependence on the unreliable distal OCT signal tail and improves depth-resolved attenuation estimation under finite-depth and heterogeneous conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional depth-resolved OCT attenuation reconstruction can be biased when the signal does not fully decay within the imaging range or when the distal signal tail is dominated by noise or residual reflections from distal sample or support interfaces.

Method: The authors present a perturbation-based Beer-Lambert framework that combines a zeroth-order exponential estimate with a first-order correction. It was evaluated using numerical A-line simulations, Monte Carlo modeling, multilayer silicone-dye-TiO2 phantoms, and ex vivo bovine retina experiments.

Result: Numerical A-line tests showed reduced depth-dependent error against prescribed attenuation profiles, Monte Carlo comparisons showed less distal drift relative to model-defined benchmarks, and phantom profiles were compared with separately acquired single-layer OCT calibration values. In a Monte Carlo component test withholding vessel-mask and vessel-boundary information from reconstruction and using it only for post hoc evaluation, the first-order correction recovered 61% of the segmentation-informed target vessel contrast and reduced vessel ROI mean absolute error by 65% relative to the fixed zeroth-order baseline.

Conclusion: Perturbation-based reconstruction may provide a practical approach for depth-resolved reconstruction of effective OCT attenuation when the fully decayed A-line condition is not satisfied.

Abstract: Depth-resolved attenuation in optical coherence tomography (OCT) can provide quantitative optical contrast beyond structural imaging. However, conventional reconstruction methods can be biased when the OCT signal does not fully decay within the imaging range or when the distal signal tail is dominated by noise or residual reflections from distal sample or support interfaces. Here, we present a perturbation-based Beer-Lambert framework that combines a zeroth-order exponential estimate with a first-order correction, reducing reliance on the unreliable distal signal tail below the current depth. Numerical A-line simulations, Monte Carlo modeling, multilayer silicone-dye-TiO2 phantoms, and ex vivo bovine retina experiments were used to evaluate the method under finite-depth and heterogeneous imaging conditions. The numerical A-line tests showed reduced depth-dependent error against prescribed attenuation profiles; the Monte Carlo comparisons showed less distal drift relative to model-defined benchmarks, while the phantom profiles were compared with separately acquired single-layer OCT calibration values. In a Monte Carlo component test that withheld vessel-mask and vessel-boundary information from reconstruction and used that information only for post hoc evaluation, the first-order correction recovered 61% of the segmentation-informed target vessel contrast and reduced vessel ROI mean absolute error by 65% relative to the fixed zeroth-order baseline. These results suggest that perturbation-based reconstruction may provide a practical approach for depth-resolved reconstruction of effective OCT attenuation when the fully decayed A-line condition is not satisfied.

</details>


### [70] [Generalized Lorentz Framework for Active Semiconductor Quantum Surface Plasmonics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04933)
*Wancong Li, Qiang Zhou, Runzhi Zou, Zi He, Pu Zhang, Hongwei Han, Shuangchun Wen, Xue-Wen Chen*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a generalized Lorentz framework and quasinormal-mode analysis for quantum surface plasmonics in deeply nanoscale nanostructured semiconductors, enabling efficient modeling of nonclassical carrier dynamics under electrical tuning and doping, and demonstrates electrically modulated plasmonic responses in a semiconductor nanoantenna.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nanostructured semiconductors are an actively tunable platform for light-matter interactions, but at feature sizes near ~10 nm, nonclassical carrier dynamics and surface plasmonic responses become prominent, creating substantial challenges for rigorous and efficient theoretical treatment.

Method: The authors develop a generalized Lorentz framework for quantum surface plasmonics in nanostructured semiconductors with arbitrary energy-momentum dispersion relations. It treats carrier nonlocality, carrier surface accumulation and depletion, and nonlocal damping in three-dimensional semiconductor nanostructures under electrical tuning and across doping densities. They also establish a rigorous quasinormal-mode analysis for multiscale semiconductor structures.

Result: The framework enables efficient quantitative treatment of nonclassical carrier effects and transparent physical insight into complex surface plasmonic responses. The authors demonstrate electrically controlled deep-nanoscale carrier accumulation and depletion in a semiconductor nanoantenna, producing pronounced plasmonic-response modulation interpreted through eigenmode shifts, creation, and annihilation. No numerical performance metrics are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: By quantifying and linking complex nonclassical carrier dynamics with plasmonic eigenmodes, the work establishes a comprehensive quantitative framework for active semiconductor quantum surface plasmonics and is expected to foster the convergence of electronics and photonics at the deep nanoscale.

Abstract: Nanostructured semiconductors provide an actively tunable platform for light-matter interactions across a broad spectral range.
