As feature sizes shrink to the deep nanoscale (~10 nm scale), nonclassical carrier dynamics and surface plasmonic responses become prominent, creating substantial challenges for a rigorous and efficient theoretical treatment. Here we develop a generalized Lorentz framework for quantum surface plasmonics in nanostructured semiconductors with arbitrary energymomentum dispersion relations. The framework enables efficient quantitative treatment of carrier nonlocality, carrier surface accumulation and depletion, and nonlocal damping in three-dimensional semiconductor nanostructures, under electrical tuning and across a wide range of doping densities. Building on this framework, we further establish a rigorous quasinormal-mode analysis that provides transparent physical insight into complex surface plasmonic responses of multiscale semiconductor structures. We demonstrate electrically controlled deep-nanoscale carrier accumulation and depletion in a semiconductor nanoantenna, leading to pronounced plasmonic-response modulation that can be interpreted in terms of eigenmode shifts, creation, and annihilation. By quantifying and linking complex nonclassical carrier dynamics with plasmonic eigenmodes, our work establishes a comprehensive quantitative framework for active semiconductor quantum surface plasmonics and will foster the convergence of electronics and photonics at the deep nanoscale.

</details>


### [71] [Observation of pseudospin-locked chiral edge transport in gyromagnetic photonic crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04827)
*Chengle Fan, Kaizhou Shi, Xiang Xi, Zhen Gao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper experimentally demonstrates pseudospin-locked chiral edge transport in gyromagnetic photonic crystals by combining pseudospin-selective excitation with nonreciprocal unidirectional propagation, and uses multidomain interfaces to realize photonic pseudospin multiplexing, demultiplexing, and waveguide crossing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photonic pseudospins allow selective excitation and routing of light, but perturbations that mix opposite pseudospins can cause backscattering in helical edge channels; the work aims to combine pseudospin selectivity with nonreciprocal chiral edge transport.

Method: The authors jointly engineer lattice deformation and magnetic bias in gyromagnetic photonic crystals to control dipolar-quadrupolar band inversions and construct interfaces supporting pseudospin-selective chiral channels. They use microwave near-field and transmission measurements and extend the design to multidomain structures that combine, separate, and route two pseudospin channels through intersecting interfaces.

Result: Measurements reveal a single chiral edge branch selectively excited by only one source handedness with pronounced nonreciprocal transport. Multidomain configurations function as a nonreciprocal photonic pseudospin multiplexer and demultiplexer and as a waveguide crossing with average measured crosstalk of -14.1 and -15.8 dB for its two input channels.

Conclusion: The results provide an experimental route to integrating pseudospin-selective channel control with chiral edge transport and establish a foundation for nonreciprocal photonic networks that use pseudospin to organize and route multiple wave channels.

Abstract: Photonic pseudospins enable selective excitation and routing of light, but perturbations that mix opposite pseudospins can induce backscattering in helical edge channels. Here, we experimentally demonstrate pseudospin-locked chiral edge transport in gyromagnetic photonic crystals, combining pseudospin selectivity with nonreciprocal unidirectional propagation. By jointly engineering lattice deformation and magnetic bias, we control dipolar-quadrupolar band inversions and construct interfaces supporting pseudospin-selective chiral channels. Microwave near-field and transmission measurements reveal a single chiral edge branch that is selectively excited by only one source handedness and exhibits pronounced nonreciprocal transport. Extending this mechanism to multidomain structures, we combine two pseudospin channels into a common waveguide, separate them into distinct outputs, and route them through intersecting interfaces. These configurations realize a nonreciprocal photonic pseudospin multiplexer and demultiplexer, together with a waveguide crossing exhibiting average measured crosstalk of -14.1 and -15.8 dB for its two input channels. Our results present an experimental route to integrating pseudospin-selective channel control with chiral edge transport, providing a foundation for nonreciprocal photonic networks that use pseudospin to organize and route multiple wave channels.

</details>


### [72] [LIPS-PC: Learning Illumination Patterns for Single-Pixel Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04780)
*Tomer Porat, Talya Eden, Eliahu Cohen*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: LIPS-PC learns a fixed linear single-pixel measurement operator, initialized as a residual correction to a 2D-DCT, jointly with a compact classifier and without reconstruction. Across MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and industrial X-ray weld radiographs, it improves low-budget classification over the best tested fixed transforms, with 2x-4x measurement savings at comparable accuracy, though gains diminish at higher budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-pixel/ghost imaging requires one costly measurement per illumination pattern, consuming acquisition time and photons and, for ionizing probes, delivered dose. Automated radiographic defect screening often needs only a class label, motivating task-driven pattern learning for classification rather than reconstruction.

Method: The authors introduce Learning Illumination Patterns for Single-Pixel Classification (LIPS-PC). The measurement operator is one fixed linear matrix shared across inputs, parameterized as a residual correction to a 2D discrete cosine transform, D=D_base+Δ. The correction and a compact classifier are trained end-to-end using cross-entropy without a reconstruction objective, yielding a task-driven linear embedding.

Result: LIPS-PC was evaluated on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST under a controlled protocol and on RIAWELC, a real industrial X-ray weld-radiograph dataset. Across budgets M∈{8,16,32,64,128}, corresponding to sampling ratios below 1% to 12.5%, the learned operator outperformed the best tested fixed transform by 9-15 percentage points at M=8 on all three datasets. Accuracy obtained with eight measurements required roughly 16-32 measurements with the best fixed transform, indicating a 2x-4x saving at comparable accuracy. The advantage generally shrank as M increased, with a small reversal on RIAWELC at higher budgets.

Conclusion: Learning what to measure is especially valuable when measurements are scarce. LIPS-PC shows that jointly optimizing a fixed linear measurement matrix and a classifier can provide substantial low-budget gains for single-pixel classification, although the benefit diminishes as the measurement budget grows.

Abstract: In single-pixel (ghost) imaging, known illumination patterns probe an object and a detector with no spatial resolution returns one number per pattern. Each measurement costs acquisition time and photons and, for ionizing probes, contributes to delivered dose. Motivated by automated radiographic defect screening, where a class label suffices, we introduce Learning Illumination Patterns for Single-Pixel Classification (LIPS-PC). The patterns are learned end-to-end while the measurement operator remains one fixed linear matrix, identical for every input. We parameterize it as a residual correction to a two-dimensional discrete cosine transform (2D-DCT), $D=D_{\mathrm{base}}+Δ$, and jointly train the correction and a compact classifier using cross-entropy, without a reconstruction objective. This yields a task-driven linear embedding through optimization. We evaluate LIPS-PC numerically on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST under a controlled protocol, then on RIAWELC, a real industrial X-ray weld-radiograph dataset. Across five budgets, $M\in{8,16,32,64,128}$, corresponding to sampling ratios from below 1% to 12.5%, the learned operator outperforms the best tested fixed transform by 9-15 percentage points at $M=8$ on all three datasets. Its eight-measurement accuracy requires roughly 16-32 measurements with the best fixed transform, indicating a $2\times$ to $4\times$ saving at comparable accuracy. The advantage generally shrinks as $M$ increases, with a small reversal on RIAWELC at higher budgets. These results show that learning what to measure is especially valuable when measurements are scarce.

</details>


### [73] [Impedance-controlled Laser Dynamics in Near-Zero Index Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04578)
*Adrien Debacq, Andrei V. Lavrinenko, Elizaveta Semenova, Olivier Deparis, Michaël Lobet*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This theoretical study shows that lasing in near-zero-index media depends on the specific NZI class and can be controlled through impedance. ENZ media with high impedance lower the lasing threshold via enhanced electric-field interaction, while EMNZ media preserve single-mode emission, unlike ENZ and MNZ media, which exhibit multimode emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-zero-index media have been studied for controlling spontaneous emission, but their effects on stimulated emission and lasing across epsilon-near-zero, mu-near-zero, and epsilon-and-mu-near-zero classes remained unexplored.

Method: The authors theoretically derive Maxwell-Bloch equations that include dispersive electric and magnetic properties, analyze laser rate equations, and perform numerical simulations to compare lasing behavior across NZI classes.

Result: The ENZ class, with high impedance, can reduce the lasing threshold through enhanced electric-field interaction. The NZI class also determines the spectral response: ENZ and MNZ classes suffer from multimode emission, whereas the EMNZ class preserves single-mode emission. No quantitative threshold or spectral values are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Lasing emission depends on the class of NZI medium and can be controlled through its impedance. Selecting the NZI class is therefore important for both lowering the lasing threshold and controlling single-mode versus multimode emission.

Abstract: Near-zero-index (NZI) media have been explored for controlling spontaneous emission, but their impact on stimulated emission and lasing properties remains unexplored within their different classes, i.e., epsilon-near-zero (ENZ), mu-near-zero (MNZ), or epsilon-and-mu-near-zero (EMNZ). Here, we show theoretically that lasing emission depends on the class of NZI medium and can be controlled through its impedance. The major difference, as compared to conventional laser media, arises from the incorporation of dispersive electric and magnetic properties in the derivation of the Maxwell Boch equations. In this framework, the analysis of laser rate equation reveals that the ENZ class, with high impedance, can reduce the lasing threshold through enhanced electric-field interaction. Numerical simulations further show that the NZI class also governs the spectral response: whereas ENZ and MNZ classes suffer from multimode emission, the EMNZ class preserves single-mode emission.

</details>


### [74] [Reference-Beam-Assisted Perturbation-Resistant Imaging Through A Single Multimode Fiber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04427)
*Bowen Sun, Maohan Li, Jingwei Luo, Zhuoren Wan, Zhaoyang Wen, Yuan Chen, Wending Huang, Zijian Wang, Ming Yan, Heping Zeng*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A reference-beam-assisted neural-network method reconstructs target images through a multimode fiber across multiple mechanical configurations by jointly using signal and target-independent reference speckles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimode fiber imaging is highly sensitive to mechanical perturbations that alter modal coupling and disrupt the speckle-to-target mapping, creating a need for reconstruction across multiple fiber configurations without a separate configuration-sensing structure.

Method: An independent reference beam propagates through the same multimode fiber as the imaging signal but without target modulation, so its speckle patterns provide target-independent transmission-state information. A neural network jointly processes the signal and reference speckles to reconstruct target images. Experiments used MNIST and Fashion-MNIST across 10 fiber configurations, with further evaluation across 40 configurations.

Result: Experiments showed improved reconstruction quality, faster training convergence, and more accurate configuration identification. Evaluation across 40 configurations confirmed the reconstruction benefit of reference-beam assistance over a larger set of perturbed fiber states.

Conclusion: The results provide a foundation for perturbation-adaptive multimode fiber imaging systems for flexible endoscopy and minimally invasive optical diagnostics.

Abstract: Multimode fiber (MMF) imaging is highly sensitive to mechanical perturbations, which alter modal coupling and disrupt the speckle-to-target mapping. Here, we propose a reference-beam-assisted method for image reconstruction across multiple fiber configurations. An independent reference beam propagates through the same MMF as the imaging signal without target modulation, providing target-independent transmission-state information through its speckle patterns without requiring a separate configuration-sensing structure. A neural network jointly processes the signal and reference speckles to reconstruct target images. Experiments with MNIST and Fashion-MNIST across 10 fiber configurations demonstrate improved reconstruction quality, faster training convergence, and more accurate configuration identification. Further evaluation across 40 configurations confirms the reconstruction benefit of reference-beam assistance over a larger set of perturbed fiber states. These results provide a foundation for developing perturbation-adaptive MMF imaging systems for flexible endoscopy and minimally invasive optical diagnostics.

</details>


### [75] [Asymptotic electromagnetic transition conditions for thin layers: the arithmetic-mean field and susceptibility hybridization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04324)
*Asbjorn Hansen*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper derives zero-thickness sheet transition conditions for thin in-plane homogeneous, isotropic layers with general nonlocal susceptibility, showing when the usual arithmetic-mean field and zeroth-moment characterization are justified and how profile asymmetry can produce an effective tensor surface susceptibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective transition conditions replacing a thin polarizable layer by a zero-thickness sheet are widely used for two-dimensional materials and metasurfaces, but the usual arithmetic mean of fields and characterization by the zeroth moment of susceptibility are typically imposed rather than derived.

Method: A single-scale asymptotic expansion is used in η = l max(k0, q), where l is layer thickness, k0 is free-space wavenumber, and q is in-plane wavenumber, for a finite-thickness, in-plane homogeneous and isotropic layer with general nonlocal susceptibility. The out-of-plane depolarizing field is resummed before taking the sheet limit, and the derived conditions are compared with exact solutions for local and separable layers.

Result: The arithmetic mean emerges as the leading-order polarizing field for general susceptibility profiles. Asymmetries in the layer profile modify the effective susceptibility rather than the arithmetic-mean field prescription. If the spatial profile is not even in both out-of-plane coordinates, in-plane and out-of-plane responses hybridize, turning the diagonal volumetric susceptibility into a q-dependent effective tensor surface susceptibility with off-diagonal components; mirror symmetry alone does not remove this hybridization. The derived conditions remain accurate to O(η) for propagating and evanescent excitations, whereas standard sheet conditions fail once hybridization becomes appreciable.

Conclusion: Resumming the out-of-plane depolarizing field before the sheet limit yields a sheet susceptibility independent of material thickness and clarifies the origin and limitations of standard sheet transition conditions. General nonlocal and asymmetric profiles can require a q-dependent effective tensor surface susceptibility, and the derived conditions are more accurate than standard sheet conditions when hybridization is significant.

Abstract: Effective transition conditions that replace a thin polarizable layer by a zero-thickness sheet are commonly applied in electromagnetic models of two-dimensional (2D) materials and metasurfaces. These conditions usually take the arithmetic mean of the fields on either side as the polarizing field at the boundary, and characterize the layer by the $0^\mathrm{th}$ moment of its susceptibility, but both choices are typically imposed rather than derived. We derive transition conditions for a finite-thickness, in-plane homogeneous, in-plane isotropic layer with a general nonlocal susceptibility, using a single-scale asymptotic expansion in $η= l\max(k_0,q)$, where $l$ is the layer thickness, $k_0$ the free-space wavenumber and $q$ the in-plane wavenumber. The sheet limit must be taken after resumming the out-of-plane depolarizing field, as this ensures that the resulting sheet susceptibility is independent of the material thickness. The arithmetic mean then emerges as the leading-order polarizing field for general susceptibility profiles. Asymmetries in the layer profile modify the effective susceptibility appearing in the transition conditions, rather than altering the arithmetic-mean field prescription. If the spatial profile is not even in both out-of-plane coordinates, in-plane and out-of-plane response hybridize, turning the diagonal volumetric susceptibility into a $q$-dependent effective tensor surface susceptibility with off-diagonal components. Mirror symmetry alone does not remove this hybridization. Comparison with exact solutions for local and separable layers shows that the derived conditions remain accurate to $O(η)$ across propagating and evanescent excitations, whereas the standard sheet conditions fail once hybridization becomes appreciable.

</details>


### [76] [Reconfigurable multi-tone mmWave/THz generation powered by high-efficiency Kerr parametric oscillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03997)
*Dong IL Lee, Fanqi Shen, Wenzheng Liu, Xinghe Jiang, Anika Tabassum, Alwaleed Aldhafeeri, Manas Choure, Jasmine Lu, Mona Jarrahi, Chee Wei Wong*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The abstract reports reconfigurable photonic generation of multi-tone millimeter-wave/terahertz signals using a strongly asymmetric dual-ring silicon-nitride microresonator and a broadband spiral plasmonic photomixer, producing selectable comb spacings and simultaneous tones across 100–900 GHz with high conversion efficiency and characterized frequency noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Broadband, simultaneously accessible multi-tone millimeter-wave and terahertz signals are attractive for multi-band wireless communication and spectroscopy, but their generation is limited by electronic bandwidth constraints and inefficient optical frequency conversion.

Method: The work uses a strongly asymmetric dual-ring silicon-nitride microresonator with a localized avoided-mode crossing introduced by an auxiliary ring, which supports a fixed optical parametric oscillation mode pair over an extended pump-detuning range, consistent with passive compensation of detuning-dependent phase mismatch. Direct photomixing of the optical states with a broadband spiral plasmonic photomixer generates multi-frequency tones without intermediate optical amplification; optical modulation is also used.

Result: The system achieves comb spacings of 100, 200, 300, and 400 GHz and simultaneous multi-frequency tones spanning 100 to 900 GHz. It reaches a maximum pump-to-generated-line conversion efficiency of 67.3% with 25.06 dB of pump depletion across different pump wavelength selections. Characterization by down-converted spectra, frequency-noise power spectral density, and Allan deviation gives a beta-separation linewidth of 26 kHz at an observation time of 0.1 s. Optical modulation provides radiated power exceeding 2 mW at 100 GHz at a 1% duty cycle while mitigating thermal breakdown in the photomixer.

Conclusion: High optical conversion efficiency, reconfigurable and selectable comb spacings, and broadband multi-tone generation on a chip-scale platform provide a compact route toward reconfigurable multi-band millimeter-wave/terahertz sources for wireless communication, spectroscopy, and sensing.

Abstract: Broadband and simultaneously accessible multi-tone millimeter-wave (mmWave) and terahertz (THz) signals are attractive for multi-band wireless communication and spectroscopy applications, yet their generation remains constrained by electronic bandwidth limitations and inefficient optical frequency conversion. Here, we demonstrate reconfigurable photonic generation of multi-tone mmWave/THz signals with comb spacings of 100, 200, 300, and 400 GHz, using a strongly asymmetric dual-ring silicon-nitride (SiN) microresonator and a broadband spiral plasmonic photomixer. A localized avoided-mode crossing (AMX) introduced by an auxiliary ring supports a fixed optical parametric oscillation (OPO) mode pair over an extended pump-detuning range, consistent with passive compensation of detuning-dependent phase mismatch. This enables a maximum pump-to-generated-line conversion efficiency of 67.3 % with 25.06 dB of pump depletion across different pump wavelength selections. Direct photomixing of these optical states produces simultaneous multi-frequency tones spanning 100 to 900 GHz without intermediate optical amplification. The generated carriers are further characterized through down-converted spectra, frequency-noise power spectral density (PSD), and Allan deviation measurements, yielding a \b{eta}-separation linewidth of 26 kHz at an observation time of 0.1 s. Optical modulation offers high radiated power levels exceeding 2 mW at 100 GHz at a duty cycle of 1 %, while mitigating thermal breakdown in the photomixer. High optical conversion efficiency, reconfigurable and selectable comb spacings, and broadband multi-tone generation on a chip-scale platform provide a compact route toward reconfigurable multi-band mmWave/THz sources for wireless communication, spectroscopy, and sensing.

</details>


### [77] [Contrast-Enhanced Nonlinear Imaging with Moiré Flatband Superlattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03951)
*Amin Zamani, Lihang Feng, Jianji Liu, Ze Zheng, Gabriel Sanderson, Mohsen Rahmani, Peilong Hong, Lei Xu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A one-dimensional photonic moiré superlattice with multiple near-infrared quasi-flat bands enables efficient third-harmonic generation over a broad angular range and is used for high-contrast infrared-to-visible nonlinear imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear metasurfaces provide a compact platform for infrared-to-visible conversion and imaging, but resonant enhancement often restricts angular acceptance and limits the transverse spatial frequencies that can efficiently interact with nonlinear modes. The paper aims to overcome this trade-off.

Method: The authors use a one-dimensional photonic moiré superlattice supporting multiple quasi-flat bands in the near-infrared. These moiré modes combine strong near-field enhancement with broad momentum-space accessibility. They experimentally study third-harmonic generation under broadband excitation from 1180 to 2000 nm and use displacement-dependent THG signals for infrared imaging, encoding image information in the nonlinear response and decoding its spatial variations.

Result: Strong THG emission is experimentally observed near 467 nm under broadband excitation from 1180 to 2000 nm, with stable excitation over a large angular range. In infrared imaging, the nonlinear moiré superlattice resolves weak features and internal textures with improved contrast compared with THG imaging using nonlinear films or conventional nonlinear metasurfaces.

Conclusion: Photonic moiré superlattices are established as a versatile platform combining efficient frequency conversion, broad angular acceptance, and mode-sensitive spatial encoding for high-contrast, label-free nonlinear imaging with enhanced feature discrimination.

Abstract: Nonlinear metasurfaces offer a compact platform for infrared-to-visible conversion and imaging, yet resonant enhancement is often accompanied by restricted angular acceptance, limiting the range of transverse spatial frequencies that can interact efficiently with the nonlinear modes. Here, we address this challenge using a one-dimensional photonic moiré superlattice supporting multiple quasi-flat bands in the near-infrared. These moiré modes combine strong near-field enhancement with broad momentum-space accessibility, enabling efficient third-harmonic generation (THG) while maintaining stable excitation over a large angular range. Experimentally, we observe strong THG emission near $467~\mathrm{nm}$ under broadband excitation from $ 1180$ to $ 2000~\mathrm {nm} $. We further employ the nonlinear moiré superlattice for infrared imaging by converting an infrared target into visible through the THG process. By encoding image information into displacement-dependent THG signals and decoding their spatial variations, we experimentally resolve weak features and internal textures with improved contrast compared with THG imaging using nonlinear films or conventional nonlinear metasurfaces. These results establish photonic moiré superlattices as a versatile platform combining efficient frequency conversion, broad angular acceptance, and mode-sensitive spatial encoding for high-contrast, label-free nonlinear imaging with enhanced feature discrimination.

</details>


### [78] [Ray propagation of circularly polarized light in an inhomogeneous medium: the optical Magnus effect from Maxwell's equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03914)
*Igor Kuzmenko, Y. B. Band*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper derives ray propagation for circularly polarized light in an inhomogeneous, isotropic, lossless medium, including the first geometrical-optics correction—the optical Magnus (spin Hall) effect—using a first-order eikonal expansion of Maxwell's equations and Berry-phase methods. It obtains a Berry-corrected ray action and Hamiltonian, anomalous-velocity ray equations, equivalent non-canonical and time-parametrized formulations, and validates the results against analytic and numerical checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not state an external motivation beyond the need to derive the ray propagation of circularly polarized light in an inhomogeneous, isotropic, lossless medium including the first correction to geometrical optics, the optical Magnus (spin Hall) effect, and to set up the formulation for numerical propagation of rays in a medium with spatially varying refractive index.

Method: Starting from Maxwell's equations and an eikonal expansion to first order in 1/k0, the authors show that polarization is parallel transported along the ray, identify the Berry connection and curvature of helicity states, and derive a ray action in which the Berry phase corrects the optical path length. This yields a Hamiltonian H_sigma(r,p)=|p|-n(r+A_sigma(p)/k0) approximately |p|-n(r)-k0^-1 grad n · A_sigma(p). In terms of physical position, the equations of motion contain the Berry curvature as an anomalous velocity. The paper also gives an equivalent non-canonical bracket form, the gauge transformation, and an equivalent time-parametrized formulation with H=hbar omega=hbar c|k|/n, and states the approximations involved.

Result: The derivation produces Berry-corrected ray equations and Hamiltonian formulations for circularly polarized light in an inhomogeneous, isotropic, lossless medium. The resulting ray equations are compared with analytic and numerical results. Checks include a planar interface, the helical ray in a cylindrical medium, and exact conservation of angular momentum. The formulation is set up for numerical propagation of rays in a medium with a spatially varying refractive index. The abstract does not provide quantitative numerical results or specific error magnitudes.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the first-order geometrical-optics correction to ray propagation can be formulated through the Berry connection and curvature of helicity states, yielding a ray action with Berry phase, a shifted-index Hamiltonian, anomalous-velocity equations of motion, and equivalent bracket, gauge, and time-parametrized descriptions. The formulation is intended for numerical ray propagation in spatially varying refractive-index media and is supported by the stated analytic and numerical checks, within the approximations discussed in the paper.

Abstract: We derive the ray propagation of circularly polarized light in an inhomogeneous, isotropic and lossless medium, including the first correction to geometrical optics, the optical Magnus (spin Hall) effect. Starting from Maxwell's equations and the eikonal expansion to first order in $1/k_0$, we show that the polarization is parallel transported along the ray, identify the Berry connection and curvature of the helicity states, and obtain a ray action in which the Berry phase appears as a correction to the optical path length. This action leads to a Hamiltonian in which the refractive index is evaluated at a position shifted by the Berry connection, $\mathcal H_σ(\mathbf r,\mathbf p)=|\mathbf p|-n(\mathbf r+\mathbf A_σ(\mathbf p)/k_0) \simeq|\mathbf p|-n(\mathbf r)-k_0^{-1}\boldsymbol\nabla n\cdot\mathbf A_σ(\mathbf p)$. In terms of the physical position, the equations of motion contain the Berry curvature as an anomalous velocity. We give the equivalent non-canonical bracket form, the gauge transformation, and the equivalent time-parametrized formulation with the Hamiltonian $\hbarω=\hbar c|\mathbf k|/n$, and we state the approximations involved. The resulting ray equations are compared with analytic and numerical results. A planar interface, the helical ray in a cylindrical medium and the exact conservation of angular momentum provide checks. The formulation is set up for numerical propagation of rays in a medium with a spatially varying refractive index.

</details>


### [79] [Reading Why: Born-Measured Attention with Exact Word Evidence on Light and Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05355)
*Teerawat Tongloy, Jiraporn Pongsopa, Rangsan Jomtarak*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper builds a transformer whose steps are Born measurements, giving exactly computable integrated-gradient evidence; on e-SNLI it matches a same-size softmax transformer in accuracy, gives more faithful deletion-based explanations than softmax or attention x gradient, and compiles exactly to optoelectronic and qubit hardware, with an IBM device reproducing its answers and evidence correlations in tested cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When a language model answers, we can usually only estimate why. The authors seek a transformer whose internal steps are Born measurements so that explanations/evidence are exactly computable and the same weights can be implemented on quantum/optoelectronic hardware.

Method: The model uses attention |<q|k>|^4 normalised over each row, a measured product in place of the feed-forward nonlinearity, and a Born readout. With denominators held at their input values, each class probability is a polynomial in word amplitudes, so integrated-gradient evidence is computed by finite quadrature with no quadrature error. The authors evaluate on e-SNLI natural language inference with about 98k parameters outside the embedding, compare with a same-size softmax transformer, and use single-word deletion tests against softmax evidence and attention x gradient. They also compile the weights to optoelectronic optics and qubits; a coherent attention layer becomes one circuit with 852 CNOTs on 23 qubits. On IBM ibm_kingston, they read attention overlaps by Hadamard tests and use calibration circuits as deep as the overlaps.

Result: Word contributions sum to the class probability above its no-information share to 10^-11 or better. Test accuracy is 76.98 ± 0.17%, versus 76.62 ± 0.36% for the same-size softmax transformer. Deleting the top-evidence word changes the answer in 45% of test pairs, out of the 53% any single deletion can change, versus 32% for the softmax model explained the same way and 40% for its attention x gradient. Attention can resemble annotator-highlighted words as much as the evidence does, but deleting the word attention ranks first changes the Born Transformer's answer in only 32% of pairs. On ibm_kingston, reading only attention overlaps by Hadamard tests, the device gave the model's answers, including when a word was deleted. With calibration circuits as deep as the overlaps, each word's evidence using the device's attention row sums followed the exact evidence, with correlations 0.997 and 0.932 on two pairs chosen in advance; the abstract notes an exception where a pair's evidence is a small total of large opposite terms, saying earlier runs indicate it does not follow and explaining why.

Conclusion: The Born-measurement transformer matches softmax accuracy while providing exact finite-quadrature evidence and stronger deletion-based faithfulness than softmax evidence or attention x gradient; attention alone is less faithful to the answer. Its weights compile exactly to optoelectronic optics and qubits, and an IBM device reproduced its answers and, in tested cases, its evidence, with a stated caveat for cancellation-dominated evidence.

Abstract: When a language model answers, we can usually only estimate why. We build a transformer in which every step is a Born measurement - attention $|\langle q|k\rangle|^4$ normalised over each row, a measured product in place of the feed-forward nonlinearity, and a Born readout. With its denominators held at their values for the input, each class probability is a polynomial in the word amplitudes, so its integrated-gradient evidence is computed by a finite quadrature with no quadrature error: the words' contributions sum to the class probability above its no-information share, to $10^{-11}$ or better. On e-SNLI natural language inference, with about 98k parameters outside the embedding, the model reaches $76.98 \pm 0.17$% test accuracy, level with a softmax transformer of the same size ($76.62 \pm 0.36$%). Deleting the word its evidence ranks first changes the answer in 45% of test pairs, out of the 53% any single deletion can change, against 32% for the softmax model explained the same way and 40% by its attention x gradient. Attention can look as much like the words annotators highlighted as the evidence does, yet accounts for the answer less well: deleting the word it ranks first changes the Born Transformer's answer in 32% of pairs. The same weights compile exactly to optoelectronic optics and to qubits, and a coherent attention layer becomes one circuit equal to the model (852 CNOTs on 23 qubits). On IBM's ibm_kingston, reading only the attention overlaps by Hadamard tests, the device gave the model's answers, also when a word was deleted. With calibration circuits as deep as the overlaps, each word's evidence with the device's attention row sums followed the exact evidence (correlations 0.997 and 0.932 on two pairs chosen in advance); where a pair's evidence is a small total of large opposite terms the earlier runs say it does not, and we say why.

</details>


### [80] [Effect of high odrer dispersion on soliton interaction in coupled Kerr cavities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05305)
*Andrei G. Vladimirov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Third-order dispersion qualitatively changes the interaction of dissipative Kerr solitons in two coupled driven microresonators, producing asymmetric forces and multiple soliton clusters whose stability depends on driving-phase and group-velocity mismatch; analytical predictions are confirmed numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates how dissipative Kerr solitons interact in two nearly identical, coherently driven, coupled microresonators when third-order dispersion is present.

Method: The authors asymptotically reduce the coupled Lugiato--Lefever equations for weak intercavity coupling and derive effective equations governing soliton separation and collective drift; they also perform numerical simulations of the full coupled system.

Result: Third-order dispersion breaks the parity symmetry of the soliton profile and qualitatively modifies the effective interaction, causing asymmetric interaction forces and multiple stable and unstable soliton clusters. Cluster existence and stability are controlled by the relative phase of the driving fields and the group-velocity mismatch between resonators; an appropriate mismatch can yield multiple stable bound states, while deviations trigger successive saddle-node bifurcations and ultimately periodic soliton collisions. Numerical simulations confirm the analytical predictions.

Conclusion: Third-order dispersion qualitatively alters coupled dissipative Kerr soliton interactions, and the resulting clusters can be controlled through the driving-field relative phase and group-velocity mismatch; the analytical predictions are validated by full numerical simulations.

Abstract: We investigate the interaction of dissipative Kerr solitons in two nearly identical coherently driven, coupled microresonators in the presence of third-order dispersion (TOD). Using an asymptotic reduction of the coupled Lugiato--Lefever equations, we derive effective interaction equations governing the soliton separation and collective drift for weak intercavity coupling. TOD breaks the parity symmetry of the soliton profile and qualitatively modifies the effective interaction, leading to asymmetric interaction forces and multiple stable and unstable soliton clusters. The existence and stability of these clusters are controlled by the relative phase of the driving fields and the group-velocity mismatch between the resonators. In particular, an appropriate value of the group-velocity mismatch can give rise to multiple stable bound states, whereas deviations from this value trigger successive saddle-node bifurcations, ultimately leading to periodic soliton collisions. The analytical predictions are confirmed by numerical simulations of the full coupled system.

</details>


### [81] [Synchronization in one-dimensional arrays of photon condensates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04989)
*Klaas Luyckx, Michiel Yzewyn, Michiel Wouters*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper studies synchronization of coupled photonic Bose-Einstein condensates in dye-filled microcavity arrays under a potential gradient. It identifies the desynchronization threshold as a dynamical instability, derives parameter dependence for two sites with a key role for the thermalization rate, finds an N^{-3} scaling of the critical potential difference for N sites, and shows that quantum fluctuations broaden the transition via stochastic phase slips while phase velocity and first-order spatial coherence still identify the instability threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand synchronization and desynchronization in coupled photonic Bose-Einstein condensates in dye-filled microcavity arrays when a potential gradient is applied, including the role of noise and quantum fluctuations.

Method: The authors investigate coupled photonic Bose-Einstein condensates in dye-filled microcavity arrays subject to a potential gradient. In the noiseless case, they analyze the desynchronization threshold as a dynamical instability. For a two-site system, they analytically derive the maximal potential difference permitting a synchronized solution as a function of system parameters. For N-site systems, they determine the scaling of the critical potential difference. They also include quantum fluctuations and consider stochastic phase slips.

Result: In the absence of noise, the desynchronization threshold arises from a dynamical instability. For two sites, the maximal potential difference for a synchronized solution depends crucially on the thermalization rate. For N sites, the critical potential difference follows an N^{-3} power law. With quantum fluctuations, stochastic phase slips broaden the sharp transitions, but phase velocity and first-order spatial coherence still reliably identify the instability threshold.

Conclusion: The synchronization-desynchronization transition in coupled photonic Bose-Einstein condensates under a potential gradient is governed by a dynamical instability whose threshold depends strongly on the thermalization rate and scales as N^{-3} with system size. Under quantum fluctuations, the transition is broadened by stochastic phase slips, yet phase velocity and first-order spatial coherence remain reliable indicators of the instability threshold.

Abstract: We investigate the synchronization of coupled photonic Bose-Einstein condensates in dye-filled microcavity arrays, subject to a potential gradient. We show that in the absence of noise the desynchronisation threshold is the result of a dynamical instability. For a two-site system, we analytically derive the maximal potential difference that allows for a synchronized solution as a function of the system parameters and find that it crucially depends on the thermalization rate. For systems with $N$ sites, we find a $N^{-3}$ power law scaling for the critical potential difference. When quantum fluctuations are included, stochastic phase slips broaden the sharp transitions, yet the phase velocity and first order spatial coherence still reliably identify the instability threshold.

</details>


### [82] [Temporal stretching of single-photon pulses by intra-cavity frequency conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04771)
*Mathias L. H. Korsgaard, Eva M. González-Ruiz, Anders S. Sørensen*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes a free-space cavity with an embedded nonlinear crystal that uses frequency conversion and slow cavity leakage to simultaneously address spectral-bandwidth and frequency-mismatch problems by stretching photon pulses to different wavelengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral bandwidth and frequency mismatch between quantum light emitters and quantum components is a common problem in realizing hybrid quantum information networks, and the paper aims to solve both issues simultaneously.

Method: The proposed setup uses a free-space cavity with an embedded nonlinear crystal; frequency conversion facilitates coupling into a cavity mode, and slow leakage from the cavity stretches the photon pulse, producing a much longer pulse at a different wavelength. The stretching is strictly limited by the cavity finesse and length, and realistic internal cavity losses are included in the analysis.

Result: The paper shows that, including realistic internal losses, a photon can be stretched by at least two orders of magnitude with high efficiency; the exact quantitative bounds beyond this are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed setup can constitute a valuable addition to the toolbox of quantum information technologies.

Abstract: Spectral bandwidth and frequency mismatch between quantum light emitters and quantum components is a common problem in the realization of hybrid quantum information networks. Here we propose a simultaneous solution to both of these problems by using a free-space cavity with an embedded nonlinear crystal that, through frequency conversion, facilitates perfect coupling into a cavity mode. The slow leakage of the cavity effectively stretches the photon pulse, resulting in a much longer photon pulse at a different wavelength. We show that the amount of stretching is strictly limited by the finesse and length of the cavity. Including realistic internal losses in the cavity, we show that it is possible to stretch a photon by at least two orders of magnitude with high efficiency. The proposed setup can thus constitute a valuable addition to the toolbox of quantum information technologies.

</details>


### [83] [Nonlinearity-Induced Suppression of Dissipation in Bath-Coupled Floquet Topological Insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04752)
*Bhoomija Chaurasia, Sebabrata Mukherjee*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Nonlinear interactions provide all-optical control of dissipation in chiral edge states of a laser-written Floquet topological insulator coupled to a photonic bath; moderate nonlinearity increases loss, while an optimal strength suppresses it via traveling edge solitons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chiral topological edge states are disorder-robust but dissipate energy when coupled to an external bath, motivating control over this edge-state dissipation.

Method: The authors study a laser-written Floquet topological insulator periodically coupled to a photonic bath and use nonlinear interactions to control edge-state dissipation.

Result: Moderate nonlinearity enhances dissipation through mode mixing, whereas increasing nonlinear strength to an optimal value strongly suppresses dissipation by forming highly localized traveling edge solitons.

Conclusion: Engineered system-bath coupling combined with nonlinear interactions offers a route to suppress dissipation of chiral topological edge transport.

Abstract: Topological insulators can host chiral, disorder-robust edge states that dissipate energy when coupled to an external bath. We show that nonlinear interactions enable all-optical control over edge-state dissipation in a laser-written Floquet topological insulator periodically coupled to a photonic bath. Although moderate nonlinearity enhances dissipation due to mode mixing, increasing the nonlinear strength to an optimal value dramatically suppresses dissipation by forming highly localized traveling edge solitons. These results establish engineered system-bath coupling and nonlinear interactions as a route to suppress dissipation of chiral topological edge transport.

</details>


### [84] [A New Bound on Light-by-Light Scattering in the Low-Energy Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04531)
*Aimé Matheron, Michal Šmíd, Felix Karbstein, Sebastian Göde, Willi Hippler, Robert Lötzsch, Naser Ahmadiniaz, Michal Andrzejewski, Sergey Antipov, Carsten Baehtz, Christina Bömer, Victorien Bouffetier, Erik Brambrink, Jakub Bulička, Samuele Di Dio Cafiso, Rafael Celestre, Jaromír Chalupský, Thomas E. Cowan, James P. Edwards, Sebastián Franchino-Viñas, Holger Gies, Jörg Grenzer, Travis Griffin, Věra Hájková, Jens Hauser, Thomas Heinzl, Uwe Hernandez Acosta, Peter Hilz, Hauke Höppner, Alžběta Horynová, Lingen Huang, Oliver Humphries, Šimon Jelínek, Libor Juha, Pooyan Khademi, Ben King, Christian Kohlfürst, Dietrich Krebs, Alejandro Laso-García, Alexander J. Macleod, Berit Marx-Glowna, Masruri Masruri, Nikita Medvedev, Anthony Mercuri-Baron, Elena A. Mosman, Motoaki Nakatsutsumi, Gerhard G. Paulus, Jennifer Popp, Thomas R. Preston, Isabella Probst, Sripati V. Rahul, Lisa Randolph, Ralf Röhlsberger, Thomas Roth, Sakshath Sadashivaiah, Felipe C. Salgado, Roland Sauerbrey, Alexander Sävert, Hans-Peter Schlenvoigt, Sebastian M. Schmidt, Ulrich Schramm, Ralf Schützhold, Jan Patrick Schwinkendorf, Frank Seiboth, Daniel Seipt, Georgiy Shoulga, Thomas Stöhlker, Minxue Tang, Abha Thazhathe Peedika, Monika Toncian, Toma Toncian, Maksim Valialshchikov, Vojtěch Vozda, Edgar Weckert, Colin Wessel, Jan Wild, Andreas Wipf, Ulf Zastrau, Matt Zepf*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper reports an X-ray–optical laser collision experiment using dark-field detection and polarimetry, yielding the most stringent low-energy bound on light-by-light scattering and improving previous limits by ten orders of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to test the breakdown of the optical superposition principle: electromagnetic fields can couple weakly through quantum vacuum fluctuations, with consequences such as light-by-light scattering and vacuum birefringence.

Method: The reported approach collides X-ray free-electron laser pulses with high-intensity optical laser pulses and combines dark-field detection with polarimetry for background suppression. Further experimental details are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The approach yields the most stringent bound for light-by-light scattering in the low-energy regime achieved to date, improving upon previous experimental limits by ten orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: The experiment demonstrates a sensitive route toward precision measurements of quantum-vacuum-induced light-by-light interactions and paves the way for future precision studies.

Abstract: The superposition principle at the heart of optics states that electromagnetic fields do not interact in vacuum. This principle is not universal, instead electromagnetic fields couple weakly via quantum vacuum fluctuations. Striking consequences are light-by-light scattering and vacuum birefringence. Experimental results from colliding X-ray free-electron and optical high-intensity lasers combined with dark-field detection and polarimetry for background suppression are reported. This approach yields the most stringent bound for light-by-light scattering in the low-energy regime achieved to date -- improving upon previous experimental limits by ten orders of magnitude -- and paves the way for precision measurements.

</details>


### [85] [Thermally enhanced magnon-phonon resonance in van der Waals antiferromagnet FePS3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04229)
*Kai-Xuan Zhang, Beom Hyun Kim, Seungbok Lee, Hao Li, Eugene Park, Heejun Yang, Feng Jin, Qingming Zhang, Je-Geun Park*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: High-field Raman spectroscopy shows that magnon-phonon coupling in FePS3 remains robust with temperature, while decreasing detuning drives the modes toward resonance near the antiferromagnetic transition, explaining the unusual long-lived terahertz-driven magnetic state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong coupling between collective excitations offers routes to control quantum states, but its evolution under thermal fluctuations is poorly understood. In FePS3, a long-lived terahertz-induced metastable magnetic state occurs near the antiferromagnetic transition rather than at base temperature, challenging expectations that fluctuations near TN should hinder coherent control and requiring independent examination.

Method: The authors use high-field Raman spectroscopy to systematically investigate the temperature evolution of magnon-phonon coupling in the van der Waals antiferromagnet FePS3.

Result: A pronounced avoided crossing between magnon and phonon modes is observed over a wide temperature range, and the coupling strength remains remarkably robust as temperature increases. The zero-field magnon-phonon detuning decreases progressively with warming, driving the system toward resonance and enhancing hybridization near the transition temperature.

Conclusion: This anomalous thermal evolution provides a key to understanding the unusual terahertz-driven states reported in FePS3. More broadly, van der Waals magnets are highlighted as promising platforms for strong coupling among collective excitations and for controlling magnetic states through light-matter interactions.

Abstract: Strong coupling between collective excitations provides a route to controlling quantum states of matter, yet its evolution under thermal fluctuations remains poorly understood. In the van der Waals antiferromagnet FePS3, strong magnon-phonon coupling is established at low temperatures. At the same time, recent terahertz experiments revealed the counterintuitive stabilization of a long-lived metastable magnetic state with an exceedingly long lifetime of 5 msec at the antiferromagnetic transition, not at the base temperature. This was attributed to a strongly coupled spin-lattice scenario, challenging the conventional wisdom that increased magnetic and thermal fluctuations near TN should be detrimental to the coherent control of a metastable phase. Thus it requires an independent examination for correct understanding. Using high-field Raman spectroscopy, we systematically investigate the temperature evolution of magnon-phonon coupling in FePS3. We observe a pronounced avoided crossing between magnon and phonon modes across a wide range of temperatures and find that the coupling strength remains remarkably robust with increasing temperatures. The zero-field magnon-phonon detuning decreases progressively with warming, driving the system toward resonance and thereby enhancing their hybridization near the transition temperature. This anomalous thermal evolution provides the crucial key to understanding the unusual terahertz-driven states recently reported in FePS3. More broadly, our results highlight van der Waals magnets as a promising platform for strong coupling among collective excitations and for controlling magnetic states through light-matter interactions.

</details>


### [86] [Pump quantization changes optimal measurements for quantum sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03943)
*S. A. Wadood, L. Bello, T. Ilias, J. W. Fleischer*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper analyzes phase sensing with a quantized, depleting pump in squeezed-light generation and finds that quantum advantage survives pump quantization, but Heisenberg scaling of N^2 emerges at strong pump depletion and requires a joint nonlinear pump-signal measurement, whereas maximum squeezing yields QFI scaling of N^{3/2}.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard quantum phase sensing analyses treat the pump as an undepleted classical resource and neglect the overhead of generating squeezed light. Realistically, the pump is a quantum object that depletes significantly and entangles with the produced squeezed light, so it was unclear how the crossover from a classical to a quantum pump limits quantum advantage in sensing.

Method: The authors analyze interferometric sensing of an unknown phase using a nonlinear χ^(2) squeezed-light source with a quantized pump, evaluating the quantum Fisher information. They compare operating regimes, including maximum signal squeezing versus strong or maximal pump depletion, and assess standard photon counting versus a joint nonlinear measurement of the entire pump-signal system. The abstract does not provide experimental details or a specific physical platform.

Result: Quantum advantage persists despite pump quantization but depends critically on the nonlinear χ^(2) source dynamics. At maximum squeezing, the QFI scales as N^{3/2}, where N is the pump photon number, surpassing the shot-noise limit but not reaching Heisenberg scaling of N^2. At maximal pump depletion, where signal flux is maximized, the QFI scales as N^2. In this regime, standard photon counting is suboptimal, while a joint nonlinear measurement of the entire pump-signal system is shown to achieve Heisenberg scaling.

Conclusion: Pump quantization changes both the optimal operating point and the measurement strategies required to preserve a realistic quantum advantage in phase estimation and, more broadly, in many-body quantum dynamics.

Abstract: Standard analyses of quantum phase sensing often neglect the overhead of generating squeezed-light by treating the pump as an undepleted classical resource. Realistically, the pump is a quantum object that undergoes significant depletion and entangles with the produced squeezed light, raising the question of how the crossover from a classical to a quantum pump limits the quantum advantage in sensing. Contrary to prior analyses, we show that quantum advantage persists despite pump quantization, but depends critically on the nonlinear $(χ^{(2)})$ source dynamics. In particular, we demonstrate that Heisenberg scaling emerges in the regime of strong pump depletion, rather than maximum signal squeezing. At maximum squeezing, the quantum Fisher information (QFI) for interferometric sensing of an unknown phase scales as $N^{3/2}$, where $N$ is the pump photon number, surpassing the shot-noise limit but establishing that squeezing itself is insufficient to attain Heisenberg scaling of $N^2$. At maximal pump depletion, where the signal flux is maximized, the QFI scales as $N^2$. In this regime, standard photon counting is suboptimal, while a joint nonlinear measurement of the entire pump-signal system is shown to achieve Heisenberg scaling. Our work shows that pump quantization changes both the optimal operating point and the measurement strategies required to preserve a realistic quantum advantage in phase estimation and, more broadly, many-body quantum dynamics.

</details>


### [87] [Coherent 1-μm-Band Co-Transmission with C+L-Band Channels over 50-km Hollow-Core Fiber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03354)
*Siyu Chen, Zichen Qian, Mingming Zhang, Huibin Zhou, Wuyan Wang, Xuchen Hua, Yunlong Li, Boran Duan, Yuan Gao, Peng Li, Lei Zhang, An Pan, Cheng Zeng, Yingbin Xing, Hao Wu, Siqi Yan, Jinsong Xia, Ming Tang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper experimentally demonstrates simultaneous coherent transmission over 1-μm and C+L bands, carrying 54×125-GBaud PDM-16QAM C+L channels and 2×15-GBaud PDM-QPSK 1-μm channels over 50 km.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work highlights the 1-μm band as a promising additional transmission window and aims to avoid gas-absorption constraints in the C/L band of hollow-core fibers (HCFs).

Method: The authors experimentally demonstrate simultaneous coherent transmission across 1-μm and C+L bands over 50 km, using 54×125-GBaud PDM-16QAM C+L channels and 2×15-GBaud PDM-QPSK 1-μm channels.

Result: Simultaneous coherent transmission across 1-μm and C+L bands over 50 km is experimentally demonstrated with the stated C+L and 1-μm channel configurations. The abstract does not provide BER, Q-factor, capacity, or other performance metrics.

Conclusion: The 1-μm band is presented as a promising additional transmission window, and its use can avoid gas-absorption constraints in the C/L band of HCFs.

Abstract: We experimentally demonstrate simultaneous coherent transmission across 1-$μ$m and C+L bands, carrying $54\times125$-GBaud PDM-16QAM C+L channels and $2\times15$-GBaud PDM-QPSK 1-$μ$m channels over 50 km, highlighting 1 $μ$m as a promising additional transmission window while avoiding gas-absorption constraints in C/L-band of HCFs.

</details>


### [88] [Axion polarimetry with parametric readout: photon-number conversion and sensitivity limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03340)
*Antonio Capolupo, Gabriele Pisacane, Raoul Serao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes a bias-assisted active photon-number conversion scheme in a four-port optical ring cavity with parametric readout to linearize the quadratic response of summed output photon number for axion-induced polarization rotation, derives photon moments and extracted-field covariance, and reports a conditional low-mass sensitivity projection plus a modest high-frequency readout advantage for OPO-based total counting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by readout of axion-induced polarization rotation and by the need to convert the otherwise quadratic response of the summed output photon number into a linear signal, with particular interest in low-mass sensitivity; the abstract does not provide a broader motivation beyond this.

Method: A calibrated real polarization rotation is applied before the final optical parametric oscillator (OPO) in a four-port optical ring cavity with parametric readout, converting the quadratic response into a linear signal. The authors derive the resulting photon moments and extracted-field covariance, retaining the four-port optical transfer, finite axion coherence, and linewidth.

Result: The principal phenomenological result is a prospective low-mass sensitivity window at m_a=10^-18–10^-16 eV, extending below published exclusion bounds included in the comparison, notably NuSTAR M82 limits. This is a conditional noise-equivalent projection using the A/σ_A=1 stochastic-power criterion and a parameterized technical-noise budget, not a confidence-level exclusion forecast. Separately, OPO-based total counting reduces the noise-equivalent coupling by approximately 5% relative to matched selective attenuation in a favorable higher-frequency band; 5% internal loss and a 0.10-rad pump-phase offset leaves a 3.5% advantage in the stated example. A coherent input with only the final OPO retains this high-frequency benefit, whereas optimized coherent polarimetry remains more sensitive in the baseline.

Conclusion: The results identify the operating principle of active number conversion and the stability requirements for a future implementation; the reported sensitivity is a conditional projection rather than an exclusion forecast.

Abstract: We propose a bias-assisted active photon-number conversion scheme for axion-induced polarization rotation in a four-port optical ring cavity with parametric readout. A calibrated real polarization rotation applied \emph{before} the final optical parametric oscillator (OPO) converts the otherwise quadratic response of the summed output photon number into a linear signal. We derive the resulting photon moments and the extracted-field covariance, retaining the four-port optical transfer, finite axion coherence and linewidth. Our principal phenomenological result is a prospective low-mass sensitivity window at $m_a=10^{-18}$--$10^{-16}\,\mathrm{eV}$, extending below the published exclusion bounds included in our comparison, notably the NuSTAR M82 limits. This is a conditional noise-equivalent projection, using the adopted $A/σ_A=1$ stochastic-power criterion and a parameterized technical-noise budget, not a confidence-level exclusion forecast. As a separate readout result, OPO-based total counting reduces the noise-equivalent coupling by approximately $5\%$ relative to matched selective attenuation in a favorable higher-frequency band; $5\%$ internal loss together with a $0.10$-rad pump-phase offset leaves a $3.5\%$ advantage in the stated example. A coherent input with only the final OPO retains this high-frequency benefit, whereas optimized coherent polarimetry remains more sensitive in the baseline. Our results identify both the operating principle of active number conversion and the stability requirements for a future implementation.

</details>


### [89] [Inline inter-core phase control in multi-core fiber with a commercial polarization controller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03304)
*A. Contreras, A Muñoz, G. dos Santos, G. Cañas, G. Saavedra, C. Hermann-Avigliano, E. S. Gómez, G. Lima, S. P. Walborn*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A commercial inline polarization controller can serve as an inline inter-core phase shifter for multi-core fiber interferometry, producing a reversible 0.95 ± 0.02 rad inter-core phase from one device, with a 0.38 rad operating-point-independent lower bound and polarization-dependent response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inter-core phase control is required for interferometry in multi-core fiber, but no compact inline element is currently available, and existing approaches require fanout to single-mode fiber or free-space optics.

Method: The authors operate a commercial inline polarization controller as an inline inter-core phase shifter and demonstrate two-core interferometry on a jacketed four-core patchcord through repeated load-unload cycles. They use a diametrically compressed disk model to predict the operating condition and verify polarization behavior by polarimetry on all four cores.

Result: A single device gives a reversible inter-core phase of 0.95 ± 0.02 rad, with an operating-point-independent lower bound of 0.38 rad. With input polarization aligned to a principal stress axis, the device imparts a relative phase between cores without rotating either core’s polarization state, as verified by polarimetry. The response is polarization dependent, falling to less than 0.58 rad for the orthogonal input.

Conclusion: The demonstration provides a practical inline route to inter-core phase control and stabilization in all-MCF interferometers without requiring fanout to single-mode fiber or free-space optics.

Abstract: Inter-core phase control is a prerequisite for interferometry in multi-core fiber (MCF), but no compact inline element for this purpose is presently available, and existing approaches require fanout to single-mode fiber or free-space optics. We show that a commercial inline polarization controller, a low-loss and widely available component, can be operated as an inline inter-core phase shifter. Two-core interferometry on a jacketed four-core patchcord gives a reversible inter-core phase of $0.95 \pm 0.02$~rad from a single device, with an operating-point-independent lower bound of $0.38$~rad, driven through repeated load-unload cycles at a rate set by the actuator. A diametrically compressed disk model is used to predict the operating condition: with the input polarization aligned to a principal stress axis the device imparts a relative phase between cores without rotating the polarization state of either, which we verify by polarimetry on all four cores. The response is polarization dependent, falling to less than $0.58$~rad for the orthogonal input. The result is a practical, inline route to inter-core phase control and stabilization in all-MCF interferometers, requiring neither fanout to single-mode fiber nor free-space optics.

</details>


### [90] [Geometric Phase and Spinor Beam Dynamics of Paraxial Beams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03275)
*Jonathan M. Nichols, Christopher Griffin, Steven N. Rodriguez, Daniel V. Nickel, Frank Bucholtz*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper treats a paraxially propagating electric field as a spinor to incorporate geometric phase into beam propagation models, exploits differences between geometric and dynamical phase accumulation, and experimentally validates a designed beam whose centroid follows a curved path in free space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to provide a natural way to include geometric phase in beam propagation models and to exploit the distinct accumulation of geometric and dynamical phase during propagation to reveal new beam-dynamics effects.

Method: The electric field is modeled as a spinor under paraxial propagation. By using spatial variations in geometric phase and differences from dynamical phase, the authors design a beam whose centroid is predicted to follow a curved trajectory through free space, then test this prediction experimentally.

Result: A beam was designed with a centroid predicted to follow a curved path through free space, and this prediction was validated in experiment.

Conclusion: The model does not contradict existing propagation models; instead, it shows how the extra degree of freedom provided by the spinor angle can be leveraged to make new predictions, including surprising consequences for beam dynamics.

Abstract: We show that treating a paraxially propagating electric field as a spinor provides a natural way to include geometric phase in beam propagation models. Importantly, by exploiting differences in the way geometric phase and dynamical phase accrue during propagation, we show that spatial variations in the former can have surprising and rather remarkable consequences for beam dynamics. As an example, we design a beam with a centroid that is predicted to follow a curved path through free space, a prediction we subsequently validate in experiment. The model presented herein does not contradict existing propagation models, but rather shows how to leverage the extra degree of freedom afforded by the spinor angle to make new predictions.

</details>


### [91] [Writing with Near-Field: towards Atomic Resolution in Material Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03144)
*Haoran Mu, Hsin-Hui Huang, Tomas Katkus, Darius Gailevicius, Saulius Juodkazis*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This review argues that ultrafast direct laser writing can exceed diffraction-limited resolution by making the material itself a transient nanoscale absorber: threshold effects and a transient epsilon-near-zero dielectric-to-metal state confine energy to a dipole-like nano-seed with r^-6 near-field decay, so feature size follows seed size and material response rather than focal wavelength. It surveys mechanisms from 100 nm to single-nanometer scales, polarization-controlled anisotropic deposition, large-area stitch-free patterning, high-aspect-ratio crystal dicing/wafering, and conjectures microwave annealing via laser-inscribed lossy nano-seed antennas. 10-20 nm features are demonstrated; deterministic single-defect and single-bond modification remain goals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the conventional judgment of ultrafast direct laser writing resolution against the optical diffraction limit. Its motivation is to explain how the finest and most reproducible 2D/3D material modifications can arise not from shortening the laser wavelength but from localizing the absorber, and to connect that mechanism to practical nanoscale and large-area engineering.

Method: The work is a review and thesis-driven analysis. It invokes the threshold effect and a transient epsilon-near-zero dielectric-to-metal state of the excited solid, with energy confined to a dipole-like nano-seed whose near-field intensity falls as r^-6 for an ideal point dipole. It argues that localization is set by seed size, while final features are shaped by post-pulse heat flow, cavitation, and quenching. It examines polarization control of deposition anisotropy through quasi-static near-field and interface boundary conditions, surveys energy-deposition mechanisms from 100 nm to the single-nanometer scale, and links them to engineering demonstrations. Specific laser wavelengths, pulse parameters, and quantitative benchmarking are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The review reports that polarization and interface boundary conditions can select writing direction and modification orientation down to a single defect; that energy-deposition mechanisms span 100 nm to single-nanometer scales; and that the approach enables stitch-free large-area patterning and record aspect-ratio dicing and wafering of transparent crystals at 10^3-10^4. It states that 10-20 nm features have been demonstrated, while deterministic single-defect and single-bond modification remain goals. It also conjectures guided volumetric deposition in which laser-inscribed lossy nano-seeds act as antennas for cavity-microwave annealing of modified nano-volumes from within, analogous to 2.45 GHz water-dipole heating in an oven.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that ultrafast direct write can beat diffraction-limited intuition because the material forms a transient nanoscale absorber whose near-field and boundary conditions govern energy deposition. Resolution is therefore dictated by absorber localization and material response rather than by the focal wavelength. Atomic resolution is used in a quantum-confined sense as a direction of travel, not as an achieved deterministic single-atom or single-bond capability; 10-20 nm features are demonstrated, and deterministic single-defect and single-bond modification remain future goals.

Abstract: Direct laser writing with ultra-short pulses is the most flexible route to two- and three-dimensional (2D/3D) material modification, yet its resolution is judged against the optical diffraction limit. This review argues the opposite: the finest, most reproducible modifications come not from shortening the wavelength but from localising the absorber. Through the threshold effect and a transient epsilon-near-zero (ENZ) state - the dielectric-to-metal (Die-Met) state of the excited solid - the deposited energy is confined to a dipole-like nano-seed whose near-field intensity falls as r^-6 (ideal point dipole). The localisation is thus set by the seed size, not the focal wavelength, and the final feature is shaped by the material response: post-pulse heat flow, cavitation and quenching. We show that polarisation controls the anisotropy of deposition: the quasi-static near-field and interface boundary conditions select the writing direction and the modification orientation, down to a single defect. We survey energy-deposition mechanisms from 100 nm to the single-nanometer scale and link them to working engineering: stitch-free large-area patterning and record aspect-ratio (10^3-10^4) dicing and wafering of transparent crystals. Finally, we conjecture guided volumetric deposition, in which laser-inscribed lossy nano-seeds act as antennas that let cavity microwaves anneal the modified nano-volumes from within, as a 2.45 GHz oven heats food through water-dipole absorption. Throughout, atomic resolution is used in a quantum-confined sense and denotes a direction of travel: 10-20 nm features are demonstrated, while deterministic single-defect and single-bond modification remain goals. The thesis is that ultrafast direct write beats diffraction-limited intuition because the material itself forms a transient nanoscale absorber whose near-field and boundary conditions govern energy deposition.

</details>


### [92] [Shot-to-shot statistical fluctuations during beam self-cleaning in multimode fibers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03004)
*Alessia Cirimele, Lucas Zanaglia, Nelson Manuel Mora Fernandez, Alfredo Mazzulla, Pasquale Pagliusi, Bertrand Kibler, Guy Millot, Gabriella Cipparrone, Mario Ferraro*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: As beam self-cleaning develops in multimode optical fibers, the transverse output beam profile becomes progressively more reproducible from shot to shot, consistent with a thermodynamic equilibrium interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Beam self-cleaning has recently been interpreted thermodynamically, with support from modal-population and entropy measurements, but the role of shot-to-shot spatial fluctuations of the output beam had not been explored.

Method: The authors experimentally characterize the shot-to-shot reproducibility of the transverse output beam profile as beam self-cleaning develops; further experimental details are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The transverse output beam profile becomes progressively more reproducible from shot to shot as beam self-cleaning develops.

Conclusion: This behavior is consistent with the thermodynamic expectation that relative fluctuations become increasingly small as the system approaches equilibrium.

Abstract: Beam self-cleaning in multimode optical fibers has recently been interpreted within a thermodynamic framework, in which nonlinear propagation drives the optical field towards a statistical equilibrium state. While this interpretation has been supported by measurements of modal populations and entropy, the role of shot-to-shot spatial fluctuations of the output beam has so far remained unexplored. Here, we experimentally show that the transverse output beam profile becomes progressively more reproducible from shot to shot as beam self-cleaning develops. This behavior is consistent with the thermodynamic expectation that relative fluctuations become increasingly small as the system approaches equilibrium.

</details>


### [93] [Electromagnetic Information Theory for Programmable Metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02935)
*Philipp del Hougne, Marco Di Renzo, Owen D. Miller, David A. B. Miller*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This Perspective argues that programmable metasurfaces and programmable wave systems require a unified electromagnetic information theory to characterize both system-specific performance limits and the physical resources that determine them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Programmable metasurfaces enable fine-grained electromagnetic-wave control and new opportunities in information extraction, transfer, and processing, but their fundamental capabilities remain poorly understood despite exciting experimental demonstrations.

Method: The authors present a Perspective and propose an electromagnetic information theory addressing limits at two levels: bounding achievable information manipulations for a given physical platform, and determining which non-programmable and programmable physical resources bound those system-specific limits and what minimum resources are needed for prescribed performance. They discuss emerging theoretical approaches and their connections to experimentally realizable systems, examining device thickness, programmable degrees of freedom, nonlocality, coupling, and multiple scattering as constraints.

Result: The abstract reports no new experimental or quantitative results. It articulates the need for the proposed unified framework, discusses emerging theoretical approaches, and identifies physical factors that constrain information-manipulation limits across communications, sensing, and computing.

Conclusion: Establishing the envisioned electromagnetic information theory could clarify the ultimate capabilities of programmable metasurfaces and guide the design of future wave-based information systems.

Abstract: Programmable metasurfaces enable fine-grained control of electromagnetic waves and are creating new opportunities for information extraction, information transfer and information processing. Yet, despite exciting experimental demonstrations, their fundamental capabilities remain poorly understood. In this Perspective, we argue that programmable metasurfaces, and programmable wave systems more generally, call for an electromagnetic information theory that addresses limits at two levels, in a unified manner across applications like communications, sensing and computing. First, for a given physical platform, such a theory should bound achievable information manipulations, such as sensing precision, information-transfer rates, and computational performance. Second, at a more fundamental level, it should determine what non-programmable and programmable physical resources bound these system-specific limits, and what minimum resources are required to attain a prescribed level of performance. We discuss emerging theoretical approaches to these questions, and their connection to experimentally realizable systems. We examine how device thickness, programmable degrees of freedom, nonlocality, coupling and multiple scattering constrain these limits. Establishing the envisioned framework could clarify the ultimate capabilities of programmable metasurfaces and guide the design of future wave-based information systems.

</details>
