### [94] [A plug-and-play platform for self-aligning 3D-printed structures on optical fibers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02850)
*Lukáš Zezulka, Zdeněk Nováček, Martin Konečný, Milan Matějka, Štěpán Černý, Katarína Rovenská, Jiří Spousta, Tomáš Šikola*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper presents a fiber-decoupled, planar-substrate fabrication approach for two-photon-polymerized lab-on-fiber devices, using modular platforms with self-aligning sockets and replaceable printed tips, demonstrated as exchangeable scanning probe microscopy tips validated by three-channel correlative imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Widespread adoption of two-photon polymerization 3D printing for lab-on-fiber devices is limited by the inefficiencies of sequential, direct-on-fiber printing.

Method: The authors develop a custom modular platform with a compliant, spring-like socket enabling passive self-alignment and reversible fiber insertion, plus a customizable frontal device. Fabrication is shifted to planar substrates for parallel batch processing, with the substrate acting as a functional magazine. Demonstrator platforms with functional tips are printed, plasma-etched for sharpening, metallized, and given an optical aperture by focused ion beam milling while still on the substrate, then used as exchangeable scanning probe microscopy tips mounted in situ in a scanning electron microscope.

Result: The design is validated through simultaneous three-channel correlative imaging combining atomic force microscopy topography, secondary electron imaging, and cathodoluminescence, supporting optomechanical stability. Quantitative performance metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Decoupling the delicate optical device's lifespan from the optical fiber turns lab-on-fiber tools into scalable, seamlessly replaceable consumables, providing an adaptable pathway for photonics, sensing, and microscopy applications.

Abstract: Two-photon polymerization 3D printing advances optical fibers into complex lab-on-fiber devices. However, widespread adoption remains bottlenecked by the inherent inefficiencies of sequential, direct-on-fiber printing. To address this, a custom-designed modular platform and a corresponding fiber-decoupled fabrication methodology on planar substrates are presented. This platform features a compliant, spring-like socket that enables passive self-alignment and reversible fiber insertion, paired with a customizable frontal device. The shift to fabrication on planar substrates enables parallel batch processing and allows the substrate to serve as a functional magazine for device mounting. To demonstrate this approach, modular platforms featuring a functional tip are printed, sharpened via post-process plasma etching, metallized, and equipped with an optical aperture using focused ion beam milling, while remaining on the planar substrate. Subsequently, these are used as exchangeable scanning probe microscopy tips mounted in situ within a scanning electron microscope. Simultaneous three-channel correlative imaging (atomic force microscopy topography, secondary electron, and cathodoluminescence) is used to validate the optomechanical stability of the design. Ultimately, by decoupling the delicate optical device's lifespan from the optical fiber, this architecture transforms lab-on-fiber tools into scalable, seamlessly replaceable consumables, offering a highly adaptable pathway for broader applications in photonics, sensing, and microscopy.

</details>


### [95] [Phase-locked beam splitting with continuously tunable intensity ratio using stochastic phase masks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02755)
*Melvin Redon, Titouan Gadeyne, Gaspard Beaufort, Saga Westerberg, Vartika Vishnoi, Peter Smorenburg, Anne-Lise Viotti, Thierry Ruchon, Cord L. Arnold*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A common-path beamsplitter is demonstrated using an SLM phase mask divided into randomly assigned square patches that steer light to two phase-locked foci and a dump. Patch fractions tune the focal intensity ratio, while patch size controls the speckle background; a closed-form ensemble-averaged model is derived and validated experimentally and numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation; the work targets a common-path SLM-based beam splitter that can tune the intensity ratio between two phase-locked foci while preserving their spatial modes and maintaining a stable relative phase.

Method: The SLM aperture is divided into square patches randomly assigned to one of three phase ramps, steering light to two independent phase-locked foci and a dump for surplus power. The beams are observed in a lens focal plane; patch fractions set focal intensities and ramp slopes set focus positions. A closed-form expression for the ensemble-averaged focal intensity is derived and validated against experiment and numerical Fraunhofer propagation.

Result: The intensity ratio between the two foci was tuned experimentally from 0 to 1 without distorting either spatial mode, with relative phase constant to within a few tens of mrad. The model decomposes the focal intensity into coherent foci of the desired pattern and a speckle background whose shape and amplitude are set by the patch size, with quantitative agreement over about three orders of magnitude in intensity.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a common-path SLM beam splitter with tunable focal intensity ratio and stable relative phase, supported by a validated closed-form model. Patch size is identified as the central design parameter, and its experimental bounds are characterized.

Abstract: We introduce a common-path beam splitter based on a randomly partitioned phase mask displayed on a spatial light modulator (SLM). The SLM aperture is divided into square patches, each randomly assigned to one of three phase ramps that steer light to three independent foci: two phase-locked beams and a dump that takes up the surplus power. We observe the beams in the focal plane of a lens. The fractions of patches assigned to each ramp set the focal intensities, with the focus positions controlled by the ramp slopes.We first demonstrate experimentally that the intensity ratio between the two foci can be tuned from 0 to 1 without distorting either of the spatial modes, and with their relative phase constant to within a few tens of mrad. We then derive a closed-form expression for the ensemble-averaged focal intensity, which decomposes into coherent foci of the desired pattern and a speckle background whose shape and amplitude are set by the patch size. The model is validated against experiment and against numerical Fraunhofer propagation, with quantitative agreement over about three orders of magnitude in intensity. We also identify the patch size as the central design parameter and characterize its experimental bounds.

</details>


### [96] [Edge photocurrent distribution in luminescent solar concentrators: analytical treatment and solar cell arrangement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02668)
*Ilya Sychugov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: An analytical model is developed for the optical path distribution and non-uniform edge light flux in rectangular and convex polygonal luminescent solar concentrators, including reabsorption and scattering. It is validated against Monte Carlo simulations and measured edge-cell currents, then used to quantify series current mismatch and formulate edge solar cell arrangement rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In luminescent solar concentrators, the light flux along the edges is non-uniform, causing current mismatch between series-connected edge solar cells. A model is needed to describe this edge flux distribution and inform cell arrangement.

Method: The optical path distribution of photons arriving at an edge point is derived analytically for a rectangular luminescent solar concentrator and generalized to convex polygons, with reabsorption and scattering included as a geometric series. The model is compared with Monte Carlo simulations and with measured edge cell currents of a 60×60 cm² luminescent solar concentrator.

Result: The model agrees with Monte Carlo simulations and reproduces measured edge cell currents of a 60×60 cm² luminescent solar concentrator within 2–3%. In a square luminescent solar concentrator, series mismatch reduces the current by 8–12% for four cells per edge. Rules for edge solar cell arrangement are formulated.

Conclusion: The analytical model captures non-uniform edge light flux and series current mismatch in luminescent solar concentrators and supports the formulation of edge solar cell arrangement rules. The specific arrangement rules are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: In luminescent solar concentrators (LSCs), the light flux along the edges is non-uniform, which causes current mismatch between series-connected edge solar cells. Here, the optical path distribution of photons arriving at an edge point was derived analytically for a rectangular LSC and generalized to convex polygons, with reabsorption and scattering included as a geometric series. The model agrees with Monte Carlo simulations and reproduces measured edge cell currents of a $60\times60$ cm$^2$ LSC within 2-3%. In a square LSC, the series mismatch reduces the current by 8-12% for four cells per edge. Rules for edge solar cell arrangement are formulated.

</details>


### [97] [Physics-Informed Surrogate for Sb2S3 Dispersion Trimming in Thin-Film Lithium Niobate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02652)
*Shoumik Debnath, Anup Kumar Modak, Sudipta Saha*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A numerical study shows that segmented non-volatile phase-change cladding can post-fabrication trim group-velocity dispersion in thin-film lithium niobate: per-die bit patterns from a constrained spectral surrogate brought 96.8% of dies within ±25 ps² km⁻¹, with Sb₂S₃ offering high β₂ contrast without modeled absorption and Sb₂Se₃ at higher loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An 8 nm film-thickness error, within commercial thin-film lithium niobate wafer tolerance, shifts β₂ at 1550 nm by about 28 ps² km⁻¹, motivating correction after fabrication.

Method: Mode solves of 3012 geometries compared Sb₂S₃ and Sb₂Se₃ claddings; four independently switched binary segments were used to access a two-dimensional (β₂,β₃) region, with eigenmode expansion at junctions. Full-wave solves of 65 die geometries informed per-die bit selection using a spectral surrogate constrained by exact identities of the lossless Maxwell problem; a thermal model estimated a candidate write window for a 532 nm beam.

Result: At 40 nm strip thickness, Sb₂S₃ shifted β₂ by a median 181 ps² km⁻¹ with no modeled material absorption, while Sb₂Se₃ gave comparable contrast at median modal loss 5.6 dB cm⁻¹. Segment states varied with die error. The constrained surrogate had training-domain accuracy similar to unconstrained baselines, maintained β₂–β₃ consistency, and reduced error at an unseen film thickness to 8.3 vs 13.0 ps² km⁻¹. Using a bare test ring and a second ring with a crystalline strip already on the mask, 96.8% of dies were trimmed within ±25 ps² km⁻¹ by full-wave scoring.

Conclusion: The abstract reports numerical evidence that segmented phase-change cladding with per-die bit patterns is a viable post-fabrication dispersion-trimming approach for thin-film lithium niobate and provides a candidate 532 nm write window from a thermal model; experimental validation is not reported in the abstract.

Abstract: A film-thickness error of 8 nm, within the tolerance of commercial thin-film lithium niobate wafers, shifts the group-velocity dispersion $β_2$ at 1550 nm by about 28 ps$^2$ km$^{-1}$. We study numerically whether a segmented, non-volatile phase-change cladding can correct this error after fabrication. In mode solves of 3012 geometries, Sb$_2$S$_3$ switches $β_2$ by a median of 181 ps$^2$ km$^{-1}$ at a 40 nm strip thickness with no material absorption under the adopted optical constants, whereas Sb$_2$Se$_3$ reaches a comparable contrast at a median modal loss of 5.6 dB cm$^{-1}$. Because phase adds along the guide, four independently switched binary segments reach a two-dimensional region of the $(β_2,β_3)$ plane; eigenmode expansion supports this description at the junctions. Full-wave solves at 65 die geometries show that the segment states move with the die error, so the bit pattern is chosen from a per-die model: a spectral surrogate constrained by exact identities of the lossless Maxwell problem. Its accuracy within the training domain is similar to that of unconstrained baselines; it keeps $β_2$ and $β_3$ consistent, and its error at an unseen film thickness is 8.3 rather than 13.0 ps$^2$ km$^{-1}$. With a bare test ring and a second ring carrying a crystalline strip already on the mask, 96.8% of the dies are trimmed into $\pm25$ ps$^2$ km$^{-1}$, scored on full-wave solutions. A thermal model gives a candidate write window for a 532 nm beam.

</details>


### [98] [Chiral phonon magnetism from localized Raman excitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02594)
*Yuhan Wang, Chuanshan Tian*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: In tetragonal α-TeO2, phase-resolved terahertz spectroscopy combined with magneto-optical Kerr measurements shows that the magnetic response tracks mechanical phonon angular momentum, not pseudo-angular momentum alone; the Kerr rotation reaches about 14 mT, scales linearly with the emitted terahertz field, and is accounted for by a localized Raman-excitation model involving Te lone-pair states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the unresolved microscopic relationship between phonon angular momentum and the transient magnetic response produced when optically driven chiral phonons induce magnetism in non-magnetic crystals.

Method: The authors combined phase-resolved terahertz spectroscopy with magneto-optical Kerr measurements to reconstruct the motion of a doubly degenerate phonon in tetragonal α-TeO2 and continuously control its angular momentum. They compared rotating phonons with non-rotating phonons, including those carrying finite pseudo-angular momentum, and tested the magnetic-field scaling. A localized Raman-excitation model involving Te lone-pair states, including electronic enhancement, was used to interpret the results.

Result: The MOKE response followed the sign, magnitude, and dynamics of phonon angular momentum, whereas non-rotating phonons, including those with finite pseudo-angular momentum, produced no detectable magnetic signal. The MOKE rotation corresponded to an effective magnetic field of approximately 14 mT, nearly seven orders of magnitude above the estimate for spatially uniform ionic currents. The Kerr response scaled linearly with the emitted terahertz field rather than quadratically as predicted by the uniform-current model. The localized Raman-excitation model accounted for this scaling and yielded a field magnitude comparable to the MOKE data when electronic enhancement was included.

Conclusion: Mechanical lattice rotation is identified as the dominant dynamical variable for the observed magnetic response, and localized Raman coupling is suggested to contribute to its unexpectedly large magnitude.

Abstract: Optically driven chiral phonons can induce transient magnetism in non-magnetic crystals, yet the microscopic relationship between phonon angular momentum and the resulting magnetic response remains unresolved. Here we distinguish the roles of pseudo-angular momentum and mechanical angular momentum in tetragonal α-TeO2. Combining phase-resolved terahertz spectroscopy with magneto-optical Kerr measurements (MOKE), we reconstruct the motion of a doubly degenerate phonon and continuously control its angular momentum. The MOKE response follows the sign and magnitude of phonon angular momentum as well as its dynamics, whereas non-rotating phonons, including those with finite pseudo-angular momentum, produce no detectable magnetic signal. The MOKE rotation corresponds to an effective magnetic field of approximately 14 mT, nearly seven orders of magnitude above the estimate for spatially uniform ionic currents. Moreover, the Kerr response scales linearly with the emitted terahertz field, rather than quadratically as predicted by the uniform-current model. A localized Raman-excitation model involving Te lone-pair states accounts for this scaling and yields a comparable field magnitude with the MOKE data when electronic enhancement is included. These findings identify mechanical lattice rotation as the dominant dynamical variable for the observed magnetic response and suggest that localized Raman coupling contributes to its unexpectedly large magnitude.

</details>


### [99] [Deterministic and dynamic synthesis of optical skyrmions through an ultrathin multimode fibre](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02549)
*R. E. Bautista Gonzalez, R. Mouthaan, A. McIntosh, K. Dholakia*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates deterministic generation and dynamic reconfiguration of arbitrary optical skyrmions through an ultrathin multimode fibre by combining vectorial transmission-matrix control with phase-retrieval optimisation, enabling high-fidelity synthesis of multiple skyrmionic states confirmed by Stokes-vector measurements and skyrmion-number quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic generation of topological light fields, particularly optical skyrmions, in complex scattering media remains challenging; in highly multimode fibres, strong mode coupling and polarisation mixing obscure the precise control needed to synthesise structured vectorial states.

Method: The authors use an ultrathin multimode fibre and combine vectorial transmission-matrix control with a phase-retrieval optimisation framework, exploiting the fibre's full polarisation-resolved modal space to reconstruct user-defined vector fields with high fidelity at the distal facet; wavefront reprogramming is used for dynamic control.

Result: The approach synthesises Néel, Bloch, anti- and higher-order skyrmions, and bimerons; their topological properties are confirmed through polarisation-resolved Stokes-vector measurements and quantitative determination of the skyrmion number, and wavefront reprogramming enables reversible transitions between distinct skyrmionic states.

Conclusion: Multimode fibres are established as programmable platforms for topological light generation and a scalable route towards minimally invasive topological photonics, with potential applications in imaging, sensing, optical communications, and information processing in complex environments.

Abstract: Topological light fields offer powerful opportunities for information encoding, transport, and manipulation, yet their deterministic generation in complex scattering media remains an outstanding challenge. In highly multimode fibres, strong mode coupling and polarisation mixing can obscure the precise control required to synthesise structured vectorial states, including optical skyrmions. Here, we demonstrate the deterministic generation and dynamic reconfiguration of arbitrary optical skyrmions through an ultrathin multimode fibre. By combining vectorial transmission-matrix control with a phase-retrieval optimisation framework, we exploit the full polarisation-resolved modal space of the fibre to reconstruct user-defined vector fields with high fidelity at the distal facet. This approach enables the synthesis of Néel, Bloch, anti- and higher-order skyrmions, and bimerons. Their topological properties are confirmed through polarisation-resolved Stokes-vector measurements and quantitative determination of the skyrmion number. Wavefront reprogramming further provides dynamic control over polarisation textures, enabling reversible transitions between distinct skyrmionic states. Our results establish multimode fibres as programmable platforms for the generation of topological light and introduce a scalable route towards minimally invasive topological photonics, with potential applications in imaging, sensing, optical communications, and information processing in complex environments.

</details>


### [100] [Conditional stopping guarantees for a folded parametrization of reduced Stokes vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02421)
*Hiroyasu Usami, Ayato Tsuboi, Keisuke Hara*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops conditional stopping guarantees for a folded parametrization of reduced Stokes vectors, using normalized first derivatives and a radial lower-curvature test to bound the physical objective gap. An exact recovery identity restores the physical-gradient component lost at the fully linearly polarized fold. In a nominal quadratic test, an outward-rounded implementation certifies a gap of at most 10^-6 for 8,861 of 9,284 endpoints, rejects all 295 historical false successes and 128 accurate endpoints, and runs in 7.32 seconds with 52 scalar curvature evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stopping decisions made in parametrization coordinates need not certify physically accurate estimates. The paper is motivated by the need for conditional stopping guarantees for a folded parametrization of reduced Stokes vectors, including partial linear polarization, without requiring prior localization near an optimum or a positive-semidefinite head Hessian.

Method: The paper establishes conditional stopping guarantees for strongly convex physical objectives on the bounded-intensity inner sheet. It uses normalized first derivatives and one radial lower-curvature test to derive an explicit objective-gap bound with sharp tolerance orders ε^(4/3)+ν^2. The guarantee relies on an exact recovery identity in which one signed radial curvature restores the physical-gradient component lost at the fully linearly polarized fold, without stationarity or knowledge of the physical Hessian. A nominal quadratic model is tested with an outward-rounded implementation, including a full 128-fractional-bit pass.

Result: The explicit objective-gap bound has sharp tolerance orders ε^(4/3)+ν^2. For the nominal quadratic model, the outward-rounded implementation certifies a gap of at most 10^-6 for 8,861 of 9,284 stored endpoints; it rejects all 295 historical false successes and 128 accurate endpoints. The full 128-fractional-bit pass takes 7.32 seconds and includes 52 scalar curvature evaluations. Controlled fits and released observations distinguish stopping certification from local fold diagnosis and subsequent coordinate repair. Twenty shared-network fits contain no saved near-fold negative-curvature candidates, and direct physical solvers are cheaper on the tested quadratics.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the proposed conditional stopping guarantees can certify physical objective gaps for the folded parametrization under the stated assumptions, and that stopping certification should be distinguished from local fold diagnosis and coordinate repair. The abstract also reports that the implementation can reject historical false successes but may also reject accurate endpoints, that tested shared-network fits showed no saved near-fold negative-curvature candidates, and that direct physical solvers were cheaper on the tested quadratics. Broader empirical scope and formal proof details are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Stopping decisions made in parametrization coordinates need not certify physically accurate estimates. We establish conditional stopping guarantees for a folded parametrization of reduced Stokes vectors, including partial linear polarization. For strongly convex physical objectives on the bounded-intensity inner sheet, normalized first derivatives and one radial lower-curvature test give an explicit objective-gap bound with sharp tolerance orders $\varepsilon^{4/3}+ν^2$. The guarantee requires neither prior localization near an optimum nor a positive-semidefinite head Hessian. Its information basis is an exact recovery identity: one signed radial curvature restores the physical-gradient component lost at the fully linearly polarized fold, without stationarity or knowledge of the physical Hessian. For a nominal quadratic model, an outward-rounded implementation certifies a gap of at most $10^{-6}$ for 8,861 of 9,284 stored endpoints; it rejects all 295 historical false successes and 128 accurate endpoints. The full 128-fractional-bit pass takes 7.32 seconds and includes 52 scalar curvature evaluations. Controlled fits and released observations distinguish stopping certification from local fold diagnosis and subsequent coordinate repair. Twenty shared-network fits contain no saved near-fold negative-curvature candidates, and direct physical solvers are cheaper on the tested quadratics.

</details>


### [101] [Nondestructive 3D Metrology of Through-Glass Via Geometry and Internal Wall Texture Using Holotomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02225)
*Dohyeon Lee, Herve Hugonnet, YongKeun Park*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Holotomography was used to nondestructively reconstruct the refractive index distribution of intact, unfilled through-glass vias before metallization, enabling localization of internal boundaries and quantification of via geometry, taper, and wall texture in a single optical workflow. Numerical measurement values and independent validation are not provided in the abstract.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Through-glass vias are increasingly important for semiconductor packaging, but their internal geometry and wall texture are difficult to characterize nondestructively. The work aims to inspect these features in intact, unfilled TGVs before metallization.

Method: Holotomography (HT) was applied to reconstruct the three-dimensional refractive index (RI) distribution of the glass and via. RI maps and spatially filtered RI maps were used to localize the internal boundary. Geometric fitting quantified via dimensions and taper, while spatial filtering separated slowly varying wall form from finer texture.

Result: The reconstructed surface supported volumetric visualization, dimensional measurements, and spatial characterization of wall texture within one optical inspection workflow. The measurements revealed internal features inaccessible from surface views and provided quantitative descriptors for evaluating TGVs. Specific quantitative values are not reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: HT provides a nondestructive optical approach for characterizing the internal geometry and wall texture of intact, unfilled TGVs before metallization, yielding quantitative descriptors intended to support TGV evaluation. The abstract does not provide numerical results or independent validation.

Abstract: Through-glass vias (TGVs) are increasingly important for semiconductor packaging, yet their internal geometry and wall texture remain difficult to characterize nondestructively. Here, we use holotomography (HT) to inspect the three-dimensional (3D) geometry and internal wall texture of intact, unfilled TGVs before metallization. HT reconstructs the refractive index (RI) distribution of the glass and via. Maps of RI and spatially filtered RI then localize the internal boundary. Geometric fitting quantifies via dimensions and taper, while spatial filtering separates slowly varying wall form from finer texture. The reconstructed surface supports volumetric visualization, dimensional measurements and spatial characterization of wall texture within one optical inspection workflow. These measurements reveal internal features inaccessible from surface views and provide quantitative descriptors to evaluate TGVs

</details>


### [102] [Optical spectral asymmetry enables differential refractive-index sensing under near-perfect common-mode suppression in a single-cavity dual-comb fiber laser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02150)
*Masayuki Higaki, Yoshiaki Nakajima, Shuji Taue, Eiji Hase, Yu Tokizane, Takeshi Yasui*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A single-cavity dual-comb fiber laser with an intracavity multimode-interference sensor enables differential refractive-index sensing based on repetition-frequency difference despite near-perfect common-mode suppression; optical spectral asymmetry, mediated by cavity dispersion, provides the differential signal and supports highly stable sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-cavity dual-comb fiber lasers provide near-perfect common-mode suppression, but this suppression is generally expected to eliminate differential sensing signals. The work examines whether and how differential refractive-index sensing can still be achieved in such a platform.

Method: An intracavity multimode-interference fiber sensor is integrated into a shared cavity, simultaneously generating and interrogating two counter-propagating combs. Differential refractive-index sensing is performed using the repetition-frequency difference. Spectral analysis is used to relate optical spectral asymmetry to asymmetric repetition-frequency responses through cavity dispersion.

Result: The architecture achieves near-perfect common-mode suppression and reduces temperature-induced drift by more than two orders of magnitude compared with a conventional dual-cavity dual-comb system. Optical spectral asymmetry produces asymmetric repetition-frequency responses via cavity dispersion, explaining the physical origin of the differential sensing signal. A trade-off is identified: sensitivity is reduced because the sensor is shared, while improved frequency stability enhances robustness against environmental perturbations. Specific sensitivity and noise values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Near-perfect common-mode suppression does not preclude differential sensing; instead, it reveals a sensing mechanism governed by optical spectral asymmetry. Optical spectral asymmetry is established as a design principle for differential sensing in single-cavity dual-comb systems and provides a framework for highly stable long-term photonic sensing.

Abstract: Single-cavity dual-comb fiber lasers offer near-perfect common-mode suppression, but such suppression is generally expected to eliminate differential sensing signals. Here we show that optical spectral asymmetry enables differential refractive-index (RI) sensing despite near-perfect common-mode suppression. By integrating an intracavity multimode interference (MMI) fiber sensor into a shared cavity, two counter-propagating combs are simultaneously generated and interrogated, enabling differential RI sensing based on the repetition-frequency difference (Δfrep). This architecture achieves near-perfect common-mode suppression, reducing temperature-induced drift by more than two orders of magnitude compared with a conventional dual-cavity dual-comb system. Spectral analysis reveals that optical spectral asymmetry gives rise to asymmetric repetition-frequency responses through cavity dispersion, thereby providing the physical origin of the differential sensing signal. A trade-off between sensitivity and noise suppression is identified: while the sensitivity is reduced due to the shared sensor, the improved frequency stability enhances robustness against environmental perturbations. These results demonstrate that near-perfect common-mode suppression does not preclude differential sensing, but instead reveals a new sensing mechanism governed by optical spectral asymmetry. These findings establish optical spectral asymmetry as a design principle for differential sensing in single-cavity dual-comb systems and provide a framework for highly stable long-term photonic sensing.

</details>


### [103] [Scan-less Hyperspectral Polarimetric Imaging Using Dual-Comb Single-Pixel Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02149)
*Shogo Tanimura, Eiji Hase, Hidenori Koresawa, Kyuki Shibuya, Yu Tokizane, Akifumi Asahara, Kaoru Minoshima, Takeshi Yasui*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Dual-comb spectro-polarimetric single-pixel imaging (DCSP-SPI) combines dual-comb spectroscopic polarimetry with structured illumination and single-pixel detection to achieve scan-less hyperspectral polarimetric imaging without a camera, reconstructing spectrally resolved amplitude-ratio and phase-difference images and extending to full Jones matrix elements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional spectroscopic polarimetry relies on mechanical polarization modulation and is difficult to extend to fast, broadband imaging. Dual-comb spectroscopic polarimetry offers rapid, modulation-free polarization-resolved amplitude and phase spectra but has been limited to point-based measurements, motivating an imaging-capable extension.

Method: The proposed DCSP-SPI framework combines dual-comb spectroscopic polarimetry with structured illumination and single-pixel detection. It exploits the intrinsic amplitude and phase sensitivity of dual-comb interferometry to reconstruct spectrally resolved images of amplitude ratio psi and phase difference delta across a broad optical spectrum. Multiple incident polarization states are used to extend the approach to hyperspectral reconstruction of full Jones matrix elements. Imaging experiments use a birefringent resolution target and a Fabry-Perot etalon.

Result: Frequency-dependent psi and delta images were obtained and shown to follow the expected spectral and polarization characteristics. The approach was further extended to hyperspectral reconstruction of full Jones matrix elements using multiple incident polarization states.

Conclusion: DCSP-SPI provides a pathway toward scan-less, hyperspectral polarization imaging and offers a flexible platform for applications requiring quantitative spatial, spectral, and polarization analysis.

Abstract: Spectroscopic polarimetry enables detailed characterization of light-matter interactions by combining spectral and polarization information, yet conventional implementations rely on mechanical polarization modulation and are difficult to extend to fast, broadband imaging. Dual-comb spectroscopic polarimetry (DCSP) addresses these limitations by providing rapid, modulation-free access to polarization-resolved amplitude and phase spectra, but has so far been limited to point-based measurements. In this work, we introduce dual-comb spectro-polarimetric single-pixel imaging (DCSP-SPI), a new framework that combines DCSP with structured illumination and single-pixel detection to achieve scan-less hyperspectral polarimetric imaging without a camera. By exploiting the intrinsic amplitude and phase sensitivity of dual-comb interferometry, spectrally resolved images of the amplitude ratio psi and phase difference delta are reconstructed across a broad optical spectrum. The performance of DCSP-SPI is demonstrated through imaging experiments using a birefringent resolution target and a Fabry-Perot etalon, where frequency-dependent psi and delta images are obtained and shown to follow the expected spectral and polarization characteristics. Furthermore, the approach is extended to hyperspectral reconstruction of full Jones matrix elements by using multiple incident polarization states. The proposed DCSP-SPI framework provides a new pathway toward scan-less, hyperspectral polarization imaging, offering a flexible platform for applications requiring quantitative spatial, spectral, and polarization analysis.

</details>


### [104] [Controlling the Sign and Magnitude of the Thermoreflectance Coefficient of Gold-MoS$_2$ Metamaterials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02024)
*D. Saleta Reig, B. Liu, K. Xu, J. S. Reparaz, K. -J. Tielrooij*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Ultrathin MoS2 buried under a 50 nm gold film enables control of the thermoreflectance coefficient, including suppression, sign inversion, and revival, through MoS2 thickness, probe wavelength, and ambient temperature; transfer-matrix simulations reproduce the observations and support an optothermal metamaterial platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The thermoreflectance coefficient C_TR determines temperature sensitivity of optical reflectance but can be detrimental when constant reflection is required. Previous control relied on material and wavelength selection rather than engineering C_TR itself, motivating direct control of its sign and magnitude.

Method: The authors study a 50 nm thin gold film on SiO2/Si with an ultrathin MoS2 layer underneath, using time-resolved thermoreflectance measurements and transfer-matrix simulations with temperature-dependent dielectric functions of gold and MoS2. They vary buried MoS2 thickness, probe wavelength, and ambient temperature.

Result: Time-resolved thermoreflectance measurements reveal suppression, sign inversion, and revival of the thermoreflectance signal controlled by the three knobs. Transfer-matrix simulations reproduce all experimental observations. The abstract does not provide quantitative values for the magnitude of suppression or inversion.

Conclusion: An ultrathin MoS2 layer can engineer the thermoreflectance coefficient of a gold film, demonstrating an optothermal metamaterial platform with controllable properties. These anti-thermoreflective or thermoreflective metamaterial mirrors bring C_TR under interference design rules, enabling maximization or minimization of temperature-dependent optical reflectance for specific applications.

Abstract: Heating a material changes how much light it reflects. This effect, quantified by the thermoreflectance coefficient $C_\mathrm{TR}$, enables temperature sensing, while it is a nuisance for situations where constant reflection is crucial. Whereas temperature detection requires a large $C_\mathrm{TR}$, the latter situation demands it to be as close to zero as possible. Controlling the $C_\mathrm{TR}$ has so far relied on the selection of materials and wavelengths, rather than engineering the coefficient itself. This work demonstrates that the $C_\mathrm{TR}$ of a 50 nm thin gold film on SiO$_2$/Si can be controlled in sign and magnitude by an ultrathin ($<$8 nm) MoS$_2$ film underneath it. Time-resolved thermoreflectance measurements reveal suppression, sign inversion, and revival of the thermoreflectance signal, controlled by three knobs: ($i$) the buried MoS$_2$ thickness, ($ii$) probe wavelength, and ($iii$) ambient temperature. Transfer-matrix simulations using temperature-dependent dielectric functions of gold and MoS$_2$ reproduce all experimental observations, demonstrating an optothermal metamaterial platform with controllable properties. In particular, these (anti-)thermoreflective metamaterial mirrors bring $C_\mathrm{TR}$ under the same interference design rules previously applied to the optical response itself, opening ways to maximize or minimize the temperature dependence of the optical reflectance, tailored to the specific application.

</details>


### [105] [Experimental demonstration of phase-conjugated twin-wave coherent reception over 100 km with injection locking assisted phase stabilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01946)
*Zhenlin Zhao, Xingwen Yi, Dawei Wang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper experimentally demonstrates injection-locking-assisted phase-conjugated twin-wave coherent reception of 200-MBaud signals with modulation up to 256-QAM over 100 km of optical fiber, achieving SNR above the ideal single-mode homodyne SQL and gains of about 3 dB and 6 dB over measured homodyne and conventional heterodyne reception, respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-sensitivity recovery of complex optical signals is central to coherent communication and sensing. Phase-conjugate twin-wave encoding with phase-sensitive joint detection offers enhanced sensitivity beyond conventional homodyne and heterodyne reception, but extending it to long-distance transmission of high-order-modulated signals is challenging because optical phase coherence must be maintained at the receiver.

Method: The authors experimentally demonstrate injection-locking-assisted phase-conjugated twin-wave reception of 200-MBaud signals with modulation formats up to 256-QAM over 100 km of optical fiber. Optical injection locking regenerates the local oscillator from a reference transmitted through a 16 dB-loss link, while pilot-assisted optical feedback stabilizes the relative signal-LO phase, enabling coherent combining of the phase-conjugated twin waves at a single balanced photodetector. They measure SNR, constellations, and bit-error rate and compare with homodyne and conventional heterodyne results; an ideal Gaussian-noise model is used to predict photon information efficiency.

Result: At equal received signal-photon number per quadrature in each data-carrying optical mode, the measured PCTW SNR exceeds the ideal single-mode homodyne SQL benchmark. The SNR gains over measured homodyne and conventional heterodyne results are about 3 dB and 6 dB, respectively. Constellation and bit-error-rate measurements show improved high-order data recovery relative to heterodyne reception. The ideal Gaussian-noise model predicts a photon information efficiency of 1.54 bits per received signal photon for uniform 16-QAM.

Conclusion: The work establishes an injection-locking-assisted coherent receiver architecture that implements joint detection of phase-conjugated twin waves over 100 km of fiber with enhanced sensitivity, providing a practical route for photon-efficient coherent communication.

Abstract: Recovering complex optical signals with high sensitivity is central to coherent communication and sensing. Beyond conventional homodyne and heterodyne reception, phase-conjugate twin-wave encoding and phase-sensitive joint detection offer an alternative strategy with enhanced sensitivity. Extending this approach to long-distance transmission of high-order-modulated signals is challenging because optical phase coherence must be maintained at the receiver. Here we experimentally demonstrate injection-locking-assisted phase-conjugated twin-wave reception of 200-MBaud signals with modulation formats up to 256-QAM over 100 km of optical fiber. Optical injection locking regenerates the local oscillator (LO) from a reference transmitted through the 16 dB-loss link, while pilot-assisted optical feedback stabilizes the relative signal-LO phase, enabling coherent combining of the phase-conjugated twin waves at a single balanced photodetector. At equal received signal-photon number per quadrature in each data-carrying optical mode, the measured PCTW signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) exceeds the ideal single-mode homodyne standard quantum limit (SQL) benchmark. The SNR gains over the measured homodyne and conventional heterodyne results are about 3 dB and 6 dB, respectively. Constellation and bit-error-rate measurements show that this gain improves high-order data recovery relative to heterodyne reception. An ideal Gaussian-noise model predicts a photon information efficiency of 1.54 bits per received signal photon for uniform 16-QAM. These results establish an injection-locking-assisted coherent receiver architecture that implements joint detection of phase-conjugated twin waves over 100 km of fiber with enhanced sensitivity, providing a practical route for photon-efficient coherent communication.

</details>


### [106] [Propagation invariant one-dimensional flat-top vector beam with conserved topology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01883)
*Cathy Maako, Yuancong Cao, Pedro Ornelas, Mingjian Cheng, Andrew Forbes, Angela Dudley, Isaac Nape*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper generates and controls a propagation-invariant one-dimensional flat-top vector beam by digitally superposing orthogonally polarized first-order petal and Gaussian modes, analyzes its spatial, polarization, and topological properties, and assesses it for laser cutting and optical trapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation beyond highlighting the relevance of topological structured light to laser cutting and optical trapping applications.

Method: A digitally controlled superposition of a first-order petal mode and a Gaussian mode, each with orthogonal polarizations, is used to generate and control the beam. The authors analyze its spatial and polarization profiles and topological features, examine the differential Gouy phase between spatial components, and assess the beams for laser cutting and optical trapping.

Result: The approach produces a propagation-invariant one-dimensional flat-top vector beam with preserved intensity and topological features invariant to propagation dynamics. The differential Gouy phase between spatial components is shown to rotate the great circle traced by the polarization distribution on the Poincaré sphere.

Conclusion: The beams are relevant to laser cutting and optical trapping, underscoring the relevance of topological structured light to these applications; specific experimental performance outcomes are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: We report the generation and control of a propagation invariant one-dimensional flat-top vector beam through the digitally controlled superposition of a first-order petal- and Gaussian- mode each marked with orthogonal polarizations. We present a detailed analysis of the spatial and polarization profiles and unveil their intricate topological features, showing that they have properties that are invariant to the propagation dynamics, in addition to their preserved intensity. The differential Gouy phase between the spatial components is shown to rotate the great circle traced by the polarisation distribution on the Poincaré sphere. Finally, we assess the beams for laser cutting and optical trapping, highlighting the relevance of topological structured light to these applications.

</details>


### [107] [Learned optical compression for single-pixel quantitative phase imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01675)
*Ary Portes, Yuntian Wang, Che-Yung Shen, Shiqi Chen, Aydogan Ozcan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates single-pixel quantitative phase imaging using jointly learned diffractive encoding and neural decoding, achieving about 40-fold dimensional compression and high-fidelity phase reconstruction from scalar intensity measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative phase imaging reveals optical path-length variations in transparent objects, but it typically depends on spatially resolved intensity measurements. The authors aim to make QPI more measurement-efficient and suitable for low-data-rate phase imaging and sensing, including spectral regions where high-quality detector arrays are costly or unavailable.

Method: The authors numerically and experimentally demonstrate learned optical compression for single-pixel QPI by jointly optimizing a diffractive encoder and a digital neural decoder to concentrate reconstruction-relevant phase information into a compact measurement vector. A single reconfigurable phase-modulation plane sequentially displays learned encoding patterns that are independent of the input object, each producing one scalar intensity measurement. The experimental system operates at a visible wavelength; the specific wavelength is not provided. With the optical encoder fixed, decoder-only neural adaptation is used for experimental transfer to previously unseen data distributions.

Result: The system reconstructs quantitative phase images of unknown objects with approximately 40-fold dimensional compression, using only 2.55% as many intensity measurements as reconstructed pixels. Experimental blind testing achieves mean Pearson correlation coefficients of 0.945 on handwritten digits and 0.848 on grating-like phase objects. Under the same measurement budget, benchmarking shows substantially higher reconstruction fidelity than other encoding strategies. Decoder-only neural adaptation enables transfer to previously unseen data distributions.

Conclusion: Learned optical compression is established as a measurement-efficient and reusable framework for QPI, integrating phase-information compression into single-pixel intensity measurements while retaining adaptability to new data. The approach is positioned for low-data-rate phase imaging and sensing and may help extend QPI to spectral regions where high-quality detector arrays are costly or unavailable.

Abstract: Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) reveals optical path-length variations in transparent objects but typically relies on spatially resolved intensity measurements. Here we numerically and experimentally demonstrate learned optical compression for single-pixel QPI, jointly optimizing a diffractive encoder and a digital neural decoder to concentrate reconstruction-relevant phase information into a compact measurement vector. A single reconfigurable phase-modulation plane sequentially displays the learned encoding patterns, which are independent of the input object, each yielding one scalar intensity measurement. Operating at a visible wavelength, the experimental system reconstructs quantitative phase images of unknown objects with ~40-fold dimensional compression, using only 2.55% as many intensity measurements as reconstructed pixels. Experimental blind testing on handwritten digits and grating-like phase objects achieves mean Pearson correlation coefficients of 0.945 and 0.848, respectively. Benchmarking under the same measurement budget demonstrates substantially higher reconstruction fidelity than other encoding strategies, highlighting the advantages of learned optical compression. With the optical encoder fixed, decoder-only neural adaptation enables experimental transfer to previously unseen data distributions, extending the utility of the learned measurement representation. These results establish learned optical compression as a measurement-efficient and reusable framework for QPI, integrating phase-information compression into single-pixel intensity measurements while retaining adaptability to new data. This approach paves the way for low-data-rate phase imaging and sensing, with the potential to extend QPI to spectral regions where high-quality detector arrays are costly or unavailable.

</details>


### [108] [PETE: A Hybrid Photonic and Electronic Thermoelectric Device for Direct Heat-to-Electricity Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01644)
*Yasmine Ben Mechlia, Shuo Chen, Bo Zhao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes PETE, a hybrid solid-state architecture that replaces passive spacers in a near-field photonic heat engine with thermoelectric generator legs, thereby maintaining the nanoscale vacuum gap while converting parasitic conducted heat into electricity. Using an ITO emitter, an InAs photovoltaic cell, and Half-Heusler thermoelectrics, it reports 18.6 W/cm^2 at 19.76% efficiency at 977 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photonic heat engines can have high conversion efficiencies, but far-field power density is limited by the blackbody limit. Near-field devices overcome this through photon tunneling across a nanoscale vacuum gap, but the mechanical spacers needed to maintain the gap introduce parasitic heat conduction.

Method: The authors propose PETE (Photonic and Electronic Thermoelectric), a hybrid solid-state architecture in which thermoelectric generator legs replace passive spacers, simultaneously maintaining the near-field gap and converting conducted heat into electricity. They evaluate it using an indium tin oxide emitter, an indium arsenide photovoltaic cell, and TaFeSb- and ZrNiSn-based Half-Heusler thermoelectric materials.

Result: At a hot-side temperature of 977 K, PETE achieves 18.6 W/cm^2 power density at 19.76% efficiency. This power density is approximately twice that of either the standalone near-field thermophotovoltaic device or the standalone TEG, and efficiency is 44% higher than that of the standalone TEG. Performance can be tuned through thermoelectric geometry and material selection, with adaptability demonstrated for a broad range of thermoelectric materials and operating temperatures.

Conclusion: PETE provides a general strategy for recovering spacer-mediated heat leakage in near-field photonic heat engines. The abstract does not provide further conclusions beyond this.

Abstract: Photonic heat engines can achieve high conversion efficiencies, but their far-field power density is constrained by the blackbody limit. Near-field devices overcome this limitation through photon tunneling across a nanoscale vacuum gap; however, the mechanical spacers required to maintain this gap introduce parasitic heat conduction. We propose PETE (Photonic and Electronic Thermoelectric), a hybrid solid-state architecture in which thermoelectric generator (TEG) legs replace passive spacers. The legs simultaneously maintain the near-field gap and convert conducted heat into electricity. We evaluate PETE using an indium tin oxide emitter, an indium arsenide photovoltaic cell, and TaFeSb- and ZrNiSn-based Half-Heusler thermoelectric materials. At a hot-side temperature of 977K, the device achieves a power density of 18.6 W$\cdot$cm$^{-2}$ at 19.76% efficiency. This power density is approximately twice that of either the standalone near-field thermophotovoltaic device or the standalone TEG, while the efficiency is 44% higher than that of the standalone TEG. The performance can be tuned through thermoelectric geometry and material selection, and its adaptability is demonstrated for a broad range of thermoelectric materials and operating temperatures. PETE provides a general strategy for recovering spacer-mediated heat leakage in near-field photonic heat engines.

</details>


### [109] [Relaxation characteristics and spectral compression in the system of coupled radiating oscillators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01206)
*Henrik Parsamyan, Hasmik Shahinyan, Khachatur Nerkararyan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper studies how two identical coupled oscillators respond to an ultrashort electromagnetic pulse, showing that interference in their emitted radiation produces dipole dynamics unlike an isolated oscillator and can enable efficient conversion of pulse energy into quasi-monochromatic radiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate the electromagnetic response of two identical coupled oscillators to an ultrashort pulse because interference between the radiation emitted by the constituent oscillators makes the system's dipole-radiation dynamics nontrivial and markedly different from those of an isolated oscillator.

Method: The analysis uses coupled-mode theory to describe the coupled radiating-oscillator system, supported by qualitative numerical simulations of thin conductive rods resonant in the terahertz frequency range and separated by a deeply subwavelength distance.

Result: The temporal dynamics of the system's dipole radiation are governed by a nontrivial relationship between the dipole moment amplitudes and differ markedly from those of an isolated oscillator. Under appropriate conditions, the energy of an incident ultrashort pulse can be converted into quasi-monochromatic radiation with high efficiency, and the numerical simulations support the theoretical results.

Conclusion: Interference between radiation from two identical coupled oscillators qualitatively changes their pulse-driven dynamics and can enable efficient quasi-monochromatic radiation generation; thin conductive rods resonant in the terahertz range and separated by a deeply subwavelength distance provide a physical realization of the coupled radiating-oscillator system.

Abstract: The electromagnetic response of two identical coupled oscillators to an ultrashort pulse is investigated. Due to interference between the radiation emitted by the constituent oscillators, the temporal dynamics of the dipole radiation from the system are governed by a nontrivial relationship between their dipole moment amplitudes and differ markedly from those of an isolated oscillator. An analysis based on coupled-mode theory demonstrates that, under appropriate conditions, the energy of an incident ultrashort pulse can be converted into quasi-monochromatic radiation with high efficiency. The theoretical results are supported by qualitative numerical simulations of thin conductive rods resonant in the terahertz frequency range and separated by a deeply subwavelength distance, providing a physical realization of the coupled radiating-oscillator system.

</details>


### [110] [Optical Magnetic-Field Gating of Photon Avalanching Quantum Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01179)
*Benoît Reynier, Filippo Calavaro, Natalie Fardian-Melamed, Eric Charron, Bleuenn Belz, Maria Sanz-Paz, Artiom Skripka, Sébastien Bidault, Emory M. Chan, P. James Schuck, Mathieu Mivelle*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that the magnetic component of light can gate photon avalanching in lanthanide-doped nanoparticles, enabling widely tunable threshold and nonlinearity and improved subwavelength imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Highly nonlinear photon avalanching is useful but its nonlinearity is typically fixed by material composition, limiting adaptive sensing, imaging, and neuromorphic applications; a practical tuning knob is therefore needed.

Method: The authors exploit quantum selection rules in lanthanide-doped nanoparticles: avalanche energy-looping cycles of excited-state absorption and cross relaxation are primarily electrically driven, while ground-state absorption seeding is a mixed transition allowing magnetic dipole interaction. They position emitters in an optical standing wave to spatially partition electric and magnetic fields and tune the local magnetic-to-electric balance, and they use tightly focused cylindrical vector beams to sculpt nonlinearity in a single nanocluster.

Result: The authors report giant optical magnetic-field-gated threshold and nonlinearity, with the nonlinearity order tuned from s = 15 to s = 62, and achieve subwavelength super-resolution imaging of a single nanocluster that substantially surpasses conventional photon avalanche imaging under Gaussian illumination.

Conclusion: The magnetic component of light is established as a practical knob for actively programming nonlinear optical dynamics, subdiffraction nanoscopy, and critical quantum dynamics at the nanoscale.

Abstract: Highly nonlinear phenomena, where macroscopic changes are triggered by microscopic perturbations, are ubiquitous in complex systems ranging from epidemic spreading to neural networks. In optics, photon avalanching represents a prime manifestation of such highly nonlinear behavior, characterized by a steep nonlinear response (s) following an avalanche threshold. However, this nonlinearity is typically determined by material composition, making this behavior challenging to tune for adaptive sensing, imaging, and neuromorphic applications. Here, we demonstrate giant optical magnetic field gated threshold and nonlinearity in lanthanide doped nanoparticles. We exploit the distinct quantum selection rules of the avalanche process: while the energy looping cycles of excited state absorption and cross relaxation are primarily electrically driven, the seed excitation, ground state absorption, is a mixed transition permitting magnetic dipole interaction. By positioning the emitters within an optical standing wave to spatially partition the electromagnetic field, we tune the local magnetic to electric field balance to selectively regulate the seeding and amplification probabilities. This allows us to demonstrate an unprecedented dynamic range of control over the power threshold and nonlinearity, tuning the order from s = 15 to s = 62. Furthermore, we harness this spatial separation of electric and magnetic fields using tightly focused cylindrical vector beams to locally sculpt the nonlinearity of a single nanocluster, achieving sub wavelength super resolution imaging that substantially surpasses conventional photon avalanche imaging under Gaussian illumination. These findings establish the magnetic component of light as a practical knob to actively program nonlinear optical dynamics, sub diffraction nanoscopy, and critical quantum dynamics at the nanoscale.

</details>


### [111] [Nonlinear Dynamics in Periodically Distributed Fibre Amplification Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01159)
*Sunday O. Atuba, Boris A. Malomed*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper analytically studies optical fibre systems with periodically distributed gain and longitudinally varying nonlinearity, especially exponential growth or decay of the nonlinear coefficient with constant group-velocity dispersion. Using a known integrability condition, it derives exact field solutions, periodic wavetrains, and bright and dark soliton limits, then examines their robustness and structural stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by practical applications in highly efficient nonlinear devices, including broadband light sources and advanced amplification schemes, where precise management of the interplay among group-velocity dispersion, nonlinearity, and gain is essential. The abstract does not provide specific device implementations or numerical targets.

Method: The authors consider periodically distributed amplification systems in optical fibres with longitudinally varying nonlinearity, focusing on exponential growth or decay of the nonlinear coefficient while group-velocity dispersion is held constant. Adopting a known generic integrability condition, they derive exact analytical expressions for optical-field evolution under tailored nonlinearity profiles and periodic gain. They obtain periodic wavetrain solutions and their long-wave bright and dark soliton limits, and assess robustness by introducing finite perturbations and directly evaluating effects on intensity profiles; structural stability against deviations from the integrability condition is also examined.

Result: Exact analytical expressions are derived for optical-field evolution with tailored nonlinearity profiles and periodic gain. The solutions include periodic wavetrains and bright and dark solitons in the long-wave limit, providing insight into controlled enhancement or suppression of nonlinear effects under amplification conditions. Finite-perturbation effects on intensity profiles are evaluated, and structural stability against deviations from the integrability condition is demonstrated. The abstract does not report quantitative stability metrics or specific parameter values.

Conclusion: The study contributes to understanding nonlinear pulse dynamics in engineered gain-managed fibres and offers guidance for developing novel optical systems that exploit the spatial distribution of nonlinearity to optimise performance. The abstract does not specify particular applications or experimental validation.

Abstract: We investigate effects of longitudinally varying nonlinearity on periodically distributed amplification systems in optical fibres. Emphasis is made on configurations in which the nonlinear coefficient exponentially grows or decays along the propagation distance, while the group-velocity-dispersion (GVD) coefficient is held constant. Such systems are of practical relevance to the design of highly efficient nonlinear devices, including broadband light sources and advanced amplification schemes, where precise management of the interplay between the GVD, nonlinearity, and gain is essential. Adopting the known generic integrability condition, we derive exact analytical expressions for the evolution of the optical field in the presence of the tailored nonlinearity profiles and periodically distributed gain. The resulting periodic wavetrain solutions and their long-wave limit in the form of bright and dark solitons provide insight into the controlled enhancement or suppression of nonlinear effects under realistic amplification conditions. The robustness of these solutions is examined by introducing finite perturbations and directly evaluating their effects on the corresponding intensity profiles. The structural stability of solutions against deviations from the integrability condition is demonstrated too. Outcomes of this study contribute to a deeper understanding of the nonlinear pulse dynamics in engineered gain-managed fibres and offer guidance for the development of novel optical systems, where the spatial distribution of the nonlinearity is exploited to optimise the performance.

</details>


### [112] [Chiral Unidirectional Resonances Enabled by Complete Coherent Radiation Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01080)
*Keren Wang, Wei Wang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a unified framework for controlling polarization and directionality of optical resonances, introduces chiral unidirectional resonances (CURs) with arbitrarily high radiative quality factors and near-unity single-channel selectivity, extends the theory to design prescribed coherent emission states and radiative lifetimes, and derives scaling laws and robust finite-momentum chiral unidirectional emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optical resonances are commonly characterized by quality factors, but their radiation also exhibits polarization and directional selectivity. Chiral and unidirectional guided resonances exploit complementary radiation properties, motivating a unified framework for controlling polarization and directionality together.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework and introduce chiral unidirectional resonances (CURs). They extend the theory to guide geometric design of arbitrary coherent emission states with prescribed radiative lifetimes, establish scaling laws for lifetime and radiation-state fidelity, and demonstrate robust chiral unidirectional emission over a finite momentum region.

Result: CURs are reported to have arbitrarily high radiative quality factors and near-unity single-channel selectivity. The extended theory enables design of coherent emission states with prescribed radiative lifetimes, scaling laws are established, and robust chiral unidirectional emission is demonstrated over a finite momentum region. The abstract does not provide specific quantitative values or experimental platform details.

Conclusion: The work provides a unified framework linking radiative quality factor, polarization, and directionality, with CURs and associated design and scaling laws offering a basis for single-sided circularly polarized light sources and polarization-matched optical coupling. Specific experimental validation and quantitative parameters are not detailed in the abstract.

Abstract: Optical resonances are commonly characterized by their quality factors, while their radiation exhibits richer properties associated with polarization and directional selectivity. Chiral and unidirectional guided resonances exploit complementary aspects of radiation, motivating a unified framework for controlling polarization and directionality together. Here we develop such a framework and introduce chiral unidirectional resonances (CURs) with arbitrarily high radiative quality factors and near-unity single-channel selectivity. We further extend this theory to guide the geometric design of arbitrary coherent emission states with prescribed radiative lifetimes. In addition, we establish scaling laws for the lifetime and radiation-state fidelity, and demonstrate robust chiral unidirectional emission over a finite momentum region. Our demonstrations provide a basis for single-sided circularly polarized light sources and polarization-matched optical coupling.

</details>


### [113] [Quantitative Analytical Spheroid Model for Scattering-Type Scanning Near-Field Optical Spectroscopy of Layered Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01057)
*Kirill V. Voronin, Edoardo Vicentini, Lars Mester, Rainer Hillenbrand, Alexey Y. Nikitin*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper generalizes an exact analytical prolate spheroid model for scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy from homogeneous bulk samples to multilayer samples using generalized image charge and Fourier integral methods, and validates it numerically and experimentally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Theoretical modeling of s-SNOM typically relies on computationally intensive numerical simulations or phenomenological analytical models, which limits quantitative spectral interpretation; the authors aim to extend their exact analytical homogeneous-sample framework to multilayer samples.

Method: The authors generalize the exact quasi-electrostatic prolate spheroid model of s-SNOM to multilayer samples using two equivalent approaches: a generalized image charge method and a Fourier integral method.

Result: The resulting exact spheroid model reproduces finite-element simulations within numerical precision while retaining analytical computational efficiency. It is experimentally validated for poly(methyl methacrylate) layers of different thicknesses, where a single set of geometrical parameters simultaneously reproduces multiple demodulation harmonics and all measured thicknesses, unlike the finite dipole model. It is further applied to strongly resonant SiC layers on dielectric and photoexcited InAs substrates, where existing analytical approaches fail.

Conclusion: The model provides an efficient quantitative framework for interpreting multilayer s-SNOM spectra and extracting local optical properties.

Abstract: Scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy (s-SNOM) is a versatile tool for probing optical properties at deeply subwavelength scales. Its theoretical modeling, however, typically relies on computationally intensive numerical simulations or phenomenological analytical models, limiting quantitative spectral interpretation. In our previous work, we introduced an exact analytical solution for the quasi-electrostatic prolate spheroid model of s-SNOM for homogeneous bulk samples, combining the accuracy of numerical simulations with the efficiency of analytical approaches. Here, we generalize this framework to multilayer samples using two equivalent methods: a generalized image charge method and a Fourier integral method. The resulting exact spheroid model reproduces finite-element simulations within numerical precision while retaining the computational efficiency of an analytical model. We validate the model experimentally for poly (methyl methacrylate) layers of different thicknesses, showing that a single set of geometrical parameters simultaneously reproduces multiple demodulation harmonics and all measured thicknesses, unlike the finite dipole model. We further demonstrate its applicability to strongly resonant SiC layers on dielectric and photoexcited InAs substrates, where existing analytical approaches fail. The model provides an efficient quantitative framework for interpreting multilayer s-SNOM spectra and extracting local optical properties.

</details>


### [114] [Unlocking Low-Frequency Laser Doppler Holography Spectra for Functional Retinal and Choroidal Microvascular Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01052)
*Zacharie Auray, Adrien Gordon, Gabriel Cellier, Yann Fischer, Michael Atlan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The abstract describes a method to construct a time-varying spectral-excess distribution from laser Doppler holography recordings in capillary-associated retinal and choroidal regions, summarized by a spectral excess index; a representative recording illustrates the construction, while physiological specificity and repeatability remain unquantified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-frequency components of laser Doppler holography spectra recorded at 37 kHz are typically filtered out to reveal functional blood-flow waveforms in major retinal and choroidal vessels. The paper aims to use these low-frequency components to construct a time-varying spectral-excess distribution in capillary-associated territories and provide a basis for exploring its temporal organization.

Method: Complete cardiac cycles are selected, and successive spatial and beat medians are applied in regions where vessels are unresolved and intermittent eye motion can dominate the signal. At each cardiac phase, spectral excess compares the integrated Doppler spectral area between two fixed low-frequency peaks near ±2 kHz with a reference area from the high-frequency tail near ±18.5 kHz. Spectral-excess strength is summarized by the spectral excess index, defined as the temporal median of spectral excess, with an alternative evaluation after spectral pooling also described.

Result: The resulting waveform, displayed against normalized cardiac phase, retains variation throughout the cardiac cycle. A representative recording illustrates the construction of the spectral-excess distribution. Physiological specificity and repeatability are not quantified in the abstract.

Conclusion: The constructed spectral-excess waveform provides a basis for exploring the temporal organization of capillary-associated signals. Physiological specificity and repeatability remain to be quantified.

Abstract: The low-frequency part of optical fluctuation spectra recorded by laser Doppler holography at a frame rate of 37~kHz is typically filtered out to reveal functional blood-flow waveforms in major retinal and choroidal vessels. We construct a time-varying spectral-excess (SE) distribution in capillary-associated territories by selecting complete cardiac cycles and applying successive spatial and beat medians in regions where vessels are unresolved and intermittent eye motion can dominate the signal. At each phase, spectral excess compares the integrated Doppler spectral area between two fixed low-frequency peaks near $\pm2$~kHz with a reference area defined by the high-frequency tail near $\pm18.5$~kHz. The resulting waveform, displayed against normalized cardiac phase, retains variation throughout the cardiac cycle and provides a basis for exploring its temporal organization. We summarize spectral-excess strength by the spectral excess index (SEI), defined as the temporal median of SE, and also describe an alternative evaluation after spectral pooling. A representative recording illustrates the construction. Physiological specificity and repeatability remain to be quantified.

</details>


### [115] [Semi-classical analysis of noise squeezing in Kerr microresonators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00805)
*Sara Persia, Lev Fandeev, Fuchuan Lei, Bastian Ruhnke, Alexander E. Ulanov, Vaishali Adya, Victor Torres-Company, Tobias Herr*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Semi-classical Lugiato-Lefever and Ikeda-map models with properly included vacuum fluctuations predict quadrature-noise properties of squeezed light in three canonical Kerr-microresonator configurations, closely matching quantum theory and providing practical design tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kerr microresonators are promising platforms for squeezed light sources needed in quantum sensing, communication, and computing, but designing them requires efficient numerical tools that include realistic device physics and noise processes. The paper examines whether established semi-classical frameworks used for classical frequency combs can also predict squeezing.

Method: The authors incorporate vacuum fluctuations into semi-classical frameworks based on the Lugiato-Lefever equation and the Ikeda map. They simulate three canonical configurations producing two-mode quadrature squeezing, single-mode quadrature squeezing, and intensity-difference squeezing, then compare the resulting quadrature variances and noise spectra with quantum theoretical predictions.

Result: In all three configurations, the semi-classical simulations produce quadrature variances and noise spectra in close agreement with quantum theory. The results show that key experimental signatures of squeezing can be modeled as phase-sensitive amplification of properly normalized vacuum fluctuations within stochastic semi-classical field equations.

Conclusion: The Lugiato-Lefever and Ikeda-map frameworks provide practical tools for designing squeezed light sources and can accommodate additional noise mechanisms, nonlinear effects, and complex resonator arrangements. The work also helps distinguish squeezing properties accessible through semi-classical field evolution from those intrinsically requiring a full quantum description.

Abstract: Kerr microresonators are integrated nonlinear devices widely used to generate classical optical frequency combs and nonclassical states of light. In particular, they provide a promising platform for squeezed light sources, which are important elements of emerging quantum sensing, communication, and computing technologies. Designing such sources requires numerical tools that efficiently incorporate realistic device physics and noise processes. Here, we show that established semi-classical frameworks based on the Lugiato-Lefever equation and the Ikeda map, instrumental in the development of classical light sources, can also predict the quadrature-noise properties of squeezed light when vacuum fluctuations are properly included. We consider three canonical configurations producing two-mode quadrature squeezing, single-mode quadrature squeezing, and intensity difference squeezing. In each case, the semi-classical simulations produce quadrature variances and noise spectra in close agreement with the quantum theoretical predictions. These frameworks therefore provide practical tools for designing squeezed light sources, with the capability to incorporate additional noise mechanisms, nonlinear effects, and complex resonator arrangements. More fundamentally, our results show that key experimental signatures of squeezing can be modeled as phase-sensitive amplification of properly normalized vacuum fluctuations within stochastic semi-classical field equations. This observation helps distinguish squeezing properties accessible through semi-classical field evolution from those intrinsically requiring a full quantum description.

</details>


### [116] [Energy-Centroid Trembling near Exceptional Points in Photonic Time Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00743)
*Kyungmin Lee, Younsung Kim, Minwook Kyung, Seungmin Yeon, Bumki Min*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The abstract analyzes a modulated photonic time crystal with a Lorentz restoring coefficient, showing that intracycle energy exchange, loss, mode geometry near a gap-edge exceptional point, and pulse preparation control energy-centroid trembling beyond standard band-slope and Floquet Petermann descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation. It frames the study around understanding how modulation-driven potential-energy reweighting, loss-driven kinetic-energy drain, mode geometry near a gap-edge exceptional point, and pulse preparation affect energy-centroid motion and its relation to Poynting-flux velocity.

Method: The paper theoretically examines a photonic time crystal whose Lorentz restoring coefficient is modulated.
