It analyzes offsets between polarization potential-energy reweighting and loss-induced kinetic-energy drain within a pulse, energy-centroid trembling versus bounded Poynting-flux velocity, mode geometry near a gap-edge exceptional point, a narrow-band pulse on one passband Floquet branch, and loss modulated in phase or antiphase with the restoring coefficient. It also studies a lossy example with a forward field pulse launched with polarization at rest.

Result: For a narrow-band pulse on one passband Floquet branch, the trembling-velocity amplitude generically grows as the inverse square root of the momentum detuning, rounded by finite bandwidth. Loss modulated in phase or antiphase with the restoring coefficient can change the trembling coefficient while band-slope and Petermann coefficients barely move. In the lossy example, the forward pulse exhibits delayed bursts near the beat recurrence after substantial energy loss, with monochromatic amplitudes following the inverse three-halves power of the detuning over the computed range.

Conclusion: Intracycle energy exchange and preparation shape the energy-centroid motion beyond what the band slope and the Floquet Petermann factor determine. The abstract does not provide experimental validation or detailed numerical parameters.

Abstract: In a photonic time crystal with a modulated Lorentz restoring coefficient, modulation reweights the potential energy of the polarization and loss drains its kinetic energy; when these sectors are offset within a pulse, the energy centroid trembles in every cycle while the Poynting-flux velocity stays bounded. Near the gap-edge exceptional point the mode geometry amplifies these offsets, and for a narrow-band pulse on one passband Floquet branch the trembling-velocity amplitude generically grows as the inverse square root of the momentum detuning, rounded by finite bandwidth. Loss modulated in phase or in antiphase with the restoring coefficient can change the trembling coefficient while the band-slope and Petermann coefficients barely move. In the lossy example studied, a forward field pulse launched with the polarization at rest exhibits delayed bursts near the beat recurrence after substantial energy loss, with monochromatic amplitudes following the inverse three-halves power of the detuning over the computed range. Intracycle energy exchange and preparation thus shape the centroid motion beyond what the band slope and the Floquet Petermann factor determine.

</details>


### [117] [Non-Hermitian Theory of Surface Emitting Jackiw-Rebbi States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00674)
*Hangkai Fan, Mingzhao Song, Andrey Bogdanov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a non-Hermitian Dirac formalism for surface-emitting Jackiw-Rebbi interface states in dielectric metagratings, showing that spatially varying the lattice-perturbation amplitude tunes complex Dirac mass, quality factors, and far-field radiation patterns while preserving topological character. A graded superlattice perturbation in a second-order Brillouin-zone-folded metagrating yields interface states with quality factors above 10^4 and Hermite-Gaussian beam emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological interface states in open photonic systems provide spatial localization at domain walls and temporal confinement described by a radiative quality factor. The work aims to control their quality factors and far-field radiation patterns while preserving their topological character, and to enable high-Q topological surface emitters and topological beam shaping.

Method: The authors formulate a non-Hermitian Dirac model for surface-emitting Jackiw-Rebbi states in dielectric metagratings. They vary the local amplitude of the lattice perturbation to shape the complex Dirac mass profile. They apply the framework to a metagrating with second-order Brillouin-zone folding and design a spatially graded superlattice perturbation.

Result: Varying the local lattice-perturbation amplitude controls the quality factors and far-field radiation patterns of the interface states while preserving their topological character. The designed graded superlattice perturbation supports interface states with quality factors exceeding 10^4 and Hermite-Gaussian beam emission.

Conclusion: Superlattice perturbations are established as a strategy for topological beam shaping and provide a route toward high-Q topological surface emitters.

Abstract: Topological interface states in open photonic systems combine spatial localization at domain walls with temporal confinement characterized by a radiative quality factor. Here, we develop a non-Hermitian Dirac formalism for surface-emitting Jackiw-Rebbi states in dielectric metagratings. We show how varying the local amplitude of the lattice perturbation shapes the complex Dirac mass profile, enabling control over the quality factors and far-field radiation patterns of the interface states, while preserving their topological character. Applying this framework to a metagrating with second-order Brillouin-zone folding, we design a spatially graded superlattice perturbation that supports interface states with quality factors exceeding 10$^4$ and Hermite-Gaussian beam emission. These results establish superlattice perturbations as a strategy for topological beam shaping and offer a route toward high-Q topological surface emitters.

</details>


### [118] [Experimental demonstration of response-enhanced fiber Sagnac gyroscopy via imaginary weak-value amplification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00481)
*Jing-Hui Huang, Hong-Liang Cui, Tianying Chang, Jing Zhang, TianYe Huang, Xiang-Yun Hu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A 100-m fiber Sagnac gyroscope was experimentally demonstrated using imaginary weak-value amplification to enhance the optical readout of rotation. Polarization post-selection converts a Sagnac-induced polarization phase into a spectral response, with response increasing toward orthogonal post-selection and reaching about 13 times the reference configuration. Near-orthogonal post-selection reduced the minimum Allan deviation from 0.06 rad/s to 0.0091 rad/s, about a 6.6-fold improvement, while superluminescent-diode low-frequency fluctuations limited long-term stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to enhance the optical readout response to rotation in a fiber Sagnac gyroscope and thereby support precision rotation sensing using imaginary weak-value amplification.

Method: The authors used a 100-m fiber Sagnac gyroscope with imaginary weak-value amplification. A Sagnac-induced polarization phase was converted into a measurable spectral response through polarization post-selection and spectrally resolved detection. The post-selection angle was varied to characterize the gyroscope response over the experimentally accessible rotation-rate range; the specific range is not provided in the abstract. Fast Fourier transform and Allan-deviation measurements were used to characterize noise and stability.

Result: The gyroscope response increased systematically toward orthogonal post-selection, with a maximum experimental enhancement of approximately 13 times relative to a fixed reference configuration without post-selection. Near-orthogonal post-selection reduced equivalent rotation-rate fluctuations: the minimum Allan deviation decreased from 0.06 rad/s for the reference configuration to 0.0091 rad/s for the maximum-response-enhancement configuration, an approximately 6.6-fold reduction. Low-frequency fluctuations of the broadband superluminescent diode source limited long-term stability.

Conclusion: Imaginary weak-value amplification provides a practical approach for response-enhanced fiber-based Sagnac gyroscopy and precision rotation sensing.

Abstract: We experimentally demonstrate a 100-m long fiber Sagnac gyroscope employing imaginary weak-value amplification (IWVA) to enhance the optical readout response to rotation. A Sagnac-induced polarization phase is converted into a measurable spectral response through polarization post-selection and spectrally resolved detection. By varying the post-selection angle, we characterize the gyroscope response over the experimentally accessible rotation-rate range. The response increases systematically toward orthogonal post-selection, yielding a maximum experimental enhancement of approximately 13 times relative to a fixed reference configuration without postselection. Fast Fourier transform and Allan-deviation measurements further characterize the noise and stability of the gyroscope. Near-orthogonal post-selection reduces the equivalent rotation-rate fluctuations, with the minimum Allan deviation decreasing from $0.06~\mathrm{rad/s}$ for the reference configuration to $0.0091~\mathrm{rad/s}$ for the configuration exhibiting the maximum response enhancement, corresponding to an approximately 6.6-fold reduction. Low-frequency fluctuations of the broadband superluminescent diode source limit the long-term stability. These results demonstrate that IWVA provides a practical approach for response-enhanced fiber-based Sagnac gyroscopy and precision rotation sensing.

</details>


### [119] [Nonperturbative theory of thin-vapor-layer linear optical properties: towards the optimal design of alkali-metal vapor nanocells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00400)
*Andrei V. Ermolaev, Tigran A. Vartanyan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper presents a rigorous numerical approach for calculating nonlocal optical spectra of hot alkali-metal vapor layers in nanometer-thick cells at arbitrary atomic density and thickness. It predicts a density-dependent resonance blue shift absent from first-order perturbation theory, studies reflectivity versus layer thickness, and derives an approximate expression for the thickness maximizing the first derivative of reflectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hot alkali-metal vapors in nanometer-thick spectroscopic cells are promising for miniature and low-cost frequency standards, magnetometers, and atomic sensors, but their linear optical properties at low to moderate atomic densities are not fully understood. The nonlocal optical response arises from free atomic motion and wall collisions, and prior reflection/transmission calculations were restricted to perturbation theory in atomic density for quenching atom-wall collisions, limiting practical applications.

Method: The authors introduce a rigorous numerical approach to compute thin-vapor-layer spectra with accurate treatment of the nonlocal optical response at arbitrary optical density and layer thickness. Inspired by recent experiments, they explore a wide range of vapor-layer thicknesses and analyze reflectivity and its derivative with respect to laser detuning as functions of thickness. They also derive an approximate expression for the thickness at which the first derivative of reflectivity reaches its maximum.

Result: The model predicts a density-dependent blue shift of the resonance that is absent in the first-order perturbative description. Its values are comparable to the natural linewidth of an atomic transition and therefore too large to be neglected in most use cases. The dependence of reflectivity and its laser-detuning derivative on layer thickness is studied, and the approximate expression for the thickness maximizing the first derivative agrees closely with the rigorous numerical model and reduces to the first-order analytical result. Quantitative numerical values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The results provide a practical design tool for nanometer-thick spectroscopic cells in various applications and a starting point for further development of the theory of nonlocal optical response in thin vapor layers.

Abstract: Hot alkali-metal vapors confined inside nanometer-thick spectroscopic cells are highly suitable media for miniature and cheap frequency standards, magnetometers and atomic sensors. Yet, their linear optical properties in case of low to moderate atomic densities are not fully understood. The primary reason for that is the complex, strongly nonlocal optical response of such systems to the incident resonant radiation arising as the result of the free motion of atoms and their collisions with the walls. To date, calculations of reflection and transmission spectra of these cells has been restricted to perturbation theory in atomic density for the case of quenching atom-wall collisions, which substantially limited the scope of their practical applications. Here we introduce the rigorous numerical approach for computing thin-vapor-layer spectra with an accurate account for the nonlocal optical response at arbitrary optical density and layer thickness. Our model predicts a density-dependent blue shift of the resonance that is absent in the first-order perturbative description. We show that its values are comparable to the natural linewidth of an atomic transition and, therefore, too large to be neglected in most use cases. Inspired by recent experiments, we then explore a wide range of vapor layer thicknesses and study how the reflectivity and its derivative with respect to the laser detuning depend on the layer thickness. Finally, we derive an approximate expression for the thickness at which the first derivative of the reflectivity reaches its maximum that agrees closely with the rigorous numerical model and reduces to the first-order analytical result. These results provide a practical design tool for nanometer-thick spectroscopic cells in different applications as well as a starting point for further development of the theory.

</details>


### [120] [BEEF: A Hybrid Integrated Photonic Simulation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00393)
*Jacob M. Hiesener, Stephen E. Ralph*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper presents BEEF, a Python framework that hybridizes EME and FDTD by partitioning a device into sections, simulating each with the more suitable Maxwell solver, and cascading the responses. It calibrates EME propagation constants using FDTD phase accumulation and demonstrates validation on MMI splitters, a directional coupler, Fabry-Perot circuit-level cascading, and a long 4x4 MMI-based optical hybrid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to retain first-principles electromagnetic modeling while extending simulation to devices whose length and geometric complexity exceed the practical reach of EME or FDTD used alone.

Method: BEEF partitions a device into sections, simulates sections independently with EME or FDTD according to geometry, and cascades the resulting responses. It calibrates EME propagation constants against FDTD-measured phase accumulation and provides a mode identity verification diagnostic across solver interfaces.

Result: For a 2x2 MMI splitter, the hybrid result agrees with full-device FDTD within 0.59% for bar and 0.76% for cross across the C-band; a tapered variant agrees within 0.87% and 2.01%, respectively. For a directional coupler, effective-index calibration reduces cut-position dependence 167-fold. A circuit-level cascade reproduces the measured Fabry-Perot resonance lineshape and free spectral range and explores the coupler-length and arm-length design plane by post-processing four cached simulations. BEEF also simulates a 167.3 um-long 4x4 MMI-based 90-degree optical hybrid that is computationally impractical for full-device FDTD, with a reduced-length variant showing close agreement in transmission and relative phase.

Conclusion: The hybrid EME/FDTD framework enables practical first-principles simulation of long and geometrically complex devices, with accuracy validated against full-device FDTD and measurements, support for design-space exploration via cached simulations, and applicability to devices beyond full-device FDTD feasibility.

Abstract: A Python framework is presented that retains first-principles electromagnetic modeling while extending simulation to devices whose length and geometric complexity place them beyond the practical reach of either method alone. The Bridged Engine for eigenmode expansion (EME) and finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) (BEEF) partitions a device into sections which are simulated using either EME or FDTD independently developed Maxwell solvers depending on what best suits each section's geometry, and cascades the resulting responses. BEEF calibrates the EME propagation constants against FDTD-measured phase accumulation, providing a mode identity verification diagnostic across solver interfaces. For a 2x2 multimode-interference splitter, the hybrid result agrees with full-device FDTD to within 0.59% (bar) and 0.76% (cross) across the C-band; a tapered variant agrees to within 0.87% and 2.01%, respectively. For a directional coupler, effective-index calibration reduces cut-position dependence 167-fold. A circuit-level cascade reproduces the measured Fabry-Perot resonance lineshape and free spectral range while exploring the coupler-length and arm-length design plane through post-processing of four cached simulations. Finally, we use BEEF to simulate a 167.3 um-long 4x4 MMI-based 90-degree optical hybrid that is computationally impractical for full-device FDTD, with a reduced-length variant confirming close agreement in transmission and relative phase.

</details>


### [121] [Coherent transduction and dynamical back-action between light and millimeter waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00279)
*André Garcia Primo, Jiawen Liu, Amit Vankayala, Gabriel Jülg, Aleksei Gaier, Yazan Lampert, Ileana-Cristina Benea-Chelmus*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a triply resonant millimeter-wave-to-optical transducer using coupled lithium niobate Fabry-Pérot cavities integrated with a 263 GHz gold resonator, achieving a vacuum electro-optic coupling rate of about 60 kHz, thermal millimeter-wave photon detection below one occupancy, and coherent transduction with internal efficiencies around 67%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electro-optic transduction can avoid the loss and noise of electronic frequency conversion at millimeter-wave frequencies by parametrically converting signals onto low-loss, low-noise optical carriers, with coupling rates that increase with frequency. Exploiting this scaling requires shrinking both millimeter-wave and optical resonators while preserving quality factors and spatial overlap, which had not yet been achieved.

Method: The authors demonstrate a triply resonant transducer with comparable optical and millimeter-wave resonator dimensions and minimal mode volumes. Coupled lithium niobate Fabry-Pérot cavities with free spectral ranges above 650 GHz and quality factors up to 10^6 form a photonic molecule integrated with a quarter-wave gold resonator at 263 GHz.

Result: The device reaches a vacuum electro-optic coupling rate g0/2π ≈ 60 kHz, six times larger than previous millimeter-wave transducers and sixty times larger than at microwave frequencies. It optically detects room-temperature thermal millimeter-wave photons at effective occupancies below one, which also serve as a calibration tone for transduction efficiency. Coherent transduction reaches cooperativities approaching 0.3, corresponding to internal efficiencies around 67%, with electro-optic dynamical back-action broadening or narrowing the optical resonances.

Conclusion: These results demonstrate the advantage and feasibility of frequency scaling of electro-optic coupling and establish an architecture for weak-signal sensing and quantum and classical networking.

Abstract: Electro-optic transduction can bypass the loss and noise of electronic frequency conversion at millimeter-wave frequencies by parametrically converting signals onto low-loss, low-noise optical carriers, with coupling rates that grow with frequency. Exploiting this growth, however, requires shrinking both the millimeter-wave and optical resonators while preserving their quality factors and spatial overlap, which has not yet been achieved. Here, we demonstrate a triply resonant transducer whose optical and millimeter-wave resonators have comparable dimensions and minimal mode volumes. Coupled lithium niobate Fabry-Pérot cavities, with free spectral ranges above 650 GHz and quality factors up to $10^6$, form a photonic molecule integrated with a quarter-wave gold resonator at 263 GHz. The device reaches a vacuum electro-optic coupling rate $g_0/2π\approx 60$ kHz, six times larger than previous millimeter-wave transducers and sixty times larger than at microwave frequencies. The device optically detects room-temperature thermal millimeter-wave photons at effective occupancies below one, which also serve as a calibration tone for transduction efficiency. Coherent transduction reaches cooperativities approaching 0.3, corresponding to internal efficiencies around 67%, accompanied by electro-optic dynamical back-action that broadens or narrows the optical resonances. These results demonstrate the advantage and feasibility of the frequency scaling of electro-optic coupling, establishing an architecture for weak-signal sensing and quantum and classical networking.

</details>


### [122] [Animated Ultraviolet Holography via Programmable Nonlinear Phase Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00267)
*Ze Zheng, Olga Sergaeva, Davide Rocco, Luca Carletti, Can Tao, Yucheng Zhang, Guoquan Zhang, Costantino De Angelis, Lei Xu, Anatoly V. Zayats, Mohsen Rahmani*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates programmable ultraviolet holography using virtual metasurfaces on a 600-nm-thick lithium niobate film, where an SLM-encoded pump phase is transferred to the UV via second-harmonic generation, enabling animated and reconfigurable UV holograms without UV-compatible optics or direct UV spatial modulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic and programmable UV light control is challenging because conventional optics and optoelectronic devices have high material absorption and limited functionality in the UV range.

Method: The authors use virtual metasurfaces based on programmable optical patterns on a 600-nm-thick lithium niobate film as the nonlinear medium. A spatial light modulator encodes a computer-generated phase distribution onto a longer-wavelength pump beam, and second-harmonic generation transfers the programmed spatial phase to the UV field through quadratic nonlinear interaction. The nonlinear film acts as a simple frequency-conversion interface, avoiding nanostructure patterning or direct UV spatial modulation, and the nonlinear phase relationship is incorporated into the hologram design.

Result: Six distinct UV holographic patterns were experimentally reconstructed with normalized cross-correlation values from 0.68 to 0.83. Dynamic SLM updating enabled animated UV holograms at 10 Hz, tunable SHG emission from 350 to 390 nm, and dynamic beam steering at approximately 20 Hz.

Conclusion: The results indicate the potential of virtual metasurfaces for software-defined UV holography by transferring spatial programming across optical frequencies, opening opportunities for reconfigurable short-wavelength UV beam shaping and structured illumination without UV-compatible optics or optoelectronics.

Abstract: Dynamic and programmable control of ultraviolet (UV) light remains challenging because of conventional high material absorption and the limited functionality of optics such as lenses, mirrors or filters, and optoelectronic devices, such as spatial phase modulators, over the UV range. Here, by employing the concept of virtual metasurfaces based on arrays of programmable optical patterns on a 600-nm-thick lithium niobate film as the nonlinear medium, we demonstrate nonlinear phase transfer, generating programmable holography in the UV range. This arrangement provides a unique platform for generating animated holograms. A spatial light modulator encodes a computer-generated phase distribution onto a longer-wavelength pump beam, while second-harmonic generation transfers the programmed spatial phase to the UV field through the quadratic nonlinear interaction. Importantly, the nonlinear film serves as a simple frequency-conversion interface, eliminating the need for nanostructure patterning or direct UV spatial modulation. By incorporating the nonlinear phase relationship into the hologram design, we experimentally reconstruct six distinct UV holographic patterns, with normalised cross-correlation values ranging from 0.68 to 0.83, demonstrating reliable UV holographic image reconstruction. As a result, the dynamic updating of the SLM enables animated UV holograms at 10 Hz, demonstrating reconfigurable UV holography using the same nonlinear film. The system also supports tunable SHG emission from 350 to 390 nm and dynamic beam steering at approximately 20 Hz. The results demonstrate the potential of virtual metasurfaces to realise software-defined UV holography by transferring spatial programming across optical frequencies. This opens new opportunities for reconfigurable short-wavelength UV beam shaping and structured illumination, without the need for UV-compatible optics or optoelectronics.

</details>


### [123] [Aberration stabilized light bullets in normal dispersion injected Kerr resonators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03459)
*Yannick Fleck, Thomas G. Seidel, Jesús Yelo-Sarrión, Julien Javaloyes, Svetlana V. Gurevich*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A theoretical and numerical study shows that a small negative spherical aberration stabilizes multistable two-dimensional localized states and three-dimensional light bullets in an optically injected Kerr-Gires-Tournois interferometer under normal dispersion, whereas no stable three-dimensional light bullets exist without aberration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates three-dimensional light bullets in an optically injected, vertically emitting Kerr-Gires-Tournois interferometer operating in the normal dispersion regime, and examines how lens and mirror aberrations near the self-imaging condition affect their existence and stability. The abstract does not elaborate a broader motivation beyond this investigation.

Method: In the uniform field limit, the dynamics is modeled by a generalized Lugiato-Lefever equation including third-order dispersion, paraxial diffraction, and fourth-order diffraction arising from lens and mirror aberrations close to the self-imaging condition. The analysis uses direct numerical simulations.

Result: Without aberration, only transversely drifting, tilted multi-peak localized states are stable in two dimensions, and no stable light bullets exist in three dimensions. A small negative spherical aberration stabilizes a rich multistable family of two-dimensional localized states, including straight and tilted multi-peak states, oscillating states, and complex bound states, which persist even for negative paraxial diffraction. An Andronov-Hopf bifurcation followed by a homoclinic transition toward tilted states, accompanied by hysteresis, is identified. Stable straight and tilted three-dimensional light bullets exist in the presence of aberration, with the tilted bullets forming a continuous family parametrized by the transverse tilt direction.

Conclusion: Small negative spherical aberration is the key factor enabling stable two-dimensional multistable localized states and stable straight and tilted three-dimensional light bullets in the modeled interferometer; in the absence of aberration, no stable three-dimensional light bullets are found. The abstract does not report experimental validation.

Abstract: We investigate three-dimensional light bullets (LBs) in an optically injected, vertically emitting Kerr-Gires-Tournois interferometer operating in the normal dispersion regime. In the uniform field limit, the dynamics is governed by a generalized Lugiato-Lefever equation with third-order dispersion, paraxial diffraction, and fourth-order diffraction, stemming from lens and mirror aberrations close to the self-imaging condition. Using direct numerical simulations, we show that without aberration only transversely drifting, tilted multi-peak localized states (LSs) are stable in two dimensions, whereas no stable LBs exist in three dimensions. However, a small negative spherical aberration stabilizes a rich multistable family of two-dimensional LSs, including straight and tilted multi-peak LSs, oscillating states, and complex bound states, which persist even for negative paraxial diffraction. We identify an Andronov-Hopf bifurcation followed by a homoclinic transition toward tilted states, accompanied by hysteresis. Finally, we demonstrate that stable straight and tilted LBs exist in three dimensions in the presence of aberration, the latter forming a continuous family parametrized by the transverse tilt direction.

</details>


### [124] [Coupled Soft-Clamped Polygon Resonators with Tunable Mechanical Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02442)
*Zhihao Niu, Peng Chen, Shenggen Zheng, Yuan-Yuan Zhao, Yuanyuan Zhao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A continuum model and finite-element simulations show that the shared-tether coupler geometry of two identical square resonators controls modal frequency splitting and dissipation dilution. In the short-coupler limit, the symmetric mode has vanishing coupler torsional strain and can exceed the dissipation dilution of an isolated polygon, while the antisymmetric mode retains coupler torsion and extra loss. A Q·f expression is derived, and for a 100 μm long, 2 μm wide coupler the simulations give 17.7 Hz splitting and about 13% higher symmetric-mode dilution than the analytical isolated-polygon value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand and predict how the geometry of a shared tether coupling two identical polygon resonators governs both frequency splitting and dissipation, including dissipation dilution and mechanical quality factor, in order to inform the design of coupled resonators.

Method: The authors develop a continuum model for two identical square-shaped resonators coupled by a shared tether and compare its analytical predictions with finite element simulations. The model relates coupler length and width to the frequency splitting and dissipation dilution of the two coupled modes, analyzes the short-coupler limit k_c l_c << 1, identifies loss contributions from polygon-side bending and supporting-tether torsion, and derives an expression for Q·f in the short-coupler and short-tether limits.

Result: In the short-coupler limit, the symmetric-mode coupler undergoes nearly uniform rotation and its torsional strain energy vanishes asymptotically; remaining losses come from polygon-side bending and supporting-tether torsion. The reduced supporting-tether contribution allows the symmetric-mode dissipation dilution to exceed that of an isolated polygon with the same side and supporting-tether dimensions. The antisymmetric mode retains torsional strain in the coupler and has an additional loss contribution. The derived Q·f expression gives dependence on tensile stress, film thickness, and supporting-tether geometry. For a coupler length of 100 μm and width of 2 μm, finite element calculations give a frequency splitting of 17.7 Hz and a symmetric-mode dilution factor approximately 13% above the analytical isolated-polygon value.

Conclusion: Coupler geometry controls both frequency splitting and dissipation in coupled polygon resonators. Short couplers suppress torsional strain in the symmetric mode and improve its dissipation dilution relative to an isolated polygon, whereas the antisymmetric mode retains an additional coupler-torsion loss. The model and Q·f expression provide a basis for predicting and designing the mechanical response of such coupled resonators.

Abstract: Coupler geometry controls both the frequency splitting and the dissipation of coupled polygon resonators. We develop a continuum model for two identical square-shaped resonators coupled by a shared tether and compare the analytical predictions with finite element simulations. The model relates the coupler length and width to the frequency splitting and dissipation dilution of the two coupled modes. In the short-coupler limit, $k_cl_c\ll1$, the symmetric-mode coupler undergoes nearly uniform rotation, and its torsional strain energy vanishes asymptotically. The remaining loss contributions arise from polygon-side bending and supporting-tether torsion. The reduced supporting-tether contribution allows the symmetric-mode dissipation dilution to exceed that of an isolated polygon with the same side and supporting-tether dimensions. The antisymmetric mode retains torsional strain in the coupler and has an additional loss contribution. We also derive an expression for $Q\cdot f$ in the short-coupler and short-tether limits, where $Q$ is the mechanical quality factor and $f$ is the resonance frequency. This expression gives the dependence of $Q\cdot f$ on tensile stress, film thickness, and supporting-tether geometry. For a coupler length of $100~μ\mathrm{m}$ and width of $2~μ\mathrm{m}$, finite element calculations give a frequency splitting of $17.7~\mathrm{Hz}$ and a symmetric-mode dilution factor approximately $13\%$ above the analytical isolated-polygon value.

</details>


### [125] [Nearly Transform-Limited Pulse-Like Temporal and Narrowband Spectral Responses in Sum-Frequency Generation from Bright Twin Beams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02418)
*Yujiro Eto*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The coherent component of bright twin beams was characterized simultaneously in time and frequency using spectrally resolved, delay-dependent sum-frequency generation. The measured temporal-correlation width was 0.138 ps, close to the transform-limited 0.126 ps, while the spectral-correlation width was 0.176 rad/ps and limited by spectrometer resolution; the resulting time-frequency width product was 0.0243, far below 1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation beyond the goal of simultaneously characterizing the temporal and spectral responses of the coherent component in bright twin beams.

Method: A spectrally resolved, delay-dependent sum-frequency generation (SFG) measurement was used to characterize the coherent component of bright twin beams simultaneously in time and frequency.

Result: The measured temporal-correlation width was 0.138 ps, close to the transform-limited value of 0.126 ps estimated from the measured twin-beam spectrum. The observed spectral-correlation width was 0.176 rad/ps and was limited by the spectrometer resolution. The corresponding time-frequency width product was 0.0243, far below the value of 1 for SFG between two independent transform-limited Gaussian pulses.

Conclusion: The coherent component of the bright twin beams exhibited a time-frequency width product of 0.0243, far below the benchmark of 1 for SFG between two independent transform-limited Gaussian pulses. The temporal-correlation width was close to the transform-limited value, while the spectral-correlation width was limited by spectrometer resolution.

Abstract: We simultaneously characterize the temporal and spectral responses of the coherent component in bright twin beams using a spectrally resolved, delay-dependent sum-frequency generation (SFG) measurement. The measured temporal-correlation width is 0.138 ps, close to the transform-limited value of 0.126 ps estimated from the measured twin-beam spectrum.The observed spectral-correlation width is 0.176 rad/ps and is limited by the spectrometer resolution. The corresponding time-frequency width product is 0.0243, far below the value of 1 for SFG between two independent transform-limited Gaussian pulses.

</details>


### [126] [Optical Detection of Coefficient of Thermal Expansion via Frequency Tracking at Cryogenic Temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02347)
*Yanli Shi, Hui Wu, Guangyu Li, Qun Cao, Zhe He, Zheng Cui*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper presents an in-situ, non-destructive optical method that determines the CTE of suspended nanofilms by tracking the temperature dependence of their vibrational frequency, eliminating thickness measurements and enabling measurements on amorphous films where XRD fails, with uncertainty below 5.3 × 10^-8 K^-1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate CTE characterization of nanoscale thin films is critical for predicting residual stress and ensuring reliability in micro-electromechanical systems and advanced semiconductor architectures. Traditional methods are limited because they require precise thickness measurements or are restricted to crystalline materials.

Method: The authors use an in-situ, non-destructive, non-contact optical methodology that tracks the temperature dependence of the vibrational frequency of suspended nanofilms to determine CTE. The technique requires no thickness measurements, works for amorphous films where XRD fails, and is experimentally validated over a broad temperature range on membranes in their native microstructure state without destructive patterning or complex sample preparation.

Result: The method achieves CTE uncertainty below 5.3 × 10^-8 K^-1, substantially improving upon conventional thin-film CTE metrology.

Conclusion: Tracking the temperature-dependent vibrational frequency of suspended nanofilms provides a practical in-situ, non-destructive optical route to CTE measurement that removes thickness-measurement requirements, extends applicability to amorphous films, and improves measurement uncertainty relative to conventional thin-film CTE metrology.

Abstract: Accurate characterization of the coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE) in nanoscale thin films is critical for predicting residual stress and ensuring the reliability of modern micro-electromechanical systems and advanced semiconductor architectures. The primary limitation is that traditional methods depend on precise thickness measurements or are restricted to crystalline materials. In this work, we present an in-situ, non-destructive optical methodology to determine the CTE of suspended nanofilms by tracking the temperature-dependence of their vibrational frequency. This approach eliminates thickness measurements entirely and works for amorphous films where X-ray diffraction (XRD) fails. We experimentally validate this technique across a broad temperature range, evaluating membranes in their native microstructure state without requiring additional destructive patterning or complex sample preparation. This non-contact optical method achieves uncertainty below 5.3 * 10-8 K-1, substantially improving upon conventional thin-film CTE metrology.

</details>


### [127] [Seeing Why: Exact Evidence Maps for Quantum and Optical Image Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01993)
*Teerawat Tongloy, Rangsan Jomtarak*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces Born evidence maps (BEM), a hardware-measurable way to decompose a linear quantum or all-optical classifier's class-probability margin into per-pixel amplitude contributions, and demonstrates them on simulated phase-plate classifiers and IBM quantum hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image classifiers are usually opaque, so their decisions can only be estimated retrospectively. The authors target quantum and all-optical classifiers that act linearly on amplitude-encoded images up to measurement and contain no trained classical network before or after the classifier, seeking an exact and experimentally practical explanation of why a class was chosen without gradient circuits or sending light backwards.

Method: For each class, the Born probability above its no-information share is split exactly into contributions from encoded amplitudes using known quadratic-form results; with transmitted light fixed, this coincides with Integrated Gradients from a zero baseline and the Shapley value. The resulting Born evidence maps are measured directly from class probabilities on a quantum chip or camera frames on a lens bench, requiring 1 + 2g^2 runs for a g×g map. The classifiers use a Fourier-plane mask followed by either a brickwall qubit circuit or phase plates, read by a fixed Born-headroom readout with no trained parameter; images are centred on their own mean so dark and bright features count equally.

Result: Deleting the 5% of pixels with highest evidence reduced accuracy from 73% to 9%, comparable to occlusion requiring 1,024 extra runs per image, while gradient saliency removed much less. In simulation, four phase plates at one wavelength reached 72.3% on Caltech-10 colour photographs. On ibm_kingston, with the mask applied before approximate loading, 300 test images were classified at 37.7% versus 10% chance, compared with 56.3% in exact simulation; 2×2 maps for ten correctly classified images, one per class selected by a stated rule, agreed with exact maps at r = 0.77. The maps also exposed a data shortcut, but because the trim removing it was selected on test images, the Caltech-10 results are exploratory.

Conclusion: Born evidence maps provide an exact, hardware-measurable attribution method for linear quantum and all-optical image classifiers, can match established attribution values under stated conditions, and can reveal influential pixels and dataset shortcuts. The reported hardware performance is below exact simulation, and the Caltech-10 findings are explicitly exploratory because of test-set-dependent shortcut removal.

Abstract: When an image classifier decides, we can usually only estimate why. For quantum and all-optical classifiers that act linearly on amplitude-encoded pictures up to the measurement, with no trained classical network before or after them, each class's Born probability above its no-information share, $P_g - b_g$, splits exactly into contributions of the encoded amplitudes, shown as Born evidence maps (BEM) over the picture. The split itself follows from known results on quadratic forms: with the transmitted light held fixed, it is what Integrated Gradients from a zero baseline and the Shapley value give. What we add is a way to measure it on the hardware that classifies, from class probabilities on a quantum chip or from camera frames on a lens bench, with no gradient circuit and no light sent backwards; a $g\times g$ map takes $1 + 2g^2$ runs. Deleting the 5% of pixels with the highest evidence drops accuracy from 73% to 9%, as occlusion does with 1,024 extra runs per picture, whereas gradient saliency removes much less. The classifiers share one architecture, a Fourier-plane mask followed by a brickwall circuit on qubits or by phase plates on a lens bench, read by a fixed Born headroom readout with no trained parameter; centring each picture on its own mean lets dark features count as much as bright ones. In simulation, four phase plates at a single wavelength reach 72.3% on Caltech-10 colour photographs. On IBM's ibm_kingston, with the mask applied before approximate loading, 300 test pictures were classified at 37.7% against 10% by chance (56.3% in exact simulation), and $2\times2$ maps of ten correctly classified pictures, one per class chosen by a stated rule, measured on the chip, agreed with the exact ones ($r = 0.77$). The maps also revealed a shortcut in the data; as the trim that removed it was chosen on test pictures, the Caltech-10 results are exploratory.

</details>


### [128] [Spin-induced lateral acoustic forces on chiral and achiral particles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01852)
*Yurou Jia, Shanjun Liang, Wanyue Xiao, Shubo Wang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates and experimentally validates spin-induced lateral acoustic forces on chiral and achiral particles coupled to a substrate, enabling chirality-selective transport and spin-controlled acoustic manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acoustic spin supports chiral sound-matter interactions, but its mechanical consequences have been much less explored.

Method: Under plane-wave incidence, left- and right-handed chiral particles scatter fields with opposite acoustic spin that selectively couple to counterpropagating guided waves; achiral particles are addressed using acoustic vortex-beam illumination. The spin-dependent guided-wave excitation and lateral forces are experimentally validated.

Result: Opposite acoustic spin from chiral enantiomers selectively couples to counterpropagating guided waves and produces lateral forces of opposite signs; lateral forces on achiral particles are also realized with acoustic vortex beams. Experiments confirm spin-dependent guided-wave excitation and lateral forces.

Conclusion: The results provide a route to spin-controlled acoustic manipulation and chirality-selective particle transport.

Abstract: Acoustic spin enables intriguing chiral sound-matter interactions, yet its mechanical consequences remain much less explored. Here, we demonstrate spin-induced lateral acoustic forces on chiral and achiral particles coupled to a substrate. Under plane-wave incidence, left- and right-handed chiral particles scatter fields with opposite acoustic spin that selectively couple to counterpropagating guided waves, generating lateral forces of opposite signs on the enantiomers. The lateral forces can also be realized for achiral particles by employing acoustic vortex-beam illumination. We experimentally validate the spin-dependent excitation of guided waves and lateral forces. Our results provide a route to spin-controlled acoustic manipulation and chirality-selective particle transport.

</details>


### [129] [Self-driven beta-radiation detection through a graphene/water/silicon heterostructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01681)
*Tengfei Wu, Kangchen Xiong, Minhui Yang, Xin Chen, Shisheng Lin*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a zero-bias graphene/water/silicon heterostructure detector that converts beta irradiation into transient electrical currents via changes in interfacial water polarization, achieving millisecond-scale rise and recovery times and resolving individual pulses in a 17 Hz sequence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-volume neutrino observatories such as IceCube use Cherenkov light from neutrino-induced charged particles, motivating complementary water-based detection strategies. Beta decay emits an electron and an electron antineutrino; the electron enables beta-radiation detection in this work, while the antineutrino motivates future extensions toward neutrino detection.

Method: A graphene/water/silicon heterostructure is used for electrical transduction under beta irradiation at zero applied bias. Energetic electrons deposit energy in silicon, creating nonequilibrium carriers that perturb the semiconductor surface potential and the electric displacement across the adjacent aqueous interface. The resulting redistribution of interfacial water polarization drives a transient compensation current, and carrier relaxation restores the initial polarization state, producing an opposite-polarity recovery signal. Water-layer thickness and ionic composition are varied, and integration with Ce:YAG is used to introduce a scintillation-mediated excitation pathway.

Result: The irradiation-detector-electrometer system shows exponential rise and recovery time constants of approximately 1.0 ms and 3.6 ms, respectively. The response is strongly regulated by water-layer thickness and ionic composition, and all 17 beam pulses of a programmed 1 s sequence at 17 Hz are resolved individually. Ce:YAG integration introduces a scintillation-mediated excitation pathway.

Conclusion: Interfacial water polarization is established as a tunable, zero-bias electrical transduction mechanism for time-resolved beta-radiation monitoring, and the findings motivate future investigations of higher-energy charged particles and secondary particles relevant to neutrino detection.

Abstract: Large-volume neutrino observatories, exemplified by IceCube, exploit Cherenkov light generated by neutrino-induced charged particles in Antarctic ice, highlighting the potential of water-based media for high-energy particle detection. In $β$-decay, a neutron converts into a proton via the weak interaction, emitting an electron and an electron antineutrino. The electron constitutes the beta radiation detected in this work, while the accompanying antineutrino motivates future extensions toward neutrino detection. Here we demonstrate a complementary electrical transduction strategy using a graphene/water/silicon heterostructure that generates reversible current transients under beta irradiation at zero applied bias. Energetic electrons deposit energy in silicon, producing nonequilibrium carriers that perturb the semiconductor surface potential and electric displacement across the adjacent aqueous interface. The resulting redistribution of interfacial water polarization drives a transient compensation current, whereas carrier relaxation restores the initial polarization state and generates an opposite-polarity recovery signal. The complete irradiation-detector-electrometer system exhibits exponential rise and recovery time constants of approximately 1.0 and 3.6 ms, respectively. The response is strongly regulated by water-layer thickness and ionic composition, and the 17 beam pulses of a programmed 1 s sequence at 17 Hz are resolved individually. Integration with Ce:YAG further introduces a scintillation-mediated excitation pathway. These findings establish interfacial water polarization as a tunable, zero-bias electrical transduction mechanism for time-resolved beta-radiation monitoring and motivate future investigations of higher-energy charged particles and secondary particles relevant to neutrino detection.

</details>


### [130] [From Rock Tumbling to Telescope Mirror Grinding: How Optical Perfection Emerges from Random Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01673)
*Emmanuel Schaan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper derives a first-principles explanation, starting from Preston's law, for how two hand-rubbed glass disks with abrasive spontaneously converge to nearly perfect matching spheres. Symmetries make planes and spheres generic equilibria, and a hierarchy of grinding, spherizing, and deformation timescales governs their stability. The framework also unifies telescope mirror grinding with rock tumbling and sanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to explain how optical precision—surfaces matching spherical curves to tens of nanometers—emerges from an essentially random manual grinding process, and to show that the same framework can unify telescope mirror grinding with everyday phenomena such as rock tumbling and sanding. The abstract also presents these examples as an accessible classroom context for stable and unstable equilibria, exponential relaxation, diffusion, multi-timescale analysis, and the emergence of the Laplacian operator from local averaging.

Method: Starting from Preston's law of material removal, the authors derive a first-principles stability analysis. They use symmetries of the grinding stroke to identify generic equilibria and analyze stability through a hierarchy of timescales. They compare the grinding timescale for small-scale irregularities, the longer spherizing timescale for large-scale deviations, and a deformation timescale associated with gravity and finite disk size.

Result: The analysis finds that planes and spheres are generic equilibria of the process. Small-scale irregularities decay exponentially on the grinding timescale T_grind, while large-scale deviations from a sphere diffuse away on the longer spherizing timescale T_sphere proportional to (R_mirror/R_stroke)^2 T_grind. Gravity and finite disk size destabilize planar surfaces, but for sufficiently short strokes spheres remain stable when T_deform is much greater than T_sphere, with only the radius of curvature varying adiabatically. The framework also explains why rotary tumbling rounds edges more than vibratory tumbling, why neither produces spheres, and why small flat surfaces inevitably round during sanding.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the spontaneous spherical convergence of hand-ground glass disks can be explained from Preston's law plus symmetry and multiscale stability analysis. The same framework unifies telescope mirror grinding with rock tumbling and sanding and offers an accessible classroom context for stable and unstable equilibria, exponential relaxation, diffusion, multi-timescale analysis, and the Laplacian operator emerging from averaging over a local area.

Abstract: When two glass disks are rubbed together by hand with an abrasive between them, their surfaces spontaneously converge to perfectly matching spherical curves, accurate to tens of nanometers. How does such optical precision emerge from an essentially random manual process? Starting from Preston's law of material removal, we derive a first-principles explanation. Symmetries of the grinding stroke imply that planes and spheres are the generic equilibria of the process. Their stability is governed by a hierarchy of timescales: small-scale irregularities decay exponentially on the grinding timescale $T_\text{grind}$, while large-scale deviations from a sphere diffuse away on the longer spherizing timescale $T_\text{sphere} \propto (R_\text{mirror}/R_\text{stroke})^2\, T_\text{grind}$. Gravity and finite disk size destabilize planar surfaces, but for sufficiently short strokes spheres remain stable ($T_\text{deform} \gg T_\text{sphere}$), with only their radius of curvature varying adiabatically. The same framework unifies telescope mirror grinding with everyday phenomena: rock tumbling (explaining why rotary tumbling rounds edges more than vibratory, and why neither produce spheres) and sanding (explaining why small flat surfaces inevitably round). These examples offer an accessible classroom context for stable and unstable equilibria, exponential relaxation, diffusion, multi-timescale analysis, and the emergence of the Laplacian operator from averaging over a local area.

</details>


### [131] [Optimal Fan-Out and Break-Even Routing Loss in Waveguide-Integrated Ge-on-Si SPAD Arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01111)
*Thammarat Yawisit, Pittaya Pannil*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Splitting a waveguide mode across multiple Ge-on-Si SPADs reduces dead-time saturation but multiplies dark counts. The paper quantifies this trade-off using an asymptotic count-based information rate and an optimal weighted moment estimator, derives optimal fan-out and critical flux for a non-paralyzable pixel, and simulates four routing architectures on 64 16-pixel arrays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To relieve dead-time saturation in Ge-on-Si single-photon avalanche diodes by splitting a waveguide mode over several pixels, while accounting for the resulting increase in dark counts, and to determine the optimal splitting and routing strategy.

Method: The trade-off is quantified with the asymptotic count-based information rate, attained by an optimally weighted moment estimator. For a non-paralyzable pixel, information per pre-dead-time signal event peaks at signal rate y* = sqrt(D(D+1/tau)) with exact efficiency (sqrt(1+D tau) - sqrt(D tau))^2, setting the optimal fan-out and a critical flux above which equal splitting is optimal. Four routing architectures are compared on 64 simulated 16-pixel arrays: an equal passive tree, static trees (uncalibrated and an idealized calibration-informed benchmark), an ideal programmable tree, and a thermo-optic Mach-Zehnder interferometer tree with 1.32 dB excess loss. Event-level Monte Carlo simulations and sensitivity analyses cover dark-count level, heterogeneity, detector model, and random seed.

Result: Ideal programmability gains up to 15.9 dB over equal splitting at low flux. At one known flux, the calibrated static splitter beats the MZI tree by 0.14-2.7 dB, and the penalty tends to twice the excess loss when dark counts dominate. In ensemble-median worst-case regret, the MZI tree breaks even only for flux windows wider than 2.55 decades, and not within six decades above about 3.4 dB excess loss. Monte Carlo simulations reproduce the count statistics and predicted estimator variances within bootstrap confidence intervals.

Conclusion: The count-based information rate provides a unified metric that prices dark counts and dead time, yielding an optimal fan-out and critical flux. Ideal programmability is most beneficial at low flux, while calibrated static splitting can outperform lossy MZI routing at a known flux. All results are simulations using representative literature parameters; experimental validation is not provided.

Abstract: Splitting a waveguide mode over several Ge-on-Si single-photon avalanche diodes (SPADs) relieves dead-time saturation but multiplies dark counts. We quantify this trade-off with the asymptotic count-based information rate, which prices dark counts and dead time in one unit and is attained by an optimally weighted moment estimator. For a non-paralyzable pixel, the information per pre-dead-time signal event peaks at a signal rate $y^*=\sqrt{D(D+1/τ)}$ with exact efficiency $(\sqrt{1+Dτ}-\sqrt{Dτ})^2$, which sets the optimal fan-out and a critical flux above which equal splitting is optimal. We then compare four routing architectures on 64 simulated 16-pixel arrays: an equal passive tree, static trees (uncalibrated, and an idealized calibration-informed benchmark), an ideal programmable tree, and a thermo-optic Mach-Zehnder interferometer (MZI) tree with 1.32 dB excess loss. Ideal programmability gains up to 15.9 dB over equal splitting at low flux. At one known flux, however, the calibrated static splitter beats the MZI tree by 0.14-2.7 dB, and the penalty tends to twice the excess loss when dark counts dominate. In ensemble-median worst-case regret, the MZI tree breaks even only for flux windows wider than 2.55 decades, and not within six decades above about 3.4 dB excess loss. Event-level Monte Carlo simulations reproduce the count statistics and the predicted estimator variances within bootstrap confidence intervals. Sensitivity analyses cover dark-count level, heterogeneity, detector model and random seed. All results are simulations with representative literature parameters.

</details>


### [132] [Discovering Models for Experimental Quantum Systems with Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00620)
*Hannah C. Kleidermacher, Bennet Windt, Miguel Bello, James Zou, Jelena Vučković*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: An LLM-based agent with tools and a structured workflow discovers Hamiltonian and Lindbladian models from three published cavity-QED datasets on diamond and silicon carbide color centers, characterizes disorder, and shows that run-to-run variability arises mainly during research-plan generation rather than computational analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A central obstacle for color-center cavity-QED quantum technologies is inhomogeneity, or disorder, among individual emitters. As the number of emitters increases, experimental data become more complex, making it difficult to quantify the physical mechanisms of disorder.

Method: The paper introduces an artificial-intelligence-driven model-discovery framework in which a large language model (LLM)-based agent, equipped with tools and a structured workflow, proposes candidate Hamiltonian and Lindbladian models, generates computational tools, and quantitatively evaluates competing physical hypotheses. The workflow is applied to three previously published experimental cavity-QED datasets collected from color centers in diamond and silicon carbide.

Result: Across the experiments, the agent successfully identified physical models for the system and characterized the disorder. In assessing reproducibility, run-to-run variability was concentrated primarily in the research-plan generation step, while the computational analysis itself was substantially more consistent. Specific model forms, dataset sizes, and quantitative accuracy metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Structured LLM-agent workflows can support model discovery from quantum optical experimental data while simultaneously allowing for human expert supervision and collaboration.

Abstract: A central obstacle in realizing color center cavity-QED-based quantum technologies is inhomogeneity, or disorder, among individual emitters. As the number of emitters increases, so does the complexity of the experimental data, making it difficult to quantify the present physical mechanisms of disorder. Here we introduce an artificial intelligence-driven model discovery framework in which a large language model (LLM)-based agent, equipped with tools and a structured workflow, proposes candidate Hamiltonian and Lindbladian models, generates computational tools, and quantitatively evaluates competing physical hypotheses. We apply the workflow to three previously published experimental cavity-QED datasets collected from color centers in diamond and silicon carbide. Across the experiments, the agent successfully identifies physical models for the system and characterizes the disorder. In assessing reproducibility, we find that run-to-run variability is concentrated primarily in the research plan generation step, while the computational analysis itself is substantially more consistent. Our results suggest that structured LLM agent workflows can support model discovery from quantum optical experimental data while simultaneously allowing for human expert supervision and collaboration.

</details>


### [133] [Ultra-broadband quantum infrared spectroscopy extending to 20 {\textmu}m](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00561)
*Yu Mukai, Ryo Okamoto, Shigeki Takeuchi*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A gallium selenide-based wavelength-tunable photon-pair source extends quantum infrared spectroscopy coverage from 8.6 to 20 µm, enabling measurements beyond 10 µm and demonstration on a polystyrene film.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum infrared spectroscopy (QIRS) enables infrared spectroscopy using only visible and near-infrared light sources and detectors, but extending its spectral coverage to the long-wavelength infrared region up to 20 µm is important for identifying and characterizing many materials. Implementation beyond 10 µm has been challenging because previously used nonlinear materials do not provide suitable photon-pair sources in that region.

Method: The authors designed a wavelength-tunable photon-pair source based on a gallium selenide crystal and developed a QIRS system covering a broad spectral range from 8.6 to 20 µm. They used this system to measure the transmittance spectrum of a polystyrene film.

Result: The QIRS system covered 8.6 to 20 µm, and the polystyrene transmittance measurement revealed characteristic absorption bands beyond 10 µm relevant to identifying polystyrene and probing local chain conformation.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that QIRS can cover the entire molecular fingerprint region.

Abstract: Quantum infrared spectroscopy (QIRS), which exploits quantum interference between photon-pair generation processes, enables infrared spectroscopy using only visible and near-infrared (VNIR) light sources and detectors. Extending the spectral coverage of QIRS into the long-wavelength infrared region up to 20 {\textmu}m is crucial for the identification and characterization of a broad range of materials. However, implementing QIRS beyond 10 {\textmu}m has been challenging because the nonlinear materials used to date do not provide suitable photon-pair sources in this spectral region. Here, we design a wavelength-tunable photon-pair source based on a gallium selenide crystal and develop a QIRS system covering a broad spectral range from 8.6 to 20 {\textmu}m. Using this system, we measure the transmittance spectrum of a polystyrene film and observe characteristic absorption bands beyond 10 {\textmu}m relevant to identifying polystyrene and to probing local chain conformation. These results demonstrate that QIRS is capable of covering the entire molecular fingerprint region.

</details>


### [134] [Do Multimodal Models Decide Consistently on Equivalent Optical Spectra?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00428)
*Ziyuan Wang, Genliang Zhu, Xiaobo Chen*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A benchmark on measured optical spectra finds that changing only the representation can shift model accuracy by up to 41 percentage points, that unadapted models rarely abstain when evidence is missing, and that LoRA abstention gains can reflect a truncation shortcut; training with answerable truncations removes it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific decision models should make the same decision for equivalent spectrum representations and should state when required data are absent, but evaluation has emphasized average accuracy rather than representation invariance and abstention.

Method: The authors build tasks from measured optical spectra of silicon metasurfaces (MOCLIP) and gold nanoparticles with labels computed from the data. Each spectrum is presented as four tables (nm, μm, scientific notation, eV) and two plots (nm, eV); partial spectra remove data inside or outside the queried range. They evaluate d3-nano, Jev 1.13, three GPT-6 Luna configurations, and Qwen3.5-2B, and test LoRA fine-tuning of d3-nano with answerable truncations versus extra original-task data across six seeds.

Result: Representation alone changes accuracy by up to 41 percentage points within one model, mostly for photon-energy tables and plots. Asking in photon energy instead of wavelength helps some model-view pairs by 16-50 points and hurts others by up to 25. Unadapted models rarely answer undetermined when the relevant extremum is removed. LoRA improves comparisons on trained table formats, but much of its unanswerable-question gain is a shortcut of abstaining whenever input is truncated. Training with answerable truncations avoids the shortcut in all six seeds, producing a 90-100 point gap between abstention after inside and outside cuts on held-out structures; with the same amount of extra original-task data, five of six seeds retain the shortcut. On gold spectra the two training sets perform similarly.

Conclusion: Multimodal language models for scientific decisions should be tested on equivalent representations and missing-evidence conditions, not only average accuracy. The tasks, per-response results, and analysis code are released.

Abstract: A spectrum can be given to a model as a table in nanometers or as a plot in photon energy, and the decision should not depend on which one it sees. When the data needed for an answer are missing, the model should say so. We test representation invariance and abstention on measured optical spectra of silicon metasurfaces (MOCLIP) and gold nanoparticles, with labels computed from the data. Each spectrum is shown as four tables (nm, $μ$m, scientific notation, eV) and two plots (nm, eV), and partial spectra remove data inside or outside the queried range. We evaluate the decision models d3-nano and Jev 1.13, three GPT-6 Luna configurations and the Qwen3.5-2B base model. Changing only the representation moves accuracy by up to 41 percentage points within one model, mostly on photon-energy tables and plots. With the same evidence, asking in photon energy instead of wavelength helps some model-view pairs by 16-50 points and hurts others by up to 25. When the relevant extremum is removed, unadapted models rarely answer "undetermined". LoRA fine-tuning of d3-nano improves comparisons on the trained table formats, but much of its gain on unanswerable questions comes from a shortcut: it abstains whenever the input is truncated. Training with answerable truncations avoids the shortcut in all six seeds, with a 90-100 point gap between abstention after inside and outside cuts on held-out structures; with the same amount of extra original-task data, five of six seeds keep the shortcut. On gold spectra the two training sets perform similarly. Multimodal language models used for scientific decisions should be tested on equivalent representations and on missing evidence, not only on average accuracy. We release the tasks, per-response results and analysis code.

</details>


### [135] [Detectability of an orbital-angular-momentum-based gravitational wave detector](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00085)
*Haorong Wu, Xilong Fan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a readout and noise-budget analysis for an orbital-angular-momentum-based gravitational-wave detector from 1 mHz to 100 Hz, showing that balanced detection suppresses common-mode laser-intensity and mode-impurity noise and that a compact arm configuration can achieve high signal-to-noise ratios for selected binary-black-hole sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The orbital angular momentum of light offers a new route to probing gravitational waves through transitions between OAM states. The authors aim to assess the detectability of such an OAM-based gravitational-wave detector.

Method: The authors develop a readout scheme and evaluate the detector noise budget over 1 mHz to 100 Hz, including laser-intensity noise, laser-phase noise, mode-impurity noise, and shot noise. They exploit the symmetries α_+=-α_-^* for the gravitational-wave-induced transition amplitudes and n_+=n_-^* for noise-induced mode-crosstalk coefficients, so the signal is differential while laser-intensity and mode-impurity noises are common-mode and can be suppressed by balanced detection. They adopt L=4 m, N=160 round trips, paraxial parameter γ=0.1, P_BS=10 kW, and λ_opt=1064 nm, noting that under a fixed beam-waist constraint a shorter arm allows a larger paraxial parameter and reduced equivalent angular-noise strain sensitivity.

Result: Using representative noise spectra for gravitational waves from an equal-mass binary black hole with source-frame component masses M_src=10^4 M_sun at redshift z=1, balanced detection yields an SNR of 24.7 for imbalance parameter ε=10^-3 and 239 for ε=10^-5; in the latter case the sensitivity approaches the shot-noise limit.

Conclusion: The results indicate that a compact optical configuration and strong common-mode rejection are important for realizing an OAM-based gravitational-wave detector.

Abstract: The orbital angular momentum (OAM) of light provides a new approach to probing gravitational waves (GWs) through transitions between OAM states. Here, we assess the detectability of an OAM-based GW detector by developing a readout scheme and evaluating its noise budget over the frequency range from $1~\mathrm{mHz}$ to $100~\mathrm{Hz}$, including laser-intensity noise, laser-phase noise, mode-impurity noise, and shot noise. For a symmetric superposition OAM state, the GW-induced transition amplitudes satisfy $α_+=-α_-^*$, whereas the noise-induced mode-crosstalk coefficients obey $n_+=n_-^*$. Hence, the GW-induced signal forms a differential-mode contribution, while laser-intensity and mode-impurity noises enter as common-mode contributions, enabling their suppression by the balanced detection. Under a fixed beam-waist constraint, a shorter arm permits a larger paraxial parameter, reducing the equivalent angular-noise strain sensitivity. We adopt an arm length of $L=4\,\mathrm{m}$, $N=160$ round trips, a paraxial parameter of $γ=0.1$, a laser power incident on the beam splitter of $P_{\mathrm{BS}}=10\,\mathrm{kW}$, and a wavelength of $λ_{\mathrm{opt}}=1064\,\mathrm{nm}$. Using representative noise spectra, we find that, for GWs from an equal-mass binary black hole with source-frame component masses of $M_{\mathrm{src}}=10^{4}\,M_{\odot}$ at redshift $z=1$, balanced detection yields an SNR of $24.7$ for an imbalance parameter of $ε=10^{-3}$ and $239$ for $ε=10^{-5}$, with the sensitivity approaching the shot-noise limit in the latter case. These results highlight the importance of a compact optical configuration and strong common-mode rejection for realizing an OAM-based GW detector.

</details>



<div id="physics.flu-dyn"></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)


### [136] [Discovering like a scientist: Literature-informed dimensionless learning for physical closure of data-driven equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05780)
*Hao Xu, Tianhao Wu, Dongxiao Zhang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper introduces π-former, a knowledge-guided dimensionless-learning framework that uses a corpus of 1,288 established dimensionless groups to propose interpretable groups under dimensional constraints, optimizes their combinations against observations, and integrates them with equation discovery. It identifies predictive dimensionless coordinates across seven physical problems, recovers established scalings in multi-condition simulations, and derives a unified trajectory equation for evaporation-driven wetting that generalizes to held-out conditions and outperforms existing empirical models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven methods can recover compact equations, but the physical dependence of their coefficients across operating conditions often remains unresolved. Dimensionless groups can explain this variation, yet selecting relevant groups from the many dimensionally admissible combinations is challenging.

Method: The authors introduce π-former, a knowledge-guided framework for dimensionless learning. It learns patterns of variable association and exponent assignment from a corpus of 1,288 established dimensionless groups. Under exact dimensional constraints, it proposes interpretable basic groups whose combinations are optimized against observations. The framework is also integrated with equation discovery to express condition-dependent coefficients through the learned dimensionless coordinates. Architectural and training details are not provided in the abstract.

Result: Across seven physical problems, the framework identifies predictive dimensionless coordinates within seconds. Integration with equation discovery recovers established physical scalings in multi-condition simulations. Applied to experimental measurements of evaporation-driven wetting, it discovers dimensionless coordinates and constructs a unified trajectory equation; the resulting model generalizes to held-out conditions across liquids and initial volumes and outperforms existing empirical models.

Conclusion: The results establish dimensionless learning as a transferable bridge between accumulated scientific knowledge and data-driven equation discovery, enabling artificial intelligence to uncover physically meaningful laws from observations.

Abstract: Data-driven methods can recover compact equations, yet the physical dependence of their coefficients across operating conditions often remains unresolved. Dimensionless groups can explain this variation, but selecting relevant groups from the many dimensionally admissible combinations remains challenging. Here, we introduce a knowledge-guided framework for dimensionless learning, called π-former. It learns patterns of variable association and exponent assignment from a corpus of 1,288 established dimensionless groups. Under exact dimensional constraints, the model proposes interpretable basic groups whose combinations are optimized against observations. Across seven physical problems, the framework identifies predictive dimensionless coordinates within seconds. Integration with equation discovery further expresses condition-dependent coefficients through these coordinates, recovering established physical scalings in multi-condition simulations. Applied to experimental measurements of evaporation-driven wetting, the framework discovers dimensionless coordinates and uses them to construct a unified trajectory equation. The resulting model generalizes to held-out conditions across liquids and initial volumes, outperforming existing empirical models. These results establish dimensionless learning as a transferable bridge between accumulated scientific knowledge and data-driven equation discovery, enabling artificial intelligence to uncover physically meaningful laws from observations.

</details>


### [137] [FlowReconBench: Accuracy, calibration and cost of learned and classical flow reconstruction from sparse sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06912)
*Nicolas Tricard, Linzheng Wang, Jason Chen, Sili Deng*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: FlowReconBench benchmarks ten learned and five classical full-flow reconstruction methods on a laminar cylinder wake across Reynolds numbers, two-dimensional Kolmogorov turbulence, and large-scale three-dimensional isotropic turbulence using leakage-controlled splits and shared sensor draws. It finds no universal winner: classical estimators are strong in low-order and dense-sensing regimes, while learned methods become superior at lower sensor density or poor observability; rankings also depend on whether error, ensemble quality, spectra, calibration, or cost is prioritized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing full flow fields from sparse sensors requires prior assumptions about unmeasured regions and physical variables, but it remains unclear when learned models outperform classical estimators and which aspects of their predictions can be trusted. The paper motivates a controlled benchmark to compare these method classes across physically distinct flow regimes and multiple evaluation criteria.

Method: The paper introduces FlowReconBench, which evaluates ten learned and five classical reconstruction methods on three regimes: a laminar cylinder wake across Reynolds numbers, two-dimensional Kolmogorov turbulence, and large-scale three-dimensional isotropic turbulence. Methods use shared leakage-controlled data splits and sensor draws and are scored on per-channel error, ensemble spread, calibration, spectra, and cost. The benchmark also examines performance under decreasing sensor density and poor sensor observability, sampler-budget effects for generative models, memory scaling in three dimensions, and a setting where only two of four fields are observed.

Result: The leading method changes for each regime. Classical methods perform strongly in low-order and dense-sensing regimes. On the cylinder wake, linear stochastic estimation and gappy proper orthogonal decomposition lead, including for unobserved pressure. In three dimensions, inverse-distance weighting matches the best learned methods on observed channels. Data-driven methods become superior at decreasing sensor density or poor sensor observability. On Kolmogorov flow, continuous ranked probability score and error produce different method rankings: generative models offer broader but underdispersed ensembles, while deterministic models prioritize pointwise error. Generative models balance this tradeoff through their sampler budget, with a larger budget improving ensemble quality at higher computational cost. In three dimensions, queryable methods are flat in memory, whereas full-grid construction methods run out of memory. Observing only two of four fields leaves the problem kinematically underconstrained, yet a latent model can reproduce structural similarities to the ground truth.
