Conclusion: No single reconstruction method dominates across all regimes and metrics. Classical estimators remain competitive or superior for low-order and dense-sensing problems, while learned methods are preferable as sensor density decreases or observability worsens. Trust in predictions depends on the evaluated property, since error and probabilistic scores can rank methods differently, and generative ensemble quality depends on sampler budget and cost. The results also favor queryable methods for memory-efficient three-dimensional reconstruction and show that latent models can capture structural features even when partial observations leave the problem kinematically underconstrained.

Abstract: Reconstructing full flow fields from sparse sensors requires prior assumptions about unmeasured regions and physical variables. Yet it remains unclear when learned models outperform classical estimators and which aspects of their predictions can be trusted. FlowReconBench evaluates ten learned and five classical methods on three regimes: a laminar cylinder wake across Reynolds numbers, two-dimensional Kolmogorov turbulence and a large-scale three-dimensional (3D) isotropic turbulence. Methods share leakage-controlled splits and sensor draws and are scored on per-channel error, ensemble spread, calibration, spectra and cost. The leading method changes for each regime. Classical methods perform strongly in low-order and dense-sensing regimes. On the cylinder wake, linear stochastic estimation and gappy proper orthogonal decomposition lead, including for unobserved pressure. In 3D, inverse-distance weighting matches the best learned methods on observed channels. Data-driven methods become superior at decreasing sensor density or poor sensor observability. On Kolmogorov flow, continuous ranked probability score and error yield different method rankings: generative models offer broader but underdispersed ensembles, while deterministic models prioritize pointwise error. Generative models balance this tradeoff through their sampler budget, with a larger budget improving ensemble quality at higher computational cost. In 3D, scaling tests demonstrate that queryable methods are flat in memory, while full-grid construction methods run out of memory. Observing only 2 of 4 fields leaves the problem kinematically underconstrained, yet a latent model can reproduce structural similarities to the ground truth.

</details>


### [138] [A coupled lattice Boltzmann model for pore-scale mushy-layer dynamics with application to sea ice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06407)
*Nicola Trippella, Dario Maggiolo*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A pore-scale lattice Boltzmann framework couples solute transport with conjugate heat transfer and phase change, introducing link-based Volume-of-Pixel interface motion and a generalized Robin boundary treatment for solute redistribution at moving interfaces; it is validated and applied to a sea ice brine channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide a pore-scale numerical capability for resolving coupled temperature evolution, solute redistribution, and phase-boundary dynamics in phase-changing porous media and mushy layers, with application to sea ice brine channels.

Method: The authors develop a pore-scale lattice Boltzmann model for reactive transport in phase-changing porous media, coupling liquid-phase solute transport with conjugate heat transfer across solid and liquid phases. They introduce a link-based Volume-of-Pixel formulation for direction-dependent interface motion and a generalized Robin boundary treatment for solute redistribution at moving interfaces. The phase-change formulation is validated against thermal benchmarks, and the Robin treatment is assessed with literature test cases. The coupled model is applied to a local pore-scale segment of a sea ice brine channel in a purely diffusive regime.

Result: The Robin treatment exhibits convergence rates between first and second order. The simulations recover expected ideal mushy-layer behavior under appropriate scale separation. At sea-ice-relevant concentration ratios, thermal perturbations produce relatively large variations in brine salinity, and freezing rather than melting reshapes the pore geometry non-uniformly. Quantitative validation errors and specific parameter values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The resulting framework provides a pore-scale numerical tool for resolving coupled temperature, solute redistribution, and phase-boundary dynamics in mushy layers.

Abstract: A pore-scale lattice Boltzmann model is developed for reactive transport in phase-changing porous media, coupling solute transport in the liquid with conjugate heat transfer across the solid and liquid phases. Two methodological developments are introduced: a novel link-based Volume-of-Pixel formulation for direction-dependent interface motion, and a generalized Robin boundary treatment for solute redistribution at moving interfaces. The phase-change formulation is validated against thermal benchmarks, while the Robin treatment is assessed through literature test cases, exhibiting convergence rates between first and second order. The coupled model is then applied to a local pore-scale segment of a sea ice brine channel in a purely diffusive regime. The simulations recover the expected ideal mushy-layer behaviour under appropriate scale separation. At sea-ice-relevant concentration ratios, thermal perturbations produce relatively large variations in brine salinity, causing freezing rather than melting to reshape the pore geometry in a non-uniform way. The resulting framework provides a pore-scale numerical tool for resolving coupled temperature, solute redistribution, and phase-boundary dynamics in mushy layers.

</details>


### [139] [Odd Capillarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06882)
*Nadia Bihari Padhan, Axel Voigt*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper introduces odd capillarity, a parity-breaking nonvariational capillary stress, into Cahn--Hilliard--Navier--Stokes equations. Simulations show arrested domain coarsening with sustained turbulent motion without a conventional inertial energy cascade, and dynamically arrested emulsions in asymmetric mixtures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capillarity governs interfacial relaxation and hydrodynamic motion in binary fluids through parity-even mechanical stresses. The paper aims to introduce a parity-breaking interfacial mechanical response and establish odd capillarity as a distinct mechanism for controlling phase separation and sustaining nonequilibrium fluid motion.

Method: A symmetric, nonvariational, parity-odd capillary stress is incorporated into the Cahn--Hilliard--Navier--Stokes (CHNS) equations. The authors perform direct numerical simulations, including for asymmetric mixtures.

Result: Odd capillarity generates forces along interfaces, transverse to ordinary capillary forces, and performs mechanical work not balanced by changes in the composition free energy. Simulations show that it arrests domain coarsening while sustaining turbulent fluid motion without a conventional inertial energy cascade, with energy injection predominantly balanced by viscous dissipation. In asymmetric mixtures, odd-capillarity-driven deformation and breakup interrupt droplet growth, producing dynamically arrested emulsions with composition hyperuniformity and suppressed large-scale vorticity fluctuations.

Conclusion: Odd capillarity is established as a distinct interfacial mechanism for controlling phase separation and sustaining nonequilibrium fluid motion.

Abstract: Capillarity governs interfacial relaxation and hydrodynamic motion in binary fluids through parity-even mechanical stresses. Here, we introduce \emph{odd capillarity}, a parity-breaking interfacial mechanical response, by incorporating a symmetric, nonvariational, parity-odd capillary stress into the Cahn--Hilliard--Navier--Stokes (CHNS) equations. This stress generates forces along interfaces, transverse to ordinary capillary forces, and performs mechanical work that is not balanced by changes in the composition free energy. Direct numerical simulations reveal that odd capillarity arrests domain coarsening while sustaining turbulent fluid motion without a conventional inertial energy cascade, as energy injection is predominantly balanced by viscous dissipation. In asymmetric mixtures, odd capillarity driven deformation and breakup interrupt droplet growth, producing dynamically arrested emulsions with composition hyperuniformity and suppressed large-scale vorticity fluctuations. These findings establish odd capillarity as a distinct interfacial mechanism for controlling phase separation and sustaining nonequilibrium fluid motion.

</details>


### [140] [Controls on the deformation and reorientation of mafic enclaves during magma mingling: A computational study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05575)
*Pragati Sonali, Arghya Choudhury, Urmi Dutta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Using 96 two-dimensional Level-Set simulations, the study models a dense, low-viscosity droplet in a lighter, much more viscous host under shear and gravity to interpret mafic microgranular enclave shapes. It identifies sinking and non-sinking regimes with six outcomes, shows that Ca/Bo separates sinking from shear-dominated behavior near unity, and concludes that enclave shape reflects flow regime rather than finite strain directly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mafic microgranular enclaves in granitic plutons record magma mingling, and their shapes are often interpreted as a record of the flow that deformed them. The paper investigates how a dense, low-viscosity droplet deforms in a lighter, much more viscous host under shear and gravity.

Method: The authors perform 96 two-dimensional Level-Set simulations with viscosity ratio from 10^-6 to 10^-3, capillary number from 10^6 to 8×10^9, Bond number of 3.5×10^6 and 9.8×10^6, Reynolds number below 10^-2, and a fixed density contrast of 400 kg m^-3. They classify sinking and non-sinking regimes and six outcomes: sinking, edge, mid and multiple break-up, rotation, and retraction. They analyze Ca/Bo, confinement, strain at maximum stretch, and major-axis orientation.

Result: Two broad regimes are identified: sinking and non-sinking, spread over six outcomes including sinking, edge, mid and multiple break-up, rotation, and retraction. Ca/Bo separates sinking from shear-dominated behavior near unity, although the transition spans a range of Ca/Bo values that shifts with confinement. In the shear-dominated regime, a lower viscosity ratio promotes stretching. Multiple pinch-off occurs mainly at viscosity ratios of 10^-6 to 10^-5, rotation occurs only at the higher vertical confinement, and retraction occurs only at the lower vertical confinement. Strain at maximum stretch averages 1.1 to 1.4 and increases with Ca/Bo, with a stronger increase at higher confinement. The orientation of the major axis decreases by approximately 10° per unit strain and is lowest for rotating droplets. The capillary number exceeds conventional critical values for droplet breakup.

Conclusion: Because the capillary number exceeds conventional critical values for breakup, breakup is interpreted in terms of accumulated deformation rather than capillary stability alone. Enclave shape therefore reflects the flow regime and is not a direct measure of finite strain.

Abstract: Mafic microgranular enclaves in granitic plutons record magma mingling, and their shapes are often interpreted as a record of the flow that deformed them. We investigate how a dense, low-viscosity droplet deforms in a lighter, much more viscous host under shear and gravity, using 96 two-dimensional Level-Set simulations. The viscosity ratio ($μ_r$) spans $10^{-6}$ - $10^{-3}$, the capillary number ($\mathrm{Ca}$) ranges from $10^{6}$ to $8\times10^{9}$, the Bond number ($\mathrm{Bo}$) takes values of $3.5\times10^{6}$ and $9.8\times10^{6}$, and the Reynolds number ($\mathrm{Re}$) remains below $10^{-2}$, with a fixed density contrast ($Δρ$) of $400~\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$. Two broad regimes identified- sinking and non-sinking which spread over six outcomes like sinking, edge, mid and multiple break-up, rotation, and retraction. The ratio $\mathrm{Ca/Bo}$ separates sinking from shear-dominated behaviour near unity, although the transition spans a range of $\mathrm{Ca/Bo}$ values that shifts with confinement. In the shear-dominated regime, a lower viscosity ratio promotes stretching. Multiple pinch-off occurs mainly at viscosity ratios of $10^{-6}$ - $10^{-5}$, rotation occurs only at the higher vertical confinement, and retraction occurs only at the lower vertical confinement. The strain at maximum stretch averages 1.1 - 1.4 and increases with $\mathrm{Ca/Bo}$, with a stronger increase at higher confinement. The orientation of the major axis decreases by approximately $10^\circ$ per unit strain and is lowest for rotating droplets. Because the capillary number exceeds conventional critical values for breakup for droplets, breakup is interpreted in terms of accumulated deformation rather than capillary stability alone. Enclave shape therefore reflects the flow regime and is not a direct measure of finite strain.

</details>


### [141] [Experimental evaluation of Kolmogorov's first similarity hypothesis for shear-dependent viscosity fluids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06834)
*Luciano Hergenreder, Serge Simoëns, Wernher Brevis*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This experimental study tests whether Kolmogorov's first similarity hypothesis applies to shear-dependent viscosity fluids. Using stereo particle image velocimetry in aqueous Carbopol solutions at two concentrations and multiple forcing conditions, the authors find that second- and third-order structure functions collapse when normalized by mean viscosity and mean dissipation rate, with functional forms compatible with classical Newtonian turbulence and generally consistent with recent numerical work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the need to determine whether Kolmogorov's first similarity hypothesis, originally established for Newtonian fluids, can be applied to shear-dependent, specifically shear-thinning, fluids. It aims to assess whether dissipative-range statistics in nearly homogeneous and isotropic turbulence exhibit self-similar behavior under such non-Newtonian conditions and to connect the experimental findings with recent numerical proposals for extending Kolmogorov's similarity hypotheses.

Method: The authors performed experiments using stereo particle image velocimetry in aqueous Carbopol solutions, a shear-thinning fluid, across two concentrations and a range of forcing conditions. They analyzed dissipative-range statistics by examining second- and third-order structure functions normalized by the mean viscosity and mean dissipation rate, and compared the observed functional forms with classical Newtonian results and with recent numerical predictions.

Result: When normalized by the mean viscosity and mean dissipation rate, the second- and third-order structure functions collapsed across the different experimental conditions and exhibited functional forms compatible with classical Newtonian results. These observations were in general agreement with the recent numerical results of Rosti [J. Fluid Mech. 1012, R5 (2025)], which proposed that Kolmogorov's similarity hypotheses can be extended to shear-thinning fluids by using dissipation scales based on the mean viscosity, mean dissipation rate, and flow index.

Conclusion: The study concludes that the experimental observations support the applicability of Kolmogorov's first similarity hypothesis to shear-dependent viscosity fluids, at least for the nearly homogeneous and isotropic turbulence conditions and Carbopol solutions examined, when dissipative-range statistics are normalized using the mean viscosity and mean dissipation rate. The results are generally consistent with an extension of Kolmogorov's similarity hypotheses to shear-thinning fluids based on mean viscosity, mean dissipation rate, and flow index.

Abstract: We experimentally investigate the applicability of Kolmogorov's first similarity hypothesis to shear-dependent viscosity fluids. We assess whether dissipative range statistics exhibit a self-similar behavior in nearly homogeneous and isotropic turbulence, using stereo particle image velocimetry in aqueous Carbopol solutions across two concentrations and a range of forcing conditions. We observe that, when normalized by the mean viscosity and mean dissipation rate, second- and third-order structure functions collapse across the different experimental conditions and exhibit functional forms compatible with classical Newtonian results. These observations are in general agreement with the recent numerical results of Rosti [J. Fluid Mech. 1012, R5 (2025)], who proposed that Kolmogorov's similarity hypotheses can be extended to shear-thinning fluids by considering dissipation scales based on the mean viscosity, the mean dissipation rate, and the flow index.

</details>


### [142] [Interscale energy transfer in a propeller-driven homogeneous and isotropic turbulence generation device](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06825)
*Luciano Hergenreder, Serge Simoëns, Cristián Escauriaza, Wernher Brevis*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper experimentally studies interscale energy dynamics in nearly homogeneous isotropic turbulence produced by a spherically forced propeller-driven device, analyzing spatial energy transfer with the Kármán-Howarth-Monin framework, temporal variability without Taylor's frozen-turbulence hypothesis, and global links between turbulent scales and propeller frequency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to understand how external forcing influences the complex interscale energy dynamics of turbulent flows from spatial, temporal, and global perspectives, including conditions where Taylor's frozen turbulence hypothesis does not apply.

Method: Nearly homogeneous isotropic turbulence was generated in a spherically forced, propeller-driven device. Scale-by-scale energy transfer was analyzed using the Kármán-Howarth-Monin framework without isotropy assumptions. Temporal dynamics were examined via the Eulerian frequency spectrum, and a global framework was proposed to relate main turbulent variables to external experimental parameters, including propeller frequency, and to previously reported HIT chambers.

Result: Spherical forcing produced a purely forward energy cascade across the entire scale space, unlike other forcing strategies where inverse and forward cascades can coexist. The energy distribution remained highly isotropic and orientation-averaged structure functions aligned with Kolmogorov's two-thirds law, while the energy flux showed directional dependence. The reduced mean flow enabled investigation without Taylor's frozen turbulence hypothesis, providing experimental validation of Tennekes' Eulerian frequency spectrum, with intermediate scales showing an inertial-convective range determined by the integral velocity scale and dissipation rate. The proposed global framework captured the propeller frequency dependence of main turbulent variables in the present measurements and previously reported HIT chambers.

Conclusion: The findings clarify how external forcing influences interscale energy dynamics in turbulent flows across spatial, temporal, and global perspectives, and support the use of the proposed framework for linking main turbulent scales to experimental forcing parameters.

Abstract: We investigate the interscale energy dynamics of nearly homogeneous isotropic turbulence (HIT) generated in a spherically forced, propeller-driven device. From a spatial perspective, by employing the Kármán-Howarth-Monin (KHM) framework, we analyze the scale-by-scale energy transfer without isotropy assumptions. In contrast to other forcing strategies, where regions with inverse and forward cascades often coexist in scale space, we show that spherical forcing produces a purely forward energy cascade across the entire scale space. While the energy distribution remains highly isotropic and orientation-averaged structure functions align with Kolmogorov's two-thirds law, the energy flux exhibits a directional dependence, suggesting a higher sensitivity to residual forcing asymmetries. Regarding the temporal dynamics, the reduced mean flow generated by this device allows us to investigate the temporal variability in conditions where Taylor's frozen turbulence hypothesis does not apply. Our results provide experimental validation of Tennekes' Eulerian frequency spectrum, where intermediate scales evidence an inertial-convective range with statistics determined by the integral velocity scale and the dissipation rate. Finally, we adopt a global approach to investigate whether the main turbulent scales can be linked to external experimental parameters. We propose a framework that captures the propeller frequency dependence of the main turbulent variables obtained in our measurements and those of previously reported HIT chambers. These findings contribute to the understanding of how external forcing influences the complex interscale energy dynamics of turbulent flows from a spatial, temporal, and global perspective.

</details>


### [143] [A Drift-Diffusion Approach for Pearson Type IV Heavy-Tail Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06749)
*Daniele Belardinelli, Simone Benella, Mirko Stumpo, Giuseppe Consolini*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper derives the Pearson type IV distribution as a stationary solution of a standardized Fokker-Planck equation with linear drift and quadratic diffusion, establishes conditions for its emergence, provides closed-form distributional properties, and interprets heavy tails and asymmetry as arising from diffusion curvature and gradient, respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Gaussian stationary fluctuations with heavy tails and finite-order moment cutoffs are common in non-equilibrium systems, creating a need for a general stochastic description of such asymmetric heavy-tailed behavior.

Method: The authors solve a standardized Fokker-Planck equation with linear drift and quadratic diffusion to obtain the Pearson type IV stationary distribution; they establish necessary and sufficient conditions for its emergence, show that the linear-quadratic structure is sufficient but not necessary, and derive closed expressions for the cumulative distribution function, moment recurrence relation, skewness, kurtosis, and mode.

Result: Pearson type IV distributions emerge under specified conditions, and the paper provides closed-form expressions for key distributional properties. Heavy tails are linked to local nonlinear curvature of state-dependent diffusion, while asymmetry is linked to its gradient; asymmetric stationary fluctuations can occur even with a symmetric linear restoring drift.

Conclusion: The framework provides a general stochastic description of asymmetric heavy-tailed fluctuations in non-equilibrium systems, including space plasma turbulence; the abstract reports no empirical validation details.

Abstract: Non-Gaussian stationary fluctuations with heavy tails and finite-order moment cutoffs are ubiquitous in non-equilibrium systems. Here, we derive the Pearson type IV distribution as the stationary solution of a standardized Fokker-Planck equation with linear drift and quadratic diffusion. We establish necessary and sufficient conditions for its emergence and show that the linear-quadratic structure, although sufficient, is not necessary. We also give explicit closed expressions for the cumulative distribution function, a moment recurrence relation, skewness, kurtosis, and mode. From a physical perspective, our results reveal that heavy tails and asymmetry originate from distinct features of state-dependent diffusion: its local nonlinear curvature and gradient, respectively. In particular, asymmetric stationary fluctuations can emerge even in the presence of a symmetric linear restoring drift. This framework provides a general stochastic description of asymmetric heavy-tailed fluctuations in non-equilibrium systems, including space plasma turbulence.

</details>


### [144] [On flow unsteadiness in strongly separated high-speed ramp flows using kinetic and data-driven methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04684)
*Irmak Taylan Karpuzcu, Mert Senkardesler, Deborah A. Levin*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: High-speed compression-expansion ramp flows with large separation regions are studied using DSMC and data-driven modal methods (SPOD and DMD) to assess scaled-angle effects and identify mechanisms of unsteadiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the role of the scaled angle predicted by triple-deck theory in recirculation strength, separation size, and secondary recirculation regions that lead to flow unsteadiness, and to characterize complex stochastic DSMC flowfields with data-driven methods.

Method: The particle-kinetic direct simulation Monte Carlo (DSMC) approach was used for compression-expansion ramp geometries with large separation regions, including four free stream conditions for the same geometry and comparison with literature configurations having similar recirculation regions. Stochastic DSMC flowfields were analyzed using spectral proper orthogonal decomposition (SPOD) and dynamic mode decomposition (DMD).

Result: The stability of a single recirculation region was more sensitive to wall temperature and free stream velocity than to physical ramp angle, where stability refers to the appearance of secondary separation regions. Literature trends regarding unsteadiness mostly held, but the flows were too complex to be characterized by scaled angle alone. SPOD and DMD yielded similar frequency components and mode shapes for spatio-temporal coherent structures. The main unsteadiness mechanisms identified were reattachment-shock oscillations, Kelvin-Helmholtz-like shear-layer instability, and a c-shaped separation-bubble mode. The abstract does not provide quantitative values for separation size, frequencies, or flow conditions.

Conclusion: Scaled angle alone is insufficient to characterize these complex unsteady separation flows. DSMC combined with SPOD and DMD reveals coherent structures and identifies key unsteadiness mechanisms; this is the first application of SPOD to a DSMC-generated flowfield and comparison with DMD.

Abstract: The gas dynamics of high speed flows over several compression-expansion ramp geometries with large separation regions were investigated using a particle kinetic approach and data driven methods. These large separation regions allow study of the role of scaled angle predicted by triple deck theory on recirculation strength, separation size, and appearance of secondary recirculation regions, all of which lead to flow unsteadiness. Examining four different free stream conditions for the same geometry using the direct simulation Monte Carlo (DSMC) approach, we show that the stability of single recirculation in the separation region is more sensitive to wall temperature and free stream velocity than physical ramp angle. Stability in this context refers to appearance of secondary separation regions. In addition, configurations with similar types of recirculation regions from literature considered, we find that general trends regarding unsteadiness, such as appearance of secondary recirculation regions, mostly hold; however, the flows are too complex to be characterized by only scaled angle. Thus, stochastic flowfields generated by DSMC were investigated using data driven methods, namely spectral proper orthogonal decomposition and dynamic mode decomposition. We found that both methods yield similar frequency components and mode shapes for spatio-temporal coherent structures of unsteady DSMC flows. With the help of these structures, the main mechanisms of unsteadiness were identified as oscillations of the reattachment shock, Kelvin-Helmholtz like instability of the shear layer, and c-shaped mode of the separation bubble. This is the first time SPOD has been applied to a DSMC generated flowfield and compared with DMD.

</details>


### [145] [A Finite Element Model for Fluid--Poroelastic Structure Interaction with Dissipative Interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06733)
*Mohammad Jannesari, Francesco Costanzo*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper extends interface conditions for two poroelastic domains to a poroelastic domain interacting with a pure fluid. It develops an arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian formulation, enforces normal filtration velocity continuity with a Lagrange multiplier, transfers fluid traction to the porous domain via the mixture momentum jump condition, and includes a dissipative interface model derived from Hamilton's principle. The model is verified with manufactured solutions and compared for inert versus fully dissipative interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the need to extend prior interface conditions between two poroelastic domains to the case of a deformable poroelastic medium interacting with a pure fluid, including the effects of interface dissipation and jumps in fluid velocity and pressure.

Method: A poroelastic medium is modeled using mixture theory with incompressible constituents when not in a mixture. An arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian formulation is developed for both the fluid and porous domains. Fluid velocity and pressure may be discontinuous across the interface, while a globally continuous displacement field represents both solid-skeleton deformation and mesh motion in the pure fluid. Continuity of the normal filtration velocity is enforced via a Lagrange multiplier, the mixture momentum jump condition transfers traction from the fluid side to the porous domain, and a dissipative interface behavior is incorporated into the fluid momentum jump condition derived from Hamilton's principle. The implementation is verified using the method of manufactured solutions, and a sample problem compares an inert interface with a fully dissipative one.

Result: The results show satisfactory convergence rates. The proposed model reproduces the analytical solution of a benchmark problem using conventional jump conditions. The findings also indicate that the selected interface model has a significant influence on the behavior of the system.

Conclusion: The proposed formulation successfully extends the interface treatment to poroelastic--pure fluid interaction and is verified through convergence tests and a benchmark comparison. The choice between inert and fully dissipative interface models materially affects system behavior, highlighting the importance of the interface model.

Abstract: This paper extends our prior work on interface conditions between two poroelastic domains to the case when a poroelastic domain interacts with a pure fluid. The porous medium is assumed to be deformable and modeled following mixture theory. When not in a mixture, each constituent of the porous domain is assumed to be incompressible. An arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian formulation is developed for both the fluid and porous domains. The fluid velocity and pressure fields are allowed to be discontinuous across the pure fluid/porous domain interface. A globally continuous displacement field is defined to represent both the deformation of the solid skeleton of the poroelastic medium and the mesh motion in the pure fluid domain. As an interface condition, the continuity of the normal component of the filtration velocity is enforced via a Lagrange multiplier. The mixture momentum jump condition is implemented so as to transfer the traction from the fluid side to the porous domain. A dissipative behavior is attributed to the interface, and the jump condition for the fluid momentum, derived from Hamilton's principle, incorporates this prescribed behavior. The model implementation is verified using the method of manufactured solutions, and a sample problem is solved to compare an inert interface with a fully dissipative one. The results show satisfactory convergence rates, while the proposed model successfully reproduces the analytical solution of a benchmark problem using conventional jump conditions. Furthermore, the findings highlight the significant influence of the selected interface model on the behavior of the system.

</details>


### [146] [Quantitative infrared absorption imaging of vapor concentration fields during evaporation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06467)
*Zihao Song, Weitian Wang, Chao Sun, Xing Chao*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops and quantitatively assesses infrared absorption imaging for full-field vapor mole-fraction measurements during evaporation, validating axial concentration profiles against Stefan predictions in a Hele-Shaw cell for three hydrocarbon liquids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative full-field measurements of vapor concentration remain challenging near receding liquid-gas interfaces, and there is a need for a tracer-free diagnostic for evaporating flows in geometries and regimes where analytical concentration fields are unavailable.

Method: Broadband mid-infrared attenuation is converted into vapor mole fraction using a spectrally weighted absorption coefficient. A vertical Hele-Shaw cell provides a controlled, nearly one-dimensional evaporation benchmark with calibrated object-plane sampling of 30.5 μm/pixel. Small transverse concentration variations justify reducing two-dimensional fields to axial profiles. Five profiles spanning different retained gas-phase interval lengths and three hydrocarbon liquids are compared with Stefan predictions using only measured interval lengths and endpoint concentrations, without fitting interior concentration data.

Result: At higher vapor loading with n-pentane, measurements resolve the nonlinear Stefan concentration profile and distinguish it from the linear diffusion limit; at lower vapor loadings with n-hexane and n-heptane, profiles progressively approach that dilute limit. Profiles agree with Stefan predictions with normalized root-mean-square deviations of 0.0055–0.0185 and collapse onto the predicted identity relation after the logarithmic Stefan transformation, demonstrating quantitative recovery of vapor concentration magnitude and spatial structure under the tested conditions.

Conclusion: The method provides a full-field, tracer-free diagnostic for investigating evaporating flows in geometries and regimes where analytical concentration fields are unavailable.

Abstract: Quantitative full-field measurements of vapor concentration remain challenging near receding liquid-gas interfaces. We develop and quantitatively assess an infrared absorption imaging method for measuring vapor mole-fraction fields during evaporation. Broadband mid-infrared attenuation is converted into mole fraction using a spectrally weighted absorption coefficient. A vertical Hele-Shaw cell provides a controlled, nearly one-dimensional evaporation benchmark, with calibrated object-plane sampling of $30.5\,μ\mathrm{m/pixel}$. Small transverse concentration variations in the central measurement region justify reducing the two-dimensional fields to axial profiles. Five profiles spanning different retained gas-phase interval lengths and three hydrocarbon liquids are compared with Stefan predictions using only measured interval lengths and endpoint concentrations, without fitting interior concentration data. At the higher vapor loading of n-pentane, the measurements resolve the nonlinear Stefan concentration profile and distinguish it from the linear diffusion limit. At the lower vapor loadings of n-hexane and n-heptane, the profiles progressively approach that dilute limit. The profiles agree with Stefan predictions, with normalized root-mean-square deviations of 0.0055-0.0185, and collapse onto the predicted identity relation after the logarithmic Stefan transformation. These results demonstrate quantitative recovery of both the magnitude and spatial structure of vapor concentration fields under the tested conditions. The method provides a full-field, tracer-free diagnostic for investigating evaporating flows in geometries and regimes where analytical concentration fields are unavailable.

</details>


### [147] [Microdroplet Generator Surrogate Enhancement and Inverse Design Through an Uncertainty-Guided Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06464)
*David Gabriel Harispe, Matías Gerard, Pablo A. Kler*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper couples adaptive surrogate enhancement with simulation-driven inverse design for microfluidic droplet generation, using high-fidelity simulations, neural network ensembles, uncertainty-guided enrichment, and particle swarm optimization to improve droplet predictions and identify target droplet-size configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient design in high-dimensional microfluidic parameter spaces is computationally prohibitive because of complex multiphase flow dynamics, creating a need for accurate, affordable surrogates and reliable inverse design.

Method: High-fidelity Volume-of-Fluid simulations provide ground-truth data. Neural network ensembles predict flow regimes and droplet diameters. Ensemble-based uncertainty quantification uses consensus levels for regime classification and coefficient of variation for diameter prediction, showing uncertainty concentrates near regime transition boundaries. A physics-informed enrichment strategy adds targeted simulations in these critical zones, addresses class imbalance, and retrains the ensembles. Particle Swarm Optimization is used for constrained inverse design, balancing accuracy, predictive confidence, and physical consistency.

Result: Validation on independent test datasets shows improved accuracy for enriched ensembles: the fraction of droplet-diameter predictions within 0-3% error nearly doubles, and high-error cases above 10% drop substantially. The inverse design procedure reliably identifies physically realizable configurations meeting target droplet sizes. Specific numerical values and computational-cost reductions are not provided.

Conclusion: Adaptive uncertainty-guided surrogate enrichment combined with simulation-driven inverse design reduces computational costs while ensuring reliable predictions, offering a framework potentially applicable to optimization of a broad range of microfluidic systems.

Abstract: Microfluidic droplet generation is critical for many applications, yet efficient device design in high-dimensional parameter spaces remains computationally prohibitive due to complex multiphase flow dynamics. This work presents a unified methodology that couples adaptive surrogate enhancement with simulation-driven inverse design. High-fidelity Volume-of-Fluid simulations provide ground-truth data, while neural network ensembles predict flow regimes and droplet diameters. Ensemble-based uncertainty quantification identifies epistemic uncertainty using consensus levels for regime classification and the coefficient of variation for diameter prediction, revealing that uncertainty concentrates near regime transition boundaries where physics is highly sensitive to parameter perturbations. A physics-informed enrichment strategy targets these critical zones and addresses class imbalances by deploying additional targeted simulations and retraining the surrogates forming the ensembles. A framework that integrates Particle Swarm Optimization for constrained inverse design, balancing accuracy with predictive confidence and physical consistency is therefore proposed. Validation on independent test datasets demonstrates that the enriched ensembles achieve higher accuracy, with the fraction of predictions within the 0-3% error range on droplet diameter nearly doubling, and high-error cases (above 10%) dropping substantially. The inverse design procedure reliably identifies physically realizable configurations meeting target droplet sizes. This approach significantly reduces computational costs while ensuring reliable predictions, offering a framework with potential applicability to the optimization of a broad range of microfluidic systems.

</details>


### [148] [Boundary Layers, Displacement Flow, and the High-Frequency Response of Porous Media: Beyond the Viscous and Thermal Characteristic Lengths, Order by Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06354)
*D. Lafarge, A. Bouchendouka, Z. E. A. Fellah*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper extends the Johnson-Koplik-Dashen/Champoux-Allard high-frequency theory for fluid-saturated porous media by deriving recursive higher-order boundary-layer expansions of dynamic tortuosity and compressibility, defining new microgeometric wall-integral parameters, interpreting them geometrically and spectrally, and showing persistence for sharp edges via apparent logarithmically varying parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The leading viscous characteristic length Lambda and thermal analogue Lambda' govern only the O(omega^-1/2) boundary-layer behavior and omit higher microgeometric effects associated with interface curvature and field gradients; the authors aim to go beyond these leading terms.

Method: The authors develop asymptotic high-frequency expansions in the thin boundary-layer limit by resolving oscillatory Stokes and heat-conduction problems. They establish a recursive hierarchy continuable to any order, with WKB-type transport equations across the boundary layer closed by a non-secularity condition and a Neumann problem for the boundary-layer-induced potential displacement flow. They also use the Avellaneda-Torquato relaxation-mode representation.

Result: The hierarchy defines successive microgeometric parameters as wall integrals. Applied to fitted values for highly absorbing foams, a negative surface parameter is consistent with strong in-parallel pore-size disorder, while a large positive third-order term indicates strong constrictions or enlargements along part of the flow paths. Thermal parameters reduce to geometrical quantities: integral mean curvature of pore walls, a Willmore-type measure of departure from umbilicity, and a mean-curvature-weighted next order. All parameters are shown to be spectral data of the relaxation-time distribution. For isolated sharp edges across which fluid flows, singular terms cancel and the description persists with apparent logarithmically varying parameter values. Specific numerical values beyond these fitted signs and interpretations are not provided.

Conclusion: The work provides a recursive, microgeometrically interpretable, and spectrally characterizable extension of the JKD/Champoux-Allard framework beyond leading boundary-layer terms, including sharp-edge cases described by apparent logarithmically varying parameters.

Abstract: The Johnson-Koplik-Dashen viscous characteristic length $Λ$ and its thermal analogue $Λ'$ introduced by Champoux and Allard govern the leading $O(ω^{-1/2})$ boundary-layer behaviour of fluid-saturated porous media, but omit higher microgeometric effects associated with interface curvature and field gradients. We develop the asymptotic high-frequency expansions of dynamic tortuosity and dynamic compressibility beyond the leading boundary-layer terms. By resolving the oscillatory Stokes and heat conduction problems in the thin boundary-layer limit, we establish a recursive hierarchy, which can be continued to any order, defining successive microgeometric parameters, given by wall integrals. Each order requires transport equations of WKB type across the boundary layer, closed by a non-secularity condition, together with one Neumann problem for the potential displacement flow that the boundary layer induces. Applied to the values fitted on highly absorbing foams, these expressions show that the negative surface parameter is consistent with strong in-parallel disorder of the pore sizes, whereas the large positive third-order term points to strong constrictions or enlargements along part of the flow paths. The thermal parameters reduce to geometrical quantities: the integral mean curvature of the pore walls, a Willmore-type measure of their departure from umbilicity and, at the next order, this measure weighted by the mean curvature. Using the Avellaneda-Torquato relaxation-mode representation, we show that all these parameters are spectral data of the distribution of relaxation times. For isolated sharp edges across which the fluid flows, the singular terms of the formal expansion cancel, and the description persists with apparent, logarithmically varying values of the parameters.

</details>


### [149] [A plea for Miles' approach to wind-wave interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06317)
*Norbert Kern, Miguel M. Manna, Frédéric Bouchette, Christophe Chaubet*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper recasts Miles' wind-wave growth theory into a simple functional form, clarifies that its growth-rate predictions are not underestimates when the full wave spectrum is considered, and argues that extensions to complex hydrodynamic situations are straightforward, making the approach easier to use in practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Miles' theory is a cornerstone for understanding wave growth under wind and relies mainly on physical arguments with minimal phenomenological input, but it can appear intimidating, particularly for concrete applications. The authors aim to make it easier to exploit in practice.

Method: The authors argue that Miles' results can be summarized by a simple functional form. They also analyze the claim that Miles' predictions underestimate growth rates by accounting for the presence of a whole wave spectrum, and they illustrate generalization of Miles' original result to complex hydrodynamic situations.

Result: Miles' results can be expressed in an easy-to-use functional form. Once the presence of a whole wave spectrum is accounted for, Miles' predictions do not underestimate growth rates, contrary to what is often stated. Generalization to complex hydrodynamic situations can be quite straightforward.

Conclusion: The paper provides a hands-on summary that should make Miles' approach very easy to exploit in practice. Specific equations, quantitative comparisons, and application details are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Although the phenomenon of wave growth under the effect of wind still holds many open questions, the approach initiated by Miles [1] is a cornerstone of the current comprehension. This theory is based on physical arguments, keeping phenomenological input to a minimum, but it can be perceived as intimidating, especially in view of concrete applications. Here we argue that Miles' results can be summarised in terms of a simple functional form, which provides an easy-to-use expression to work with. We furthermore show that Miles' predictions do not, as is often stated, underestimate growth rates, once the presence of a whole wave spectrum is accounted for. Finally, we illustrate that the generalisation of Miles' original result to complex hydrodynamic situations can be quite straightforward. Our hands-on summary should make it heretofore very easy to exploit Miles' approach in practice.

</details>


### [150] [Finite-time mixing and reaction in packed bed reactors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06239)
*Dario Maggiolo, Lucien Magson, Gunther Munz, Diego Sampedro, Angela Sasic Kalagasidis*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Pore-scale lattice-Boltzmann simulations and Lagrangian particle tracking are used to study finite-time mixing in randomly packed porous media. A lamellar-aggregation model built from Lagrangian kinematics predicts Eulerian scalar fields and identifies an early-time shear-driven mixing regime with a specific concentration-variance decay law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates mixing dynamics in porous media at finite times, focusing on how fluid deformation and shear influence solute mixing in randomly packed beds.

Method: The authors use pore-scale lattice-Boltzmann simulations combined with Lagrangian particle tracking. Fluid deformation in randomly packed beds is computed using the moving Protean frame approach introduced by Lester (2018). From the extracted Lagrangian kinematics, they construct a mixing model based on lamellar aggregation and compare its predictions with Eulerian scalar fields from simulations.

Result: The results reveal an early-time mixing regime dominated by shear-driven fluid deformation, in which solute mixing arises from random overlap of diffusive concentration elements. Mixing proceeds slowly and follows the concentration-variance decay σ_c^2 ∼ Pe^{-α/(2α+1)} t^{-1/2}, where Pe is the Peclet number and α is the shear-deformation exponent. This regime occurs when Pe is small relative to the ratio between exponential-mixing and shear-deformation timescales. The analysis also indicates that shear-induced mixing governs homogenization of early-stage reactions at the fluid-solid interface in finite-size random packed beds, typically at moderate Peclet numbers Pe = O(10^2).

Conclusion: Shear-driven fluid deformation controls early-time mixing and the homogenization of early-stage interfacial reactions in finite-size random packed beds at moderate Peclet numbers, and a lamellar-aggregation model based on Lagrangian kinematics can predict the observed Eulerian scalar fields.

Abstract: We investigate mixing dynamics in porous media at finite times, using pore-scale lattice-Boltzmann simulations combined with Lagrangian particle tracking. We compute fluid deformation in randomly packed beds based on the moving Protean frame approach introduced by Lester 2018. From the extracted Lagrangian kinematics, we construct a mixing model based on lamellar aggregation that well predicts the Eulerian scalar fields obtained from simulations. Our results reveal an early time mixing regime dominated by shear-driven fluid deformation, where solute mixing arises from the random overlap of diffusive concentration elements. In this regime, mixing proceeds slowly and follows a temporal decay of concentration variance, $σ_c^2 \sim Pe^{-α/(2α+1)} t^{-1/2}$, where Pe is the Peclet number and $α$ the exponent characterizing shear deformation. This dynamic arises when the Peclet number is small relative to the ratio between the exponential-mixing and shear-deformation timescales. This analysis also demonstrates that shear-induced mixing govern the homogenization of early-stage reactions at the fluid-solid interface in finite-size random packed beds, typically operating at moderate Peclet numbers $Pe =O(10^2)$.

</details>


### [151] [Rheology and Velocity Distribution of Couette Flow for Protoplasmic Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05732)
*Tomoya Souma, Fumitake Kato, Hiroshi Koibuchi*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A combined Langevin and Navier-Stokes simulation of two-dimensional Couette flow between parallel plates is used to model protoplasmic streaming in plant cells, with Finsler-geometry modeling making the fluid-biological material interaction strength depend on an internal degree of freedom. The model reproduces a linear velocity profile, a velocity-distribution peak consistent with laser light scattering observations, and shear-thinning behavior, and links the rheology to a conformal transformation of the fluid-IDOF interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to model protoplasmic streaming in plant cells using two-dimensional Couette flow between parallel plates and to represent fluid-biological material interactions whose strength, such as relative viscosity, varies with an internal degree of freedom associated with the biological materials.

Method: The authors use a combined Langevin and Navier-Stokes simulation together with a Finsler geometry (FG) modeling technique. In this framework, the interaction strength between the fluid and biological materials, represented by quantities such as relative viscosity, varies with an internal degree of freedom (IDOF). The simulations examine the Couette flow velocity profile and velocity distribution and analyze the rheological properties in relation to conformal transformation of the fluid-IDOF interaction.

Result: The simulations show a linear velocity profile along the vertical direction perpendicular to the plates and a peak structure in the velocity distribution consistent with reported laser light scattering experimental observations. They also show shear-thinning behavior typical of non-Newtonian fluids, with viscosity minimized in the boundary region. The rheological properties are connected to the conformal transformation of the interaction between the fluid and the IDOF, and a conformally flat IDOF configuration that is neither anisotropic nor randomly isotropic is introduced as a minimum-energy state characterizing the shear-thinning rheology of two-dimensional Couette flow.

Conclusion: The FG modeling framework can describe two-dimensional Couette flow as a model of protoplasmic streaming and can connect its shear-thinning rheological properties to a conformally flat minimum-energy configuration of the IDOF. The abstract does not provide quantitative validation details or additional experimental comparisons beyond consistency with reported laser light scattering observations.

Abstract: We investigate two-dimensional Couette flow between parallel plates as a model of protoplasmic streaming in plant cells using a combined Langevin and Navier?Stokes simulation. The interaction between the fluid and biological materials is implemented using a Finsler geometry (FG) modeling technique, in which the interaction strength, represented by quantities such as relative viscosity, effectively varies with an internal degree of freedom (IDOF) associated with the biological materials. The simulations demonstrate that the Couette flow exhibits a linear velocity profile along the vertical direction perpendicular to the plates, as well as a peak structure in the velocity distribution consistent with reported experimental observations obtained by laser light scattering. The simulations also demonstrate that the model exhibits shear-thinning behavior typical of non-Newtonian fluids, with the viscosity minimized in the boundary region. Additionally, we find that the rheological properties are connected to the conformal transformation of the interaction between the fluid and the IDOF. Within the FG modeling framework, the conformally flat configuration of the IDOF, which is neither anisotropic nor randomly isotropic, is introduced as a minimum-energy state that characterizes the shear-thinning rheological properties of two-dimensional Couette flow.

</details>


### [152] [A physics-informed neural network method for pressure-driven turbine-blade optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05691)
*Yuan Huang, Francesca di Mare*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A physics-informed inverse method couples flow field and blade geometry to convert experimental surface-pressure targets into turbine-blade shape modifications, achieving low pressure-fitting errors on the LS89 cascade and broadly reproducing measured pressure trends in forward verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to support aerodynamic shape optimisation of turbine blades by translating prescribed surface-pressure distributions, including experimental-origin loading data, into corresponding geometric blade modifications rather than treating geometry and flow separately.

Method: The method treats the flow field and blade geometry as coupled unknowns. It uses pressure-target continuation and alternating flow-geometry updates, with a differentiable representation of blade surfaces so their positions and orientations can be optimised within the current flow field, followed by flow re-adaptation to modified boundaries. The inverse formulation uses two-dimensional steady compressible Euler equations as physics constraints and is evaluated on the LS89 turbine cascade with 28 pressure-target points derived from experimental data, starting from a modified profile and a warm-started flow network.

Result: Final pressure-side and suction-side root-mean-square fitting errors were 1.13% and 2.21% of inlet total pressure, respectively. Independent forward verification with SharC reproduced the principal surface-pressure trends of the experimental measurements, with remaining differences near the trailing edge.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that experimental-origin loading data can be translated into geometric blade modifications through coordinated optimisation of the flow field and moving boundaries.

Abstract: We develop a physics-informed method for aerodynamic shape optimisation of turbine blades, treating the flow field and blade geometry as coupled unknowns. The method translates prescribed surface-pressure distributions into geometric modifications through pressure-target continuation and alternating flow-geometry updates. A differentiable representation of the blade surfaces allows their positions and orientations to be optimised within the current flow field, followed by flow re-adaptation to the modified boundaries. The inverse formulation uses the two-dimensional steady compressible Euler equations as physics constraints and is evaluated on the LS89 turbine cascade using 28 pressure-target points derived from experimental data. Starting from a modified profile and a warm-started flow network, the final pressure-side and suction-side root-mean-square fitting errors are 1.13% and 2.21% of inlet total pressure, respectively. Independent forward verification with SharC reproduces the principal surface-pressure trends of the experimental measurements, with remaining differences near the trailing edge. These results demonstrate the translation of experimental-origin loading data into geometric blade modifications through coordinated optimisation of the flow field and moving boundaries.

</details>


### [153] [On the role of grazing atmospheric particles in hypersonic boundary-layer receptivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05639)
*Sean Dungan, Christoph Brehm*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: High-fidelity simulations and a multiple-scales asymptotic analysis show that submicron atmospheric particles grazing the HIFLIER boundary layer during Mach 6 ascent can generate strong instability wavepackets near the critical layer without direct surface impact, and may explain measured transition onset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates the role of small atmospheric particulates in initiating laminar-turbulent transition on the HIFLIER vehicle during Mach 6 ascent, including whether particles must impact the surface or can trigger transition while grazing the boundary layer.

Method: High-fidelity simulations examine disturbance wavepackets produced by particles on grazing trajectories or trajectories causing impact and rebound at the cone surface. A multiple-scales asymptotic formulation accounts for the extended streamwise region of particle-boundary-layer interaction and identifies the receptivity mechanisms. Root-mean-square disturbance estimates are obtained for submicron grazing particles.

Result: Grazing particles that penetrate less than halfway through the boundary layer generate wavepackets with amplitudes comparable to those from wall-impinging particles. The analysis shows that a grazing particle can produce a strong disturbance response when crossing the critical layer, where the adjoint mode's streamwise-momentum component is largest and the boundary layer is most receptive. RMS estimates for submicron grazing particles predict transition-onset locations close to those measured in flight.

Conclusion: Direct surface impact is not required for atmospheric particulates to excite dynamically significant instability waves. Submicron grazing particles may have contributed significantly to transition onset on HIFLIER at the trajectory condition considered.

Abstract: The role of small atmospheric particulates in the initiation of the laminar-turbulent transition is investigated for the HIFLIER vehicle during Mach 6 ascent condition. High-fidelity simulations are used to examine the disturbance wavepackets generated by particles following either grazing trajectories through the boundary layer or trajectories that lead to impact and rebound at the cone surface. Grazing particles that penetrate less than halfway through the boundary layer are found to generate wavepackets with amplitudes comparable to those produced by wall-impinging particles. To identify the receptivity mechanisms responsible for this behaviour, a multiple-scales asymptotic formulation is developed that accounts for the extended streamwise region over which a particle interacts with the boundary layer. The analysis shows that a grazing particle can generate a strong disturbance response when crossing the critical layer, where the streamwise-momentum component of the adjoint mode is largest and the boundary layer is most receptive. Thus, direct surface impact is not required for atmospheric particulates to excite dynamically significant instability waves. Root-mean-square disturbance estimates for submicron grazing particles predict transition-onset locations close to those measured in flight, suggesting they may have contributed significantly to the onset of transition on HIFLIER at the trajectory condition considered.

</details>


### [154] [Variational learning of energy-stable macroscopic flow equations from microscopic laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01432)
*Pei Ge, Huan Lei*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A generalized variational framework learns energy-stable macroscopic PDEs directly from microscopic physical laws by adjoining auxiliary micro-macro encoder variables, enabling cross-scale law transfer with physical guarantees and demonstrated viscoelastic modeling for polymeric fluids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to construct energy-stable macroscopic PDEs directly from microscopic physical laws instead of fitting PDE evolution for prescribed variables from macroscopic trajectories, thereby enabling systematic law-level transfer across scales and capturing effects beyond empirical closures.

Method: The approach builds an extendable variational structure by adjoining auxiliary variables and seeking autonomous reduced dynamics in an enlarged state space. These variables act as micro-macro encoders with explicit microscopic counterparts. Learning is formulated as sample-wise joint identification of the reduced state representation and variational ingredients from time-discrete data. The resulting PDEs preserve frame indifference, physical constraints, and energy stability by construction, and admit a fluctuation-dissipation-consistent stochastic extension.

Result: Numerical results for non-Newtonian polymeric-fluid hydrodynamics show that the learned models capture viscoelastic responses induced by nonlinear and heterogeneous molecular interactions beyond empirical closures. Quantitative performance metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The method enables construction of physically consistent, energy-stable macroscopic PDEs from microscopic laws with law-level transfer across scales, while preserving frame indifference and physical constraints and supporting a fluctuation-dissipation-consistent stochastic extension.

Abstract: We present a generalized variational approach for constructing energy-stable macroscopic partial differential equations (PDEs) directly from microscopic physical laws. Rather than fitting the PDE evolution for prescribed variables from macroscopic trajectories, we construct an extendable variational structure by adjoining auxiliary variables to seek autonomous reduced dynamics in an enlarged state space. By defining these variables as micro--macro encoders with explicit microscopic counterparts, we cast the learning problem as the sample-wise joint identification of the reduced state representation and the variational ingredients from time-discrete data, enabling systematic law-level transfer across scales. The resulting PDEs preserve frame indifference, physical constraints, and energy stability by construction, and admit a fluctuation--dissipation-consistent stochastic extension. Numerical results for the non-Newtonian hydrodynamics of polymeric fluids show that the learned models capture the viscoelastic responses induced by nonlinear and heterogeneous molecular interactions beyond empirical closures.

</details>


### [155] [Coupled Effects of Biofilm Dynamics and Flow on Hydrogen Transport in a Hydrogen-Generating Fracture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05610)
*Siqin Yu, Yinuo Noah Yao*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: High-fidelity simulations show that biofilm growth in a rough, reactive fracture can reduce apparent permeability by up to 65% and produces two hydrogen outlet-concentration regimes controlled by biofilm permeability and flow, distinguished by a Darcy-scale Reynolds number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biofilm growth and microbial hydrogen consumption are coupled with flow and transport in hydrogen-generating fractures, and these processes can affect hydrogen recovery in stimulated geologic hydrogen systems; the study aims to quantify these coupled effects.

Method: The authors used high-fidelity simulations of flow, biofilm dynamics, and hydrogen transport in a rough fracture with reactive walls, varying flow rate and biofilm permeability and quantifying changes in fracture permeability, biomass accumulation, and outlet hydrogen concentration.

Result: Biofilm growth reduced apparent fracture permeability by up to 65% as biomass occupied the aperture. For highly permeable Type I biofilms, outlet hydrogen concentration evolved non-monotonically because roughness-induced vortices initially restricted transport before biofilm growth suppressed them; low-permeability Type II biofilms produced an approximately monotonic decrease dominated by transport resistance and microbial consumption. The regimes were distinguished by a Darcy-scale Reynolds number (Re_D = u sqrt(K_0)/nu).

Conclusion: The identified regimes indicate that temporal hydrogen concentration variations observed in field studies may help determine intrinsic biofilm permeability; the abstract does not provide field validation or specific parameter values.

Abstract: Biofilm growth in hydrogen-generating fractures modifies fluid flow and hydrogen transport, while microbial activity consumes hydrogen. These coupled processes influence hydrogen recovery in stimulated geologic hydrogen systems. We use high-fidelity simulations to investigate flow, biofilm dynamics, and hydrogen transport in a rough fracture with reactive walls. Flow rate and biofilm permeability were varied to quantify changes in fracture permeability, biomass accumulation, and outlet hydrogen concentration. Biofilm growth reduced apparent fracture permeability by up to 65\% as biomass occupied the aperture. Two hydrogen concentration regimes are identified. For highly permeable biofilms (Type I), outlet concentration evolved non-monotonically as roughness-induced vortices initially restricted hydrogen transport before biofilm growth suppressed them. Low-permeability biofilms (Type II) result in an approximately monotonic outlet concentration decrease dominated by transport resistance and microbial consumption. The regimes were distinguished by a Darcy-scale Reynolds number ($Re_D=u\sqrt{K_0}/ν$). Temporal hydrogen concentration variations in field studies may help determine intrinsic biofilm permeability.

</details>


### [156] [Quantum HHL Algorithm for Solving 1D Linear Advection Equation Using Explicit and Implicit Finite Difference Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01167)
*Batuhan Yalcin, Lucy T. Zhang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops a circuit-level Qiskit HHL algorithm for a one-dimensional periodic advection equation discretized by finite differences, exploits circulant/Fourier structure, benchmarks explicit and implicit schemes in noiseless simulation and on IBM hardware, and finds good noiseless reconstruction but substantial hardware errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to enable HHL-based quantum solution of linear systems arising from implicit time integration of advection equations in quantum CFD, while assessing how state preparation, measurement, matrix conditioning, and current hardware affect practical solver performance.

Method: The PDE is discretized with a classical finite-difference scheme into a linear system. A newly designed circuit-level HHL implementation is assembled from Qiskit components, using Fourier-based matrix evolution for the circulant periodic discretization matrices. It is benchmarked with three explicit schemes and one implicit scheme; explicit schemes use identity coefficient matrices to test state preparation and reconstruction, while the implicit scheme requires a nontrivial linear solve on progressively finer grids. Noiseless HHL solutions are validated against direct classical solves. For hardware execution, the periodic structure permits a Fourier implementation applying reciprocal eigenvalues without phase estimation; results are compared at a shared final time on the IBM Rensselaer Nighthawk processor.

Result: Noiseless reconstructed fields approach the transported profile on resolved grids, while substantial hardware errors remain for both explicit and implicit schemes. Crank-Nicolson remains exceptionally well-conditioned and uses circuit resources similar to the explicit controls, indicating that hardware degradation in these tests was not driven by poor matrix conditioning.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates new means for HHL algorithms to study quantum CFD problems involving advection equations with implicit time integration, and shows that state preparation and measurement affect the practical performance of quantum solvers on current devices.

Abstract: This study develops and implements a Harrow-Hassidim-Lloyd (HHL) quantum algorithm for solving a one-dimensional linear advection equation with periodic boundary conditions. The governing equation is first discretized using a classical finite difference (FD) scheme to obtain a linear system suitable for quantum solution. We present a newly designed, circuit-level implementation of HHL assembled from Qiskit circuit components and exploit the circulant structure of the periodic discretization matrices through Fourier-based matrix evolution. The HHL algorithm is benchmarked with three explicit schemes and one implicit scheme. The implicit scheme requires a nontrivial linear solve arising from the discretized PDE on progressively finer grids, whereas the explicit schemes provide different input fields for testing state preparation and reconstruction with identity coefficient matrices. HHL solution states are validated against direct classical solves in noiseless simulation. For hardware execution, the periodic matrix structure permits a Fourier implementation that applies reciprocal eigenvalues without phase estimation. Results on the IBM Rensselaer Nighthawk processor are compared at a shared final time. Noiseless reconstructed fields approach the transported profile on resolved grids, while substantial hardware errors remain for both explicit and implicit schemes. Crank-Nicolson remains exceptionally well-conditioned and utilizes similar circuit re sources to the explicit controls, proving that hardware degradation in our tests was not driven by poor matrix conditioning. Ultimately, this study shows new means for HHL algorithms to study quantum CFD problems involving advection equations with implicit time integrations, while demonstrating how state preparation and measurement affect the practical performance of quantum solvers on current devices.

</details>


### [157] [Improving the performance of regularized Stokeslet simulations using Rosenbrock time integration methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05428)
*Moslem Uddin, Lisa J. Fauci, Ricardo Cortez, Tommaso Buvoli*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper evaluates linearly implicit Rosenbrock time integration for stiff regularized Stokeslet method simulations of a flexible swimming filament, reporting 20- to 2000-fold speedups over explicit Runge-Kutta methods and scaling laws linking speedup to bending rigidity and slenderness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The method of regularized Stokeslets produces stiff ordinary differential equation systems when modeling material properties such as elasticity and bending rigidity, making explicit time integration computationally expensive.

Method: The authors apply linearly implicit Rosenbrock time integration to the regularized Stokeslet ODE system for a flexible filament swimming under preferred curvature forcing, compare it with explicit Runge-Kutta methods, derive approximate scaling laws for speedup versus bending rigidity and slenderness, and discuss additional performance-improving strategies.

Result: Rosenbrock methods provide increased stability and speedups between twenty and two thousand times relative to explicit Runge-Kutta methods in the studied model problem; approximate scaling laws for speedup as functions of bending rigidity and slenderness are presented.

Conclusion: Linearly implicit Rosenbrock integration is a promising approach for greatly reducing the computational time required for stiff regularized Stokeslet simulations, with further gains possible from additional strategies.

Abstract: The method of regularized Stokeslets (MRS) is widely used to model fluid-structure interactions of microscopic bodies in viscous environments. Specifically, MRS computes the fluid velocity generated by regularized forces distributed on an immersed body, which in turn defines a system of ordinary differential equations with which to evolve the body so that the no-slip condition is enforced. To accurately model material properties such as elasticity and bending rigidity, the resulting ODE system is often stiff and, therefore, computationally expensive to solve with explicit time-integration methods. Here we demonstrate the potential of linearly implicit Rosenbrock (ROS) time integration methods to greatly reduce the computational time required to solve such ODE systems. We focus on the classic model problem of the swimming of a flexible filament driven by preferred curvature forcing. Our findings indicate that, compared to previous MRS simulations, the increased stability of ROS methods allows for speed-ups between twenty and two thousand times over explicit Runge-Kutta (ERK) methods. Moreover, we present approximate scaling laws for this speedup as a function of the key model parameters (bending rigidity and slenderness) at the root of numerical stiffness. Lastly, beyond the basic ROS approach, we discuss several additional strategies that further improve performance.

</details>


### [158] [The kinematic Kelvin-Helmholtz instability-driven dynamo: implications for merging compact objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05880)
*James R. Beattie, Amitava Bhattacharjee, Elias R. Most, Bart Ripperda, Alexander Philippov, Ralf S. Klessen, Salvatore Cielo*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: High-resolution visco-resistive Kelvin-Helmholtz instability dynamo simulations for binary neutron star mergers yield Reynolds-number asymptotic models of magnetic field growth and mean vertical field onset; the inferred dynamo is fast, reaches magnetar-strength fields on about 0.1 ms, and is likely under-resolved in most global merger simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Binary neutron star mergers excite a Kelvin-Helmholtz instability in the shear layer, and the magnetic field can parasitize this instability to drive a powerful dynamo. The paper aims to characterize the Reynolds-number dependence of magnetic field growth rates and mean vertical field dynamo onset times, addressing whether global merger simulations resolve the correct KHI-driven dynamo regime.

Method: The authors use a large ensemble of high-resolution visco-resistive KHI-driven dynamo simulations and construct analytical, asymptotic-in-Reynolds-number models for the magnetic field growth rates and mean vertical field dynamo onset times. Specific numerical parameters and simulation details are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The models suggest that for Re >> 1 the mean vertical field is seeded immediately, kinematic growth rates become asymptotic in Re, and the dynamo leaves the kinematic stage on O(0.1 ms) timescales, readily attaining magnetar-strength fields during this transition.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that most global binary neutron star merger simulations are not time-resolving the correct KHI-driven dynamo regime.

Abstract: Binary neutron star (BNS) mergers excite a Kelvin--Helmholtz instability (KHI) in the shear layer between them. It is parasitized upon by the magnetic field to form a powerful magnetic dynamo. Using a large ensemble of high-resolution visco-resistive KHI-driven dynamo simulations we construct analytical, asymptotic in Reynolds number ($\rm{Re}$) models for the magnetic field growth rates and mean vertical field dynamo onset times. Our models suggest that in the $\rm{Re} \gg 1$ limit, the mean vertical field is seeded immediately, kinematic growth rates become asymptotic in $\rm{Re}$, and the dynamo moves out of the kinematic stage on $\mathcal{O}(0.1\,\rm{ms})$ timescales, readily attaining magnetar-strength fields during this transition. This suggests that most global BNS merger simulations are not time-resolving the correct KHI-driven dynamo regime.

</details>


### [159] [Readable Is Not Steerable: Pre-Registered Tests of Physical Structure in Learned Fluid Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05577)
*Manvendra Modgil*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper tests whether self-supervised world models organize their latents around physical quantities using 2D decaying Navier-Stokes flow in three pre-registered studies, each evaluated once on held-out data. Support is mixed: a JEPA fails to outperform controls, autoencoders show enstrophy in slow latent directions with VICReg effects only partially matching pre-registered criteria, and steerable loudness and shape directions are found inconsistently. The authors conclude that latent content and controllability can come apart.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-supervised world models are often claimed to organize their latents around physical quantities. The paper directly tests this claim for 2D decaying Navier-Stokes flow using pre-registered criteria and held-out evaluations.

Method: Three pre-registered studies on 2D decaying Navier-Stokes flow, each tested once on held-out data.
