Study 1 compares a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) trained to predict the flow against an untrained network of the same architecture, a JEPA trained on non-physical linear dynamics, and a static invariance model that never sees time. Study 2 examines plain autoencoders and VICReg for enstrophy prominence in the slowest unsupervised latent directions and for linear decodability. Study 3 builds steerable directions called dials by routing: the decoder receives overall scale only through a small conditioning code, while another pathway carries a degraded copy of spectral shape; label-free rules are used to find loudness and shape dials, with controls. The program used about 70 logged hours on one consumer GPU, and pre-registrations, deviations, code, and outputs are public.

Result: Study 1 is not supported: the JEPA fails the pre-registered criteria for organizing its latent around fluid quantities better than an untrained network, and a JEPA trained on non-physical linear dynamics and a static invariance model do as well or better. Study 2 is partially supported: plain autoencoders make enstrophy prominent in their slowest unsupervised latent directions, with best r 0.80-0.86 versus 0.38 untrained on fresh seeds and sealed data; adding VICReg lowers this prominence in every comparison, though not by the pre-registered margin for vorticity, while raising linear decodability. Study 3 is partially supported: label-free rules find a loudness dial in all 8 trained models and a shape dial in 3 of 5 fresh seeds on sealed data, against a pre-registered bar of 4; neither control shows a shape dial. In exploration, a supervised direction reading spectral shape almost perfectly moved nothing when steered; during the build, the loudness dial had to be found by its effect on the output, closest to a pure rescaling, not by data variance; for shape, a variance rule and an effect-constrained rule each worked in 3 of 5 seeds.

Conclusion: Across the three studies, what a latent contains and what can be controlled through it come apart. The evidence provides mixed support for the claim that self-supervised world models organize latents around physical quantities: the JEPA result is not supported, while the autoencoder and steerable-direction results are partially supported under the pre-registered criteria.

Abstract: Self-supervised world models are often said to organize their latents around physical quantities. We test this on 2D decaying Navier-Stokes flow in three pre-registered studies, each tested once on held-out data. In Study 1, a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) trained to predict the flow fails the pre-registered criteria for organizing its latent around fluid quantities better than an untrained network of the same architecture (not supported); a JEPA trained on non-physical linear dynamics and a static invariance model that never sees time do as well or better. In Study 2, plain autoencoders that render the field make enstrophy prominent in their slowest unsupervised latent directions (best r 0.80-0.86 vs 0.38 untrained; fresh seeds, sealed data). Adding VICReg lowers this prominence in every comparison, though not by the pre-registered margin for vorticity, while raising the autoencoders' linear decodability (partially supported). In Study 3, we build steerable directions ("dials") by routing: the decoder gets overall scale only through a small conditioning code, while its other pathway carries a degraded copy of spectral shape. Label-free rules find a loudness dial in all 8 trained models and a shape dial in 3 of 5 fresh seeds on sealed data, against a pre-registered bar of 4; neither control shows a shape dial (partially supported). Across the studies, what a latent contains and what can be controlled through it come apart. In exploration, a supervised direction reading spectral shape almost perfectly moved nothing when steered. During the build, the loudness dial had to be found by its effect on the output (closest to a pure rescaling), not by data variance; for shape, a variance rule and an effect-constrained rule each worked in 3 of 5 seeds. The program used about 70 logged hours on one consumer GPU; all pre-registrations, deviations, code and outputs are public.

</details>


### [160] [Stroh--Hamiltonian Framework for Modeling Incompressible Poroelastic Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05554)
*Kinza Arshad, Vincenzo Tibullo*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper extends the Stroh sextic formalism, via its canonical Hamiltonian structure, to Biot poroelasticity under solid incompressibility and zero fluid pressure gradient, deriving canonical equations for reversible poroelastic behavior while acknowledging drained, nondissipative limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Stroh formalism effectively represents anisotropic elasticity equilibrium equations and is useful for multilayered and time-harmonic problems because it integrates physical constraints. The authors aim to extend this framework to constrained Biot poroelastic systems and clarify the interplay between solid incompressibility and fluid-solid coupling.

Method: The approach recognizes that the Stroh formalism aligns with the canonical Hamiltonian structure and uses this to formulate a Hamiltonian-based analysis of Biot's poroelasticity for an incompressible solid with no fluid pressure gradient. It derives canonical equations under these constraints.

Result: The paper provides a novel Hamiltonian-based framework and canonical equations for the specified constrained poroelastic case, enabling clearer interpretation of reversible poroelastic behavior. No quantitative results or experimental validation are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The framework is limited by its assumptions of perfectly drained conditions and neglected dissipative effects, restricting applicability to fully realistic scenarios. Nevertheless, it is presented as a foundational step toward understanding constrained poroelastic systems and as a basis for future extensions to dissipative and nonlinear regimes.

Abstract: The Stroh sextic formalism, developed by Stroh, offers a compelling framework for representing the equilibrium equations in anisotropic elasticity. This approach has proven particularly effective for studying multilayered structures and time-harmonic problems, owing to its ability to seamlessly integrate physical constraints into the analysis. By recognizing that the Stroh formalism aligns with the canonical Hamiltonian structure, this work extends its application to Biot's poroelasticity, focusing on scenarios where the solid material is incompressible and there is no fluid pressure gradient. The study introduces a novel Hamiltonian-based approach to analyze such systems, offering deeper insights into the interplay between solid incompressibility and fluid-solid coupling. A key novelty lies in the derivation of canonical equations under these constraints, enabling clearer interpretations of reversible poroelastic behavior. However, the framework assumes perfectly drained conditions and neglects dissipative effects, which limits its applicability to fully realistic scenarios involving energy loss or complex fluid dynamics. Despite this limitation, the work provides a foundational step toward understanding constrained poroelastic systems and paves the way for future extensions to more general cases, including dissipative and nonlinear regimes.

</details>


### [161] [A wider state, not wider arithmetic, for single-precision time integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05365)
*Michael F. Barad*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: FP32 is faster and more memory-efficient than FP64, but rounding of the stored state imposes an error floor and can cause error to grow as the time step is refined. The paper analyzes this effect for Runge-Kutta and linear multistep methods and proposes storing each state variable as the unevaluated sum of two FP32 numbers, updated with error-free transformations while keeping right-hand-side evaluations in FP32. This restores design-order convergence and FP64-like accuracy in test problems and large-eddy simulations while running 1.6–2.7 times faster than FP64 on GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single precision halves memory and data movement relative to double precision and is often several times faster on accelerators. Its rounding errors are generally harmless except in the stored state, where they can create a time-step-dependent error floor and bias that undermine convergence.

Method: The paper derives a modified equation for Runge-Kutta methods in Shu-Osher form and linear multistep methods that separates stored-state rounding, a forcing proportional to 1/Δt, from truncation error and right-hand-side rounding, which sets a Δt-independent floor. It bounds this forcing for strong-stability-preserving schemes and identifies two mechanisms that keep its sign fixed, causing accumulation as a bias and a turnaround step beyond which refining the step increases error. The remedy stores each state variable as the unevaluated sum of two FP32 numbers, updates it with error-free transformations, and keeps every right-hand-side evaluation in FP32; unlike compensated summation, it requires no rewriting of the scheme.

Result: For a model oscillator, the analysis predicts the error floor and the FP32-to-FP64 ratios of turnaround step and minimum error within 30%. In model problems from geophysical flow, mechanical dynamics, and celestial mechanics with five Runge-Kutta and multistep schemes, the two-float approach restores design-order convergence to the floor. In large-eddy simulations of a high-lift airplane wing on three refined grids up to 544 million cells with time steps Δt, Δt/2, and Δt/4, FP32 overpredicts lift by 2.1%, 4.5%, and 7.7%, while the two-float lift agrees with FP64 within 0.3% on every grid; on GPUs the production solver runs 1.6 to 2.7 times faster than FP64.

Conclusion: Stored-state rounding in FP32 time integration can produce a sign-fixed bias, an error floor, and a turnaround step that worsens with refinement. Representing each state variable as an unevaluated sum of two FP32 numbers with error-free transformations removes this limitation without FP64 arithmetic or scheme rewriting, restoring design-order convergence and FP64-level accuracy while retaining substantial GPU speed advantages over FP64.

Abstract: Single precision (FP32) halves the memory and data movement of double precision (FP64), and on accelerators is often several times faster. Its rounding errors are harmless except in the stored state. A modified equation for Runge-Kutta methods in Shu-Osher form and linear multistep methods separates the rounding of the stored state, a forcing proportional to $1/Δt$, from truncation and from right-hand-side rounding, which sets a $Δt$-independent floor. We bound this forcing for strong-stability-preserving schemes and identify two mechanisms that keep its sign fixed, so it accumulates as a bias: past a turnaround step, refining the step increases the error, in FP64 as in FP32. For a model oscillator the analysis predicts the floor and the FP32-to-FP64 ratios of turnaround step and minimum error within 30%. Our remedy needs no FP64 arithmetic: each state variable is stored as the unevaluated sum of two FP32 numbers, updated with error-free transformations, while every right-hand-side evaluation stays in FP32. Unlike compensated summation, it needs no rewriting of the scheme. In model problems from geophysical flow, mechanical dynamics and celestial mechanics, with five Runge-Kutta and multistep schemes, it restores design-order convergence to the floor. In large-eddy simulations of a high-lift airplane wing on three refined grids, up to 544 million cells, with time steps $Δt$, $Δt/2$ and $Δt/4$, FP32 overpredicts the lift by 2.1, 4.5 and 7.7%, while the two-float lift agrees with FP64 within 0.3% on every grid, and on graphics processing units the production solver runs 1.6 to 2.7 times faster than FP64.

</details>


### [162] [Lagrangian Irreversibility in Stratified Turbulence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05282)
*Mrinal Jyoti Powdel, Samriddhi Sankar Ray*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Using direct numerical simulations of homogeneous Boussinesq turbulence, the study examines Lagrangian power statistics across four velocity-gradient topology classes and three nonzero stratification strengths plus an unstratified reference. It finds that stronger stratification reduces global power variance but increases negative skewness, with topology-dependent and directional responses, more negatively skewed pressure contributions, altered pressure-mediated energy redistribution, and topology-dependent exponential tracer persistence-time tails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stable stratification reorganizes turbulence by suppressing vertical motion and changing the balance between vortical and strain-dominated structures, but how this affects the temporal asymmetry of kinetic-energy exchange along fluid-particle trajectories is not fully understood.

Method: The authors perform direct numerical simulations of homogeneous Boussinesq turbulence and analyze Lagrangian power statistics for four local velocity-gradient topology classes under three nonzero stratification strengths and an unstratified reference case. They also examine horizontal and vertical power components, dynamical contributions including pressure, energy-conditioned pressure statistics, and tracer persistence-time distributions.

Result: With increasing stratification, global power variance decreases while standardized skewness becomes more negative. Axial-strain-dominated regions show the most negative total-power skewness at all stratification strengths, whereas biaxial-strain-dominated regions change from positive to negative skewness. Horizontal and vertical component powers respond differently. The pressure contribution, nearly symmetric without stratification, becomes more negatively skewed with stratification, and energy-conditioned pressure statistics indicate altered pressure-mediated energy redistribution. Tracer persistence-time distributions have exponential tails with time scales that differ across topology classes and respond differently to stratification.

Conclusion: Increasing stratification is accompanied by enhanced asymmetry of Lagrangian power, modified energy redistribution by pressure, and altered temporal sampling of local flow structures by tracers. These co-occurring signatures characterize the response to stratification, although their causal relations remain to be established.

Abstract: Stable stratification reorganizes turbulence, suppressing vertical motion and altering the balance between vortical and strain-dominated flow structures. How these changes affect the temporal asymmetry of kinetic-energy exchange along fluid-particle trajectories remains incompletely understood. Using direct numerical simulations of homogeneous Boussinesq turbulence, we investigate Lagrangian power statistics across four local velocity-gradient topology classes and three nonzero stratification strengths, with an unstratified reference case. With increasing stratification, the global power variance decreases while the standardized skewness becomes more negative, indicating enhanced asymmetry despite weaker fluctuations. Conditioning on local topology reveals distinct responses: axial-strain-dominated regions exhibit the most negative total-power skewness at all stratification strengths, whereas biaxial-strain-dominated regions change sign from positive to negative under stratification. Horizontal and vertical component powers also respond differently, revealing directional dependence. Among the dynamical contributions to power, the pressure contribution, nearly symmetric in the unstratified case, becomes more negatively skewed with increasing stratification. Energy-conditioned pressure statistics further indicate altered pressure-mediated energy redistribution. Finally, tracer persistence-time distributions exhibit exponential tails, with time scales that differ across topology classes and respond differently to stratification. Increasing stratification is thus accompanied by three concurrent changes: enhanced asymmetry of Lagrangian power, modified energy redistribution by pressure, and altered temporal sampling of local flow structures by tracers. These co-occurring signatures characterize the response to stratification, though their causal relations remain to be established.

</details>


### [163] [Computational performance versus numerical accuracy in the acoustic terms of weakly-compressible SPH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02994)
*Giuseppe Bilotta, Alireza Zarei, Roberta Cristofaro, Alexis Hérault, Élie Saikali, Morteza Derakhti*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A new higher-precision explog formulation for the equation of state, its inverse, and the local speed of sound in weakly-compressible SPH improves accuracy, especially in single precision, while being faster on GPU and only marginally slower on CPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate computation of acoustic terms in weakly-compressible SPH is essential for energy conservation, robust solid boundary conditions, and correct open boundary behavior. The classic Cole equation of state with polytropic constant gamma not equal to 1 can cause significant precision loss, particularly in single precision commonly used in GPU implementations.

Method: The authors extend their previous results on optimal state-variable choice by introducing a new higher-precision explog form for all three acoustic terms: the equation of state, its inverse, and the local speed of sound. They assess its numerical accuracy and computational performance across various devices and test cases, and combine it with a rewritten expression for pressure at solid boundary particles in the dummy model.

Result: The explog form evaluates the acoustic terms with a maximum of 2 ULP for arguments less than 0.125 and has higher errors for less than 1% of arguments larger than this threshold. Combined with the rewritten solid-boundary pressure expression, it gives consistently more accurate results, with deviations from the reference pressure profile below 2%, even with rough bottom terrain. It is faster than the naive formulation on GPU and only marginally slower on CPU.

Conclusion: The proposed explog formulation provides more accurate acoustic-term evaluation in weakly-compressible SPH and practical computational performance, with GPU speedup and small CPU overhead.

Abstract: Accurate computation of the acoustic terms in weakly-compressible SPH, including the equation of state, its inverse, and the evaluation of the local speed of sound, is essential to ensure energy conservation, robust solid boundary conditions, and correct open boundary behavior. In the classic Cole's equation of state, using values of the polytropic constant $γ\ne 1$ can lead to significant precision loss, particularly when computations are performed in single precision, as is common in GPU implementations. We extend here our previous results on the optimal choice of state variable by introducing a new higher-precision form for all three acoustic terms, denoted \texttt{explog}, and assess its numerical accuracy and computational performance across a variety of devices and test cases. We find that the new form evaluates the acoustic terms with a maximum ULP of~2 for arguments less than $0.125$, and has higher errors in less than 1\% of the arguments larger than the threshold. Combined with a rewritten expression of the pressure at solid boundary particles in the ``dummy'' model, it provides consistently more accurate results, maintaining deviations from the reference pressure profile to below 2\%, even in presence of a rough bottom terrain. Computationally, the new formulation is also faster than the naive one on GPU, and only marginally slower on CPU.

</details>


### [164] [The Nonlinear Physics and Statistical Field Theory of Twilight Clouds: Hovering Dynamics and Dynamic Inertia Emergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04924)
*Mickaël D. Chekroun, Ilan Koren*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A nonequilibrium statistical field theory links twilight-cloud microphysics and turbulence to cloud-field organization, yielding a reduced non-Markovian stochastic model that captures activation intermittency, screening halos, dynamic inertia, and improved hovering behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Twilight clouds are small, optically faint warm clouds whose droplets fluctuate between haze and activated growth. The paper aims to connect this regime to cloud-field organization through Köhler microphysics, supersaturation transport, and turbulent mixing, and more broadly to identify how slow fields acquire interactions and inertia through the relaxing, diffusive environments they modify.

Method: The authors develop a nonequilibrium statistical field theory. At parcel level they analyze supersaturation tracking and a mixing-induced saddle-node bifurcation. Starting from turbulent transport of supersaturation coupled to droplet growth, they derive a droplet-supersaturation field system and formulate it in the Martin-Siggia-Rose framework. A Gurari-type variational reduction inspired by polaron theory eliminates the fast bath, producing a nonlinear, non-Markovian pseudoparabolic stochastic equation. They compare this with the parent two-field system and with a spatially correlated Markovian closure.

Result: Supersaturation rapidly tracks slow droplet growth with a finite lag. Mixing-induced supersaturation removal drives a saddle-node bifurcation, shifting the activation threshold and supporting intermittent hovering under turbulent fluctuations. Turbulence dresses the slow droplet field, shrinks the forbidden zone below the equilibrium curve, and raises the mixing threshold required to suppress activation. Each droplet population induces a screened supersaturation halo coupling neighboring cloud elements; delayed adjustment generates dynamic inertia and temporally correlated noise. The reduced equation has a bounded frozen-background response at short wavelengths, and its long-wave coupling amplifies contrasts where the dressed slow manifold turns downward. The Markovian closure preserves droplet-radius distributions and cloudlet organization. Near the activation fold, restoring residual-bath fluctuation memory improves recurrent-hovering frequency and patch extent without recalibrating the drift or fitting a correlation time.

Conclusion: The framework identifies how slow fields acquire interactions and inertia through the relaxing, diffusive environments they modify, and it provides a reduced statistical-field description of twilight-cloud organization, activation intermittency, and droplet-supersaturation coupling.

Abstract: Twilight clouds are small, optically faint warm clouds whose droplets fluctuate between haze and activated growth. We develop a nonequilibrium statistical field theory linking this regime to cloud-field organization through Köhler microphysics, supersaturation transport, and turbulent mixing. At parcel level, supersaturation rapidly tracks slow droplet growth with a finite lag. Mixing-induced supersaturation removal drives a saddle-node bifurcation, shifting the activation threshold and supporting intermittent hovering under turbulent fluctuations. Starting from turbulent transport of supersaturation coupled to droplet growth, we derive a droplet-supersaturation field system and formulate it within the Martin-Siggia-Rose framework. A Gurari-type variational reduction inspired by polaron theory eliminates the fast bath. Turbulence dresses the slow droplet field, shrinks the forbidden zone below the equilibrium curve, and raises the mixing threshold required to suppress activation. Each droplet population induces a screened supersaturation halo that couples neighboring cloud elements; its delayed adjustment generates dynamic inertia and temporally correlated noise. The resulting nonlinear, non-Markovian pseudoparabolic stochastic equation has a bounded frozen-background response at short wavelengths. Its long-wave coupling amplifies contrasts where the dressed slow manifold turns downward. Comparisons with the parent two-field system show that a spatially correlated Markovian closure preserves droplet-radius distributions and cloudlet organization. Near the activation fold, restoring residual-bath fluctuation memory improves recurrent-hovering frequency and patch extent without recalibrating the drift or fitting a correlation time. More broadly, the framework identifies how slow fields acquire interactions and inertia through the relaxing, diffusive environments they modify.

</details>


### [165] [Learning the Entropic Relaxation: Stable Coarse-Grid Emulation with Neural Discrete Equilibrium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02458)
*Jose Antonio Lara Benitez, Kareem Hegazy, Anastasis Kratsios, Ivan Dokmanić, Maarten V. de Hoop, Michael W. Mahoney*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Entropically stabilized Neural Discrete Equilibrium (NeurDE) enables stable long-time forecasting of high-Reynolds-number turbulence on extremely coarse grids and can be more stable than a stabilized numerical data-generation solver at those scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific machine learning seeks efficient and stable emulation of complicated, computationally expensive physical systems. Stable numerical solvers are costly because they resolve small spatial and temporal scales that receive and dissipate spectral energy, while learned coarse-grid forecasters remove this dissipative channel, causing unresolved frequencies to reappear as grid-scale aliasing noise. The paper aims to address stable emulation at extremely coarse grids.

Method: The paper uses the Neural Discrete Equilibrium (NeurDE) method with entropic stabilization, described as physically principled control over entropy dynamics that reinforces the physical system's dissipative mechanism. It explores stable emulation at extremely coarse grids. Architectural, training, and implementation details are not provided in the abstract.

Result: Entropically stabilized NeurDE produces stable long-time rollouts for thousands of forecasting steps on large Reynolds number turbulent flows at Re=100,000 with very coarse grids down to 16x16. At these very coarse grid scales, it can be more stable than the authors' stabilized data-generation numerical method. Quantitative error metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Entropic stabilization is presented as a physically principled approach for reinforcing dissipation and controlling aliasing, enabling stable coarse-grid scientific machine learning emulation. At very coarse grid scales, entropically stabilized NeurDE can exceed the stability of the stabilized numerical data-generation method. Other conclusions are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: The ability to emulate complicated and computationally-expensive physical systems in an efficient and stable manner is a popular motivation for scientific machine learning (SciML). Stable numerical solvers tend to be computationally expensive, as they attempt to resolve small (spatial and temporal) scales that receive and dissipate spectral energy. Learned coarse-grid forecasters remove the dissipative channel, allowing unresolved frequencies to re-emerge as grid-scale noise (aliasing). In this paper, we use the Neural Discrete Equilibrium (NeurDE) method to explore stable emulation at extremely coarse grids, demonstrating physically-principled control over entropy dynamics that reinforces the physical system's dissipative mechanism. Entropically-stabilized NeurDE produces stable long-time rollouts for thousands of forecasting steps on large Reynolds number turbulent flows ($\mathrm{Re}=100{,}000$) with very coarse grids (down to $\mathbb{R}^{16\times16}$). At these very coarse grid scales, entropically-stabilized NeurDE can be more stable than even our stabilized data-generation numerical method.

</details>


### [166] [Reconstructing Rayleigh--Bénard flows from temperature-only measurements using the Ensemble Kalman Filter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04795)
*Francesco Fossella, Luca Biferale, Alberto Carrassi, Massimo Cencini, Mathieu Desbrun, Kiwon Um*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A properly inflated Ensemble Kalman Filter reconstructs the full velocity field of two-dimensional turbulent Rayleigh--Bénard convection from noisy, sparse temperature observations alone, numerically verifying Charney's conjecture in the studied regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing the full state of a multiscale chaotic system from observations of only some degrees of freedom is a central data-assimilation problem. In turbulent Rayleigh--Bénard convection, Charney conjectured that temperature measurements alone should suffice to determine the velocity field, but synchronization-based methods such as nudging and direct replacement fail in chaotic regimes because they require measurement of the system's determining modes; velocity belongs to this set, whereas temperature does not.

Method: The authors use an Ensemble Kalman Filter with a proper inflation scheme to exploit flow-dependent cross-covariances between temperature and vorticity and recover velocity from noisy, spatio-temporally sparse temperature data alone. They assess the filter in two chaotic regimes of two-dimensional bulk convection through a scale-by-scale spectral analysis, considering observation of the whole temperature field, equispaced observation networks at varying density, and observations confined to a subdomain.

Result: When the whole temperature field is observed, both fields synchronize with the true state at all resolved scales, and the temperature estimate becomes more accurate than the measurements themselves for sufficiently frequent observations. Equispaced observation networks preserve full-spectrum synchronization down to a threshold density, below which only the largest scales remain synchronized; the same limitation occurs when observations are confined to a subdomain.

Conclusion: Charney's conjecture is numerically verified in two-dimensional turbulent convection when assimilation is performed with a properly calibrated EnKF.

Abstract: Reconstructing the full state of a multiscale chaotic system from observations of only a subset of its degrees of freedom is a central problem in data assimilation. Turbulent Rayleigh--Bénard convection offers a paradigmatic example, for which Charney conjectured that temperature measurements alone should suffice to determine the velocity field. Synchronization-based methods such as nudging and direct replacement nonetheless fail in chaotic regimes, because they require the measurement of the determining modes of the system, a set to which the velocity belongs but the temperature does not. Here we show that the Ensemble Kalman Filter (EnKF), equipped with a proper inflation scheme, removes this asymmetry by exploiting the flow-dependent cross-covariances between temperature and vorticity, and recovers the velocity from noisy and spatio-temporally sparse temperature data alone. We assess the filter in two chaotic regimes of two-dimensional bulk convection by means of a scale-by-scale spectral analysis. When the whole temperature field is observed, both fields synchronize with the true state at all resolved scales, with the temperature estimate becoming even more accurate than the measurements themselves for sufficiently frequent observations. Equispaced observation networks preserve this full-spectrum synchronization down to a threshold density, below which only the largest scales remain synchronized, as also happens when observations are confined to a subdomain. We conclude that Charney's conjecture is numerically verified in two-dimensional turbulent convection when the assimilation is performed using a properly calibrated EnKF.

</details>


### [167] [Designing turbulence through hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04706)
*Flavio Tuteri, André Freitas*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A planar chain of rigid pendula with geometrically decreasing lengths and masses, coupled by nearest-neighbor constraints, reproduces key turbulence signatures without fluid incompressibility or vortex dynamics, including a constant-flux-like cascade, intermittency, finite dissipation as viscosity vanishes, and fluctuation-dissipation noise amplification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the unclear physical ingredients that produce turbulence. In fluids, the constant-flux energy cascade, intermittency, finite dissipation in the vanishing-viscosity limit, and loss of determinism in the inviscid limit always coexist with incompressibility, spatial structure, and vortex dynamics, making their separate roles difficult to identify.

Method: The authors use a hierarchy of inertia: a planar chain of rigid pendula whose lengths and masses decrease geometrically, coupled only through nearest-neighbor rigid constraints and the purely geometric nonlinearity of those constraints. The hierarchy is tunable and the dynamics can be realized mechanically or electrically. The abstract does not provide further experimental or numerical details.

Result: The chain develops a far-from-equilibrium cascade that replaces thermal equipartition, with statistically local and time-irreversible energy transfer despite intrinsically nonlocal coupling. Its fluctuations are intermittent, but a hidden scale symmetry restores self-similarity. As viscosity vanishes, the dissipation rate saturates at a finite value, and thermal noise consistent with fluctuation-dissipation balance is amplified from the smallest to the largest scales within a fraction of a large-scale period.

Conclusion: A hierarchy of inertia coupled by geometric rigid constraints is sufficient to produce several central turbulence phenomena without fluid-specific ingredients such as incompressibility and vortex dynamics. Because the hierarchy can be tuned and realized mechanically or electrically, turbulence becomes a property that can be designed and measured in the laboratory.

Abstract: Turbulence is defined by a set of emergent and singular phenomena: a constant-flux energy cascade, intermittency, a dissipation rate that survives the vanishing of viscosity, and the spontaneous loss of determinism of the inviscid limit. Which physical ingredients produce them remains unclear, because in fluids they always come together with incompressibility, spatial structure and vortex dynamics. Here we show that a hierarchy of inertia, coupled by the purely geometric nonlinearity of rigid constraints, is enough. A planar chain of rigid pendula whose lengths and masses decrease geometrically, coupled only through nearest-neighbor constraints, develops a far-from-equilibrium cascade that replaces thermal equipartition, with statistically local and time-irreversible energy transfer despite intrinsically nonlocal coupling. Its fluctuations are intermittent, yet self-similarity is restored by a hidden scale symmetry. As the viscosity vanishes, the dissipation rate saturates at a finite value, and thermal noise consistent with fluctuation-dissipation balance is amplified from the smallest to the largest scales within a fraction of a large-scale period. Because the hierarchy can be tuned and the same dynamics can be realized mechanically or electrically, turbulence becomes a property that can be designed and measured in the laboratory.

</details>


### [168] [Pore-scale mechanisms of capillary imbibition in cement-based materials revealed by direct numerical simulations in a realistic FIB-SEM microstructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01901)
*Luka Malenica, Zhidong Zhang, Ueli Angst*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Pore-resolved direct numerical simulations of spontaneous imbibition in a FIB-SEM-reconstructed cementitious microstructure provide the first direct view of transient air-water interface dynamics in capillary porosity, revealing heterogeneous wetting fronts, progressive air entrapment, and scale-dependent multiphase effects relevant to steel-concrete interface moisture and corrosion analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Moisture transport in cement-based materials involves transient multiphase flow through irregular pore networks, and evolving air-water interfaces influence liquid invasion and gas displacement. These nanometre-to-micrometre and nanosecond-to-microsecond dynamics are inaccessible to direct experimental observation at such scales, motivating pore-resolved simulation.

Method: The study uses pore-resolved, image-based direct numerical simulations (DNS) of spontaneous imbibition in a FIB-SEM-reconstructed cementitious microstructure, resolving air-water interface dynamics across nanometre-to-micrometre spatial scales and nanosecond-to-microsecond timescales. It combines three-dimensional simulations with controlled two-dimensional analyses and examines moisture conditions at the steel-concrete interface.

Result: The three-dimensional simulations show highly heterogeneous wetting-front propagation, continuous interface reconfiguration, and progressive air entrapment. Local dynamics depend on evolving interface curvature and capillary-pressure fields rather than characteristic pore size alone. Significant air entrapment occurs within capillary pores even under perfectly water-wetting conditions, progressively reducing the saturation rate, while trapped-air cluster capillary pressure depends strongly on local interface geometry rather than gas volume alone. Two-dimensional analyses show that capillary, viscous, inertial, and gravitational effects are strongly scale-dependent, and comparison with three-dimensional simulations highlights the importance of out-of-plane interface curvature and pore-network connectivity. The framework also resolves moisture conditions at the steel-concrete interface. Quantitative values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Pore-resolved DNS provides a direct view of transient imbibition in realistic cementitious capillary porosity and demonstrates that interface geometry, pore-network connectivity, and scale-dependent multiphase balances govern wetting and air entrapment. The framework shows potential for corrosion-relevant pore-scale analysis of moisture at the steel-concrete interface.

Abstract: Moisture transport in cement-based materials involves transient multiphase flow through highly irregular pore networks, where evolving air--water interfaces strongly influence liquid invasion and gas displacement. Here, pore-resolved, image-based direct numerical simulations (DNS) are used to investigate spontaneous imbibition in a FIB--SEM-reconstructed cementitious microstructure, resolving air--water interface dynamics at nanometre-to-micrometre spatial scales and nanosecond-to-microsecond timescales. As these dynamics remain inaccessible to direct experimental observation at such scales, the simulations provide, to the best of our knowledge, the first direct view of transient imbibition within the capillary porosity of a realistic cementitious microstructure.
  The three-dimensional simulations reveal highly heterogeneous wetting-front propagation, continuous interface reconfiguration, and progressive air entrapment, showing that local dynamics depend on evolving interface curvature and capillary-pressure fields rather than on characteristic pore size alone. Significant air entrapment develops even within the capillary pores and under perfectly water-wetting conditions, progressively reducing the saturation rate, while the capillary pressure of trapped-air clusters depends strongly on local interface geometry rather than gas volume alone. Controlled two-dimensional analyses show that the balance between capillary, viscous, inertial, and gravitational effects is strongly scale-dependent. Comparison with the three-dimensional simulations highlights the importance of out-of-plane interface curvature and pore-network connectivity. The framework also resolves moisture conditions at the steel--concrete interface, illustrating its potential for corrosion-relevant pore-scale analysis.

</details>


### [169] [A flux-balanced diffuse-interface kinetic model for rarefied gas-solid boundaries with arbitrary absorption probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04622)
*Chujin Qin, Dongke Sun*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops a diffuse-interface kinetic model (DIKM) for rarefied gas transport and gas–solid interactions on a single fixed Eulerian mesh, validates it across stationary and moving boundaries, and couples it to solid evolution and thin-film growth simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rarefied gas–solid interactions determine mechanical loads, heat transfer, and material exchange in microscale devices and low-pressure processing, but modeling is challenging when gas non-equilibrium effects and complex moving boundaries must be resolved together.

Method: DIKM uses one kinetic formulation with a smooth order parameter for solid geometry and a localized molecular flux operator that distinguishes incident and reflected distributions in the interface region and specifies mass, momentum, and energy exchange, including prescribed surface absorption. Matched asymptotic analysis recovers a partially absorbing diffuse-reflection boundary condition in the sharp-interface limit. Discretization uses Strang splitting with an order-parameter-weighted discrete unified gas kinetic scheme for transport and a positivity-preserving interfacial source update; final simulations evolve the order parameter by a Cahn–Hilliard equation with degenerate mobility.

Result: Benchmarks over planar and curved, stationary and moving boundaries reproduce rarefaction-dependent velocity slip and wall heat transfer, absorption-driven flow, and feedback between gas forces and solid translation and rotation. Final simulations show thin-film growth with distinct surface profiles at different vapor incidence angles; quantitative details are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: By connecting implicit geometry to molecular surface exchange, DIKM provides a basis for coupling rarefied gas dynamics to solid motion and material evolution.

Abstract: Rarefied gas--solid interactions determine mechanical loads, heat transfer, and material exchange in microscale devices and low-pressure processings. Modeling these interactions becomes challenging when gas non-equilibrium effect and complex moving boundaries must be resolved together. We develop a diffuse-interface kinetic model (DIKM) that describes rarefied gas transport and gas--solid interactions in one kinetic formulation on a single fixed Eulerian mesh. A smooth order parameter represents solid geometry, while a localized molecular flux operator incorporates boundary interactions directly into the kinetic equation by distinguishing incident and reflected distributions in the interface region and specifying their mass, momentum, and energy exchange, including prescribed surface absorption. Matched asymptotic analysis shows that the diffuse formulation recovers the corresponding partially absorbing diffuse-reflection boundary condition for smooth stationary or moving surfaces in the sharp-interface limit. Discretization uses Strang splitting, combining an order-parameter-weighted discrete unified gas kinetic scheme for the transport substep with a positivity-preserving update of the interfacial source. Benchmarks over planar and curved, stationary and moving boundaries reproduce rarefaction-dependent velocity slip and wall heat transfer, absorption-driven flow, and feedback between gas forces and solid translation and rotation. In final simulations, the order parameter evolves by a Cahn--Hilliard equation with degenerate mobility, and thin-film growth exhibits distinct surface profiles at different vapor incidence angles. By connecting implicit geometry to molecular surface exchange, DIKM provides a basis for coupling rarefied gas dynamics to solid motion and material evolution.

</details>


### [170] [Fluid memory beyond creeping flow: a history kernel for a sphere at finite Reynolds numbers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04606)
*M. D. Łasica, J. G. M. Kuerten, S. K. F. Stoter*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper extracts the history-force kernel for a fixed sphere in uniform unsteady flow from axisymmetric Navier-Stokes simulations over initial Reynolds numbers 0.2–200, extends it with far-wake asymptotics, and shows improved history-force predictions across several flow histories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting the history force on a sphere beyond the creeping-flow limit is challenging; existing formulations were derived or calibrated for selected flow histories and have limited evidence of predictive accuracy under more general conditions.

Method: The authors systematically extract the history kernel from axisymmetric Navier-Stokes simulations of finite-duration acceleration and deceleration ramps at initial Reynolds numbers from 0.2 to 200. The resulting kernel surface recovers the Basset limit at short lags and in the creeping-flow limit and is continued beyond resolved lags using far-wake asymptotics.

Result: At higher Reynolds numbers, the kernel becomes non-monotonic at intermediate lags, associated with delayed boundary-layer and wake adjustment. For the tested cases, the kernel substantially improves history-force predictions for finite ramps, oscillatory forcing, and non-periodic composite velocity histories relative to the considered literature formulations, without case-specific adjustment.

Conclusion: The findings indicate that lag and Reynolds number capture the dominant memory response of the history force for the cases tested.

Abstract: Predicting the history force on a sphere beyond the creeping-flow limit remains challenging. Existing formulations have been derived or calibrated for selected flow histories, with limited evidence of their predictive accuracy under more general conditions. We develop a systematic approach to extract the history kernel of a fixed sphere in spatially uniform, unsteady flow from axisymmetric Navier-Stokes simulations of finite-duration acceleration and deceleration ramps at initial Reynolds numbers from 0.2 to 200. The resulting kernel surface recovers the Basset limit at short lags and in the creeping-flow limit and is continued beyond the resolved lags using far-wake asymptotics. At higher Reynolds numbers, the kernel develops non-monotonic behaviour at intermediate lags, associated with delayed adjustment of the boundary layer and wake. For the cases tested, the present kernel substantially improves history-force predictions for finite ramps, oscillatory forcing and non-periodic composite velocity histories relative to the literature formulations considered, without case-specific adjustment. These findings indicate that lag and Reynolds number capture the dominant memory response.

</details>


### [171] [Transitional Fluid-Ablation Interactions on a Compression Ramp at Mach 8](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04443)
*Ata Onur Başkaya, Sean Dungan, Stefan Hickel, Christoph Brehm*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: DNS is used to study Mach 8 flow over a 15° compression ramp with a low-temperature ablator, focusing on how fluid–ablation interaction affects boundary-layer instability and transition. Two independently developed solvers with ablation models are compared, and numerical experiments examine accelerated recession and larger perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind-tunnel experiments at these conditions exhibit characteristic heating streaks, and the paper aims to analyze fluid–ablation interaction effects on instability growth and transition of the high-speed boundary layer.

Method: DNS is performed for laminar flow over an inert ramp to replicate the heating streaks, with perturbations informed by stability calculations. The ramp is then replaced by a low-temperature ablator, and ablation models implemented in two independently developed fluid dynamics solvers are used to examine streak–surface interaction. Numerical experiments assess accelerating recession and higher-amplitude perturbations.

Result: The two solvers show excellent agreement in heat fluxes, mass blowing rates, and recession patterns. The study demonstrates accurate predictions of ablative recession and reports novel findings on how ablation influences perturbation evolution for this configuration.

Conclusion: The results support accurate simulation of ablative recession and indicate that fluid–ablation interaction affects perturbation evolution in this Mach 8 compression-ramp configuration. Specific transition-onset metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Direct numerical simulations (DNS) are performed for a Mach 8 flow over a 15 degree compression ramp undergoing ablation to analyze fluid-ablation interaction (FAI) effects on instability growth and transition of the high-speed boundary layer. Wind-tunnel experiments at these conditions show characteristic heating streaks which are first replicated for laminar flow over an inert ramp. Perturbations superimposed on the laminar baseflow are informed by stability calculations. The ramp is then replaced by a low-temperature ablator in our DNS and the interaction of the streaks with the ablating surface is examined. Ablation models implemented in two independently-developed fluid dynamics solvers are used to study this problem. Overall, both solvers have shown an excellent agreement regarding heat fluxes, mass blowing rates, and recession patterns. Numerical experiments are performed to assess the impact of accelerating recession and of higher amplitude perturbations. We demonstrate accurate predictions of ablative recession and present novel findings regarding its influence on the perturbation evolution for this configuration.

</details>


### [172] [Flow and transport characteristics of hydrogen in a square-spiral Knudsen pump with coupled thermal transpiration and Poiseuille flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04288)
*Yawei Huang, Jianjun Ye, Junlong Xie, Jian Xiao, Dinggen Li, Xuesong He*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A spiral-structured Knudsen pump is numerically studied to determine how the numbers of serial and parallel microchannels affect thermal-transpiration-driven gas transport. Serial staging reduces microchannel pressure load and improves performance, while parallel staging reduces pressure loading and enhances rarefaction; both configurations improve transport relative to fewer stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knudsen pumps based on thermal transpiration are promising for gas separation and delivery, but their performance and operational stability are strongly affected by structural design, system integration, and manufacturing complexity. The abstract identifies a need for efficient and manufacturable multi-stage configurations.

Method: The authors propose a novel spiral-structured Knudsen pump. In the microchannels, a temperature gradient drives gas from low- to high-temperature regions, inducing pressure rise and thermal transpiration; in the main channel, a pressure gradient drives gas from high- to low-temperature regions, forming Poiseuille flow. Numerical methods are used to investigate the flow mechanisms and evaluate varying numbers of serial and parallel microchannels. Specific numerical schemes, boundary conditions, geometries, and operating conditions are not provided in the abstract.

Result: Bends in the main channel cause nonlinear pressure and temperature variations and flow deceleration in curved regions. Increasing the number of serial channels reduces the pressure load across the microchannel and improves overall transport: a ten-stage serial configuration improves performance by 3.35% over the four-stage case, reaching 3.10E10-7 kg/(m*s) as reported. Increasing the number of parallel microchannels reduces pressure loading while enhancing gas rarefaction and thermal transpiration: a five-stage parallel configuration improves performance by 70.80% over the two-stage case, reaching 3.07E10-7 kg/(m*s).

Conclusion: The spiral-structured multi-stage Knudsen pump can be tuned through serial and parallel microchannel counts to enhance gas transport. The findings are presented as guidance for the design and fabrication of high-performance Knudsen pumps; the abstract does not report experimental validation or detailed manufacturing outcomes.

Abstract: Gas transport driven by thermal transpiration under temperature gradients, known as a Knudsen pump, has shown strong potential in gas separation and delivery applications. However, its performance and operational stability are strongly affected by structural design, system integration, and manufacturing complexity, highlighting the need for efficient and manufacturable multi-stage configurations. In this study, a novel spiral-structured Knudsen pump is proposed. Within the microchannels, a temperature gradient drives gas from the low- to high-temperature region, inducing pressure rise and thermal transpiration. In the main channel, a pressure gradient drives gas from the high- to low-temperature region, forming Poiseuille flow. Numerical methods are employed to investigate the flow mechanisms, and the effects of varying the numbers of series and parallel microchannels on gas transmission behavior are evaluated. The results indicate that bends of the main channel lead to nonlinear variations in pressure and temperature, as well as flow deceleration in the curved regions. Increasing the number of serial channels reduces the pressure load across the microchannel, thereby enhancing the overall transport performance. For instance, a ten-stage serial configuration improves performance by 3.35% compared with the four-stage case, reaching 3.10E10-7 kg/(m*s). In contrast, increasing the number of parallel microchannels reduces pressure loading while enhancing gas rarefaction, thereby strengthening thermal transpiration. A five-stage parallel configuration yields an 70.80% improvement over the two-stage case, reaching 3.07E10-7 kg/(m*s). These results provide valuable guidance for the design and fabrication of high-performance Knudsen pumps.

</details>


### [173] [High-order compact ALE gas-kinetic scheme on three-dimensional unstructured meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05095)
*Yibo Wang, Xing Ji, Liang Pan*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A compact high-order gas-kinetic scheme is developed for compressible ALE computations on three-dimensional unstructured meshes, using a memory-reduction reconstruction strategy, GENO-based oscillation suppression, and multi-GPU acceleration; numerical tests confirm its accuracy and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ALE computations on moving three-dimensional unstructured meshes require repeated recalculation of geometric information, and high-order spatial reconstruction demands many matrix inversions, reducing computational efficiency. The paper aims to develop an efficient compact high-order ALE gas-kinetic scheme for such computations.

Method: The scheme uses a memory-reduction reconstruction strategy to construct a quadratic polynomial on unstructured meshes. Quadratic terms are obtained by least-squares using average gradient values of the cell and its neighbors, and linear terms are also determined by least-squares; on 3D unstructured meshes this requires storing a 3x3 coefficient matrix and four least-squares procedures. GENO reconstruction and a gradient compression factor suppress numerical oscillations. A fully explicit multi-GPU code is implemented with CUDA and MPI using cell-level parallelism.

Result: Numerical examples include flows with strong discontinuities, pitching wings, and helicopter rotors. The results confirm excellent performance of the compact ALE scheme on unstructured meshes; no quantitative accuracy or speedup values are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The compact high-order ALE gas-kinetic scheme is accurate and robust for compressible ALE computations on three-dimensional unstructured meshes, and the cell-level multi-GPU acceleration framework supports efficient computation.

Abstract: In this paper, a compact high-order gas-kinetic scheme (HGKS) is developed for compressible arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE) computations on three-dimensional unstructured meshes. To achieve high-order spatial accuracy, a large number of matrix inversions are required for spatial reconstruction. In ALE computations, the mesh moves and the geometric information must be recalculated at each time step, which reduces computational efficiency. To improve efficiency, a memory-reduction reconstruction strategy is employed to construct a quadratic polynomial on unstructured meshes. By differentiating the candidate polynomial, the quadratic terms are obtained by the least-squares method using the average gradient values of the cell itself and its neighboring cells. The linear terms of the polynomial are also determined by the least-squares method. On three-dimensional unstructured meshes, a $3\times3$ coefficient matrix must be stored, and four least-squares procedures are required to construct a quadratic polynomial. To suppress numerical oscillations in flows with discontinuities, generalized essentially non-oscillatory (GENO) reconstruction and a gradient compression factor are adopted. To accelerate the computation, a multi-GPU accelerated code is implemented using Compute Unified Device Architecture (CUDA) and Message Passing Interface (MPI). The current scheme is fully explicit, and the acceleration framework can be developed based on cell-level parallelism. Numerical examples are presented to evaluate the accuracy and robustness of the scheme, including flows with strong discontinuities, pitching wings, and helicopter rotors. Numerical results confirm the excellent performance of the compact ALE scheme on unstructured meshes.

</details>


### [174] [From Sea Surface Height to Velocity - A Machine Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04270)
*Yaswanth Reddy Yaradoddi, Jinbo Wang, Elyes Serghine*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A U-Net trained on high-resolution LLC4320 simulation fields predicts surface and 50 m horizontal velocities and derivative diagnostics from SSH plus environmental inputs, outperforming geostrophic estimates and supporting transfer to SWOT applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring upper-ocean velocity from altimetric sea surface height is challenging at scales below 50 km, which SWOT now resolves; conventional approaches rely on geostrophic or cyclogeostrophic balance.

Method: The authors trained a U-Net encoder–decoder on hourly fields from the pre-SWOT Cape Basin extraction of the LLC4320 ocean simulation at 1.5-km grid spacing (11.8–15.8°E, 41–45°S). Inputs were SSH, sea surface temperature, and wind stress; targets were horizontal velocities at the surface and 50 m. They also tested training strategies.

Result: At 50 m, the best model achieved a speed RMSE of 0.049 m/s, 29% lower than the geostrophic approximation; predicted relative vorticity correlated at 0.93 versus 0.75 for geostrophy, and divergence correlated at 0.66, which geostrophic velocity cannot capture because it is nondivergent. Training tests found that SSH gradients outperform raw SSH, wind stress is the most useful covariate for total velocity prediction, SST benefits vary seasonally, and including vorticity and divergence in the loss improves accuracy.

Conclusion: Deep learning can predict the full velocity vector and derivative-based diagnostics, including spectral energy flux, from SSH and environmental data, offering a path to transfer high-fidelity simulation physics to SWOT applications where kilometer-scale SSH snapshots contain superposed balanced and unbalanced signals.

Abstract: Inferring upper-ocean velocity from altimetric sea surface height (SSH) remains challenging below 50~km, scales now resolved by the Surface Water and Ocean Topography (SWOT) mission. Conventional methods rely on geostrophic or cyclogeostrophic balance. Here, we demonstrate that deep learning can predict the full velocity vector from SSH and environmental data and recover derivative-based diagnostics, including spectral energy flux. We trained a U-Net encoder--decoder on hourly fields from the pre-SWOT Cape Basin extraction of the LLC4320 ocean simulation, with 1.5-km grid spacing ($11.8$--$15.8^\circ$E, $41$--$45^\circ$S). Inputs were SSH, sea surface temperature (SST), and wind stress; targets were horizontal velocities at the surface and 50~m, where wind influence is weaker. At 50~m, the best model achieves a speed RMSE of $0.049\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, 29% lower than the geostrophic approximation. Predicted relative vorticity has a correlation of 0.93, compared with 0.75 for the geostrophic approximation. The model also recovers divergence with a correlation of 0.66, whereas geostrophic velocity is nondivergent by definition. Tests of training strategies yield four findings: SSH gradients outperform raw SSH as inputs; wind stress is the most useful covariate for total velocity prediction; SST benefits vary seasonally; and including vorticity and divergence in the loss improves accuracy. These results offer a path for transferring high-fidelity simulation physics to SWOT applications, where kilometer-scale SSH snapshots contain superposed balanced and unbalanced signals.

</details>


### [175] [Physics-Informed Identification of Tensegrity Prestress across Taut-Slack Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04007)
*Sandesh Lamsal, Sumit Kumar, Rubi Bhandari, Subhamoy Sen, Andrzej Rutkiewicz, Leszek Małyszko, Landolf Rhode-Barbarigos*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper formulates and validates an inverse, physics-informed neural-network method for identifying prestress-related member parameters in tensegrity structures from sparse dynamic measurements, including records with taut-slack transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prestress controls tensegrity stability, stiffness, and dynamics but is not directly observable from sparse measurements and may decrease over service life; strong excitation can slacken cables and make dynamics nonsmooth. Existing identification methods are limited to the taut regime, and physics-informed networks for tensegrities address only the forward problem.

Method: The inverse problem is solved by training neural networks on the equation-of-motion residual in constrained Cartesian coordinates, where the mass matrix is constant and force densities enter linearly. Force densities, damping, and axial stiffness are trainable parameters, and the tension-only cable law is retained. Fisher-information analysis is used before inversion to determine which member parameters a record can identify. Tests use simulated planar tensegrity frames with up to five modules and a three-dimensional prism, plus measured records from a biomimetic leg and a full-scale tensegrity simplex.

Result: For the one-module frame, identifiable force densities are recovered within 1.5% from noisy accelerations at half the free coordinates, with and without taut-slack transitions; records with transitions determine members that taut records leave undetermined. For the five-module, 16-cable two-span bridge frame, median force-density error rises to 4.2%. On measured records, an identified spring stiffness is within 0.1% of its independently measured value on average, and identified simplex prestress at 13 levels agrees with cable force transducers to 2.9% root-mean-square.

Conclusion: The proposed inverse physics-informed framework can identify tensegrity force densities, damping, and axial stiffness under both taut and slackening/nonsmooth conditions, with Fisher-information analysis providing pre-inversion identifiability guidance and validation on simulated and measured structures.

Abstract: Prestress governs the stability, stiffness, and dynamic behavior of tensegrity structures, but it is not directly observable from sparse structural measurements, and it may decrease during service life. Under strong excitation, the cables may slacken and the dynamics become nonsmooth. Existing identification methods are limited to the taut regime, and physics-informed networks for tensegrities solve only the forward problem of predicting the response from known member parameters. This study formulates the inverse problem. Neural networks are trained on the equation-of-motion residual in constrained Cartesian coordinates, where the mass matrix is constant and the force densities enter linearly. The force densities, damping, and axial stiffness are trainable parameters, and the tension-only cable law is kept. A Fisher-information analysis shows, before inversion, which member parameters a record can determine. The method is tested on simulated planar tensegrity frames with up to five modules and a three-dimensional prism, and on measured records of a biomimetic leg and a full-scale tensegrity simplex. On the one-module frame, the identifiable force densities are recovered within 1.5% from noisy accelerations at half the free coordinates, on records with and without taut-slack transitions. Records with transitions determine members that taut records leave undetermined. On the five-module frame, a 16-cable two-span bridge, the median force-density error rises moderately, to 4.2%. On measured records, an identified spring stiffness is within 0.1% of its independently measured value on average, and the identified simplex prestress at 13 levels agrees with its cable force transducers to 2.9% root-mean-square.

</details>


### [176] [Dual-Band Mid-Infrared Imaging for Measurement of Fuel Concentration and Temperature in a Supersonic Cavity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03341)
*Loren C. Hahn, Erik L. Braun, Brendan J. McGann, Katherine C. Opacich, Timothy M. Ombrello*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A dual-band mid-infrared absorption imaging method measured spatially resolved ethylene concentration and temperature in a Mach-2 cavity flow, matching extractive equivalence-ratio measurements and enabling simpler full-field diagnostics than hyperspectral imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide spatially resolved measurements of ethylene concentration and temperature in a Mach-2 cavity flow using simpler spectral instrumentation than hyperspectral imaging while retaining flexibility for time-resolved flow measurements.

Method: The method used narrowband filters chosen for their differing in-band temperature sensitivities of ethylene absorption, a broadband infrared source, and a high-frame-rate mid-wave infrared camera. Temperature-dependent, filter-weighted absorption cross-sections were used to derive two-dimensional ethylene number density and temperature fields. Measurements covered cavity, curtain, and discrete fuel-injection configurations, including cases with a quasi-steady shock leading edge near the cavity. A complementary single-band implementation used a temperature-independent filter for instantaneous ethylene concentration fields at 100 Hz.

Result: Cavity equivalence ratio measurements agreed well with independent extractive measurements and reproduced trends previously observed with hyperspectral imaging. The single-band implementation revealed intermittent reductions in cavity equivalence ratio associated with plume deflection by the shock train leading edge.

Conclusion: The dual-band approach provides quantitative full-field measurements with substantially simpler spectral instrumentation than hyperspectral imaging while retaining flexibility for time-resolved flow measurements.

Abstract: A dual-band mid-infrared absorption imaging method is demonstrated for spatially resolved measurement of ethylene concentration and temperature in a Mach-2 cavity flow. The method used narrowband filters selected to exploit the differing in-band temperature sensitivities of ethylene absorption, together with a broadband infrared source and high-frame-rate mid-wave infrared camera. Temperature-dependent, filter-weighted absorption cross-sections were used to measure two-dimensional ethylene number density and temperature fields. Measurements were obtained for cavity, curtain, and discrete fuel-injection configurations, including cases with the leading edge of a quasi-steady shock near the cavity. Cavity equivalence ratio measurements agreed well with independent extractive measurements and reproduced trends previously observed using hyperspectral imaging. A complementary single-band implementation using a temperature-independent filter provided instantaneous ethylene concentration fields at 100 Hz and revealed intermittent reductions in cavity equivalence ratio associated with plume deflection by the shock train leading edge. The dual-band approach provides quantitative full-field measurements with substantially simpler spectral instrumentation than hyperspectral imaging while retaining flexibility for time-resolved flow measurements.

</details>


### [177] [Dynamics of a two-dimensional microswimmer near a slippery boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03243)
*Takahiro Kanazawa, Darren Crowdy*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A two-dimensional point-singularity microswimmer that does not self-propel in unbounded fluid is analyzed near a viscosity discontinuity and a partial-slip wall. A single boundary parameter organizes distinct modes: trapping, gliding, and oscillatory drifting, with analogous critical transitions in both settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microswimmers near walls and interfaces can switch between qualitatively different modes of motion depending on the slip properties of neighbouring boundaries. The paper aims to study this theoretically and to connect the resulting behavior with slip-induced transitions reported for three-dimensional swimmers.

Method: A two-dimensional point-singularity model of a microswimmer, which does not self-propel in unbounded fluid, is placed near a flat viscosity discontinuity between two fluids with viscosity ratio sigma, and near a partial-slip wall with Navier-slip length lambda. Complex-variable methods are used to derive the dynamical systems governing the swimmer motion explicitly in both scenarios.

Result: Near the viscosity discontinuity, below a universal critical viscosity ratio sigma_c = 1/2, the swimmer is trapped at a fixed height with its stresslet axis normal to the boundary. At sigma = sigma_c, it glides parallel to the boundary at a speed selected by its orientation, with terminal speed determined by its initial state. For sigma > sigma_c, it oscillates periodically in height and orientation while drifting along the boundary. Near a partial-slip wall, the dynamics depend on the ratio of the slip length to the length scale of the singularity and exhibit an analogous transition at a critical slip length. The dynamics near the viscosity discontinuity above sigma_c are richer than those near the partial-slip boundary, including a hydrodynamic bound state with zero net displacement over one period.

Conclusion: A single boundary parameter organizes qualitatively distinct modes of locomotion, in close analogy with slip-induced transitions reported for three-dimensional swimmers. The study is limited to two dimensions.

Abstract: Microswimmers near walls and interfaces can switch between qualitatively different modes of motion depending on the slip properties of the neighbouring boundaries. To study this theoretically, a two-dimensional point-singularity model of a microswimmer, which does not self-propel in unbounded fluid, is placed near a flat viscosity discontinuity between two fluids with viscosity ratio $σ$, and also near a partial-slip wall with Navier-slip length $λ$. Using complex-variable methods, the dynamical systems governing the swimmer motion are derived explicitly in both scenarios. Near the viscosity discontinuity, below a universal critical viscosity ratio $σ_c=1/2$, the swimmer is trapped at a fixed height with its stresslet axis normal to the boundary. When $σ=σ_c$ it glides parallel to the boundary at a speed selected by its orientation, with a terminal speed determined by its initial state. For $σ>σ_c$ it oscillates periodically in height and orientation while drifting along the boundary. The dynamics near a partial-slip wall depend on the ratio of the slip length to the length scale of the singularity and are similar in exhibiting an analogous transition at a critical slip length. It is found, however, that the dynamics near a viscosity discontinuity for $σ$ above $σ_c$ are richer than those near the partial-slip boundary, including the existence of a hydrodynamic bound state with zero net displacement over one period. Although the study is limited to two dimensions, these results show how a single boundary parameter organises qualitatively distinct modes of locomotion, in close analogy with slip-induced transitions reported for three-dimensional swimmers.

</details>


### [178] [CFD-driven symbolic regression of RANS transition models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03210)
*Louenas Zemmour, Xavier Gloerfelt, Paola Cinnella*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A CFD-driven sparse symbolic regression framework learns interpretable, bounded, closed-form corrections to the production and destruction activation functions of the k-ω-γ transition model using wall-resolved LES flat-plate data. The sparse model retains 11 of 21 candidate terms, performs comparably or slightly better than grid-search tuning, and, without retraining, improves LS89 turbine cascade heat-transfer predictions by up to 70%, though residual transition-onset and transition-length errors remain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RANS transition models depend on empirical correlations and hand-calibrated activation functions whose structural deficiencies cannot be resolved by recalibration alone. The authors seek interpretable, closed-form corrections rather than purely empirical recalibration.

Method: The paper proposes a CFD-driven sparse symbolic regression framework that corrects the production and destruction activation functions in the intermittency equation of the local, Galilean-invariant k-ω-γ model of Ge et al. Each correction combines a polynomial of a local pressure-gradient parameter, controlling amplitude and sign, with a bounded exponential of local flow features, controlling spatial support; this bounds corrections without ad hoc limiters. Coefficients are identified by gradient-free optimization with the RANS solver in the loop, accelerated by POD-Kriging field surrogates, using mean-velocity data from wall-resolved LES of the ERCOFTAC T3 flat-plate cases. Elastic-net hyperparameters are selected automatically through bi-level optimization with leave-one-case-out cross-validation.

Result: The resulting model retains 11 of 21 candidate terms, versus 20 under conventional grid-search tuning, while achieving comparable or slightly better accuracy on training and unseen cases. Both approaches recover the same leading-order correction. Applied without retraining to the transonic LS89 turbine cascade, beyond the training envelope, the augmented model remains numerically robust and improves heat-transfer predictions at all four operating points, reducing errors by up to 70% where the baseline predicts premature transition under high freestream turbulence. Residual errors in transition onset and length remain.

Conclusion: Sparse symbolic regression can produce interpretable, bounded corrections to RANS transition-model activation functions and improve extrapolative heat-transfer predictions in a turbine cascade. However, residual errors in transition onset and transition length highlight the limitations of training on flat-plate data alone.

Abstract: Laminar-turbulent transition governs skin friction, heat transfer and losses in turbomachinery, yet Reynolds-averaged Navier-Stokes (RANS) transition models rely on empirical correlations and hand-calibrated activation functions whose structural deficiencies cannot be resolved by recalibration. We propose a CFD-driven sparse symbolic regression framework to learn interpretable, closed-form corrections to the production and destruction activation functions in the intermittency equation of the local, Galilean-invariant $k$-$ω$-$γ$ model of Ge et al. Each correction combines a polynomial of a local pressure-gradient parameter, controlling amplitude and sign, with a bounded exponential of local flow features, controlling spatial support. This construction bounds the corrections without ad hoc limiters. Coefficients are identified by gradient-free optimization with the RANS solver in the loop, accelerated by POD-Kriging field surrogates, using mean-velocity data from wall-resolved large-eddy simulations (LES) of the ERCOFTAC T3 flat-plate cases. Elastic-net hyperparameters are selected automatically through bi-level optimization with leave-one-case-out cross-validation. The resulting model retains 11 of 21 candidate terms, compared with 20 under conventional grid-search tuning, while achieving comparable or slightly better accuracy on training and unseen cases. Both approaches recover the same leading-order correction. Applied without retraining to the transonic LS89 turbine cascade, far beyond the training envelope, the augmented model remains numerically robust and improves heat-transfer predictions at all four operating points, reducing errors by up to 70% where the baseline predicts premature transition under high freestream turbulence. Residual errors in transition onset and length highlight the limits of training on flat-plate data alone.

</details>


### [179] [Contrasting Eulerian and Lagrangian organization of turbulent dissipation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03029)
*Jeremie Bec, Ritwik Mukherjee, Siddhartha Mukherjee, Samriddhi Sankar Ray*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Direct numerical simulations with tracers show that Eulerian coarse-grained dissipation follows a squared-logarithmic cascade with scale-dependent multipliers, while time-averaged dissipation along trajectories shows multifractal scaling and an approximately scale-invariant temporal cascade; the classical Lagrangian bridge fails for individual trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper tests the conventional picture that turbulent kinetic energy dissipation is governed by a scale-invariant spatial multiplicative cascade, with Lagrangian statistics inferred through bridge relations, and seeks to contrast Eulerian and Lagrangian dissipation statistics directly.

Method: The authors use direct numerical simulations with tracers to analyze Eulerian coarse-grained dissipation and time-averaged dissipation along fluid trajectories, examining cascade depth, scale dependence of multipliers, multifractal scaling, and the validity of classical bridge relations.
