Result: Eulerian coarse-grained dissipation exhibits a squared-logarithmic cascade depth and scale-dependent multipliers, whereas time-averaged dissipation along trajectories displays multifractal scaling and an approximately scale-invariant temporal cascade. The classical bridge fails at the level of individual trajectories, but Eulerian multipliers have a multifractal organization whose accumulation accounts for the squared-logarithmic spatial statistics.

Conclusion: The contrast motivates a material-inheritance picture in which Eulerian dissipation statistics reflect the spatial assembly, deformation, and mixing of distinct Lagrangian histories, distinguishing the temporal cascade organization along fluid trajectories from the non-stationary spatial refinement observed in the Eulerian frame.

Abstract: The dissipation of turbulent kinetic energy is conventionally attributed to a scale-invariant spatial multiplicative cascade, with Lagrangian statistics inferred through bridge relations. Direct numerical simulations with tracers reveal instead that Eulerian coarse-grained dissipation has a squared-logarithmic cascade depth and scale-dependent multipliers, whereas time-averaged dissipation along trajectories displays multifractal scaling and an approximately scale-invariant temporal cascade. The classical bridge fails at the level of individual trajectories. Eulerian multipliers nevertheless possess a multifractal organization whose accumulation accounts for the squared-logarithmic spatial statistics. This contrast motivates a material-inheritance picture in which Eulerian dissipation statistics reflect the spatial assembly, deformation, and mixing of distinct Lagrangian histories. The results therefore distinguish the temporal cascade organization revealed along fluid trajectories from the non-stationary spatial refinement observed in the Eulerian frame.

</details>


### [180] [On Modelling the Scattering of Highly Nonlinear Transient Water Waves Due to Surface-Piercing Cylinder. Part I: Formulations and Verifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02369)
*Qianyue Chen, Jinghua Wang, Daimeng Shang, Xianlong Lin*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper presents a highly nonlinear time-domain pseudo-spectral potential-flow model for high-order wave diffraction by large-diameter vertical cylinders, using a Fredholm integral equation and Legendre polynomials for efficient surface-potential and free-surface vertical-velocity computation. It is verified against a Fourier-transform-based direct method and theoretical models for small-steepness uniform-wave scattering; convergence and efficiency are demonstrated, while highly nonlinear transient-wave validation is deferred to Part II.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme ocean waves threaten marine engineering and navigation, and accurately predicting nonlinear wave loads on large-diameter cylindrical offshore structures such as monopile wind turbines and platform columns requires a robust solution to the high-order wave diffraction problem.

Method: A novel highly nonlinear time-domain numerical pseudo-spectrum model based on potential flow theory is developed. It uses the Fredholm integral equation of the second kind and Legendre polynomials to create an efficient solver for the velocity potential on the cylinder surface and the vertical velocity at the free surface.

Result: The method is verified against a direct Fourier-transform-based approach and established theoretical models for small-steepness uniform-wave scattering. Its convergence and computational efficiency are demonstrated. Validation for highly nonlinear transient water waves is stated to be reported in Part II; no quantitative results are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed model offers a powerful and efficient framework for analyzing high-order nonlinear wave diffraction and hydrodynamic loads, with implications for scientific research and the engineering design of coastal and offshore structures.

Abstract: The presence of extreme ocean waves poses substantial threats to the safety of marine engineering applications and navigational activities. Accurate prediction of the nonlinear wave loads on offshore structures in large-diameter cylindrical configuration, such as monopile wind turbines and platform columns, requires a robust solution to the high-order wave diffraction problem. This paper introduces a novel, highly nonlinear time-domain numerical pseudo-spectrum model based on potential flow theory. By leveraging the Fredholm integral equation of the second kind and Legendre polynomials, a distinct efficient solver for computing the velocity potential on the cylinder surface and vertical velocity at free surface is developed. The present method is verified by comparing with a direct approach based on Fourier transform and established theoretical models for simulating the scattering of small steepness uniform waves. The convergence and computational efficiency of the present method is also demonstrated. The validation of the new model for simulating highly nonlinear transient water waves will be reported in Part II. The proposed model provides a powerful and efficient new framework for analyzing high-order nonlinear wave diffraction and hydrodynamic loads, with significant implications for both scientific research and the engineering design of coastal and offshore structures.

</details>


### [181] [Bistability in the stalling behavior of thick, cambered airfoils under elevated freestream turbulence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02342)
*Jaylon E. McGhee, John A. N. Farnsworth*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Time-resolved surface pressure measurements show that elevated freestream turbulence improves the mean performance of a thick cambered FFA-W3-301 airfoil near and beyond stall, but time-averaged lift polars conceal bistable switching between two separation-front positions on the suction side.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study examines the aerodynamic performance of a thick, cambered FFA-W3-301 airfoil section near and beyond stall under elevated freestream turbulence, aiming to isolate the effects of turbulence intensity and integral length scale using multiple passive grids and to compare these effects with clean inflow.

Method: The authors used time-resolved surface pressure measurements at chord-based Reynolds numbers from 125,000 to 275,000. Three passive turbulence grids were used to separate turbulence-intensity and integral-length-scale effects, with clean inflow as a comparison. The analysis included conditional averaging of surface pressure distributions and proper orthogonal decomposition (POD).

Result: Elevated turbulence largely improved mean performance, eliminating premature flow separation and yielding lift and drag polars more representative of a higher effective Reynolds number. However, time-resolved lift revealed bistable switching: beyond maximum lift, lift alternated between two distinct levels with no characteristic frequency. At the highest Reynolds number, switching persisted over an angle-of-attack range of up to nine degrees, more than an order of magnitude wider than reported for a thin, symmetric section in low-turbulence inflow. The two lift levels were nearly independent of inflow turbulence level, while the fraction of time spent in each state differed by turbulence condition. Neither state was fully attached; they corresponded to two positions of a single suction-side separation front, and a single POD pressure mode captured the switching. Conventional mean and standard-deviation statistics misrepresented the true post-stall unsteady loads.

Conclusion: The time-averaged polars conceal bistable switching, so conventional mean and standard-deviation statistics can misrepresent post-stall unsteady loads. The two states are not fully attached but reflect two positions of a single suction-side separation front, and elevated turbulence primarily changes the occupancy of these states rather than their lift levels.

Abstract: The aerodynamic performance of a thick, cambered FFA-W3-301 airfoil section near and beyond stall was examined under elevated freestream turbulence using time-resolved surface pressure measurements for chord-based Reynolds numbers from 125,000 to 275,000. Three passive turbulence grids were used to isolate the effects of turbulence intensity and integral length scale, and the results were compared with a clean inflow. The elevated turbulence largely had a positive effect on the mean performance of the airfoil section, eliminating premature flow separation and recovering lift and drag polars more representative of a higher effective Reynolds number; however, resolving the lift in time showed that the time-averaged polars concealed bistable switching. The time-averaged lift was an occupancy-weighted average of two states, and conventional mean and standard-deviation statistics misrepresented the true post-stall unsteady loads. Beyond the maximum-lift angle of attack, the lift alternated repeatedly between two distinct levels with no characteristic frequency. At the highest tested Reynolds number, the switching persisted over an angle-of-attack range of up to nine degrees, more than an order of magnitude wider than that reported for a thin, symmetric section in a low turbulence inflow. The lift levels of the two states were also found to be nearly independent of the inflow turbulence level, whereas the fraction of time spent in each differed between turbulence conditions. conditionally averaged surface pressure distributions and proper orthogonal decomposition of the surface pressure consistently showed that neither state is fully attached; the states correspond to two positions of a single separation front on the suction side, a switching accurately captured by a single pressure mode from the decomposition.

</details>


### [182] [Three-dimensional computational rheometry of viscoplastic fluids in vane-in-cup fixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02115)
*James Malcolm, Clare McColgan, Emma Mullarkey, Emad Chaparian*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper computationally studies Herschel-Bulkley viscoplastic flow in vane-in-cup rheometers, compares conventional 4- and 6-arm vanes with a 24-arm fractal vane, re-examines torque-to-stress and rotation-to-strain-rate conversions based on the circular Couette analogy, and proposes improved closure expressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to characterize the hydrodynamics and yielding behavior of viscoplastic materials in vane-in-cup rheometers and to revisit the conversion relations and circular Couette analogy used to interpret raw rheometric data and extract flow curves, building on prior two-dimensional findings that the fractal vane outperforms conventional N-arm designs.

Method: A computational framework is developed for Herschel-Bulkley viscoplastic materials. Simulations use an augmented Lagrangian finite element method for two-dimensional configurations and a regularised finite volume method for three-dimensional configurations, covering a broad range of effective parameters. Three vanes are investigated: conventional 4-arm and 6-arm bespoke vanes and a 24-arm fractal vane.

Result: Conventional bespoke vanes produce highly non-uniform yielding and significant deviations from the circular Couette analogy, whereas the 24-arm fractal vane generates nearly axisymmetric yield surfaces that effectively cloak the vane geometry, so the flow closely resembles azimuthal shear in a conventional bob-and-cup rheometer. The study identifies limitations of existing conversion relations and proposes improved closure expressions for more accurate determination of rheological properties. Three-dimensional flow structures and yielding patterns within vane-in-cup fixtures are also characterized.

Conclusion: The fractal vane provides substantially more accurate rheological measurements than conventional N-arm vanes. Existing torque-to-stress and rotation-to-strain-rate conversion relations have limitations, and the proposed improved closure expressions enable more accurate extraction of material rheological properties from vane-in-cup data.

Abstract: We develop a computational framework to investigate the flow kinematics of viscoplastic materials, described by the Herschel-Bulkley model, in vane-in-cup rheometers. Numerical simulations are performed using augmented Lagrangian finite element and regularised finite volume methods for two- and three-dimensional configurations, respectively, to characterise the hydrodynamics and yielding behaviour over a broad range of effective parameters. Three types of vanes are investigated: conventional 4- and 6-arm bespoke vanes and a 24-arm fractal vane (Owens et al., J. Rheol., vol. 64(3), 2020, 643-662). Our previous two-dimensional study (Chaparian et al., J. Non-Newtonian Fluid Mech., vol. 307, 2022, 104857) demonstrated the superiority of the fractal vane over conventional N-arm designs. The bespoke vanes produce highly non-uniform yielding, leading to significant deviations from the circular Couette analogy, whereas the 24-arm fractal vane generates nearly axisymmetric yield surfaces that effectively "cloak" the vane geometry. Consequently, the resulting flow closely resembles the azimuthal shear in a conventional "bob & cup" rheometer, providing substantially more accurate rheological measurements. Building on these findings, the present study revisits the torque-to-stress and rotation-to-strain rate conversions used in vane-in-cup rheometry. In particular, we re-examine the circular Couette analogy that underpins both the physical interpretation of the flow and the extraction of flow curves from raw rheometric data. Based on our extensive two- and three-dimensional simulations, we identify limitations of the existing conversion relations and propose improved closure expressions that enable more accurate determination of material's rheological properties. Furthermore, the three-dimensional flow structures and yielding patterns within the vane-in-cup fixtures are characterised.

</details>


### [183] [Optimizing synthetic jet placement for control stability in increasing Reynolds number flows based on deep reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01462)
*Wang Jia, Hang Xu*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Reinforcement learning is used to control synthetic jets on a cylinder for flow control across moderate to high Reynolds numbers and different jet placements. Jets near separation regions perform best, achieving large drag reduction, strong lift-oscillation suppression, and vortex-shedding suppression, with some residual wake instability at Re = 2,000.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to develop advanced control strategies for synthetic jets on a cylinder and addresses the challenge of achieving robust flow-control performance across moderate to high Reynolds numbers and varying jet placement configurations.

Method: Reinforcement learning algorithms are used to develop control strategies for synthetic jets positioned on a cylinder. The study evaluates jet placement configurations and reports multi-objective flow-control performance in terms of drag reduction, lift-oscillation suppression, vortex shedding, and wake behavior. The specific reinforcement learning algorithm is not provided in the abstract.

Result: Jets placed near flow separation regions yield optimal control performance. At Re = 500, the strategy achieves up to 37.4% drag reduction, 99% suppression of lift oscillations, and complete vortex-shedding suppression. At Re = 1,000, drag reduction reaches 49.1%, with equally effective lift suppression and complete vortex-shedding suppression. At Re = 2,000, maximum drag reduction is 58.8% and 90% of lift oscillations are suppressed, though some residual wake instabilities persist. Synthetic jets also disrupted detached vortices and stabilized the wake.

Conclusion: Jet placement has a pivotal influence on flow-control efficacy, with placement near separation regions being optimal. The reinforcement-learning-based strategies effectively address complex flow-control challenges, although residual wake instabilities remain at Re = 2,000.

Abstract: This study utilizes reinforcement learning algorithms to develop advanced control strategies for manipulating synthetic jets positioned on a cylinder, aiming to achieve effective flow control. It addresses critical challenges in achieving robust performance across moderate to high Reynolds numbers (Re) and varying jet placement configurations. The results demonstrate that placing synthetic jets near flow separation regions yields optimal control performance. At Re = 500, the multi-objective flow control strategy achieves up to 37.4% drag reduction and 99% suppression of lift oscillations, while completely suppressing vortex shedding. At Re = 1,000, drag reduction improves to 49.1%, with equally effective lift suppression and complete vortex shedding suppression. At Re = 2,000, the strategy achieves a maximum drag reduction of 58.8% and suppresses 90% of lift oscillations, although some residual wake instabilities persist. Additionally, synthetic jets effectively disrupted detached vortices and stabilized the wake, resulting in streamlined flow and suppressed vortex shedding. These findings underscore the pivotal influence of jet placement in optimizing flow control efficacy, while affirming the advanced capability of control strategies to tackle intricate challenges.

</details>


### [184] [Enriched Surface Thermodynamics for Sharp-Interface Fluid Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01227)
*Dieter Bothe*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes a local thermodynamic framework for sharp isotropic interfaces endowed with constituent surface mass densities and a thickness descriptor, distinguishing additive densities from internal descriptors and deriving balances, entropy production, constitutive relations, and reduction conditions for material storage and structural relaxation without requiring surface inertia.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A sharp interface can retain material and structural information about an unresolved fluid transition zone, motivating a thermodynamic formulation for isotropic surfaces carrying surface mass densities and a thickness descriptor.

Method: The authors formulate local balance and entropy-production relations for moving surface patches, derive an available-free-energy identity including mechanical power and boundary material/structural fluxes, distinguish additive content densities from internal descriptors, analyze independent, constrained, and rapidly equilibrating thickness kinematics, and use an explicit mass-thickness free energy together with equilibrium reduction.

Result: The framework shows that the density distinction determines the area-conjugate reversible surface stress and Gibbs-Demhem relation, while volume-per-area thickness has a pressure-like conjugate, mechanical work contribution, and separate evolution equation. Material storage can be retained without surface inertia. The explicit mass-thickness free energy has a positive-definite material Hessian and yields coupled adsorption, expansion, compression, and dissipative relaxation. Pressure response gives conditional restrictions on reduced states and shows that distinct independent and constrained states can share an entire pressure-tension curve. Geometric thickness realizations and finite-rate transfer are also treated with associated mechanical and balance requirements. No quantitative or experimental results are reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: The framework separates state selection, constitutive closure, reduction, and identification from observations, providing a structured thermodynamic basis for sharp interfaces with unresolved transition-zone material and structural information.

Abstract: A sharp interface can retain material and structural information about an unresolved fluid transition zone. We formulate a local thermodynamic framework for isotropic surfaces carrying constituent surface mass densities and a thickness descriptor. The distinction between densities of additive contents and internal descriptors determines the area conjugate, reversible surface stress, and Gibbs-Duhem relation. A volume-per-area thickness has a pressure-like conjugate, a mechanical work contribution, and a separate evolution equation. Independent, constrained, and rapidly equilibrating thicknesses require different kinematics and reductions. Material storage can be retained without retaining surface inertia.
  The balances and entropy production specify admissible exchange and structural relaxation. For a moving surface patch, an available-free-energy identity accounts for mechanical power and for the contributions of material and structural fluxes across its boundary. Equilibrium reduction retains the production and transport needed to follow the reduced state. An explicit mass-thickness free energy has a positive-definite material Hessian and gives coupled adsorption, expansion, compression, and dissipative relaxation. Pressure response yields conditional restrictions on reduced states and demonstrates that distinct independent and constrained states can share an entire pressure-tension curve. Geometric thickness realizations and finite-rate transfer are treated with their associated mechanical and balance requirements. The framework separates state selection, constitutive closure, reduction, and identification from observations.

</details>


### [185] [Burning limits and the role of small scale turbulence in high Karlovitz number premixed flames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01010)
*Praful Kumar, Ömer L. Gülder*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This study uses a piloted high-Karlovitz-number burner to examine how small-scale turbulence affects burning rates of premixed CH4-air flames (φ = 0.7–1.0) over increasing rms fluctuating velocities up to 36.0 m/s, reaching Kolmogorov scales near 4 μm. Global consumption flame speeds from OH PLIF scale with turbulent diffusivity, peak, and then decline at higher u′. CH-PLIF shows progressive straining and extinction despite a flamelet-like appearance, and the trends are captured in a scaling relation including straining and extinction effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Premixed flame burning rates increase with turbulence, but ultimate limits are governed by local extinctions. Piloted premixed burners have accessed turbulence far beyond theoretically predicted extinction limits while measured flame speeds still improve, so this study explores the role of small-scale turbulence in setting burning rates and observed limits.

Method: Experiments used a piloted high-Karlovitz-number burner with premixed CH4-air mixtures at equivalence ratios from 0.7 to 1.0, stabilized by a φ-matched pilot flame. The rms fluctuating velocity u′ was increased up to 36.0 m/s, progressively increasing turbulent kinetic energy at smaller scales, with Kolmogorov scales reaching approximately 4 μm at the highest turbulence condition. Global consumption flame speeds were derived from binarized OH Planar Laser-Induced Fluorescence (PLIF) imaging, and flame structure was assessed using CH-PLIF images.

Result: Global consumption flame speeds S_T,GC scale with turbulent diffusivity D_T. Further increases in u′ produce a maximum and eventual decline in flame speed, and the measured flame speeds exhibit a common scaling with measured upstream u′. CH-PLIF images show progressive straining and extinction events with increasing u′ even though the flames maintain a flamelet-like appearance, suggesting that the observed limits arise from increasing extinction propensity across the flame front.

Conclusion: The observed flame-speed limits are attributed to increasing straining and extinction propensity across the flame front despite a flamelet-like appearance. These findings are incorporated into a scaling relation that accounts for the role of straining and extinctions in the measured flame-speed trends.

Abstract: Premixed flames exhibit a progressive enhancement in burning rates with turbulence, with the ultimate limits governed by the onset of local extinctions. Piloted premixed burners have enabled access to turbulence far exceeding the theoretically predicted extinction limits, with the measured flame speeds still improving. This study explores the role of small scale turbulence on the burning rates of premixed flames using a piloted high Karlovitz number burner. Premixed CH$_4$-air mixtures with equivalence ratios ($φ$) from 0.7--1.0, stabilized using a $φ$-matched pilot flame, were subjected to increasing rms fluctuating velocities, $u'$, up to 36.0 m/s. The experimental setup enables a progressive increase in turbulent kinetic energy (TKE) at smaller scales, with the Kolmogorov scales reaching approximately 4 $μ$m under the highest turbulence condition. Global consumption flame speeds, $S_\mathrm{T,GC}$, derived from the binarized Planar Laser-Induced Fluorescence (PLIF) imaging of OH radicals, scale with turbulent diffusivity, $\mathcal{D}_\mathrm{T}$. Further increases in $u'$, however, lead to a maximum and an eventual decline in flame speeds. The measured flame speeds exhibit a common scaling with the measured upstream $u'$. Assessments of the flame structure using CH-PLIF images further reveal progressive straining and extinction events with increasing $u'$ despite flames maintaining their flamelet-like appearance, suggesting that the observed limits derive from the increasing extinction propensity across the flame front. Finally, these findings are incorporated into a scaling relation accounting for the role of straining and extinctions in the observed flame-speed trends.

</details>


### [186] [Instability of streaky high-supersonic boundary layers over cooled walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00846)
*Dongdong Xu, Pierre Ricco, Elena Marensi, Xuesong Wu*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This study analyzes streak instability in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers, identifying modified Mack second modes, a new varicose secondary-instability mode, and sinuous/varicose secondary modes; streaks destabilize three-dimensional secondary modes while suppressing modified Mack second modes, including radiating ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand the instability of streaks induced by free-stream vortical disturbances in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers, including the relevant instability modes and their noise implications.

Method: The abstract reports an instability investigation using identified instability modes, eigenfunctions, and numerical results; however, the specific theoretical, computational, or experimental method is not provided in the abstract.

Result: Distinct instability modes are identified: modified Mack second modes, a newly discovered varicose secondary-instability mode, and sinuous or varicose secondary-instability modes similar to those in incompressible boundary layers. The new mode has eigenfunctions concentrated in valley regions and intense near-wall temperature fluctuations. Streaks destabilize three-dimensional secondary-instability modes but suppress modified Mack second modes, both radiating and non-radiating; suppression of radiating modes reduces emitted two-dimensional noise. Unlike adiabatic-wall cases, secondary instability in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers occurs in both low-speed peak regions and high-speed valley regions.

Conclusion: Streak instability in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers exhibits distinct mode characteristics and spatial localization, with streaks having opposing effects on secondary-instability modes and modified Mack second modes; suppressing radiating modes may reduce two-dimensional noise.

Abstract: We investigate the instability of streaks induced by free-stream vortical disturbances in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers. Distinct types of instability modes are identified: modified Mack second modes, a newly discovered varicose secondary-instability mode and sinuous or varicose secondary-instability modes akin to those in incompressible boundary layers. Unique characteristics of the new secondary-instability mode are that its eigenfunctions concentrate in valley regions and feature intense temperature fluctuations near the wall. The streaks in high-supersonic boundary layers destabilise the three-dimensional secondary-instability modes, but suppress the growth of the modified Mack second modes, both radiating and non-radiating. The suppression of radiating modes amounts to a reduction of the two-dimensional noise they emit. Numerical results indicate that, unlike streaks in adiabatic-wall boundary layers, whose instability concentrates in low-speed peak regions away from the wall, the secondary instability of streaks in cooled-wall high-supersonic boundary layers occurs both in low-speed peak regions and in high-speed valley regions.

</details>


### [187] [A unifying view of averaging for wave-mean flow decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00644)
*L. R. Seitz, Beth A. Wingate*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper generalizes mean-flow/wave averaging from an infinite-time average over wave periods to finite windows, yielding a family of decompositions that retain near-resonant interactions. It shows how reference-frame choice affects the mean flow, expresses Stokes drift via resonant and near-resonant interactions, and places Eulerian, Lagrangian, and wave-frame averages in a common framework for comparing decompositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geophysical flows are routinely decomposed into mean flow and waves, but the decomposition procedure determines how motion is divided between them. The authors seek a general averaging formulation that can compare decompositions and help choose among them according to the physical processes one wants to isolate.

Method: They generalize infinite-time averaging over all wave periods to finite averaging windows, producing a family of decompositions that also retain near-resonant interactions whose phases do not fully cancel over the window. The averaging requires a choice of reference frame. Using the fast-wave evolution underlying the exact average, they define a wave reference frame in which the fast phase is steady and a corresponding wave-frame average, and they cast Eulerian, Lagrangian, and wave-frame averages in a common form.

Result: The formulation shows that the same flow averaged in two frames can yield mean flows differing by a drift. It expresses Stokes drift through resonant and near-resonant interactions, confirming the intuition that Stokes drift is a slow quantity generated by fast waves and revealing a coherent near-resonant contribution generally present in Stokes drift calculated over finite time from broadband wave fields.

Conclusion: Finite-window averaging provides a common basis for comparing mean-flow/wave decompositions and for choosing among them according to the physical processes one seeks to isolate. The chosen reference frame determines which motions enter the mean.

Abstract: Geophysical flows are routinely decomposed into mean flow and waves, and the procedure used determines how the motion is divided between the two. Ideally, the wave and mean motions of interest evolve on distinct timescales, in which case they can be separated by taking an infinite-time average over all wave periods. Individual waves average away, while exactly resonant interactions contribute to the averaged dynamics because they are steady on the fast timescale. We generalize this averaging to finite windows, yielding a family of decompositions that additionally retain near-resonant interactions whose phases do not fully cancel over the time window. Such averaging necessarily invokes a choice of reference frame; the same flow averaged in two frames may yield mean flows differing by a drift. The averaging formulation developed here enables us to express Stokes drift through resonant and near-resonant interactions, confirming the intuition that it is a slow quantity generated by fast waves and revealing a coherent near-resonant contribution generally present in Stokes drift calculated over finite time from broadband wave fields. We use the fast-wave evolution underlying the exact average to define both the wave reference frame, in which the fast phase is steady, and a wave-frame average. We cast familiar Eulerian and Lagrangian averages and this wave-frame average in a common form to further illustrate how the chosen frame determines which motions enter the mean. This view of averaging provides a common basis for comparing decompositions and choosing among them according to the physical processes one seeks to isolate.

</details>


### [188] [Active particle dynamics in uniform and patterned electroosmotic flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00600)
*Aritra Roy, Antarip Poddar, Rahil Valani*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper analyzes self-propelled spheroidal particles in electroosmotic channel flows with uniform, patterned, and combined zeta potentials, linking Hamiltonian phase-space dynamics in uniform flow to vortex-mediated confinement and transport in patterned flows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand how self-propulsion, particle shape, and electroosmotic advection couple to produce confinement and transport, and to assess surface electrostatic patterning as a way to control particle motion without changing channel geometry.

Method: For uniform zeta potential, the dynamics are reduced to a two-dimensional system in cross-stream position and orientation and characterized analytically through a non-canonical Hamiltonian structure; spherical particles are described by a nonlinear-pendulum model. Patterned and combined zeta-potential cases are studied using numerical simulations and dynamical-systems analysis.

Result: Uniform zeta potential yields a non-canonical Hamiltonian system; for spheres, the nonlinear-pendulum description gives the oscillation frequency and confined-region extent, with frequency depending non-monotonically on electrostatic screening and peaking at an intermediate Debye parameter. Patterned zeta potentials generate counter-rotating vortices and couple streamwise position, cross-stream position, and orientation, producing single-cell confinement and cross-cell migration controlled by particle shape and screening. Combined uniform and patterned components create competition between mean electroosmotic advection and vortex-induced recirculation, making transport sensitive to particle shape and initial conditions.

Conclusion: The results connect Hamiltonian phase-space dynamics in uniform electroosmotic flow with vortex-mediated trapping and transport in patterned flows, indicating that surface electrostatic patterning can control particle motion without altering channel geometry.

Abstract: We investigate the dynamics of self-propelled spheroidal particles in electroosmotic channel flows generated by uniform, patterned, and combined zeta-potential distributions. The coupling between self-propulsion, particle shape, and electroosmotic advection gives rise to distinct regimes of confinement and transport. For a uniform zeta potential, the dynamics reduce to a two-dimensional system in cross-stream position and orientation. We show that this system possesses a non-canonical Hamiltonian structure, allowing its phase-space organization to be characterized analytically. For spherical particles, the dynamics admit a nonlinear-pendulum description, yielding the characteristic oscillation frequency and extent of the confined region. The oscillation frequency depends non-monotonically on electrostatic screening and reaches a maximum at an intermediate Debye parameter. Spatially patterned zeta potentials reorganize the dynamics by generating counter-rotating vortices and coupling streamwise position, cross-stream position, and particle orientation. Numerical simulations and dynamical-systems analysis reveal regimes of single-cell confinement and cross-cell migration, controlled by particle shape and electrostatic screening. When uniform and patterned components coexist, mean electroosmotic advection competes with vortex-induced recirculation, producing transport sensitive to particle shape and initial conditions. Together, these results connect Hamiltonian phase-space dynamics in uniform electroosmotic flow to vortex-mediated trapping and transport in patterned flows, highlighting the potential of surface electrostatic patterning to control particle motion without altering channel geometry.

</details>


### [189] [Ewald summation of doubly periodic 2- and 3-dimensional Stokes multipoles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00308)
*Teunike A. van de Pol, Zhan Ma, Alexander Morozov, Joakim Stenhammar*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper derives Ewald representations for periodic Stokes force multipoles in partially periodic geometries, covering 3D Stokes flow with one unbounded coordinate and intrinsically 2D singularities, and validates them against lattice Boltzmann simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most particle-resolved low-Reynolds-number studies use triply periodic geometries, while continuum descriptions are often solved in 2D doubly periodic domains, and many microswimmer experiments are quasi-2D with finite thickness. These geometric differences are non-trivial, motivating systematic methods for partially periodic Stokes systems.

Method: The authors systematically derive Ewald representations for periodic Stokes force multipoles in geometries with two periodic directions. Starting from the periodic Stokeslet, they obtain explicit expressions for force dipoles and quadrupoles and analyze the non-trivial zero-wavevector contribution arising under partial periodicity. They treat both three-dimensional Stokes flow with one unbounded coordinate and intrinsically two-dimensional singularities.

Result: Explicit Ewald representations and expressions for periodic Stokes force dipoles and quadrupoles are obtained, including analysis of the zero-wavevector contribution under partial periodicity. The analytical expressions are independently validated by comparison with an explicit lattice Boltzmann solver in each geometry.

Conclusion: The derived and validated Ewald representations enable study of non-trivial differences between triply periodic, doubly periodic, and quasi-2D confined Stokes geometries for microswimmers and colloidal systems dominated by low-Re hydrodynamic interactions.

Abstract: In spite of the qualitative differences between Stokes flows in 2 and 3 dimensions, most particle-resolved computational studies of microswimmers and other colloidal systems dominated by low-Re hydrodynamic interactions are carried out in triply periodic geometries, while the corresponding continuum descriptions are frequently solved in 2-dimensional doubly periodic domains. Furthermore, most experimental examples of biological and synthetic microswimmers are realised in confined, quasi-2-dimensional geometries with a finite thickness in one of the three dimensions. To enable studying the non-trivial differences between these system geometries, in this paper we systematically derive Ewald representations for periodic Stokes force multipoles in geometries with two periodic directions, treating both three-dimensional Stokes flow with one unbounded coordinate and intrinsically two-dimensional singularities. Starting from the periodic Stokeslet, we obtain explicit expressions for force dipoles and quadrupoles and analyse the non-trivial zero-wavevector contribution arising under partial periodicity. The analytical expressions are independently validated by comparing with results from an explicit lattice Boltzmann solver in each geometry.

</details>


### [190] [OPINE: Orthogonal Projection in Invertible Nonlinear Embeddings for Turbulent Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00216)
*Anqiao Ouyang, Hongyi Ke, Qi Wang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: OPINE learns an invertible nonlinear coordinate transformation and applies proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) in the transformed space, so that a single trained model provides reduced coordinates for every smaller latent dimension. It outperforms POD across tested dimensions and substantially outperforms convolutional and fully connected autoencoders on Kuramoto-Sivashinsky and Burgers dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: POD gives ordered reduced coordinates but requires many modes for data near a curved manifold, while nonlinear autoencoders are more compact but lose information inside a nonlinear encoder and do not directly provide models for smaller latent dimensions from one training run. The paper aims to retain POD-style ordered projections while using an invertible nonlinear transformation for greater compactness.

Method: The paper introduces Orthogonal Projection in Invertible Nonlinear Embeddings (OPINE), which learns an invertible nonlinear change of coordinates and performs POD in those transformed coordinates. Because the map is invertible, all information loss occurs in the POD projection, and ordering the POD basis after training yields reduced coordinates for every smaller dimension from one model. Training alternates gradient updates of the map with a damped update that tracks the evolving POD subspace; the authors prove this update converges locally to the exact POD subspace.

Result: OPINE was evaluated on synthetic manifolds, chaotic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics, forced Burgers turbulence, two-dimensional Kolmogorov flow, and three-dimensional minimal channel flow. It was more accurate than POD at every tested retained dimension above one, and on Kuramoto-Sivashinsky and Burgers dynamics it was more accurate than convolutional and fully connected autoencoders by more than two orders of magnitude at the largest retained dimension.

Conclusion: OPINE combines an invertible nonlinear embedding with POD in the transformed coordinates, enabling a single model to supply ordered reduced coordinates across dimensions while confining information loss to the POD projection. The abstract concludes that it improves accuracy over POD and, for the reported dynamical systems, over convolutional and fully connected autoencoders; other limitations are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) represents physical fields with orthogonal modes ordered by variance, so that one decomposition gives reduced coordinates in every dimension, but it needs many modes when the data lie near a curved manifold. Nonlinear autoencoders are more compact, but their information loss happens inside a nonlinear encoder, and a model trained with one latent dimension gives no model with a smaller one. We introduce Orthogonal Projection in Invertible Nonlinear Embeddings (OPINE), which learns an invertible nonlinear change of coordinates and performs POD in the transformed coordinates. Because the map is invertible, all information loss occurs in the POD projection, and ordering its basis after training gives reduced coordinates of every smaller dimension from a single model. Training alternates gradient updates of the map with a damped update that tracks the evolving POD subspace, and we prove that this update converges locally to the exact POD subspace. We evaluate OPINE on synthetic manifolds, chaotic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics, forced Burgers turbulence, two-dimensional Kolmogorov flow, and three-dimensional minimal channel flow. OPINE is more accurate than POD at every tested retained dimension above one, and on the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky and Burgers dynamics, it is more accurate than convolutional and fully connected autoencoders by more than two orders of magnitude at the largest retained dimension.

</details>


### [191] [Jovian Polar Vortex Generation in a Shallow-Water Model with Quasi-Stochastic Convection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02372)
*Yuhang Ruan, Hao Fu, Cheng Li, Da Yang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A polar γ-plane shallow-water model with quasi-stochastic convective mass sinks is used to study how moist convective organization can form and maintain gas-giant polar vortices. The experiments reproduce shielded cyclones and persistent vortex crystals, show that mesoscale convective feedback controls vortex growth and organization, and identify competing Biot–Savart contributions to structural drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The formation and maintenance of polar vortices on Saturn and Jupiter remain a significant challenge in planetary atmospheric dynamics. Internal heat sources and condensable components drive widespread moist convection in the polar regions of these gas giants, but the role of moist convective organization in forming and maintaining polar vortices is poorly understood.

Method: The study develops a polar γ-plane shallow-water model coupled to a quasi-stochastic convective parameterization. Convection is prescribed as localized impulsive mass sinks whose inception probability depends on local vorticity, giving a higher convective likelihood in cyclonic regions. Numerical experiments test the interaction between convection and polar vortices, and a Biot–Savart diagnostic is used to analyze structural drift.

Result: The experiments reproduce shielded cyclones and persistent vortex crystals reminiscent of Jupiter's polar configuration. Increasing mesoscale feedback promotes vortex growth, shield formation, and spectral organization, while the characteristic vortex scale depends on both the deformation radius and the convective parameterization. The patterns show clockwise and counterclockwise structural drift whose direction and rate are not adequately captured by a conventional Rossby wave phase-speed estimate. The Biot–Savart diagnostic reveals competing contributions: predominantly counterclockwise advection induced by other vortices and clockwise advection induced by background vorticity outside the identified vortices. The abstract does not provide quantitative metrics.

Conclusion: The results suggest a possible formation pathway for shielded vortex crystals and highlight the role of convective feedback in vortex formation. They also indicate that moist convective organization and competing advective contributions are important for understanding polar vortex dynamics on gas giants.

Abstract: The mechanisms governing the formation and maintenance of polar vortices on Saturn and Jupiter represent a significant challenge in current planetary atmospheric dynamics. In the presence of internal heat sources, the existence of condensable components leads to widespread moist convection in the polar regions of these gas giants. However, the role of moist convective organization in the formation and maintenance of such polar vortices remains poorly understood. To investigate the interaction between convection and polar vortices, this study develops a polar $γ$-plane shallow-water model coupled with a quasi-stochastic convective parameterization. In this quasi-stochastic scheme, convection is prescribed as localized impulsive mass sinks, with the inception probability depending on local vorticity, thereby assigning a higher convective likelihood to cyclonic regions. Numerical experiments successfully reproduce shielded cyclones and persistent vortex crystals reminiscent of Jupiter's polar configuration. Increasing mesoscale feedback promotes vortex growth, shield formation, and spectral organization, while the characteristic vortex scale depends on both the deformation radius and the convective parameterization. The resulting patterns exhibit both clockwise and counterclockwise structural drift, whose direction and rate are not adequately captured by a conventional Rossby wave phase-speed estimate. A Biot--Savart diagnostic reveals competing contributions to structural drift, with predominantly counterclockwise advection induced by the other vortices and clockwise advection induced by the background vorticity outside the identified vortices. These results provide a possible formation pathway for shielded vortex crystals and highlight the role of convective feedback in vortex formation.

</details>


### [192] [Experimental Realization of Higher-order Topological Water Wave Crystal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01924)
*Jiayin Zhao, Yifan Liu, Jidong Song, Houqi Ai, Qinhui Jiang, Manjie Li, Mengyao Li*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper experimentally demonstrates a water-wave higher-order topological crystal in which surface waves are localized at structural corners and identified through frequency-resolved surface-elevation measurements, supported by bulk band analysis and finite-system simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order topological phases offer a route to localizing wave modes at structural corners, but their experimental realization in water-wave systems remains largely unexplored. Free-surface water waves provide a macroscopic platform where topological localization can be measured through surface-elevation fields.

Method: The authors experimentally demonstrate a water-wave higher-order topological crystal. Bulk band analysis and finite-system simulations predict topological edge and corner states, while frequency-resolved surface-elevation measurements confirm the corresponding localized modes through measured spectra and field distributions.

Result: The measured spectra and field distributions reveal pronounced in-gap corner localization consistent with the predicted higher-order topological modes. Surface waves are confined to structural corners and spectrally identified through measured free-surface responses. No quantitative performance metrics are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The results establish surface water waves as a directly measurable platform for higher-order topological physics, enabling spatial localization and spectral characterization of hydrodynamic wave modes.

Abstract: Free-surface water waves provide a macroscopic platform in which topological localization can be measured through surface-elevation fields. Higher-order topological phases offer a route to localizing wave modes at structural corners, yet their experimental realization in water-wave systems remains largely unexplored. Here, we experimentally demonstrate a water-wave higher-order topological crystal, where surface waves are confined to structural corners and spectrally identified through measured free-surface responses. Bulk band analysis and finite-system simulations predict topological edge and corner states in the designed structure, while frequency-resolved surface-elevation measurements confirm the corresponding localized modes. The measured spectra and field distributions reveal pronounced in-gap corner localization consistent with the predicted higher-order topological modes. These results establish surface water waves as a directly measurable platform for higher-order topological physics, enabling spatial localization and spectral characterization of hydrodynamic wave modes.

</details>


### [193] [Learned Warm Starts for Steady Transonic RANS Simulations of Wing and Aircraft Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01240)
*Muhammad Irfan Zafar, Saleh Abuhanieh, Şahin Yiğit*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A learned warm-start method predicts mean-flow and turbulence fields directly on native CFD meshes to initialize RANS corrections, achieving roughly 2–4× iteration speed-ups and 2.1–2.3× estimated campaign speed-ups across 3D aerodynamic cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standalone neural surrogates may be insufficiently accurate or robust for reliable design evaluations, even though they can accelerate many-query aerodynamic analyses.

Method: The authors develop a condition-modulated Transolver that combines Fourier and wall features with target-gated regularization, a Sobolev-type loss, adaptive loss weighting, and sampled training. It predicts mean-flow and turbulence fields on native CFD meshes without a preliminary flow solution; training does not require solver-residual evaluation. A common architecture and objective structure are used for subcritical and transonic flows, including shock-induced separation. Evaluations use SuperWing, NASA CRM, and DLR-F6 with Spalart-Allmaras and k-ω SST closures on million-cell meshes, assessing interpolation, extrapolation, and cross-mesh deployment.

Result: Unseen nominal cases achieve geometric-mean iteration speed-ups of approximately 2–4× under a convergence criterion based on cold-run aerodynamic coefficients. On CRM, learned initialization gives greater aggregate acceleration than nearest-condition restarts from the same solution library. Resolution invariance enables direct deployment of coarse-mesh models on finer meshes without retraining, solution interpolation, or target-condition coarse solves. Including data generation and training, estimated campaign speed-ups reach 2.1–2.3×. No warm run diverges, and fewer than 2% fail to satisfy the cold-reference convergence criterion.

Conclusion: The results support learned initialization for repeated aerodynamic analyses, while identifying initialization sensitivity near separation as a remaining limitation.

Abstract: Neural surrogates can accelerate many-query aerodynamic analyses, but their standalone predictions may be insufficiently accurate or robust to support reliable design evaluations. We develop a learned warm-start formulation that predicts mean-flow and turbulence fields directly on native CFD meshes for subsequent correction by the RANS solver. A common architecture and objective structure are used across subcritical and transonic flows, including challenging conditions with shock-induced separation. A condition-modulated Transolver combines Fourier and wall features with target-gated regularization, a Sobolev-type loss, adaptive loss weighting and sampled training. Prediction requires no preliminary flow solution, and training requires no solver-residual evaluation. Extensive 3D CFD evaluations on SuperWing, the NASA Common Research Model (CRM) and DLR-F6 assess geometric and operating-condition generalization, including interpolation, extrapolation and cross-mesh deployment, using Spalart-Allmaras and $k$-$ω$ SST closures on million-cell meshes. Unseen nominal cases achieve geometric-mean iteration speed-ups of approximately $2$-$4\times$ under a common convergence criterion based on cold-run aerodynamic coefficients. On CRM, learned initialization provides greater aggregate acceleration than nearest-condition restarts using the same solution library. Resolution invariance enables direct deployment of coarse-mesh models on finer meshes without retraining, solution interpolation or target-condition coarse solves. Including data generation and training, estimated campaign speed-ups reach $2.1$-$2.3\times$. No warm run diverges; fewer than $2\%$ fail to satisfy the cold-reference convergence criterion. These results support learned initialization for repeated aerodynamic analyses, while highlighting initialization sensitivity near separation as a remaining limitation.

</details>


### [194] [Impact of Surface Preparation on the Propulsion of Catalytic Janus Colloids and Implications for Reproducibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00274)
*Muhammad Haroon, Mitchell Lau, Christopher L. Wirth*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Platinum-coated polystyrene Janus particles are widely used in active-matter research, but reported speeds vary greatly. The abstract shows that pre-exposing the platinum cap to H2O2 and plasma cleaning the glass substrate substantially change particle speed, and it proposes a minimum reporting standard for preparation and speed measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Similar catalytic Janus particles and fuel concentrations have reported speeds differing by more than an order of magnitude, so broader use requires more consistent preparation and improved repeatability of behavior.

Method: The authors examined two routine but rarely reported preparation steps: pre-exposing the platinum cap to H2O2 before cell assembly and plasma cleaning the glass substrate. They measured particle speeds across batch histories and cell geometries, tested wall treatment and treated glass area per unit fluid volume, measured ion release into water, used XPS surface analysis, and applied a conductivity-based model. They also compared 17 published studies.

Result: Pre-exposing the platinum cap to H2O2 increased mean speed by a factor of 2.4 overall; an aged batch responded strongly, while a freshly prepared batch increased by only 16%. Plasma cleaning the glass substrate decreased speed 2.7 times. In a 120 micrometer cell, suppression was the same at both walls even when only one wall was treated. Suppression increased with treated glass area per unit fluid volume, indicating species released into the bulk solution. Plasma-cleaned glass released more ions into water than untreated glass, and XPS detected an ionic metal fluoride on the plasma-cleaned surface. A model in which released ions lower speed through solution conductivity reproduced measured speeds in all four cell geometries. Of 17 compared studies, pre-activation was reported in four and substrate cleaning in six.

Conclusion: Two routine preparation steps can significantly alter the speed of catalytic Janus particles and may contribute to variability across studies. The authors propose a minimum reporting standard for the preparation and associated speed measurements of catalytic Janus particles.

Abstract: Platinum-coated polystyrene (PS) Janus particles (JPs) propelled by the catalytic decomposition of hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) are a widely used model system for active matter, yet reported speeds for similar particles and fuel concentrations differ by more than an order of magnitude. Broad adoption of these materials requires increasing consistency in the preparation and achieving improved repeatability in their behavior. Herein, we focused on two routine, but rarely reported, preparation steps that we found to alter the particle speed. First, we found that pre-exposing the platinum cap to H2O2 before cell assembly increased the mean speed by a factor of 2.4. The size of this effect depended on batch history, where an aged batch responded strongly, while a freshly prepared batch sped up by only 16%. Second, we found that plasma cleaning of the glass substrate decreased the speed 2.7 times. In a 120 $μ$m cell the suppression was the same at both walls even when only one wall was treated. The suppression grew with the treated glass area per unit fluid volume, which pointed to species released into the bulk solution rather than to a change at the wall. Plasma-cleaned glass released more ions into water than untreated glass, and XPS detected an ionic metal fluoride on the plasma-cleaned surface. A model in which the released ions lower the speed through the solution conductivity reproduced the measured speeds in all four cell geometries. Of the 17 published studies compared here, pre-activation is reported in only four studies and substrate cleaning in only six studies. We propose a minimum reporting standard for the preparation and associated speed measurements of catalytic Janus particles.

</details>


### [195] [Computational assessment of incremental pyriform aperture enlargement in plastic surgery: effect on inhaled nasal airflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00083)
*Prosun Roy, Mohammad Yeasin, Siena Shah, Elliott Bryner, Anil Shah, Saikat Basu*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: In a CT-derived nasal airway model, computational fluid dynamics showed that progressive pyriform aperture enlargement improves inspiratory flow and lowers airway resistance, with approximately 15% area enlargement identified as an efficient aerodynamic threshold and little additional benefit from 15% to 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The pyriform aperture is a critical anterior bony constriction affecting nasal airflow and functional and aesthetic nasal surgery, but the degree of pyriform aperture enlargement needed to achieve its aerodynamic benefit was undefined. The study aimed to quantify the aerodynamic effects of progressive enlargement and identify a threshold beyond which further enlargement yields minimal benefit.

Method: A CT-derived three-dimensional nasal airway model was digitally modified to create baseline and 5%, 10%, 15%, and 20% increases in pyriform aperture cross-sectional area. Pressure-driven large-eddy simulations were performed at a fixed transnasal pressure difference of 11.25 Pa to quantify inspiratory flow rate, total airway resistance, and local airflow characteristics.

Result: Progressive enlargement monotonically increased inspiratory flow from 15.063 to 15.252 L/min, approximately 1.25%, and reduced airway resistance from 0.6138 to 0.6028 Pa.min/L, approximately 1.80%. The response was strongly nonlinear, with the greatest improvement between 10% and 15% enlargement; increasing from 15% to 20% produced negligible additional improvement.

Conclusion: Pyriform aperture enlargement improves nasal airflow by reducing resistance at the anterior bony constriction. In the model studied, approximately 15% area enlargement represented an efficient aerodynamic threshold, after which dominant resistance shifted toward downstream nasal structures and further enlargement produced little additional benefit. The findings support a conservative, anatomy-dependent approach and require validation across broader patient-specific geometries.

Abstract: Background. The pyriform aperture is a critical anterior bony constriction governing nasal airflow and is clinically relevant to both functional and aesthetic nasal surgery, yet the degree of pyriform aperture enlargement (PAE) needed to capture its aerodynamic benefit is undefined. This study employs high-fidelity computational fluid dynamics (CFD) to quantify the aerodynamic effects of progressive PAE and identify a threshold beyond which further enlargement yields minimal benefit. Methods. A CT-derived three-dimensional nasal airway model was digitally modified to produce baseline, 5%, 10%, 15%, and 20% increases in pyriform aperture cross-sectional area. Pressure-driven large-eddy simulations (LES) were performed at a fixed transnasal pressure difference of 11.25 Pa to quantify changes in inspiratory flow rate, total airway resistance, and local airflow characteristics. Results. Progressive PAE monotonically increased inspiratory flow from 15.063 to 15.252 L/min (approximately 1.25%) and reduced airway resistance from 0.6138 to 0.6028 Pa.min/L (approximately 1.80%). The aerodynamic response was strongly nonlinear, with the greatest improvement occurring between 10% and 15% enlargement. Increasing the aperture from 15% to 20% produced negligible additional improvement. Conclusions. PAE improves nasal airflow by reducing the resistance imposed by the anterior bony constriction. In the anatomical model investigated, approximately 15% area enlargement represented an efficient aerodynamic threshold, after which the dominant resistance shifted toward downstream nasal structures and further enlargement produced little additional benefit. These findings support a conservative, anatomy-dependent approach to PAE and warrant validation across a broader range of patient-specific geometries.

</details>


### [196] [Inhalation-exhalation asymmetry in pressure-driven airflow through a population-derived median unilateral human nasal cavity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00012)
*Makesh Singh, Shashwat Bhattacharya, Gaurav Bhutani*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Time-resolved CFD of a population-averaged unilateral nasal cavity under sinusoidal pressure-driven breathing shows strong inhalation-exhalation asymmetry: inhalation yields higher flow and lower resistance at matched pressure, with intensity-dependent increases in Reynolds number, nasal resistance, and olfactory airflow that reach about 6% during high-pressure inhalation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to quantify breathing-phase-dependent differences in nasal airflow and to show that pressure-driven transient simulations capture geometry-dependent inhalation-exhalation asymmetry that is suppressed when identical flow rates are prescribed for both phases.

Method: A standardized unilateral nasal geometry reconstructed from averaged CT data of 30 adults was simulated with time-resolved CFD over a complete breathing cycle using a sinusoidal nasopharyngeal pressure waveform at low, intermediate, and high pressure amplitudes. Flow rate, Reynolds number, wall shear stress, nasal resistance, and olfactory-region airflow were analyzed.

Result: At identical imposed pressure magnitudes, inhalation produced consistently larger flow rates and lower nasal resistance than exhalation. Higher breathing intensity increased Reynolds number and nasal resistance while largely preserving the spatial flow pattern. Inhalation produced deeper superior-cavity airflow and higher, more localized wall shear stress, whereas exhalation showed smoother airflow redistribution. The olfactory airflow fraction increased with inspiratory intensity to approximately 6% under high pressure, while the expiratory fraction was comparatively insensitive to breathing intensity.

Conclusion: Pressure-driven transient simulations naturally reveal geometry-dependent inhalation-exhalation differences that are suppressed under identical prescribed flow rates, with implications for global nasal resistance and regional airflow delivery.

Abstract: This study investigates inhalation-exhalation asymmetry in population-derived median unilateral human nasal cavity under pressure-driven breathing using time-resolved computational fluid dynamics (CFD). A standardized geometry reconstructed from averaged CT data of 30 adults was subjected to a sinusoidal nasopharyngeal pressure waveform over a complete breathing cycle. Low, intermediate, and high pressure amplitudes were considered to quantify breathing-phase-dependent changes in flow rate, Reynolds number, wall shear stress, nasal resistance, and olfactory-region airflow. For identical imposed pressure magnitudes, inhalation produced a consistently larger flow rate and lower aerodynamic resistance than exhalation, demonstrating a direction-dependent pressure-flow response. Increasing breathing intensity increased Reynolds number and nasal resistance while largely preserving the spatial pattern of the flow. During inhalation, stronger airflow penetrated further into the superior nasal cavity and produced higher and more localized wall shear stress, whereas exhalation exhibited smoother airflow redistribution. The breathing-phase asymmetry also affected regional ventilation: the fraction of total airflow reaching the olfactory region increased with inspiratory intensity and reached approximately 6% under the high-pressure condition, while the expiratory fraction remained comparatively insensitive to breathing intensity. These results demonstrate that pressure-driven transient simulations naturally capture geometry-dependent inhalation-exhalation differences that are suppressed when identical flow rates are prescribed for the two breathing phases, with consequences for both global nasal resistance and regional airflow delivery.

</details>

### [197] [Cryogenic suppression of correlated loss in Rydberg atom arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06613)
*Zhenpu Zhang, Brendan P. Marsh, Ting You Tan, Quinn Manning, Daniel H. Slichter, Adam M. Kaufman, Cindy A. Regal*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper suppresses Rydberg-atom avalanche decay with a 45 K radiation shield, establishes blackbody radiation as the dominant avalanche trigger, and quantitatively models the effect in arrays of up to 70 atoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blackbody radiation limits Rydberg-atom quantum science by shortening Rydberg lifetimes and is suspected to trigger avalanche events that cause correlated atom loss, which impedes fault-tolerant operation in neutral-atom quantum processors.

Method: The authors operate a Rydberg atom array either cryogenically or at room temperature and use a 45 K radiation shield to reduce ambient blackbody radiation. They measure Rydberg nS lifetimes for n from 50 to 75 and compare Rydberg-dressed arrays of up to 70 atoms for n=50 and n=60. A parameter-free rate model accounts for frequency-dependent cryogenic shielding, photoionization-triggered avalanches, laser phase noise, blackbody-radiation decay channels, and Rydberg interaction effects.

Result: The shield extends Rydberg nS lifetimes and suppresses blackbody-radiation-induced decay rates by 30%-50%, corresponding to an effective blackbody temperature of about 190 K. Cryogenically suppressed avalanche rates track the suppression in blackbody-induced decay for both n=50 and n=60 dressing, and the parameter-free model agrees with measured avalanche rates. The authors state that improved shielding should further suppress correlated loss toward the effective temperature of 45 K achievable in the current apparatus.

Conclusion: The work provides a quantitative description of avalanche decay and establishes a route to mitigating correlated errors that otherwise prevent fault-tolerant operation in neutral-atom quantum processors.

Abstract: Blackbody radiation (BBR) poses a central challenge for quantum science with Rydberg atoms. By driving transitions to nearby opposite-parity states, BBR shortens Rydberg lifetimes and has long been suspected to trigger "avalanche" events known to cause correlated atom loss. Here, we suppress avalanche decay and establish BBR as its dominant trigger using a Rydberg atom array that operates either cryogenically or at room temperature. A $45~\text{K}$ radiation shield reduces ambient BBR, extending Rydberg $nS$ lifetimes for $n$ ranging from 50 to 75. BBR-induced decay rates are suppressed by 30%-50%, corresponding to an effective blackbody temperature $T_{\rm eff}\approx190~\text{K}$. This enables controlled comparisons of Rydberg-dressed atom arrays operating in different BBR environments. Arrays of up to 70 atoms demonstrate cryogenically suppressed avalanche rates, tracking the suppression in BBR-induced decay, for both $n=50$ and $n=60$ dressing. A parameter-free rate model agrees with the measured avalanche rates after accounting for frequency-dependent cryogenic shielding, photoionization-triggered avalanches, laser phase noise, BBR decay channels, and Rydberg interaction effects. We demonstrate a quantitative description of avalanche decay and show that improved BBR shielding should further suppress correlated loss to the $T_\text{eff}=45~\text{K}$ achievable in the current apparatus. Our results establish a route to mitigating correlated errors that otherwise impede fault-tolerant operation in neutral-atom quantum processors.

</details>


### [198] [Nuclear recoil with electron vacuum polarization in muonic atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06480)
*Vojtěch Patkóš, Mateusz Pańtak, Krzysztof Pachucki*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the previously missing α(Zα)^5 m^2/M nuclear recoil correction in muonic atoms, evaluates it for several muonic systems, and finds that it substantially revises the logarithmic estimate for the 2P-2S Lamb shift in muonic helium ions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract implies the motivation is to complete the set of nuclear recoil corrections in muonic atoms by deriving the last missing α(Zα)^5 m^2/M term and assessing its numerical impact; a broader motivation is not explicitly stated.

Method: Using the heavy-particle QED approach, the authors derive the α(Zα)^5 m^2/M nuclear recoil correction arising from the combination of the (Zα)^5 m^2/M recoil with electron vacuum polarization, and present numerical results for the 1S, 2S, and 2P states of μH, μD, μ^3He^+, and μ^4He^+.

Result: Numerical values are given for the 1S, 2S, and 2P states of μH, μD, μ^3He^+, and μ^4He^+. For the 2P-2S Lamb shift in muonic helium ions, the correction has the opposite sign and is about four times smaller in magnitude than previous logarithmic estimates.

Conclusion: The missing recoil correction is derived and evaluated, and it significantly changes the previously estimated 2P-2S Lamb shift in muonic helium ions, reversing its sign and reducing its magnitude relative to earlier logarithmic estimates.

Abstract: Using the heavy-particle QED approach, we derive the last missing $α\,(Zα)^5\,m^2/M$ nuclear recoil correction in muonic atoms, which originates from the combination of the $(Zα)^5\,m^2/M$ recoil with the electron vacuum polarization. Numerical results are presented for the $1S$, $2S$, and $2P$ states of $μ$H, $μ$D, $μ^3$He$^+$, and $μ^4$He$^+$. For the $2P-2S$ Lamb shift in muonic helium ions, our results are of opposite sign and about four times smaller in magnitude than the previous logarithmic estimates.

</details>


### [199] [The two-loop Lamb shift: the final missing contribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05978)
*J. Sommerfeldt, V. A. Yerokhin, Z. Harman, C. H. Keitel*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper determines the electron self-energy contribution with a vacuum-polarization insertion in the photon line, the last significant unknown part of the two-loop Lamb shift, including higher-order alpha Z effects. This improves theoretical energy-level and transition-energy predictions for heavy hydrogen-like and lithium-like ions by up to an order of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The two-loop Lamb shift contained a remaining significant unknown contribution: electron self-energy with vacuum-polarization insertion into the photon line. Its determination is needed to improve theoretical predictions and enable more stringent tests of quantum electrodynamics in strong fields and accurate nuclear charge-radius determinations.

Method: The abstract states that the contribution is determined by calculation, including higher-order effects in alpha Z, where alpha is the fine-structure constant and Z is the nuclear charge number. Specific calculational techniques are not provided in the abstract.

Result: The electron self-energy with vacuum-polarization insertion into the photon line is determined including higher-order alpha Z effects. The calculation improves the accuracy of theoretical predictions for energy levels and transition energies in heavy hydrogen-like and lithium-like ions by up to one order of magnitude, enabling more stringent comparisons with available experimental results.

Conclusion: These results pave the way for more precise tests of quantum electrodynamics in the strong-field regime and for accurate determinations of nuclear charge radii.

Abstract: The last significant unknown contribution to the two-loop Lamb shift, namely the electron self-energy with vacuum-polarization insertion into the photon line, is determined including higher-order effects in the parameter $αZ$, with $α$ being the fine-structure constant and $Z$ the nuclear charge number. Our calculation improves the accuracy of theoretical predictions for energy levels and transition energies in heavy hydrogen-like and lithium-like ions by up to one order of magnitude, enabling more stringent comparisons between theory and available experimental results. These results pave the way for more precise tests of quantum electrodynamics in the strong-field regime, as well as for accurate determinations of nuclear charge radii.

</details>


### [200] [Sympathetic Cooling of Ionic Fullerenes with Ultracold Atoms to Millikelvin Temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05815)
*Saajid Chowdhury, Zhongqi Liang, Yu Wang, Jesús Pérez-Ríos*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes cooling C60+ ions to about 20 mK by combining He buffer-gas precooling with an ultracold Cd gas, supported by molecular-dynamics simulations with accurate ion-atom potentials and Monte Carlo predictions of Coulomb-crystal formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex molecular ions such as C60+ have intricate internal structures that prevent laser cooling, so millikelvin cooling remains a major challenge and alternative sympathetic cooling approaches are needed.

Method: The authors perform full molecular-dynamics simulations using accurate ion-atom interaction potentials derived from high-level electronic structure methods. They also use a Monte Carlo approach to predict formation of C60+ Coulomb crystals supported solely by collisions with the buffer gas, after initial precooling with He buffer gas and cooling with ultracold Cd atoms.

Result: The proposed approach can efficiently cool C60+ ions to average kinetic energies of approximately 20 mK. The simulations predict formation of C60+ Coulomb crystals supported solely by buffer-gas collisions, and the probability of reaching these cold states depends strongly on the number of trapped ions, indicating competition between neutral-atom cooling and many-ion dynamics in radio-frequency traps.

Conclusion: Ultracold Cd atoms combined with He buffer-gas precooling are predicted to be a viable route to millikelvin cooling of C60+ ions and to buffer-gas-supported Coulomb-crystal formation, with performance governed by the interplay of neutral-atom cooling and many-ion dynamics. Experimental demonstration is not reported in the abstract.

Abstract: Cooling complex molecular ions to millikelvin temperatures remains a major challenge because their intricate internal structures prevent laser cooling. Here, we propose to use an ultracold gas of Cd atoms to efficiently cool C$_{60}^+$ ions to average kinetic energies of approximately 20 mK, following an initial precooling stage with He buffer gas. Our approach is based on full molecular dynamics simulations using accurate ion-atom interaction potentials derived from high-level electronic structure methods. Furthermore, using a Monte Carlo approach, we predict the formation of C$_{60}^+$ Coulomb crystals supported solely by collisions with the buffer gas. The probability of reaching these cold states shows a pronounced dependence on the number of trapped ions, revealing the competition between neutral-atom cooling and many-ion dynamics in radio-frequency traps.

</details>


### [201] [Search for the 229Pa Parity Doublet via Internal-Conversion Spectroscopy at FRIB](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05494)
*Silviu-Marian Udrescu, Aiden Boyer, Lan Cheng, Ronald Fernando Garcia Ruiz, Phay J. Ho, Ryan Ringle, Jaideep T. Singh, Patrick R. Stollenwerk, Georg Tiebel, Margaret J. Tseng*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Penning-trap-based delayed-coincidence internal-conversion spectroscopy experiment to measure the 229Pa ground-state parity-doublet splitting with sub-eV precision, addressing a key uncertainty for using 229Pa in next-generation EDM searches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
