Motivation: 229Pa is predicted to have a nearly degenerate ground-state parity doublet, and prior gamma-ray and conversion-electron spectroscopy suggests a splitting of ΔE = (60 ± 50) eV, but the doublet remains unconfirmed. Such a small splitting could greatly enhance symmetry-violating nuclear properties, yet the splitting and thus the sensitivity of 229Pa to CP-violating physics remain uncertain by orders of magnitude.

Method: The proposed experiment would use delayed-coincidence internal-conversion spectroscopy of 229Pa ions produced by electron-capture decay of 229U+ ions stored in a Penning trap at the Facility for Rare Isotope Beams (FRIB).

Result: The abstract reports no experimental result. It proposes to measure the splitting with sub-eV precision within a few days of measurement time.

Conclusion: The proposed measurement would supply the key nuclear-structure input needed to establish 229Pa as a leading candidate for next-generation atomic and molecular electric-dipole-moment experiments.

Abstract: The radioisotope $^{229}$Pa is predicted to possess a nearly degenerate ground-state parity doublet. Previous $γ$-ray and conversion-electron spectroscopy suggests a splitting of $ΔE = (60\pm 50)~\mathrm{eV}$, although the doublet's existence remains unconfirmed. Such a small splitting would give $^{229}$Pa an exceptionally large enhancement of symmetry-violating nuclear properties. However, the splitting, and with it the sensitivity of $^{229}$Pa to CP-violating physics, remains uncertain by orders of magnitude. We propose an experiment to measure this splitting with sub-eV precision within a few days of measurement time by using delayed-coincidence internal-conversion spectroscopy of $^{229}$Pa ions produced by the electron-capture decay of $^{229}$U$^{+}$ ions stored in a Penning trap at the Facility for Rare Isotope Beams (FRIB). This measurement would provide the key nuclear-structure input needed to establish $^{229}$Pa as a leading candidate for next-generation atomic and molecular electric-dipole-moment (EDM) experiments.

</details>


### [202] [Ultracold polar molecules for quantum science and technology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05040)
*Philip D. Gregory, Simon L. Cornish*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This is a review of ultracold polar molecules as a quantum-science platform, emphasizing their intrinsic electric dipole moments, strong microwave coupling, and long-range, anisotropic, tunable dipole-dipole interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultracold polar molecules offer unique properties for experimental quantum science. Their intrinsic electric dipole moments enable strong microwave coupling and engineered dipole-dipole interactions that can generate entanglement and support studies of quantum many-body phenomena inaccessible in other systems. Recent landmark advances include controlled interactions in optical tweezers and lattices and bulk molecular gases cooled to quantum degeneracy.

Method: The abstract describes a conceptual review article intended to explain key concepts relevant to current research on ultracold polar molecules. It does not provide a specific experimental or theoretical method.

Result: No new experimental results are reported in the abstract. It cites demonstrations of controlled interactions between molecules in coherent quantum states in optical tweezers and lattices, and recent cooling of bulk molecular gases to quantum degeneracy.

Conclusion: Ultracold polar molecules constitute a rich platform for quantum science, and the article aims to explain the key concepts underlying current research in the field.

Abstract: Ultracold polar molecules present a rich platform for experimental quantum science leveraging their unique properties. Of particular relevance is the intrinsic electric dipole moment of the molecule, which results in strong coupling to microwave fields and can be used to engineer long-range, anisotropic, and tunable dipole-dipole interactions. Such interactions can generate entanglement between molecules and enable the study of novel quantum many-body phenomena in parameter regimes inaccessible in other systems. Modern experiments in optical tweezers and optical lattices have demonstrated controlled interactions between molecules in coherent quantum states, and recently bulk gases of molecules have been cooled to quantum degeneracy. Motivated by these landmark advances, we present this article to explain many of the key concepts relevant to current research in the field.

</details>


### [203] [Bayesian optimization enhanced magnetic field gradient detection based on spin-orbit coupled Bose-Einstein condensates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04719)
*Shixiang Chen, Juan Wang, Yan Li*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a magnetic-field-gradient detection scheme based on the rotational deflection angle of an expanding spin-orbit-coupled Bose-Einstein condensate, and uses Bayesian optimization to tune key parameters, improving gradiometer sensitivity by a factor of five.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic-field detection is important for medical diagnostics, geological exploration, inertial navigation, and space physics. Building on prior work on the expansion dynamics of a spin-orbit-coupled Bose-Einstein condensate, the authors aim to develop a magnetic-field-gradient measurement scheme and enhance its sensitivity.

Method: The authors establish a quantitative relationship between the condensate's rotational deflection angle and the magnetic-field gradient. They analyze the parameter dependence of the scheme, perform systematic optimization, and introduce a Bayesian optimization algorithm to automatically and efficiently search for the optimal combination of key parameters.

Result: The optimized gradiometer improves sensitivity by a factor of five. The abstract does not provide specific parameter values, sensitivity units, or experimental validation details.

Conclusion: The work offers a new approach for magnetic-field-gradient measurement and serves as a reference for machine-learning-empowered quantum precision measurement. The abstract does not provide further conclusions beyond the reported fivefold sensitivity improvement.

Abstract: Magnetic field detection is of great significance in modern fields such as medical diagnostics, geological exploration, inertial navigation, and space physics. We have previously investigated the expansion dynamics of a spin-orbit-coupled Bose-Einstein condensate. Building on these results, here we propose a scheme for magnetic-field-gradient detection by establishing a quantitative relationship between the condensate's rotational deflection angle and the magnetic-field gradient. In this work, we present the parameter dependence of this scheme and perform a systematic optimization. We introduce a Bayesian optimization algorithm to automatically and efficiently search for the optimal combination of key parameters, thereby significantly enhancing the sensitivity of the magnetic field gradient measurement. The results show that the optimized gradiometer improves the sensitivity by a factor of five. This work not only provides a new approach for magnetic field gradient measurement but also serves as a reference for machine learning-empowered quantum precision measurement.

</details>


### [204] [Accurate representation of the continuum orbital in a Gaussian basis set: application to Auger decay rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04202)
*Dibyendu Mahato, Saikat Roy, Wojciech Skomorowski*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Gaussian-based quantum-chemistry method for continuum electrons by solving the Lippmann-Schwinger equation in a discrete Gaussian-type orbital basis with an auxiliary Gaussian Green's-function representation, yielding multicentered multichannel scattering states and demonstrating it on Auger decay rates for Ne, H2O, CO2, and Ar.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuum electrons are central to processes such as autoionization, photoionization, and electron-impact collisions, but theoretical methods for modeling them are much less developed than methods for purely bound electronic states.

Method: Continuum orbitals are represented in a discrete basis of standard Gaussian-type orbitals. The continuum wave function is obtained by solving the Lippmann-Schwinger equation with proper boundary conditions, using an analytical Green's-function representation in an auxiliary Gaussian-type orbital basis set. The resulting continuum orbital is multicentered and multichannel.

Result: As an initial application, Auger decay rates are computed for Ne, H2O, CO2, and Ar. Results improve consistently with increasing quality of the continuum representation, and higher-order electron-correlation effects in the bound domain are shown to be essential for accurate treatment of satellite transitions.

Conclusion: The formalism provides a systematically improvable Gaussian-based framework for continuum-electron problems that is fully compatible with standard quantum chemistry methods.

Abstract: Many processes in chemistry and physics, such as autoionization, photoionization, or electron-impact collisions, involve unbound electrons. Despite their importance, theoretical methods for modeling continuum electrons remain significantly less developed than those for purely bound electronic states. Here we present a novel approach for treating continuum electrons within Gaussian-based quantum chemistry. The method represents continuum orbital in a discrete basis of standard Gaussian-type orbitals (GTOs), where the continuum wave function is obtained by solving the Lippmann-Schwinger equation with proper boundary conditions using an analytical representation of the Green's function in an auxiliary GTO basis set. The resulting continuum orbital is multicentered and multichannel, providing a rigorous description of scattering states. As an initial application of the new methodology, we compute Auger decay rates for several atomic and molecular systems, including Ne, H$_2$O, CO$_2$, and Ar. We demonstrate a consistent improvement of the results with increasing the quality of the continuum representation. We show also that higher-order electron-correlation effects in the bound domain are essential for an accurate treatment of satellite transitions. The present formalism therefore provides a systematically improvable Gaussian-based framework for continuum-electron problems that is fully compatible with standard quantum chemistry methods.

</details>


### [205] [Dual-Space Analysis of Vortex Configurations and Rotational Energy in a 2D Optical Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02473)
*Avra Banerjee*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A numerical mean-field study shows that a stationary two-dimensional periodic optical lattice strongly modifies vortex nucleation, spatial and momentum-space structure, and rotational energetics in a rotating, harmonically trapped Bose-Einstein condensate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates how a stationary two-dimensional periodic optical lattice affects a rotating Bose-Einstein condensate confined by an isotropic harmonic trap, focusing on the influence of lattice depth, lattice wave vector, particle number, and angular velocity on vortex configurations, real- and momentum-space densities, and energetics.

Method: The condensate is modeled within mean-field theory by the two-dimensional Gross-Pitaevskii equation in the rotating frame and solved numerically with an imaginary-time split-step Crank-Nicolson method to obtain stationary vortex states.

Result: The lattice significantly modifies vortex nucleation and spatial organization: vortices emerge progressively with increasing angular velocity and, for sufficiently strong lattice strength, rearrange and merge. These configuration changes are directly reflected in the momentum-space density. Energy per particle and rotational energy are analyzed as functions of angular velocity; the optical lattice substantially alters the rotational response, with rotational energy progressively reduced at higher angular velocities as lattice strength increases.

Conclusion: A stationary periodic optical lattice modifies vortex formation, momentum-space structure, and rotational energetics of a rotating trapped Bose-Einstein condensate.

Abstract: We investigate the effects of a stationary two-dimensional periodic optical lattice on a rotating Bose--Einstein condensate confined by an isotropic harmonic trap. Within the mean-field description, the condensate is modeled by the two-dimensional Gross--Pitaevskii equation in the rotating frame and solved numerically using an imaginary-time split-step Crank--Nicolson method to obtain stationary vortex states. We examine how the lattice depth, lattice wave vector, particle number, and angular velocity influence the vortex configurations, real-space density, momentum-space density, and energetics of the condensate. We find that the stationary periodic lattice significantly modifies vortex nucleation and spatial organization: vortices emerge progressively with increasing angular velocity and, for sufficiently strong lattice strength, undergo rearrangement and merging. Importantly, changes in the vortex configuration are directly reflected in the momentum-space density, demonstrating that vortex nucleation and rearrangement produce pronounced modifications of the condensate momentum distribution. We further analyze the energy per particle and rotational energy as functions of the angular velocity and find that the presence of the optical lattice substantially alters the rotational response of the condensate. In particular, the rotational energy is progressively reduced at higher angular velocities as the lattice strength is increased. These results demonstrate how a stationary periodic optical lattice modifies vortex formation, momentum-space structure, and rotational energetics of a rotating trapped Bose--Einstein condensate.

</details>


### [206] [Universal Threshold Memory in Bound-State Recapture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01056)
*Sajad Azizi*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A bound state slowly moved to its continuum threshold and back retains a memory of the drive rates: the recaptured fraction depends on the ratio of approach and return rates even when both are slow. The effect arises from phase-sensitive overlap of two threshold packets and is quantified in closed form for a short-range s-wave model, with a maximum recapture of 1/φ² at matched rates. A ratio of three recaptured fractions can expose this memory with finite pulses, and coupled-channel calculations for radio-frequency-dressed 85Rb halo dimers support accessibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper tests the usual expectation that a slowly driven quantum system forgets how it was driven, focusing on whether a bound state taken slowly to its continuum threshold and back also loses its drive history. It aims to identify the rate-dependent recapture mechanism and an experimentally accessible signature of threshold memory.

Method: The authors analyze recapture after a bound state is slowly taken to the continuum threshold and returned, comparing approach and return rates. They describe recapture through the phase-sensitive overlap of two threshold packets whose sizes are set by the two rates, and they show that a factorized treatment of approach and return misses the dependence. For a short-range s-wave state whose inverse scattering length touches zero quadratically in time, they derive the rate-ratio dependence in closed form. They also propose using a ratio of three recaptured fractions to access the threshold memory with finite pulses and perform coupled-channel calculations for radio-frequency-dressed 85Rb halo dimers with half-pulse widths of 30 and 120 μs.

Result: The recaptured fraction depends on the ratio of the approach and return rates however slow both rates are; common slowing preserves the ratio of the two threshold-packet sizes. For the short-range s-wave model with inverse scattering length vanishing quadratically in time, the dependence is obtained in closed form, with the maximum recapture 1/φ² at matched rates, where φ is the golden ratio. A ratio of three recaptured fractions makes the threshold memory accessible with finite pulses. For the specified 85Rb halo dimers, coupled-channel calculations give this ratio within 0.5% of its slow-limit value.

Conclusion: Slow driving does not generally erase the drive history at the continuum threshold: recapture retains a phase-sensitive memory controlled by the approach-to-return rate ratio, and a factorized approach-and-return treatment is insufficient. The closed-form s-wave result and the proposed three-fraction ratio provide a quantitative route to this threshold memory, with coupled-channel calculations indicating that it can be reached with finite pulses for the studied 85Rb dimers. The abstract does not report an experimental measurement.

Abstract: Driven slowly, a quantum system forgets how it was driven. We show that a bound state taken slowly to its continuum threshold and back does not. The recaptured fraction depends on the ratio of the approach and return rates, however slow both are. The two rates set the sizes of two threshold packets whose phase-sensitive overlap fixes recapture. Common slowing preserves their size ratio. A factorized treatment of approach and return misses this dependence. For a short-range $s$-wave state whose inverse scattering length touches zero quadratically in time, we obtain the dependence in closed form, with the maximum $1/\varphi^2$ at matched rates, where $\varphi$ is the golden ratio. A ratio of three recaptured fractions makes this threshold memory accessible with finite pulses. For radio-frequency-dressed $^{85}$Rb halo dimers with half-pulse widths of 30 and $120~μ$s, coupled-channel calculations give this ratio within $0.5\%$ of its slow-limit value.

</details>


### [207] [Coherent heat-bath configuration interaction with many-body perturbation theory for multivalent atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00731)
*Shi-Cheng Yu, Zhen-Xiang Zhong, Cheng-Bin Li*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops coherent heat-bath configuration interaction in a configuration-state-function basis with many-body perturbation theory to compress configuration-interaction spaces while retaining accuracy; benchmarks on Al, Si, and P show substantial compression with small excitation-energy deviations, and the perturbative correction improves total energies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial growth of configuration-interaction spaces limits accurate calculations as the valence-electron number and orbital space increase, motivating reduction of the configuration-interaction space size.

Method: The authors develop coherent heat-bath configuration interaction in a configuration-state-function basis and combine it with many-body perturbation theory. Configurations are selected by thresholding their coherent couplings to target wave functions, and a perturbative correction recovers residual valence correlation.

Result: For Al, a stringent threshold gives about 16–37-fold compression with excitation-energy deviations from uncompressed calculations below 0.74 /cm; a looser threshold gives 63–113-fold compression with a maximum deviation of 18.22 /cm. For Si and P, roughly threefold to eightfold compression is achieved with maximum relative deviations of approximately 0.70% and 0.32%, respectively, for optical excitation energies. The perturbative correction improves the accuracy of all calculated total energies.

Conclusion: The results support extending the method to high-precision calculations in more complex atomic systems.

Abstract: Combinatorial growth of configuration-interaction spaces limits accurate calculations as the valence-electron number and orbital space increase. To reduce the size of the configuration-interaction space, we develop coherent heat-bath configuration interaction in a configuration-state-function basis and combine it with many-body perturbation theory. Configurations are selected by thresholding their coherent couplings to target wave functions, and a perturbative correction recovers residual valence correlation. For Al, a stringent threshold gives about 16-37 fold compression with excitation-energy deviations from the uncompressed calculations below 0.74 /cm; a looser threshold gives 63-113 fold compression with a maximum deviation of 18.22 /cm. For Si and P, roughly threefold to eightfold compression is achieved with maximum relative deviations of approximately 0.70% and 0.32%, respectively, for optical excitation energies. The perturbative correction improves the accuracy of all calculated total energies. These results support extending the method to high-precision calculations in more complex atomic systems.

</details>


### [208] [$^{85}$Rb electromagnetically induced transparency and absorption on two collinear $5S_{1/2}\rightarrow5P_{1/2}\rightarrow5D_{3/2}\rightarrow 25F_{5/2}$ three-photon cascade schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00416)
*Shuaijie Li, Alisher Duspayev, Georg Raithel*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper reports a combined experimental and theoretical study of three-photon electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) and absorption (EIA) in room-temperature Rb vapor, using the 85Rb cascade 5S1/2→5P1/2→5D3/2→25F5/2 under two collinear laser-beam configurations. Rich EIT/EIA features are modeled with dressed-atom and quantum Monte Carlo trajectory methods including 108 fine- and hyperfine-structure levels and magnetic substates, with good agreement to experiment; additional spectra in Rb cells with 50 and 500 mTorr Ar probe collisional effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to explain the rich sets of EIT/EIA features and substructures observed in probe transmission maps when the detunings of the upper two cascade lasers are varied, and to explore collision-induced spectral features, collisional loss of EIT/EIA visibility, velocity-changing collisions, and hyperfine collisions. The work is motivated by multi-level EIT systems that underpin emerging quantum technologies, where accurate accounting of internal atomic structure, laser beam geometry, optical pumping, transit time, and collisional effects is critical.

Method: The authors study three-photon EIT and EIA in room-temperature Rb vapor using the 85Rb cascade 5S1/2→5P1/2→5D3/2→25F5/2 and two collinear laser-beam configurations, varying the detunings of the upper two lasers and recording probe transmission maps. They use a dressed-atom approach and a quantum Monte Carlo trajectory method that includes 108 relevant fine- and hyperfine-structure levels and their magnetic substates, accounting for coherent optical drives, optical pumping, atom recycling, electromagnetic decays via the 5PJ levels, and velocity-changing and hyperfine collisions. They find quantum-trajectory solutions of the Lindblad master equation and acquire spectra in Rb cells with 50 and 500 mTorr of Ar gas.

Result: Both collinear laser-beam configurations produce probe transmission maps with rich EIT/EIA features and substructures. The quantum-trajectory solutions of the Lindblad master equation show good agreement with the experimental data and help provide physical explanations for the observed spectra. Spectra in Rb cells with 50 and 500 mTorr Ar are acquired to further explore collision-induced spectral features, collisional loss of EIT/EIA visibility, velocity-changing collisions, and hyperfine collisions. Specific quantitative values for these effects are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the combined dressed-atom and quantum Monte Carlo trajectory treatment, with detailed atomic structure and collisional terms, can account well for the observed three-photon EIT/EIA spectra and assist in physical interpretation. The work is relevant to multi-level EIT systems used in emerging quantum technologies, where accurate treatment of internal atomic structure, laser beam geometry, optical pumping, transit time, and collisional effects is critical.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive study of three-photon electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) and absorption (EIA) in room-temperature Rb vapor. Using the cascade system $5S_{1/2}\rightarrow5P_{1/2}\rightarrow5D_{3/2}\rightarrow 25F_{5/2}$ in $^{85}$Rb, we explore two collinear laser-beam configurations. The detunings of the upper two lasers of the cascade system are varied. In both configurations, the probe transmission maps contain rich sets of EIT/EIA features and substructures. To explain these, we utilize a dressed-atom and a quantum Monte Carlo trajectory method. The latter includes 108 relevant fine- and hyperfine-structure levels and their magnetic substates, accounting for coherent optical drives, optical pumping, atom recycling, electromagnetic decays via the $5P_{J}$ levels, and velocity-changing and hyperfine collisions. We find quantum-trajectory solutions of the Lindblad master equation, which show good agreement with experimental data and aid in finding physical explanations for the observed spectra. To further explore collision-induced spectral features, collisional loss of EIT/EIA visibility, velocity-changing and hyperfine collisions, we acquire spectra in Rb cells with 50 and 500~mTorr of Ar gas. Our work is of interest to studies of multi-level EIT systems that underpin emerging quantum technologies, where accurate accounting of internal atomic structure, laser beam geometry, optical pumping, transit time, and collisional effects is critical.

</details>


### [209] [Nuclear Charge Radii from Precision Bound-Electron g-Factor Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00343)
*Bastian Sikora*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes determining absolute nuclear charge radii from high-precision bound-electron g-factor measurements in hydrogenlike ions. Using recent QED advances, it reports up to two-order-of-magnitude precision improvements for several poorly known heavy isotopes and a new independent route to test nuclear structure models and anomalies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for an efficient, nuclear-model-independent determination of absolute nuclear charge radii and for independent tests of nuclear structure models and resolution of nuclear structure anomalies.

Method: The authors put forward a method to extract absolute nuclear charge radii from high-precision bound-electron g-factor measurements in hydrogenlike ions, assuming a nuclear-model-independent radius determination is possible and using recent breakthroughs in the QED theory of the bound-electron g-factor.

Result: The method is demonstrated to improve the precision of the nuclear radius for several poorly known heavy isotopes by up to two orders of magnitude compared with current literature values.

Conclusion: The proposed approach opens a new, independent pathway for testing nuclear structure models and resolving nuclear structure anomalies.

Abstract: An efficient method to determine absolute nuclear charge radii from high-precision bound-electron g-factor measurements in hydrogenlike ions is put forward. Assuming that a nuclear model independent radius determination is possible, we demonstrate that this method can improve the precision of the nuclear radius for several poorly known heavy isotopes by up to two orders of magnitude compared to current literature values, by utilizing recent breakthroughs in the quantum electrodynamics (QED) theory of the bound-electron g-factor [B. Sikora, V. A. Yerokhin, C. H. Keitel, and Z. Harman, Phys. Rev. Lett. 134, 123001 (2025)]. This opens a new, independent pathway for testing nuclear structure models and resolving nuclear structure anomalies.

</details>


### [210] [Reversibility of Nonequilibrium Dynamics in a Unitary Fermi Gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00563)
*Xi-Zhi Wu, Xiangchuan Yan, Xin Xie, Dali Sun, Seong-Ho Shinn, Adolfo del Campo, Kaijun Jiang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper theoretically and experimentally demonstrates reversibility of a nonequilibrium state in a unitary Fermi gas held in a time-dependent trap, showing that reversal requires the accumulated dynamical phase to be an integer multiple of 2π, Δφ = 2ω0 s(T) = 2nπ. Two reversible pathway types are identified, and the condition makes the round-trip time discrete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to establish a clear criterion for quantum control of nonequilibrium many-body systems beyond linear response and to provide practical pathways to reversibility out of equilibrium.

Method: The authors theoretically describe and experimentally observe reversibility in an isotropic unitary Fermi gas confined in a time-dependent optical trap. They drive the trap through a round trip and analyze the condition Δφ = 2ω0 s(T) = 2nπ, where ω0 is the initial trapping frequency and s(T) is the rescaled time after total evolution time T. They identify space-time scaling-invariant and polynomial shortcut-to-adiabaticity (STA) protocols as reversible pathways.

Result: Reversibility of an initial nonequilibrium state is demonstrated experimentally and described theoretically. Two types of reversible pathways are found: space-time scaling-invariant and polynomial STA protocols. The phase condition restricts the round-trip time T to discrete values, a distinguishing feature of nonequilibrium dynamics.

Conclusion: The work establishes a clear criterion for quantum control of nonequilibrium many-body systems beyond linear response and provides practical pathways to reversibility, with the discrete round-trip-time condition arising from the nonequilibrium phase requirement.

Abstract: We theoretically describe and experimentally observe the reversibility of a nonequilibrium state in a unitary Fermi gas confined in a time-dependent trap. Unlike the equilibrium case, reversibility out of equilibrium imposes a stringent condition: the accumulated dynamical phase must be an integer multiple of $2π$, i.e., $Δφ= 2ω_{0} s(T) = 2nπ$, where $ω_{0}$ is the initial trapping frequency and $s(T)$ is the rescaled time after a total evolution time $T$. We demonstrate the reversibility of an initial nonequilibrium state in an isotropic unitary Fermi gas by driving the optical trap through a round trip. We find two types of reversible pathways, the space-time scaling-invariant and polynomial shortcut-to-adiabaticity (STA) protocols. The phase condition restricts the round-trip time $T$ to discrete values, a distinguishing feature of nonequilibrium dynamics. Our work establishes a clear criterion for the quantum control of nonequilibrium many-body systems beyond linear response and provides practical pathways to reversibility.

</details>


### [211] [The Valence and Inner Shell Ionization Properties of Sr I - III by Non-Thermal Electrons in Kilonovae](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00335)
*Steven Bromley, Luke Shingles, Evan Garbe, Niall McElroy, Connor Ballance, Michael Fogle, Phillip Stancil, Stuart Loch*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper calculates valence and inner-shell ionization cross sections for Sr I, Sr II, and Sr III with the Flexible Atomic Code, incorporates them into Spencer-Fano non-thermal electron degradation and ionization-balance models for kilonova spectral analysis, and shows that excitation autoionization and final-charge-state-resolved Auger branching ratios materially affect ionization balance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strontium was identified in spectra of the first multi-messenger kilonova, AT2017gfo, and its evolving spectral signature indicates a rapidly changing ionization balance. Late-time spectral interpretation requires better understanding of recombination and ionization, including ionization by non-thermal electrons produced by beta decay. Published Sr I and Sr II ionization cross sections show complex structures likely from excitation autoionization, motivating consistent calculations for Sr I, Sr II, and Sr III.

Method: The authors use the Flexible Atomic Code to calculate valence and inner-shell ionization cross sections for Sr I, Sr II, and Sr III. These cross sections are incorporated into Spencer-Fano models of non-thermal electron degradation coupled to an ionization balance solver. Excitation autoionization is treated as excitation with additional Auger electron production, and its effect on non-thermal degradation spectra is explored. Calculations are performed at level- and configuration-average resolution, and the impact of final-charge-state-resolved branching ratios for inner-shell ionization, including multi-ionization from successive Auger cascades, is tested.

Result: The calculations recover complex structures similar to those in published Sr I and Sr II cross sections and suggest negligible excitation-autoionization contributions for Sr III, for which no measurements exist. Level- and configuration-average calculations give ion fractions agreeing within 5-20%. Including final-charge-state-resolved branching ratios for inner-shell ionization produces multi-ionization via successive Auger cascades, and omitting these branching ratios is shown to have large effects on the ionization balance.

Conclusion: The new Sr I, Sr II, and Sr III ionization cross sections support more detailed non-thermal electron and ionization-balance modeling relevant to kilonova spectra. Excitation autoionization and final-charge-state-resolved Auger branching are important for accurate ionization balance. The calculated cross sections will be provided on Zenodo.

Abstract: The presence of strontium (Z = 38) was identified from spectra of the first multi-messenger kilonova transient, AT2017gfo, with the evolution of its spectral signature indicating a rapidly changing ionization balance. Interpretation of spectra at later times requires a more detailed understanding of recombination and ionization processes, including ionization by `non-thermal' electrons produced by beta decay. Published measurements of the ionization cross sections of Sr I and Sr II show complex structures likely arising from excitation autoionization. Using the Flexible Atomic Code we report calculations of the valence and inner shell ionization cross sections for Sr I, Sr II, and Sr III, and recover similar structures in the ionization cross sections. Though no measurements exist for Sr III, the calculations suggest negligible contributions from excitation autoionization. These new data are incorporated into Spencer-Fano models of non-thermal electron degradation and coupled to an ionization balance solver. The effects of excitation autoionization, treated as excitation with the additional production of an Auger electron, on the degradation spectra of non-thermal are explored. Calculations at both level- and configuration-average resolution return ion fractions that agree within 5-20%. The calculations probe the impact of including final-charge-state-resolved branching ratios for inner shell ionization which result in multi-ionization events from successive Auger cascades. Omission of these branching ratios is shown to have large impacts on the ionization balance. The calculated cross sections will be provided on Zenodo.

</details>



<div id="physics.comp-ph"></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)


### [212] [Quantum Spin Wave Theory with Multi-Spin Interactions for Magnetic Materials Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06833)
*Andrey Rybakov, Marco Marino, Yaroslav M. Blanter, Eugenio Coronado, Jaime Ferrer*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies linear spin wave theory (LSWT) to a polynomial localized spin model (PLSM) containing spin-operator terms of arbitrary order, enabling energy-landscape and spin-wave-spectrum analysis for arbitrary collinear and non-collinear magnetic phases, and presents an open-source LSWT implementation called Magnopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate and flexible methods are needed to simulate physical properties of effective spin models obtained from ab initio calculations or experimental fits, especially because parameterization often involves multi-stage calculations relating parameters to material properties or mapping a Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian onto an effective spin model.

Method: The authors use a new spin-Hamiltonian formulation, the polynomial localized spin model (PLSM), which includes terms of arbitrary order in the spin operators and cleanly separates classical and quantum parts. They apply LSWT directly to analyze the energy landscape and spin-wave spectrum of arbitrary collinear and non-collinear phases in realistic magnetic materials. They also present Magnopy, an open-source LSWT implementation that interfaces with modern parameterization software, supports arbitrary collinear and non-collinear systems, and is designed for scripting and standalone use.

Result: LSWT can be applied directly to the PLSM to analyze energy landscapes and spin-wave spectra for arbitrary collinear and non-collinear magnetic phases, with a clean separation between classical and quantum contributions. Magnopy is presented as an open-source implementation with the stated interfacing, magnetic-structure, and usage capabilities. The abstract does not provide quantitative results, benchmarks, or validation details.

Conclusion: The work provides a formulation and open-source computational tool for applying LSWT to general polynomial spin models across collinear and non-collinear magnetic phases. Specific performance, accuracy comparisons, and empirical validation are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: In the field of magnetism within condensed matter physics, magnetic systems are microscopically described by a spin Hamiltonian. Its parameterization is accessible through both ab initio calculations and fits to experimental data. Frequently, this is achieved via multi-stage calculations that either relate the parameters to observable material properties or map a Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian onto an effective spin model. In this realm, accurate and flexible methods are essential for simulating the physical properties of the latter. The present study focuses on one such method: spin wave theory. The approach is applied to a new formulation of the spin Hamiltonian, named polynomial localized spin model (PLSM), which includes terms of arbitrary order in the spin operators. This enables the direct application of linear spin wave theory (LSWT) to the analysis of the energy landscape and spin-wave spectrum of arbitrary collinear and non-collinear phases in any realistic magnetic material. Moreover, the classical and quantum parts of the PLSM are cleanly separated. Finally, an open-source implementation of LSWT, named Magnopy, is presented. It interfaces with modern parameterization software, supports arbitrary collinear and non-collinear systems, and is designed for both scripting and standalone use.

</details>


### [213] [Energy-Guided Transport for Projection-Free Physics-Informed Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06711)
*Giuliano Galadini, Aurelio Uncini, Danilo Comminiello*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Energy-Guided Transport is a training-free Flow Matching framework that enforces non-linear physical constraints by applying a continuous dissipative force to intermediate generative states, avoiding rigid algebraic projections and preserving the flow's natural dynamics with O(N) complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Flow Matching models are statistically driven and agnostic to governing equations, so they can violate fundamental physical constraints during inference. Existing zero-shot constraint-enforcement methods rely on rigid algebraic projections, which are effective for sparse boundary conditions but may disrupt the probability path, deviate from the learned manifold, and introduce substantial computational overhead for dense, full-domain PDEs.

Method: The paper formulates non-linear physical constraints as a smooth potential energy landscape and applies a continuous dissipative force directly to intermediate generative states rather than interrupting ODE integration. The force is evaluated implicitly via reverse-mode automatic differentiation, preserving the flow's natural dynamics while maintaining O(N) algorithmic complexity.

Result: The framework was evaluated on various 1D and 2D physical systems, including the chaotic Navier-Stokes equations. It achieved stable generation without structural divergence, significantly improved the stability-accuracy trade-off compared with state-of-the-art methods, and substantially accelerated inference relative to exact projection strategies.

Conclusion: Energy-Guided Transport provides a training-free, projection-free approach for enforcing physical constraints in Flow Matching by smoothly steering generative states toward the physical manifold, yielding stable and efficient generation with an improved stability-accuracy trade-off.

Abstract: Deep generative models, particularly Flow Matching frameworks, have emerged as powerful tools for synthesizing complex physical systems. However, standard architectures remain purely statistically driven and agnostic to the underlying governing equations, often violating fundamental physical constraints during inference. Existing zero-shot approaches designed to enforce these constraints typically rely on rigid algebraic projections. While effective for sparse boundary conditions, applying such discrete geometric corrections to dense, full-domain PDEs may disrupt the probability path, \rev{potentially deviating from the learned manifold and introducing substantial computational overhead}. To address these challenges, we introduce Energy-Guided Transport, a novel training-free framework that formulates non-linear physical constraints as a smooth potential energy landscape. Rather than interrupting the ODE integration, our method applies a continuous dissipative force directly to the intermediate generative states, steering them toward the physical manifold. By evaluating this force implicitly via reverse-mode automatic differentiation, our framework preserves the natural dynamics of the flow while maintaining an efficient $\mathcal{O}(N)$ algorithmic complexity. We extensively evaluate our framework across various 1D and 2D physical systems, including the chaotic Navier-Stokes equations. Our method achieves stable generation without structural divergence, significantly improves the stability-accuracy trade-off compared to state-of-the-art methods, and substantially accelerates inference with respect to exact projection strategies. Code available at: https://github.com/ispamm/Projection-free-energy-guided-flow-matching.

</details>


### [214] [Remap for ALE Hydrodynamics via Direct Interpolation and Constraint Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05327)
*Brendan Keith, Dohyun Kim, Boyan Lazarov, Ketan Mittal, Cosmin G. Petra, Mathias R. Schmidt, Vladimir Z. Tomov*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes an optimization-based remap method for ALE hydrodynamics that combines direct physical-space interpolation with a conservation-restoring optimization step to preserve sharp features and physical bounds while conserving volume, mass, momentum, and total energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The main challenge in remap methods is simultaneously preserving sharp features and physical bounds while conserving global quantities. The approach targets high-order finite element discretizations and ALE methods with large, purely Lagrangian displacements, and seeks to avoid computationally costly and diffusive pseudo-transient PDE-based advection.

Method: All fields are first transferred by direct physical-space interpolation using recent parallel, GPU-capable interpolation routines, producing bounded fields with minimal numerical diffusion. Because this interpolation may violate global conservation, a subsequent optimization step restores conservation while preserving physical bounds. The method supports arbitrary-order finite element fields, directly remaps quantities defined at integration points without projection onto finite element spaces, and mitigates synchronization issues between primal variables and conserved quantities in advection-based remap methods.

Result: The interpolation step yields bounded fields with minimal numerical diffusion but may violate conservation; the optimization step restores conservation while preserving bounds. The method is evaluated using standard two- and three-dimensional remap benchmarks and full ALE hydrodynamics simulations. Quantitative performance results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed optimization-based remap provides minimally diffusive remapping of finite element fields of arbitrary order, conserves global volume, mass, momentum, and total energy, preserves physical bounds and sharp features, directly remaps integration-point quantities without projection, and mitigates synchronization challenges typical of advection-based remap methods.

Abstract: This work presents an optimization-based remap approach for arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE) hydrodynamics that preserves sharp features and physical bounds while conserving global quantities such as volume, mass, momentum, and total energy. Achieving these properties is the main challenge in remap methods. The presented approach is well-suited for high-order finite element (FE) discretizations and ALE methods that allow for large, purely Lagrangian displacements between successive remeshing and remapping steps. Recent advances in parallel, GPU-capable interpolation routines enable the initial transfer of all fields via direct physical-space interpolation, avoiding computationally costly and diffusive pseudo-transient PDE-based advection. The interpolation produces bounded fields with minimal numerical diffusion. However, it may violate the desired conservation of global properties. Thus, a subsequent optimization step restores conservation while preserving physical bounds. The presented method provides minimally diffusive remapping of finite element fields of arbitrary order. Moreover, it directly remaps quantities defined at integration points, eliminating the need for projection onto finite element spaces. The approach also mitigates typical challenges associated with synchronizing primal variables and conserved quantities in advection-based remap methods. The proposed optimization-based remap method is evaluated using standard two- and three-dimensional remap benchmarks and full ALE hydrodynamics simulations.

</details>


### [215] [ConvexAD: differentiable phase retrieval with geometric regularization for high-strain, low-signal Bragg coherent diffraction imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05020)
*Matteo Masto, Ewen Bellec, Etienne Polack, Jiangtao Zhao, Vincent Favre-Nicolin, Steven J. Leake, Tobias Schülli, Marie-Ingrid Richard*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: ConvexAD is a gradient-descent phase-retrieval method for Bragg coherent diffraction imaging that represents the nanocrystal as a convex polyhedron with enforced flat amplitude, reducing amplitude-phase mixing and trainable parameters and improving reconstruction under high strain, low signal, and reduced coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BCDI phase retrieval is a non-convex, ill-posed inverse problem whose conditioning is degraded by noise, partial coherence, and strain or crystallographic defects. Conventional alternating-projection and deep learning methods use dense voxel grids, which are prone to amplitude-phase mixing and become increasingly ill-posed, often preventing reliable three-dimensional mapping of particle shape and strain.

Method: ConvexAD parametrizes the object shape as a convex polyhedron formed by the intersection of half-spaces and enforces flat amplitude within it by construction. This geometric regularization eliminates amplitude-phase mixing and reduces the number of trainable parameters, facilitating phase-retrieval convergence. It is implemented in JAX for native GPU acceleration and forward-model flexibility and is validated on simulated and experimental BCDI data.

Result: ConvexAD outperforms conventional and deep-learning-assisted phase retrieval at high strain, low signal, and reduced coherence. The abstract does not provide quantitative performance metrics.

Conclusion: ConvexAD extends the conditions under which quantitative BCDI reconstruction is achievable by using convex-polyhedron shape parametrization and flat-amplitude enforcement to improve phase-retrieval conditioning.

Abstract: Bragg Coherent Diffraction Imaging (BCDI) relies on phase retrieval (PR), a non-convex, ill-posed inverse problem whose conditioning is degraded by factors like noise, partial coherence, and strain or crystallographic defects in the imaged nanocrystal. Conventional approaches, including alternating projections and deep learning methods, represent the object on a dense voxel grid, which is prone to amplitude-phase mixing and becomes increasingly ill-posed under these factors, often preventing reliable three-dimensional mapping of the particle's shape and strain. Here we present ConvexAD, a gradient descent PR model that parametrizes the object shape as a convex polyhedron, built by the intersection of half-spaces and enforces flat amplitude within it by construction. This geometric regularization eliminates amplitude-phase mixing and reduces the number of trainable parameters, hence facilitating the convergence of the PR. Implemented in JAX for native GPU acceleration and forward-model flexibility, ConvexAD is validated on simulated and experimental BCDI data, where it outperforms conventional and deep learning assisted PR at high strain, low signal, and reduced coherence, extending the conditions under which quantitative BCDI reconstruction is achievable.

</details>


### [216] [SCANO: Restoring Boundary-Condition Completeness in Sampled Meshes with a Sliced-Cross-Attention Neural Operator for 3D Thermal Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04603)
*Tianyu Li, Zhiwei Cao, Qingang Zhang, Ruihang Wang, Binyang Song, Yonggang Wen*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Training neural operators on million-cell meshes is memory-limited, and naive subsampling distorts boundary conditioning. The paper derives measurable consistency conditions for boundary conditioning under subsampling and proposes two backbone-agnostic modules, BQA and AGS, combined in SCANO, which improve whole-mesh and regional accuracy and enable fast room-scale prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operators trained on million-cell meshes can exceed GPU memory limits, so models are trained on subsamples but deployed on whole meshes or selected regions. Subsampling can distort boundary information and degrade accuracy; the paper asks when boundary conditioning under sampling remains consistent with whole-mesh training and how to aggregate and inject boundary data.

Method: The solution is represented as an integral of boundary data against type-dependent measures. The analysis identifies three subsampling distortions: incomplete boundary coverage or a sample-dependent global state, face weighting by count rather than area, and compression of boundary inputs into a low-dimensional vector. This yields four measurable consistency conditions checkable before training. Two backbone-agnostic modules satisfy them: Boundary Quadrature Attention (BQA) aggregates all boundary faces, typed and area-weighted, into slice tokens that every query point reads at every layer; Anchored Global State (AGS) estimates the global state from anchors independent of the sampled points. Adding both to a scalable message-passing and slice-attention backbone yields SCANO.

Result: On controlled and public benchmarks and a data-centre thermal simulation dataset (DCTS) with million-cell meshes, BQA improves every backbone, reducing whole-mesh error up to 15-fold. On all three datasets, AGS aligns regional with whole-mesh predictions, cutting regional error at least tenfold for most backbones on the public benchmark. Together, the modules nearly halve the best original backbone's whole-mesh error on both benchmarks. SCANO predicts a room-scale field in under one second, over 100 times faster than whole-room anchor self-attention.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that boundary conditioning under subsampling can be analyzed through measurable consistency conditions and addressed with backbone-agnostic BQA and AGS modules. Combined in SCANO, these modules improve whole-mesh accuracy, regional consistency, and computational efficiency for large-mesh neural operator settings.

Abstract: Training neural operators on million-cell meshes can exceed GPU memory limits. Models are therefore trained on subsamples and deployed on whole meshes or selected regions. However, subsampling can distort boundary information, degrading accuracy. We ask when boundary conditioning under sampling stays consistent with whole-mesh training, and how to aggregate and inject boundary data. Representing the solution as an integral of boundary data against type-dependent measures, we show that subsampling distorts this conditioning in three ways: incomplete boundary coverage or a sample-dependent global state, face weighting by count rather than area, and compression of boundary inputs into a low-dimensional vector. This analysis yields four measurable consistency conditions checkable before training. We satisfy them with two backbone-agnostic modules. Boundary Quadrature Attention (BQA) aggregates all boundary faces, typed and area-weighted, into slice tokens that every query point reads at every layer. Anchored Global State (AGS) estimates the global state from anchors independent of the sampled points. Adding both to a scalable message-passing and slice-attention backbone yields SCANO. We evaluate the approach on controlled and public benchmarks and a data-centre thermal simulation dataset (DCTS) containing million-cell meshes. On both benchmarks, BQA improves every backbone, reducing whole-mesh error up to 15-fold. On all three datasets, AGS aligns regional with whole-mesh predictions, cutting regional error at least tenfold for most backbones on the public benchmark. Together, the modules nearly halve the best original backbone's whole-mesh error on both benchmarks. SCANO predicts a room-scale field in under one second, over 100 times faster than whole-room anchor self-attention.

</details>


### [217] [samQEq: universal charge equilibration for molecules and conductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03687)
*Nicholas Solan, David Sun, Tod A. Pascal*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: samQEq is a unified LAMMPS charge-equilibration package that combines molecular neutrality constraints with conductor-compatible self-energy to model molecules, metals, and their interfaces in one matrix-free solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard charge equilibration with a single global constraint allows unlimited charge transfer between molecules, whereas fluctuating-charge models use one neutrality constraint per molecule. Metals instead require a global constraint and hardness near zero, which makes the charge operator indefinite unless each charge's self-energy is included. The paper aims to unify these constructions for molecules, conductors, and their interfaces.

Method: The authors present samQEq, implemented in LAMMPS, using one energy functional and one matrix-free solve. An orthogonal projector enforces one neutrality constraint per molecule, while a finite-width Gaussian self-energy on designated conductor types, narrower than the conductor's own Gaussian, keeps the conductor part of the operator positive definite at any hardness. Reciprocal-space electrostatics is evaluated inside the matrix-vector product, and three shielding kernels are run-time settings.

Result: On a gold slab with a water film, the operator remains definite down to the model's conductor limit; the metal screens a point charge by a constant amount at every height beyond 4 Å, and the film's charges remain bounded under dynamics. The implementation reproduces a published fluctuating-charge water model without refitting and the native ReaxFF charge-equilibration step to approximately 1e-9 e. On a single four-V100 node, the largest tested systems run at approximately twice the throughput of a 128-core CPU node.

Conclusion: samQEq provides a single charge-equilibration layer for molecules, conductors, and the interfaces between them.

Abstract: Charge equilibration assigns atomic charges by equalizing an atomic chemical potential built from electronegativity, hardness and the Coulomb interaction. Under one global constraint it lets charge flow without limit between molecules, which fluctuating-charge models prevent with one neutrality constraint per molecule. A metal needs the opposite: a global constraint and a hardness near zero, and there the charge operator becomes indefinite unless it includes each charge's own self-energy. Here, we present samQEq (solids-and-molecules QEq), a unified charge-equilibration package for LAMMPS that combines the two constructions in one energy functional and one matrix-free solve. An orthogonal projector imposes one neutrality constraint per molecule, and the finite-width self-energy of a Gaussian charge on designated conductor types, narrower than the conductor's own Gaussian, keeps the conductor's part of the operator positive definite at any hardness. On a gold slab carrying a water film, the operator stays definite down to the conductor limit of the model, the metal screens a point charge by a constant amount at every height beyond 4 Å, and the film's charges stay bounded under dynamics. Reciprocal-space electrostatics is evaluated inside the matrix-vector product, and three distinct shielding kernels are run-time settings. We verify the implementation by reproducing a published fluctuating-charge water model without refitting, and the native ReaxFF charge-equilibration step to approximately $10^{-9}\,e$. On a single four-V100 node the largest tested systems run at approximately twice the throughput of a 128-core CPU node. samQEq thus provides one charge-equilibration layer for molecules, conductors and the interfaces between them.

</details>


### [218] [Identifying the Shear Piezoelectric Coefficient using Physics-Informed Neural Networks under Exact Boundary and Initial Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03485)
*Suhas Suresh Bharadwaj*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A constrained physics-informed neural network recovers the shear piezoelectric coefficient e15 non-destructively from sparse, noisy subsurface wavefield data by embedding exact spatial distance functions and temporal multipliers in the network output, thereby satisfying clamped, grounded boundaries and the resting initial state automatically and avoiding tunable penalty weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shear piezoelectric coefficient e15 governs shear-mode electromechanical coupling in PMUTs and SAW devices, but its measurement typically requires destructive resonance testing on dedicated specimens. The paper aims to recover e15 non-destructively from sparse, noisy subsurface wavefield observations.

Method: The authors use a constrained PINN on simulated data. Exact spatial distance functions and temporal multipliers are built directly into the network output so that clamped, grounded boundaries and the resting initial state hold automatically; the loss then contains only the interior equations of coupled electro-elastodynamics and the data misfit. Non-dimensional scaling brings governing coefficients within one decade of O(1), and the formulation removes boundary and initial-condition penalty weights required by soft-constraint methods.

Result: In a six-way comparison using plane-strain PZT-5H manufactured wavefield data with 30 dB noise, a soft-penalty baseline with adaptive weights converged to an erroneous e15 in four of five runs (51.7% mean error), while fixed penalties reached 0.85% error only after a second-order optimization stage. Constrained formulations achieved mean e15 errors of 0.15% to 0.51% with held-out displacement errors below 1%. The spatial multiplier gave rapid first-order convergence, and the quadratic temporal multiplier reduced the early-time electrostatic residual by a factor of 6.8 versus a linear one. On finite-element data along one subsurface line, the full formulation recovered e15 with 1.19% mean error at 30 dB and 0.65% to 2.71% across a 40 to 0 dB noise sweep. Training on displacements alone recovered e15 with mean errors of 0.45% on finite-element and 1.64% on manufactured data.

Conclusion: The constrained PINN formulation enables accurate, non-destructive recovery of e15 from sparse, noisy wavefield observations, outperforms soft-penalty baselines, avoids boundary and initial-condition penalty-weight tuning, and remains robust across noise levels and when trained on displacements alone. The reported evidence is based on simulated data.

Abstract: The shear piezoelectric coefficient $e_{15}$ governs the shear-mode electromechanical coupling in piezoelectric micromachined ultrasonic transducers (PMUTs) and surface acoustic wave (SAW) devices, but measuring it typically requires destructive resonance testing on dedicated specimens. On simulated data, $e_{15}$ can instead be recovered non-destructively from sparse, noisy subsurface wavefield observations using a constrained physics-informed neural network (PINN). By building exact spatial distance functions and temporal multipliers directly into the network output, the clamped, grounded boundaries and resting initial state hold automatically, leaving the loss with only the interior equations of coupled electro-elastodynamics and the data misfit. Non-dimensional scaling brings all governing coefficients within one decade of O(1), and the setup removes the boundary and initial-condition penalty weights that soft-constraint methods must tune. In a six-way comparison (plane-strain PZT-5H, manufactured wavefield, 30 dB noise), a soft-penalty baseline with adaptive weights converges to an erroneous $e_{15}$ in four of the five runs (51.7% mean error), and fixed penalties reach an error of 0.85% only after a second-order optimization stage. The constrained formulations reach mean $e_{15}$ errors between 0.15% and 0.51% with held-out displacement errors below 1%. Within these variants, the spatial multiplier gives rapid first-order convergence, and the quadratic temporal multiplier lowers the early-time electrostatic residual by a factor of 6.8 versus a linear one. On finite-element data along one subsurface line, the full formulation recovers $e_{15}$ with 1.19% mean error at 30 dB and keeps it between 0.65% and 2.71% across a 40 to 0 dB noise sweep. Finally, training on displacements alone still recovers $e_{15}$ with mean errors of 0.45% on finite-element and 1.64% on manufactured data.

</details>


### [219] [Fracture-Bench: A Controlled Benchmark Suite for Machine Learning Surrogates in Phase-Field Fracture Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03473)
*Meenu Krishnan U., Maryam Hakimzadeh, Indrashish Saha, Meer Mehran Rashid, Lori Graham-Brady, Somdatta Goswami*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Fracture-Bench is an open benchmark comparing FNO, DeepONet, Latent DeepONet, UNet and a conditional diffusion model on two phase-field fracture datasets with fixed splits and common metrics. Architecture rankings are task-dependent: FNO and Latent DeepONet perform best on Dataset 1, while the diffusion model is most accurate but far slower on Dataset 2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine-learning surrogates are increasingly proposed to replace phase-field fracture simulations for many-query tasks such as uncertainty quantification, inverse analysis and design, but there is little controlled evidence showing which architectures are reliable, for which fracture problems, and how far they generalize beyond their training data.

Method: The authors introduce Fracture-Bench, an open benchmark with two phase-field datasets, fixed splits, common test metrics and descriptions of each dataset's physical regime. Dataset 1 contains 1,000 AT2 simulations of multiple cracks growing from a notched hole in a hyperelastic plate under random non-uniform biaxial boundary displacements, with 44 load steps on a 128x128 grid. Dataset 2 contains 4,000 PF-CZM simulations of a pre-cracked plate with two inclusions that are each ten times stiffer or more compliant than the matrix, on a 128x256 grid. FNO, DeepONet, Latent DeepONet, UNet and a conditional diffusion model are trained on identical splits and compared using one test metric per dataset, training time, inference time and model size.

Result: On Dataset 1, FNO and Latent DeepONet achieve mean test errors of order 10^-6, about ten times lower than UNet and DeepONet. On Dataset 2, the diffusion model is most accurate, with about one sixth of the UNet error, but requires 706 s per prediction versus 6 ms for UNet. For all architectures, most error occurs in the final load steps after crack localization. Dataset 2 includes initial cracks less than one grid cell apart that produce qualitatively different paths. Architecture rankings therefore depend on the task, and errors averaged over densely sampled inputs reveal little about near-bifurcation cases.

Conclusion: The benchmark shows that no single architecture is universally best for phase-field fracture surrogates and that rankings depend on the fracture task and evaluation regime. Averaged test errors can obscure near-bifurcation behavior, so benchmarking should include such cases and generalization splits. Data, codes, splits, regime descriptors and generalization splits are public, with a protocol for adding under-represented regimes such as mixed-mode, three-dimensional and experimentally paired fracture.

Abstract: Machine-learning surrogates are increasingly proposed to replace phase-field fracture simulations in many-query tasks such as uncertainty quantification, inverse analysis and design. Little controlled evidence shows which architectures are reliable, for which fracture problems and how far beyond their training data. We introduce Fracture-Bench, an open benchmark of two phase-field datasets with fixed splits, common test metrics and a description of each dataset's physical regime. Dataset~1 contains 1,000 AT2 simulations of multiple cracks growing from a notched hole in a hyperelastic plate under random non-uniform biaxial boundary displacements (44 load steps, $128\times128$ grid). Dataset~2 contains 4,000 PF-CZM simulations of a pre-cracked plate with two inclusions, each ten times stiffer or more compliant than the matrix ($128\times256$ grid). We train a Fourier neural operator (FNO), DeepONet, Latent DeepONet, a UNet and a conditional diffusion model on identical splits and compare one test metric per dataset, training time, inference time and model size. On Dataset~1, FNO and Latent DeepONet attain mean test errors of order $10^{-6}$, about ten times lower than UNet and DeepONet. On Dataset~2, the diffusion model is most accurate, with about one sixth of the UNet error, but requires \SI{706}{s} per prediction versus \SI{6}{ms} for UNet. For all architectures, most of the error occurs in the final load steps, after crack localization. Dataset~2 contains initial cracks less than one grid cell apart that produce qualitatively different paths. Architecture rankings therefore depend on the task, and errors averaged over densely sampled inputs reveal little about near-bifurcation cases. Data, codes, splits, regime descriptors and generalization splits are public, with a protocol for adding under-represented regimes such as mixed-mode, three-dimensional and experimentally paired fracture.

</details>


### [220] [Noise-resilient collective order through temporal memory in self-propelled particles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03003)
*Mihir Durve, Sauro Succi, Antonio Celani*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies how temporal memory in Vicsek-type collective-motion models both filters noise and impedes adaptation, using two memory implementations and comparing stationary order with transient recovery. Deeper effective memory improves noise resilience and restores polar order where memoryless models are disordered, but recovery after perturbation is fastest at intermediate memory and slows with excessive persistence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temporal memory can stabilise collective motion by filtering noisy local observations, but persistent information can also impede adaptation when the collective state changes. The paper investigates this competition in Vicsek-type systems.

Method: Two distinct alignment rules are used: a finite buffer of past observations and a recursive update of an internal directional state. The stationary responses of the two models are compared when parameterised by a kernel-based effective memory, and transient dynamics following a collective perturbation are examined.

Result: In both models, increasing effective memory shifts the loss of polar order to stronger noise and can restore ordered motion under conditions for which the corresponding memoryless Vicsek model is disordered. The stationary responses show an approximate correspondence when parameterised by kernel-based effective memory, consistent with temporal averaging playing a common role rather than the details of memory implementation. Transient dynamics are not determined by effective memory alone: recovery is fastest at an intermediate memory depth and becomes slower when outdated observations are retained too strongly.

Conclusion: Temporal integration produces a trade-off: deeper memory improves noise resilience, whereas excessive persistence reduces responsiveness. Memory depth is identified as a control parameter that separates the optimisation of stationary order from that of dynamical recovery in noisy active systems.

Abstract: Temporal memory can stabilise collective motion by filtering noisy local observations, but persistent information can also impede adaptation when the collective state changes. We investigate this competition in Vicsek-type systems using two distinct alignment rules: a finite buffer of past observations and a recursive update of an internal directional state. In both models, increasing effective memory shifts the loss of polar order to stronger noise and can restore ordered motion under conditions for which the corresponding memoryless Vicsek model is disordered. The stationary responses of the two models show an approximate correspondence when parameterised by a kernel-based effective memory, consistent with temporal averaging playing a common role in their ordering behaviour rather than from the details of the memory implementation. Their transient dynamics, however, are not determined by effective memory alone. Following a collective perturbation, recovery is fastest at an intermediate memory depth and becomes slower when outdated observations are retained too strongly. Temporal integration therefore produces a trade-off: deeper memory improves noise resilience, whereas excessive persistence reduces responsiveness. These results identify memory depth as a control parameter that separates the optimisation of stationary order from that of dynamical recovery in noisy active systems.

</details>


### [221] [Site-orbit masked twin-branch Transformer for symmetry-constrained crystal generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02906)
*Junchi Liu, Haoran Si, Luo Yan, Sergei Tretiak, Liujiang Zhou*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: ST2M is a symmetry-guided discrete–continuous diffusion framework with a twin-branch Transformer and Wyckoff-pattern conditioning that generates crystal structures with high validity and can satisfy stability, uniqueness, and novelty criteria, yielding 21 previously unreported stable candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models for crystal structure prediction have expanded the search space and improved screening efficiency, but simultaneously generating stable and novel structures remains challenging because of complex structural and physical constraints governing crystals.

Method: The paper proposes ST2M, a site-orbit masked twin-branch Transformer model. It is a symmetry-guided discrete–continuous diffusion framework incorporating degree-of-freedom masks and conditioned on Wyckoff patterns. ST2M represents crystal structures as sequences of independent occupied Wyckoff orbits and uses a twin-branch Transformer to jointly denoise discrete and continuous variables.

Result: ST2M achieves 96.37% structural validity. For known patterns in the dataset, 23.86% of generated structures simultaneously satisfy stability, uniqueness, and novelty criteria, a relative improvement of 68.15% over the best-performing baseline. First-principles calculations identify 21 previously unreported stable structures among selected candidates with low predicted energies. ST2M also generalizes to unknown Wyckoff patterns.

Conclusion: ST2M can generate chemically viable crystal candidates under prescribed crystallographic constraints and provides a practical route to accelerated materials discovery.

Abstract: Generative models for crystal structure prediction (CSP) have expanded the search space for novel materials and improved screening efficiency. However, simultaneously generating stable and novel structures remains challenging because of the complex structural and physical constraints governing crystals. To address this challenge, we propose site-orbit masked twin-branch Transformer model (ST2M), a symmetry-guided discrete--continuous diffusion framework incorporating degree-of-freedom masks and conditioned on Wyckoff patterns. ST2M represents crystal structures as sequences of independent occupied Wyckoff orbits and employs a twin-branch Transformer to jointly denoise discrete and continuous variables. In our experiments, ST2M achieves a structural validity of 96.37\%. For known patterns in dataset, 23.86\% of generated structures simultaneously satisfy stability, uniqueness, and novelty criteria, representing a relative improvement of 68.15\% over the best-performing baseline. First-principles calculations identify a total of 21 previously unreported stable structures among selected candidates with low predicted energies. These results demonstrate that ST2M can generate chemically viable crystal candidates under prescribed crystallographic constraints and generalize to unknown Wyckoff patterns, providing a practical route to accelerated materials discovery.

</details>


### [222] [Maximal correlation and optimal transformations of probability distributions in tensor-train format: a variational algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02731)
*Kayo Kinjo, Jun Ohkubo*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a variational tensor-train algorithm for computing the Hirschfeld-Gebelein-Rényi maximal correlation and its optimal transformations from a discrete joint distribution without forming the full configuration matrix. It updates one tensor core at a time in closed form, maintains feasible iterates with a monotone lower bound, scales linearly in the number of variables, and is demonstrated on small exact cases, synthetic angular-variable distributions, and a bipartite antiferromagnetic model with 2^32 configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard subsystem correlations depend on a pair of observables chosen in advance. The Hirschfeld-Gebelein-Rényi maximal correlation removes this choice by maximizing over all functions of subsystem A and subsystem B, with the maximizing functions serving as the observables that carry the correlation. Computing it requires a singular value decomposition of the joint distribution normalized by its marginals, whose matrix dimension is the number of configurations; the paper aims to avoid forming this matrix for large discrete systems such as Boltzmann distributions of spin models.

Method: The maximal correlation is formulated through the SVD of the joint distribution normalized by its marginals. For classical discrete-variable distributions supplied in tensor-train (matrix-product) format, the SVD is computed variationally: the optimal transformations are represented as tensor trains, each step updates a single core in closed form, and division by the marginals is performed core by core using the inverse of a small matrix contracted from the distribution and the remaining cores. Every iterate is a feasible pair, so the objective is a monotonically increasing lower bound on the maximal correlation, and the computational cost grows linearly with the number of variables.

Result: The algorithm reproduces the exact SVD on small systems, recovers known nonlinear optimal transformations for synthetic distributions with up to ten angular variables and 10^15 configurations, and for the bipartite antiferromagnetic model with 2^32 configurations returns an optimal transformation that is a function of the group magnetization: linear above the transition and sign-like below it. Thus the staggered order parameter is read off from the distribution rather than postulated.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that Hirschfeld-Gebelein-Rényi maximal correlation and its optimal transformations can be computed variationally in tensor-train format without ever forming the full normalized joint-distribution matrix, with monotone feasible updates and linear scaling in the number of variables. The demonstrated cases show exact recovery on small systems, recovery of known nonlinear transformations, and extraction of an order parameter directly from a large spin-model distribution.

Abstract: The correlation between two subsystems $A$ and $B$ of a many-body system is usually measured through a pair of observables chosen in advance. The Hirschfeld-Gebelein-Rényi maximal correlation removes the choice: it is the largest correlation coefficient between any function of the configuration of $A$ and any function of the configuration of $B$, and the two functions that reach it, the optimal transformations, are the observables that carry the correlation. Both follow from a singular value decomposition (SVD) of the joint distribution normalized by its marginals, a matrix whose dimension is the number of configurations. We show that this SVD can be computed variationally for a classical distribution of discrete variables, such as the Boltzmann distribution of a spin model, given in the tensor-train (matrix-product) format, without ever forming the matrix. The optimal transformations are sought as tensor trains; each step updates a single core in closed form, and the inverse of a small matrix contracted from the distribution and the remaining cores performs the division by the marginals core by core.
