Every iterate is a feasible pair, so the objective is a lower bound on the maximal correlation that increases monotonically, and the cost grows linearly with the number of variables. The algorithm reproduces the exact SVD on small systems, recovers the known nonlinear optimal transformations of synthetic distributions of up to ten angular variables ($10^{15}$ configurations), and, for the bipartite antiferromagnetic model with $2^{32}$ configurations, returns an optimal transformation that is a function of the group magnetization, linear above the transition and sign-like below it: the staggered order parameter is read off from the distribution instead of being postulated.

</details>


### [223] [Quantum simulation of dissipative acoustic and elastic waves by Schrödingerisation: absorbing boundaries and explicit polynomial-size circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02357)
*Hoang Anh Nguyen, Ali Tura*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs quantum circuits for open-domain acoustic and elastic wave equations by adding a graded sponge-layer absorber and simulating the resulting dissipative dynamics with Schrödingerisation, using one auxiliary variable; it derives the full PDE-to-circuit chain in 1D, 2D and 3D, shows exact recovery on the nonnegative auxiliary half-line, periodic truncation, a success probability of about 0.4 times the unabsorbed energy fraction with only one or two auxiliary qubits measured, and an exact polynomial-size factorisation of the damping step into multi-controlled phase gates, validated against classical finite-difference solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit quantum circuits obtained by Hamiltonian simulation of the first-order acoustic and elastic wave equations conserve energy and therefore model closed domains, so outgoing waves reflect from the computational boundary or re-enter periodically. The work aims to open the domain using a simple physical absorber and to implement the resulting non-unitary dissipative dynamics efficiently on a quantum simulator.

Method: A graded sponge layer damps every wave field at a rate increasing toward the outer edge. The dissipative dynamics are implemented by Schrödingerisation, which converts a non-unitary linear evolution into a family of unitary evolutions using a single auxiliary variable. The authors derive the chain from the partial differential equation to the circuit in one, two and three dimensions. An independent finite-difference solver compares full-field damping with velocity-only damping. The damping step is factorised exactly into polynomially many multi-controlled phase gates rather than generic synthesis with exponentially many controlled-NOT gates, and gate-level simulations are performed on up to 25 qubits.

Result: Damping all wave fields in the sponge absorbs far better than the common velocity-only damping mask in the finite-difference comparison. For the sponge, quantum recovery is exact on the entire nonnegative half of the auxiliary axis at every time; that axis can be truncated periodically at a length determined by the largest damping rate and the simulated time; and the success probability is a fixed constant of about 0.4 times the fraction of energy not yet absorbed, with only one or two auxiliary qubits measured. The damping step factorises exactly into polynomially many multi-controlled phase gates, despite generic synthesis requiring exponentially many controlled-NOT gates. Gate-level simulations on up to 25 qubits agree with classical solutions of the damped wave equations.

Conclusion: The paper provides an end-to-end quantum-circuit construction for open-domain acoustic and elastic wave equations using a graded sponge absorber and Schrödingerisation. It obtains sharp auxiliary-variable recovery and truncation properties, a constant-factor success probability with few auxiliary qubit measurements, and polynomial gate scaling for the damping step, with numerical agreement between quantum simulations and classical damped-wave solutions.

Abstract: Explicit quantum circuits for the acoustic and elastic wave equations, obtained by Hamiltonian simulation of their first-order forms, conserve energy and therefore describe closed domains: an outgoing wave reflects off the edge of the computational region or, with periodic boundaries, wraps around and re-enters from the opposite side. We open the domain with the simplest physical absorber, a graded sponge layer in which every wave field is damped at a rate that grows towards the outer edge, and implement the resulting dissipative dynamics on a quantum simulator by Schrödingerisation, which turns a non-unitary linear evolution into a family of unitary ones by means of a single auxiliary variable. We derive the whole chain from the partial differential equation to the circuit, in one, two and three dimensions. An independent finite-difference solver confirms that damping all wave fields absorbs far better than the common velocity-only damping mask. For the sponge the quantum analysis is sharp: recovery is exact on the whole non- negative half of the auxiliary axis at every time; that axis can be truncated periodically at a length set by the largest damping rate and the simulated time; and the success probability is a fixed constant, about 0.4, times the fraction of the energy not yet absorbed, with only one or two auxiliary qubits measured. On the circuit side, the damping step, which generic synthesis compiles into a number of controlled-NOT gates exponential in the number of qubits, factorises exactly into polynomially many multi-controlled phase gates. Gate-level simulations on up to 25 qubits agree with the classical solutions of the damped wave equations.

</details>


### [224] [Structure-Preserving Full Approximation Scheme for Real-Space Hartree-Fock Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00880)
*Yuejia Zhang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops FAS-H, a structure-preserving full approximation scheme for real-space restricted Hartree-Fock equations, combining FAS cycles with a residual-triggered horizontal Newton correction on the finest grid; it reports 4-11 cycle convergence on eight closed-shell systems and 1.60-5.02x speedups over SCF across 24 system-grid comparisons, with 68-91% less weighted FFT work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state a broader application motivation, but it motivates a multilevel treatment because analysis of the full self-consistent response identifies an elliptic kinetic principal part. The method also aims to handle orbital equations, Coulomb and exchange pair potentials, and orthonormality constraints as a coupled nonlinear system while preserving constraint compatibility and occupied-rotation equivariance under suitable assumptions.

Method: A structure-preserving full approximation scheme (FAS) is developed for the real-space restricted Hartree-Fock equations. The orbital equations, Coulomb and exchange pair potentials, and orthonormality constraints are treated as a coupled nonlinear system on a hierarchy of finite-element spaces. Forced Gram targets, occupied-frame alignment, and auxiliary-variable reconstruction provide constraint compatibility and occupied-rotation equivariance under suitable assumptions. The FAS-H solver combines FAS cycles with a residual-triggered horizontal Newton correction on the finest grid.

Result: Numerical experiments on eight closed-shell systems show convergence in 4-11 cycles across three grid levels. Across 24 system-grid comparisons, FAS-H is 1.60-5.02 times faster than SCF and uses 68-91% less weighted FFT work. Standalone horizontal Newton is faster than FAS-H in some cases.

Conclusion: FAS-H provides a structure-preserving multilevel solver for real-space restricted Hartree-Fock equations and is empirically faster than SCF on the reported system-grid comparisons, with substantially reduced weighted FFT work. The abstract does not provide broader conclusions beyond these numerical experiments; it also notes that standalone horizontal Newton can be faster in some cases.

Abstract: We develop a structure-preserving full approximation scheme (FAS) for the real-space restricted Hartree-Fock equations. The method treats the orbital equations, Coulomb and exchange pair potentials, and orthonormality constraints as a coupled nonlinear system on a hierarchy of finite-element spaces. Forced Gram targets, occupied-frame alignment, and auxiliary-variable reconstruction provide constraint compatibility and occupied-rotation equivariance under suitable assumptions. Analysis of the full self-consistent response identifies an elliptic kinetic principal part, motivating the multilevel treatment. The resulting solver, FAS-H, combines FAS cycles with a residual-triggered horizontal Newton correction on the finest grid. Numerical experiments on eight closed-shell systems show convergence in 4-11 cycles across three grid levels. Across 24 system-grid comparisons, FAS-H is 1.60-5.02 times faster than SCF and uses 68-91% less weighted FFT work, while standalone horizontal Newton is faster in some cases.

</details>


### [225] [ROSE: Deep Learning exchange-correlation functional from reduced density-matrix representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00826)
*Kirill Kulaev, Alexander Ryabov, Bogdan Protsenko, Alexander Guda, Alexander Soldatov, Evgeny Burnaev, Vladimir Vanovskiy*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: ROSE is a deep-learning exchange-correlation energy functional using the occupied-state density matrix; it outperforms matched neural-network functionals and is competitive with or better than most hybrid and range-separated hybrid functionals on GMTKN55 and additional benchmarks, with leading accuracy on the considered experimental benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation. It implies an aim to improve learned exchange-correlation functionals by using nonlocal density-matrix representations and to assess ROSE against neural-network, hybrid, and range-separated hybrid functionals.

Method: The authors introduce ROSE, a deep-learning exchange-correlation energy functional built from the occupied-state density matrix. The abstract does not provide further architectural or training details.

Result: On GMTKN55 and additional external benchmarks considered, ROSE improves over modern neural-network functionals trained under matched protocols and is on average more accurate than, or on par with, most hybrid and range-separated hybrid functionals considered. On the experimental benchmarks considered, it has leading accuracy across hybrids, range-separated hybrids, and neural functionals.

Conclusion: The results support nonlocal density-matrix representations as a route to systematic improvement of learned exchange-correlation functionals with low computational cost.

Abstract: We introduce ROSE, a deep-learning exchange--correlation energy functional built from the occupied-state density matrix. On GMTKN55 and on additional external benchmarks considered in this work, ROSE improves over modern neural network functionals trained under matched protocols and is on average more accurate than, or on par with, most hybrid and range-separated hybrid functionals considered here. On the experimental benchmarks considered here it has leading accuracy across hybrids, range separated hybrids, and neural functionals. These results motivate nonlocal density-matrix representations as a route to systematic improvement of learned XC functionals with low computational cost.

</details>


### [226] [Transient subtraction method: An alternative approach for variance reduction in dissipative particle dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00330)
*Xinyi Wu, Xiaocheng Shang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends the transient subtraction method from Langevin dynamics to dissipative particle dynamics to reduce variance in transport coefficient estimates, demonstrating improved statistical precision for mobility and shear viscosity in a Lennard-Jones fluid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient and accurate computation of transport coefficients in dissipative particle dynamics is numerically challenging because it can require substantial sampling to control statistical uncertainty. The authors aim to adapt a variance-reduction approach previously used in Langevin dynamics to DPD.

Method: The transient subtraction method initializes the system by perturbing equilibrium samples and monitors relaxation toward equilibrium under the reference dynamics. A reference trajectory, initialized at equilibrium and driven by the same Brownian noise, serves as a control variate for the transient response. The method is applied to mobility and shear viscosity in a Lennard-Jones fluid in DPD; shear viscosity is obtained from the time-integrated response of a Fourier observable associated with longitudinal velocity.

Result: Numerical comparisons with the naive transient estimator show that the transient subtraction method reduces variance at various integration times. The reduction is more pronounced with a higher friction coefficient.

Conclusion: The transient subtraction method can improve the statistical precision of transport coefficient estimations in DPD and reduce the number of trajectories needed to attain a prescribed standard error.

Abstract: The efficient and accurate computation of transport coefficients remains a numerical challenge in dissipative particle dynamics (DPD), which can require substantial sampling to control statistical uncertainty. We extend recent results obtained in Langevin dynamics to consider the transient subtraction method for reducing the variance of transport coefficient estimations in DPD. The system is initialised by perturbing equilibrium samples, and its relaxation towards equilibrium is monitored under the reference dynamics. A reference trajectory, initialised at equilibrium and driven by the same Brownian noise, provides a control variate for the transient response. We apply the transient subtraction method to the computation of the mobility and the shear viscosity in a Lennard-Jones fluid in DPD. The shear viscosity is obtained from the time-integrated response of a Fourier observable associated with the longitudinal velocity. Numerical comparisons with the naive transient estimator show that the transient subtraction method reduces the variance at various integration times, and the reduction is more pronounced with a higher friction coefficient. These results indicate that the transient subtraction method is able to improve the statistical precision of transport coefficient estimations in DPD and reduce the number of trajectories needed to attain a prescribed standard error.

</details>


### [227] [A $θ$-implicit hybrid particle-in-cell algorithm for kinetic simulations of magnetized plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00127)
*Prabhat Kumar, William J. White, Stephen E. Clark*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a θ-implicit hybrid particle-in-cell algorithm in WarpX that advances fields and particles as one nonlinear system, eliminates particles from the linear Newton–Krylov iterations, and demonstrates improved energy behavior and stability on wave, beam, pinch, and field-reversed-configuration tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid PIC models with kinetic ions and an inertialess electron fluid are widely used for magnetized plasmas at ion scales, but explicit time advances are constrained by the whistler-wave CFL condition Δt ∝ Δx². Sub-cycling and artificial dissipation used to mitigate this constraint carry accumulating costs.

Method: The authors develop a θ-implicit hybrid PIC algorithm, implemented in the open-source GPU-capable WarpX code. Fields and particles are advanced as a single nonlinear system solved with a particle-suppressed Jacobian-free Newton–Krylov method. Particles are nonlinearly eliminated, the electric field from the generalized Ohm’s law is the sole unknown, and mass matrices remove all particle operations from the linear iterations. Electron pressure is advanced inside the nonlinear solve. The scheme satisfies a discrete energy law with a single dissipation term that vanishes at θ=1/2 and, for θ>1/2, damps only temporally under-resolved modes.

Result: The advance reproduces parallel and perpendicular wave dispersion, including ion Bernstein modes; conserves total energy to machine roundoff where the explicit advance heats secularly; does not develop the cold-beam finite-grid instability; and reproduces the m=0 instability of a Bennett pinch in RZ geometry. The paper also reports 3D field-reversed-configuration simulations initialized from a Grad–Shafranov equilibrium, comparing explicit and implicit advances for tilt-instability growth and trapped magnetic flux preservation.

Conclusion: The θ-implicit hybrid PIC formulation provides a nonlinear-solve-based advance with the stated discrete energy property and demonstrated wave, stability, and pinch results, and it is applied to 3D field-reversed-configuration simulations comparing implicit and explicit evolution of the tilt instability and trapped flux. The abstract does not provide quantitative growth rates or flux-retention values.

Abstract: Hybrid particle-in-cell (PIC) models with kinetic ions and an inertialess electron fluid are widely used to simulate magnetized plasmas at ion scales, but explicit time advances are constrained by the whistler-wave CFL condition $\dt \propto Δx^2$, and the sub-cycling and artificial dissipation used to mitigate it carry accumulating costs. We present a $θ$-implicit hybrid PIC algorithm, implemented in the open-source GPU-capable code WarpX, in which fields and particles are advanced as a single nonlinear system solved by a particle-suppressed Jacobian-free Newton--Krylov method: the particles are nonlinearly eliminated, the electric field of the generalized Ohm's law is the sole unknown, and mass matrices remove all particle operations from the linear iterations. The electron pressure is advanced inside the nonlinear solve, and the scheme satisfies a discrete energy law whose single dissipation term vanishes at $θ= 1/2$ and damps only temporally under-resolved modes for $θ> 1/2$. The advance reproduces parallel and perpendicular wave dispersion including the ion Bernstein modes, conserves total energy to machine roundoff where the explicit advance heats secularly, does not develop the cold-beam finite-grid instability, and reproduces the $m=0$ instability of a Bennett pinch in RZ geometry. We conclude with 3D simulations of a field-reversed configuration initialized from a Grad--Shafranov equilibrium, comparing the explicit and implicit advances on the growth of the tilt instability and the preservation of trapped magnetic flux.

</details>


### [228] [Mercury (Hg): a relativistic $N$-body simulation for Horndeski gravity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03637)
*Ahmad Nouri-Zonoz, Farbod Hassani, Julian Adamek, Emilio Bellini, Martin Kunz*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Mercury is a relativistic N-body particle-mesh code for cosmological simulations in the general linear Horndeski α-basis, evolving the extra scalar degree of freedom and modified metric dynamics alongside nonlinear matter. It is validated against hi_class in the linear regime and used to show how a running effective Planck mass affects gravitational slip, vector/tensor friction, and the effective dark-energy density contrast.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to extend previous nonlinear implementations of Horndeski subclasses, specifically k-essence and kinetic gravity braiding, to the general linear Horndeski α-basis. The goal is to evolve the additional scalar degree of freedom and its effective stress-energy together with nonlinear matter and metric fields so that nonlinear structure formation can source scalar-field evolution and feed back on gravitational potentials.

Method: The authors present Mercury, a relativistic N-body code based on a particle-mesh scheme. It incorporates the dynamics of the additional scalar degree of freedom and the modified Einstein equations expressed through the time-dependent functions α_K, α_B, α_M, α_T and the effective Planck mass M_*^2. Mercury interfaces with hi_class to support both phenomenological α-function parametrisations and covariant Horndeski theories. The implementation is validated against hi_class in the linear regime and checked against expected nonlinear k-essence and kinetic gravity braiding limits; demonstrations use nKGB, the Cubic Galileon, and Brans-Dicke models.

Result: Mercury reproduces hi_class results in the linear regime and recovers the expected nonlinear k-essence and kinetic gravity braiding limits. For the luminal Horndeski models considered, a running effective Planck mass generates gravitational slip through the scalar anisotropic stress and modifies the friction terms governing vector and tensor metric perturbations. The evolution of M_*^2 also modifies the normalisation and scale dependence of the effective dark-energy density contrast, leaving an imprint on its power spectrum.

Conclusion: The framework provides an intermediate step towards nonlinear Horndeski simulations in which nonlinear scalar self-interactions and screening effects can be incorporated consistently within the same relativistic framework.

Abstract: We present Mercury, a relativistic $N$-body code for cosmological simulations in the $α$-basis formulation of Horndeski gravity. Mercury extends a particle-mesh scheme by incorporating the dynamics of the additional scalar degree of freedom and the corresponding modified Einstein equations, expressed in terms of the time-dependent functions $α_{\rm K}, α_{\rm B}, α_{\rm M}, α_{\rm T}$ and the effective Planck mass $M_*^2$. This generalises our previous implementation of Horndeski subclass models, namely $k$-essence and kinetic gravity braiding (KGB), to the general linear Horndeski $α$-basis. The code evolves the additional scalar degree of freedom and its effective stress--energy tensor alongside the nonlinear matter and metric fields, so that nonlinear structure formation sources the scalar-field evolution and feeds back on the gravitational potentials. Through its interface with hi_class, Mercury supports both phenomenological $α$-function parametrisations and covariant Horndeski theories; we demonstrate the latter using nKGB, the Cubic Galileon, and Brans--Dicke models. We validate the implementation against hi_class in the linear regime and recover the expected nonlinear $k$-essence and KGB limits. For the luminal Horndeski models considered here, we show how a running effective Planck mass generates gravitational slip through the scalar anisotropic stress and modifies the friction terms governing the vector and tensor metric perturbations. The evolution of $M_*^2$ also modifies the normalisation and scale dependence of the effective dark-energy density contrast, leaving a corresponding imprint on its power spectrum. The present framework provides an intermediate step towards nonlinear Horndeski simulations, in which nonlinear scalar self-interactions and screening effects can be incorporated consistently within the same relativistic framework.

</details>


### [229] [Machine-Learned Interatomic Potential for the Mo-S-Al-O System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02403)
*Emir Bilgili, Nicholas Taormina, Simon R. Phillpot, Youping Chen*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The authors develop and validate a machine-learned neuroevolution potential for the Mo-S-Al-O system to model MoS2 heteroepitaxy on c-plane sapphire and enable atomic-scale studies of defect formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work targets MoS2 heteroepitaxy on c-plane sapphire, alpha-Al2O3(0001), and aims to enable future studies of the atomic-scale mechanisms of defect formation in two-dimensional heteroepitaxy.

Method: A neuroevolution potential (NEP) is trained for the Mo-S-Al-O system on 63,611 PBE+D3 structures distilled from about 1.1 million curated DFT configurations, using an energy-difference loss function that directly targets interfacial and orientation-dependent energies. It is validated against DFT for physical properties, interfaces, edges, defects, twin boundaries, grain boundaries, and stacking faults.

Result: On held-out data, energy and force errors are 29 meV/atom and 0.23 eV/angstrom. The NEP reproduces lattice constants within 0.3%, elastic constants within 17%, phonon dispersions with mean deviations below 2.5%, and surface energies within 1.3%. At the MoS2/sapphire interface, it reproduces the adhesion energy minimum within 0.8%, interfacial energy within 2.2%, and the most stable monolayer orientation. It preserves DFT-predicted energetic ordering of edges, point defects (Spearman rho = 0.995), and mirror-twin boundaries (rho = 1.0), and reproduces tilt grain-boundary energies within 8% (rho = 0.98) and stacking-fault energy barriers within 14%.

Conclusion: The resulting potential enables future studies of the atomic-scale mechanisms of defect formation in two-dimensional heteroepitaxy.

Abstract: We develop a machine-learned neuroevolution potential (NEP) for the Mo-S-Al-O system, targeting MoS$_2$ heteroepitaxy on c-plane sapphire, $α$-Al$_2$O$_3$(0001). The potential is trained on 63,611 PBE+D3 structures distilled from about 1.1 million curated density-functional-theory (DFT) configurations, using an energy-difference loss function that directly targets interfacial and orientation-dependent energies. On held-out data, the energy and force errors are 29 meV/atom and 0.23 eV/$\mathring{A}$. Validated against DFT on a suite of physical properties, the NEP reproduces the lattice constants within 0.3 %, the elastic constants within 17 %, the phonon dispersions with mean deviations below 2.5%, and the surface energies within 1.3 %. At the MoS$_2$/sapphire interface, it reproduces the adhesion energy minimum within 0.8 %, the interfacial energy within 2.2 % and the most stable orientation of the monolayer. It preserves the DFT-predicted energetic ordering of edges, point defects (Spearman $ρ$ = 0.995) and mirror-twin boundaries ($ρ$ = 1.0), and reproduces tilt grain-boundary energies within 8 % ($ρ$ = 0.98) and stacking-fault energy barriers within 14 %. The resulting potential enables future studies of the atomic-scale mechanisms of defect formation in two-dimensional heteroepitaxy.

</details>


### [230] [Atomistic Insights into MOCVD Growth of GaAs using a Closed Loop Autonomous System Combining Directed Reinforcement Learning and Kinetic Monte Carlo Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01646)
*Hrishi V. Harsora, Ashutosh Krishna Amaram, Raghavan Ranganathan, Tarun K. Agarwal*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a closed-loop framework that calibrates Kinetic Monte Carlo growth parameters directly from experimental data using a degree-of-rate-control-guided Monte Carlo Tree Search, demonstrated for GaAs MOCVD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional MOCVD process optimization requires iterative, resource-intensive experiments. Predictive modeling is limited because continuum and first-principles methods struggle to balance experimental length/time scales with atomistic accuracy. Kinetic Monte Carlo bridges this gap but depends on kinetic rate parameters that normally require costly DFT calculations.

Method: A forward KMC growth model is coupled in a closed loop with a Monte Carlo Tree Search optimizer that navigates the high-dimensional parameter space. The search is steered by the degree of rate control of each reaction rate parameter, and rate parameters are calibrated directly against experimental growth data without material-specific assumptions. The approach is demonstrated for GaAs MOCVD.

Result: The calibrated model captures the characteristic temperature dependence of the measured GaAs growth rate and recovers three well-known kinetic regimes characterized by a change in the rate-determining step from precursor chemisorption to desorption.

Conclusion: The framework converts routinely measured growth data into a mechanistically interpretable model of the deposition process and is applicable to any deposition process whose forward kinetics can be reduced to a set of rate-controlled events.

Abstract: Conventional process optimization of the Metal-Organic Chemical Vapor Deposition (MOCVD) growth parameters to achieve high-quality thin films relies on iterative, resource-intensive experimental runs. While predictive modeling can circumvent this cost and effort, simulating thin-film growth is constrained by the vastly different length and time scales of the deposition process. While continuum (e.g., Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD)) and first-principles methods (e.g., Molecular Dynamics (MD)) struggle to balance experimental length/time scales and atomistic accuracy, Kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) bridges this divide by resolving atomistic events whilst remaining tractable at experimental length and time scales. However, the accuracy of KMC relies on kinetic rate parameters that require costly density functional theory (DFT) calculations. In order to circumvent this, we present a framework that calibrates the rate parameters directly against experimental growth data. A forward KMC growth model is coupled in a closed loop with a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) optimizer that navigates the high-dimensional parameter space, with the search steered by the degree of rate control of each reaction rate parameter. The approach invokes no material-specific assumptions and hence, any deposition process whose forward kinetics can be reduced to a set of rate-controlled events is amenable to the same treatment. We demonstrate it for the MOCVD of GaAs, where the calibrated model captures the characteristic temperature dependence of the measured growth rate. The framework is also able to recover the three well known kinetic regimes characterized by change in the rate determining step from precursor chemisorption to desorption. As a result, the framework converts routinely measured growth data into a mechanistically interpretable model of the deposition process.

</details>


### [231] [Optimally Weighted Constrained Shadow Tomography for Reduced Density Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01079)
*Yuchen Wang, Irma Avdic, Michael Rose, Lillian I. Payne, Noah Garrett, Madhubani Mukherjee, Leah P. Weisburn, Nate Earnest-Noble, Kevin J. Sung, David A. Mazziotti*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper improves constrained shadow tomography for reconstructing reduced density matrices by optimally weighting orthogonalized measurement constraints, and demonstrates more accurate 2-RDM reconstruction without extra measurements for hydrogen chains and azobenzene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate measurement of reduced density matrices is central to quantum simulations of many-fermion systems, but measurement resources remain a significant challenge. Constrained shadow tomography reconstructs physically valid RDMs from limited data, motivating further improvement in measurement efficiency and reconstruction accuracy.

Method: The authors orthogonalize the measurement constraints in constrained shadow tomography and use the resulting singular values to assign relative weights to independent measurement directions according to how strongly each is sampled by the quantum computer. These optimally weighted constraints are incorporated into a semidefinite program through a least-squares objective implemented with a Schur-complement relaxation.

Result: The approach is demonstrated for hydrogen chains on a quantum simulator and for azobenzene on a quantum processor. In both cases, optimally weighted constrained shadow tomography systematically improves the accuracy of the reconstructed 2-RDM without increasing the measurement budget.

Conclusion: Optimally weighted constrained shadow tomography provides a more measurement-efficient approach to partial tomography for quantum simulations of correlated fermions.

Abstract: Accurate measurement of reduced density matrices (RDMs) is central to quantum simulations of many-fermion systems, but the required measurement resources remain a significant challenge. Constrained shadow tomography (CST) combines randomized measurements with $N$-representability conditions to reconstruct physically valid RDMs from limited measurement data. Here we improve CST by orthogonalizing the measurement constraints and using the resulting singular values to determine the relative weight of each independent measurement direction according to how strongly it is sampled by the quantum computer. These optimally weighted constraints are incorporated into the semidefinite program through a least-squares objective implemented with a Schur-complement relaxation. We demonstrate the approach for hydrogen chains on a quantum simulator and for azobenzene on a quantum processor. In both cases, optimally weighted CST systematically improves the accuracy of the reconstructed 2-RDM without increasing the measurement budget, providing a more measurement-efficient approach to partial tomography for quantum simulations of correlated fermions.

</details>


### [232] [Efficient Interpolation of the Bethe-Salpeter Hamiltonian at Arbitrary Momenta](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00928)
*Stefano Mocatti, Xiangzhou Zhu, Giulio Volpato, Pierluigi Cudazzo, Matteo Calandra, Giovanni Marini*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient reciprocal-space approach that reconstructs static Bethe-Salpeter Hamiltonians at arbitrary crystal momenta from coarse first-principles grids, benchmarks it on hBN, MoS2, and GaAs, and demonstrates accuracy, speedups, and access to long-wavelength exciton dispersion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to overcome the limitations of conventional uniform first-principles sampling for excitonic calculations by decoupling electron-hole transition momenta and transferred momentum from the underlying ab initio grid, thereby enabling exciton energies and amplitudes at arbitrary crystal momenta.

Method: Bloch states are interpolated within an optimally smooth Wannier subspace, and the screened Coulomb interaction is reconstructed from the dielectric response. This allows reconstruction of the static Bethe-Salpeter Hamiltonian at arbitrary crystal momenta from coarse first-principles grids. The method is benchmarked against explicit ab initio calculations for monolayer hBN, monolayer MoS2, and bulk GaAs.

Result: The method shows excellent agreement for dense-grid Bloch states, dielectric screening, and BSE optical spectra, with estimated speedups of up to 22 times on the grids considered. In hBN and MoS2, arbitrary transferred-momentum sampling resolves nonanalytic behavior from long-range electron-hole exchange at momenta well below practical uniform-grid spacing. For bulk GaAs, locally refined transition-momentum sampling gives a converged binding energy for the threefold-degenerate lowest exciton, in close agreement with experiment and reference calculations, using fewer BSE points than conventional uniform meshes.

Conclusion: Because the framework provides exciton energies and amplitudes at arbitrary crystal momenta, it enables incorporation of ab initio excitonic effects into nonlocal electron-phonon vertices and supports extensions toward exciton-phonon interactions and nonequilibrium exciton dynamics.

Abstract: We develop an efficient reciprocal-space method to reconstruct the static Bethe-Salpeter (BSE) Hamiltonian at arbitrary crystal momenta from coarse first-principles grids. Bloch states are interpolated within an optimally smooth Wannier subspace, while the screened Coulomb interaction is reconstructed from the dielectric response. This decouples the electron-hole transition momenta and transferred momentum from the underlying ab initio sampling. We benchmark the method on monolayer hBN, monolayer MoS$_2$, and bulk GaAs against explicit ab initio calculations, finding excellent agreement for dense-grid Bloch states, dielectric screening, and BSE optical spectra. Furthermore, our cost analysis estimates speedups of up to $22\times$ on the grids considered here. Arbitrary transferred-momentum sampling gives direct access to the long-wavelength exciton dispersion: in hBN and MoS$_2$, we resolve the nonanalytic behaviour induced by long-range electron-hole exchange at momenta well below the spacing of practical uniform grids. For bulk GaAs, locally refined transition-momentum sampling yields a converged binding energy for the threefold-degenerate lowest exciton, in close agreement with experiment and reference calculations, using far fewer BSE points than conventional uniform meshes. As the present framework provides exciton energies and amplitudes at arbitrary crystal momenta, it opens the way to incorporating ab initio excitonic effects into nonlocal electron-phonon vertices and to extensions towards exciton-phonon interactions and nonequilibrium exciton dynamics.

</details>


### [233] [A geometry-aware framework for electromagnetic fields in GATE 10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00376)
*Marc Ballestero Ribó, Carla Winterhalter, Antony John Lomax, Sairos Safai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: GATE 10 gains a geometry-aware electromagnetic-field framework that leaves Geant4 transport unchanged; it reproduces native Geant4 results and shows compiled field representations are substantially faster than Python-function fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: More medical-physics applications need charged-particle transport modelling in electromagnetic fields. The authors wanted to give GATE 10 users a flexible way to define such fields in Monte Carlo simulations while retaining Geant4's native particle-transport machinery.

Method: The framework separates coordinate handling from electromagnetic-field evaluation. Fields are attached to geometry volumes, defined in each volume's local frame, and represented by analytic expressions, field maps, or arbitrary Python functions. They follow volume position and orientation, including nested and dynamic geometries. The authors validated against native Geant4 transport, tested coordinate transformations, nested volumes, and dynamic geometry, and compared the computational cost of the three representations.

Result: The framework exactly reproduced native Geant4 trajectories for all tested field types with direct Geant4 counterparts. Coordinate transformations introduced only floating-point-level differences, and dynamically changing geometry exactly reproduced equivalent static configurations. All three representations produced equivalent transport. In single-threaded runs, mapped fields added at most 18% to analytic-field runtime, whereas Python functions required 6.9 times as much. Compiled representations scaled with thread count, while Python functions became slower as threads were added.

Conclusion: The framework provides flexible, geometry-aware electromagnetic-field definitions for GATE 10 without modifying the underlying Geant4 transport machinery. It is part of the open-source GATE 10 codebase and provides a basis for adding further field representations in future.

Abstract: Objective. An increasing number of medical-physics applications require the modelling of charged-particle transport in electromagnetic (EM) fields. We aim to provide GATE 10 users with a flexible mechanism for defining such fields in Monte Carlo simulations while preserving the native Geant4 particle-transport machinery.
  Approach. Our framework separates the handling of coordinates from EM field evaluation. Fields are attached to geometry volumes, defined in their local frame, and represented by analytic expressions, field maps, or arbitrary Python functions. They automatically follow the position and orientation of these volumes, including in nested and dynamic geometries. We validated the framework against native Geant4 transport and tested coordinate transformations, nested volumes, and dynamic geometry. We also compared the computational cost of the three field representations.
  Main results. The framework reproduced the native Geant4 trajectories exactly for all tested field types with direct Geant4 counterparts. Coordinate transformations introduced only floating-point-level differences, while simulations with dynamically changing geometry reproduced the equivalent static configurations exactly. All three representations produced equivalent transport. Mapped fields added at most 18% to the single-threaded runtime of analytic fields, while Python functions required 6.9 times as much. The compiled representations scaled with the number of threads, whereas Python functions became slower as threads were added.
  Significance. The framework adds flexible, geometry-aware EM field definitions to GATE 10 without modifying the underlying Geant4 transport machinery, and is part of the open-source GATE 10 codebase. It also provides a basis for extending GATE 10 with additional field representations in the future.

</details>
